- We see this in a different maamar chazal which talks about for people who are born red and have a desire for blood. The advice to them is to become a shochet or a mohel, to avoid becoming a robber or murderer. We don't just tell the person to control himself.[^128]
- It seems that there is only so much one is able to squelch techunos. As such, the way to deal with it is try to channel it properly as opposed to fighting against it.[^128]
- It is mistaver to assume that a person should try to some degree to limit one's speech (in line with the many other maamarei chazal) but it a person with this techuna is not going to be able to totally smother it, and so they have to utilize it.[^128]
- Once they are doing that, they should try and make sure that they are using this properly.[^128]
- I don't quite understand the difference between middos and techunos. Are techunos just middos which are way more entrenched into a person?[^7]
- In English, we would probably refer to them as character traits versus personality traits. (This doesn't answer the question in the previous point. I'm just putting this out there).[^7]
- The daily activity schedule: make two columns: prospective and retrospect. Each row should represent one hour of your day.. In the prospective column make a plan for your day. In the retrospective column write what you actually did during that time no matter what it was even if it was just staring at the walls.[^129]
- when writing what you did in the retrospective column write the letter M or P. m (mastery) is for accomplishment type tasks. This can be anything from applying for a job to brushing your teeth.[^129]
- P (pleasure) is for fun activities..[^129]
- By each of these tasks also add a number Between one and five. For mastery type of tasks this number is a rating of how difficult it was. For pleasure taped tasks the number is how much pleasure one receipt from the task.[^129]
- This helps in multiple ways. Firstly one proves themselves that they can actually be more productive than they originally thought. In addition it also shows oneself that things are more fun and rewarding then they thought. In addition it also helps one actually do things because doing nothing can be extremely damaging and depressing.[^129]
- this is particularly important for people who live alone and particularly on weekends and holidays when a person is less likely to intermingle with other people.[^129]
- the anti procrastination sheet: If someone is struggling with procrastination they can use the following method.[^130]
- On a piece of paper one writes down a task that they are procrastinating. This text should be broken down into subtasks.[^130]
- Before doing a each subtask, write down a level of expected difficulty between zero and 100 and expected satisfaction between zero and 100.[^130]
- after completing the subtask write down the actual level of difficulty with and actual satisfaction Between zero and 100.[^130]
- a person may be amazed by how Negatively off base their predictions were.[^130]
- This helps a person disregard the thoughts that get a person to procrastinate.[^130]
- Daily Record of Dysfunctional Thoughts: This method has been discussed before for dealing with irrational thoughts (see there).[^131]
- If someone has a chiddush, they should not be quick to discard it, even in the face of a strong question.[^126]
- A mashal for this is if someone has what they think may be a precious stone they have evaluated. If the evaluator says that the stone is worthless, the person isn't going to throw the stone away. The person will obviously continue to do research to hopefully find out that the evaluator was wrong. This is because the stone has become precious in the eyes of the owner.[^126]
- The same is with a chiddush. After working on something and coming out with a chiddush, it becomes precious to you, so you shouldn't be so quick to disregard it.[^126]
- There is a Medrash that discusses Rav Yochanan walking past some fields and the like that he sold to be able to learn. Rav Yochanan was happy to see them. He didn't feel bad about the fact that he had to sell his valuable assets to learn Torah. Instead, he had tremendous appreciation for what he was able to get in exchange for these worldly possessions. We need to try and develop this appreciation for the Torah (especially the chiddushim that we sweat and toiled over).[^132]
- Rav Dovid was known to give shiur with tremendous excitement because of his tremendous love of the Torah.[^132]
- Pleasure Prediction Sheet: Make a few columns. One is to write down a fun activity you are going to do. One for a prediction for how much pleasure you think you are going to get from a particular task. Another for how much pleasure you actually got. And another for who you did it with (if it was by yourself, write "self").[^133]
- This can be useful for dispelling some false beliefs as discussed before. In addition, this can also be used for dispelling the belief that you need to be doing things with others to have a good time. (Obviously, the things have to be on the same level. If you go to a fancy restaurant with friends and compare that to staying home by yourself and eating a microwave dinner, of course the activity alone is going to be worse.)[^133]
- What if you predict that you won't have a good time and you are right? This probably comes from negative thoughts (like if you are eating in a restaurant by yourself, you might be thinking that people are looking at you and thinking that you are a loser that has no friends).[^133]
- Use other methods as previously described to try and deal with these negative false beliefs.[^133]
- If you constantly shoot down any compliment you give yourself, do the same things that have been discussed, Write down the negative thoughts which you don't compliment and a rational response to that thought.[^134]
- Mandate of Palestine was given to Britain at the Paris Peace Conference.[^135]
- Menachen Ussishkin became a leader of the Jews in Palestine. He made sure the Sefaric Jews in Palestine were respected by the Ashkenazim immigrants.[^135]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Need to do more research into the Mandate system</span>}
- In the Northern Galilee after WWI, Jews were caught between the French and local Arabs fighting.[^135]
- The British took over in 1919, but the Arabs continued to attack Jewish settlements.[^135]
- Joseph Trumpeldor was sent to organize defenses beginning in 1920.[^135]
- He was killed while defending the village of Tel Hai.[^135]
- He became somewhat of a national legend.[^135]
- The four years following WWI became known as the Third Aliyah. 35,000 Jews immigrated to Israel during this time.[^136]
- Many of these people has socialist leanings and they set up moshavs.[^136]
- The first moshav was called Nahalal, set up in the Jezreel Valley.[^136]
- Over the next few years, a few more moshavim began in the valley, including at least one religious one.[^136]
- The Jewish National Fund bought some land near Mount Gilboa in the Jezreel Valley for kibutzim, which started being created there.[^136]
- The kibutzim here formed the Labor Legion, which advocated for a single countrywide commune.[^136] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Were they advocating for communism/socialism? Also, why did they form kibutzim instead of moshavim</span>}
- Some kibutzim were able to form good relationships with their Arabic neighbors. For example, Kiryat Anavim was on good terms with the Arabic-Muslim village of Abu Ghosh for many years.[^137]
- Menachem Ussishkin led a push to build cities between Jerusalem and the coast to keep it linked to other Jewish settlements. This became known as the Jerusalem Corridor.[^137]
- In 1920, the Jewish National Fund purchased 40 square km of land in the Jezreel Valley (called the Emek). This was the fund's largest purchase until that time. Many thought the Jews overpaid for the land.[^137]
- In 1920, many disparate Labor Parties joined together to form the General Federation of Jewish Labor (the Histadrut). David Ben-Gurion became a leader of the group.[^138]
- They prioritized sharing supplies and training people to work.[^138] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't understand what the party stood for.</span>}
- In 1920, there were 500,000 Arabs to 65,000 Jews.[^139]
- The Arabs were already upset by the Jewish immigration and that continued.
- Arabs rioted in 1920 and 1921.[^139]
- In response, the Histadrut set up the Haganah in March 1921. This was a country-wide defensive organization.[^139]
- They had to train members and smuggle in arms (which was illegal under British law).[^139]
- Over this time, many settlements, and the Jewish quarter in Jerusalem were attacked.[^139]
- In a particularly bad week of rioting in 1921, 47 Jews were killed.[^139]
- Numerous Jewish settlements were destroyed, such as Kfar Malal.[^139]
- To appease the Arabs, the British High Commissioner issued a temporary ban on Jewish immigration.[^139]
- Arabs saw that violence could lead to results.[^139]
- In 1921 Haj Amin Al-Husseini, a leader of the 1921 riots, was appointed as Mufti (the senior Muslim cleric). He was supposed to be a moderating voice, but he was not.[^139] (I don't know why they thought he would be a moderate.)
- He pressed the British to fully stop Jewish immigration.[^139]
- Over time, his power and influence in the Arabic world grew.[^139]
- Economic conditions were not easy for Jews in Palestine, partially because of the riots and partially because the land was not fertile.[^139] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I assume that there were other reasons as well. Also, I am not sure if this is specific to this time post WWI, or really since the beginning of Jewish settlement.</span>}
- The Mandate authorities issued a law that immigration was dependent on "economic absorption capacity" of Palestine, meaning, that immigration was dependent on whether Palestine could economically support the immigrants.[^140]
- Even the Zionists somewhat acknowledged the issue with widespread immigration and the harsh economic conditions.[^140]
- During this time, anti-Semitism increased greatly in Eastern Europe. 100,000 Jews were killed in Ukraine after WWI followed by the the rise of communism there, which did not allow for freedom of religion. The Polish government issued antisemitic laws. [^140]
- Also, the US started imposing immigration restrictions.[^140]
- As a result, immigration to Israel stayed high in the early 1920s.[^140] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know what happened with the "economic absorption capacity" rule.</span>}
- Arabs had a delegation in England to try and persuade the English to restrict the flow of Jewish immigrants.[^141]
- This was largely unsuccessful. This was because of article four of the League of Nations Mandate for Palestine which granted power to a Jewish organization {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't really understand the details about what power they were given</span>}, which was ratified by the League Council in 1922. The organization which was chosen for this was the World Zionist Organization, which became known as the Jewish Agency.[^141]
- The British government released the Churchill White Papers which gave support to the Jews.[^141]
- At this point, there were around 85000 Jews in Palestine, or 11%, compared to around 750000 Arabs.[^141]
- Russia began making immigration difficult (nothing to do with the Arabs).[^141]
- In the mid-1920s there began being More of a focus on Urban settlements as opposed to rural ones.[^141] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know why this was</span>}
- This gave increased power to the Histadrut.[^141] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">which didn't really represent farmers it seems. I would need to check that</span>}
- In 1924 israel De Haan Tried setting up an ultra Orthodox settlement.[^141]
- He was willing to get support from enemies of Zionist such as Emir Hussein.[^141]
- He was going to go to Britain to support the anti-Zionists when he was assassinated by the Haganah. Many Zionist leaders later condemned this.[^141]
- The Hebrew University was established in 1924.[^141]
- In 1924, groups of religious Jews increased their immigration to Palestine and started settlements, such as Kfar Chassidim.[^141]
- During these years, there was a lot of development in the Jezreel Valley (like the building of a hospital, and many settlements).[^141]
- Most immigrants during the mid to late 1920s were Polish, but there were Jewish immigrants from all over, including some Arabic countries, like Iraq and Kurdistan.[^141]
- The tremendous influx of immigrants was too much for the country to handle. There was not enough work for all of the immigrants. Also, there wasn't enough money to pay for all of the necessary construction to deal with all of the new immigrants. Money largely came from the Jewish community outside of Israel, and the movement was somewhat losing its excitement.[^141]
- This led to an even worse financial situation.[^141]
- In 1927, there were twice as many people leaving the country then were coming in.[^141]
- The Jews in Palestine hoped to have the majority of the population over the Arabs someday, but it seemed unrealistic at this point. It did not seem like the country could handle enough immigrants in a given year to actually catch up to the Arabs.[^141]
- There were various ideas about economic structures. Some groups pushed for full Marxist, while others were (at least mostly) capitalist. Some wanted kids to be raised by their parents and families to eat at home, while others wanted to raise kids in group homes and have collective meals.[^142]
- To try and get more money, the Jewish Agency tried to get a loan from the British government in 1928. Sir Arthur Balfour and Winston Churchill supported it, although the British cabinet ultimately rejected it.[^142]
- In 1928, there was an increase in Arab violence toward Jews. This was caused by  a rumor circulated by the Mufti that the Jews were going to try to get the Al-Aqsa Mosque.[^142]
- This led to the Arabs to push for restrictions on Jews praying and making speeches at the Western Wall.[^142]
- Numerous Jewish settlements needed to be abandoned because of the rioting, such as Migdal Eder.[^143]
- Between the economic hardships, the seemingly practical limit on immigration, and the Arab riots, the mood amongst Jews in Palestine was very bleak.[^142]
- In the late 1920s, however, archeological finds were being found, such as the Beit Alpha Shul. This led to inspiration and wonder and caused a renewed hope amongst the Jews.[^142]
- Even if the questions seem powerful, one still shouldn't give in so easily. One needs to realize that sometimes, a small maneuver can answer many difficult questions, and can even turn them into proofs.[^144] 
- In 1929, the Jewish Agency expanded its members to include those outside of Palestine to try to raise funds.[^145]
- This was unsuccessful, largely because the Great Depression began.[^145]
- Despite this, the Arabs felt threated by this expansion of the Agency.[^145]
- This led to massive riots from the Arabs.[^145]
- Between August 23 and August 29, thousands of Arabs rioted, looting many Jewish homes, forcing the abandonment of numerous Jewish settlements, such as Kfar Uriyyah and killing 133 Jews.[^145]
- 87 Arabs were killed during this time as well, mostly by British troops trying to stop the violence.[^145]
- There were also instances of Jewish violence towards Arabs in retaliation although it was far less than the amount of Arab violence toward Jews, according to a British report at the time.[^145]
- After the violence, there was still extremely strong feelings of antisemitism amongst the Arabs.[^146]
- In September and October, numerous pamphlets were circulated, calling the Jews "your strongest enemy" and called for boycotts on Jewish stores.[^146]
- Some of the pamphlets were distributed to foreign Arabic countries.
- The President of the Arab Executive in Palestine, Musa Kazim Pasha, told a British official that unless the British stopped supporting the Jews in Palestine, there would be an armed uprising from the Arabs. In addition, Arabs from Syria and Transjordan would help as well.[^146]
- The Arabs were stockpiling weapons.[^146]
- Some Jews, such as Arthur Ruppin and Judah Magnes, wanted to make peace with the Arabs. They wanted to create a bi-national state with Jews and Arabs living peacefully, side by side, both part of on state. They formed an organization called Brit Shalom.[^146]
- The Zionist leaders were not pro this. They wanted to create a Jewish state.[^146]
- There was diverse political opinion. Some wanted a more Communist-style approach, while others, like the Mapai, Mifleget Poalei Eretz Yisroel (The Party of the Workers of Eretz Yisroel) wanted a socialist system.[^147]
- Some wanted to create a country immediately, even though that would mean that Arabs would have to be included in the country, while others wanted an all Jewish country, and they would be willing to wait to create a country.[^147]
- The British government sent a commission to Palestine following the riots of 1929 to try and understand what was bothering the Arabs.[^147]
- Numerous Jews tried to convince the commission that the Zionists just wanted Jews and Arabs to have neighboring countries alongside each other and that there was enough resources to accomplish this.[^147]
- However, the commission didn't view it that way. They released a report, which became known as the White Papers, in March 1930 saying that the Arabs had legitimate concern that they would end up under a Jewish state and be deprived of livelihood.[^147]
- Also, the Jews were not following the "economic capacity of Palestine" rule (discussed earlier).[^147]
- The British wanted to reign in immigration.[^147]
- The Arabs wanted more, though. They wanted an end to the Balfour Declaration, an end to Jewish immigration, and a prhibition on the sale of land to Jews.[^147]
- A second report said that there was not enough land based on the current farming methods to support the Jewish immigrants. As such, it wanted to further cap Jewish immigration.[^147]
- Chaim Weizmann resigned from his position as the President of the Zionist organization and the Jewish Agency in protest and had meetings with British officials where he voiced his displeasure.[^147]
- He discussed the fact that many countries were closing their borders to immigration and the countries of Eastern Europe were passing antisemitic policies.[^147]
- Because of this, the British agreed to not put these suggestions into practice, but said that the Zionists could not continue to replacing existing Arab laborers with Jewish laborers.[^147]
- The Arabs were gaining political ground with Britain.[^147]
- Jews did not want to be a minority under an Arab-controlled country. Zionists were coming from Eastern Europe where they were a minority and the results were catastrophic. A major point of Zionism was the self-determination aspect.[^147]
- The British did not want to offend the Jews and they did not want to offend the Arabs as the British had relationships with Arab countries and they had about 60,000,000 Muslims living in British-controlled India.[^147]
- The 1929 riots did not curb immigration.[^148]
- In response to the riots, a group of Jews thought that the only way to deal with the Arabs was with violence.[^148]
- They created a group called Betar and Jabotinsky was the leader.[^148] {According to [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betar) this group was in Poland.}
- Mainstream Zionists criticized it as having fascist character, like Mussolini's Blackshirts.[^148] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know what this means.</span>}
- The group focused on military training for its members.[^148]
- The membership went from around 22,000 in 1931, to around 90,000 by 1938.[^148]
- Overall, many Jews became pessimistic about the prospect of peace with the Arabs, such as the aforementioned Ruppin.[^149]
- By the early 1930s, Russian immigration to Israel dried up due to new laws in Russia.[^149]
- By the early 1930s, the Jews in Palestine had some scientific institutions and had done some agricultural research.[^149]
- This somewhat helped with the food supply.[^149]
- Some Jews wanted to overthrow the British government and take control of the state to be able to bring in unlimited amounts of migrants and to deal with the threat of Arab violence.[^149]
- There were 192,137 Jews and 1,073,827 Arabs in Palestine according to a British census in 1932.[^149]
- Poland became increasingly antisemitic, causing many Jews to (attempt to) make aliyah.[^150]
- In 1933, Hitler came to power in Germany, leading to tremendous antisemitism there. This led to a tremendous influx of German Jews to Palestine, as the British were being somewhat lax about immigration.[^150]
- In the summer of 1933, the Mapai leader, Chaim Arlosoff was murdered.[^150]
- Revisionist Zionism focuses on trying to expand territory into Transjordan and has a strong leaning toward military training.[^151] Betar was a group that was a part of this movement.[^152] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I may need to do more research into this</span>}
- The Revisionists were fine with committing crimes for the sake of the State of Israel.
- The Revisionists disagreed strongly with the Labor Party. The Revisionists felt that is was utmost importance to create a state and to acquire lots of land.[^153]
- A member of Jabotinsky's Revisionists was accused of the crime.[^150]
- Eventually, though, he was acquitted due to a lack of evidence.[^150]
- Even though it wasn't proven that the murder was done by a Revisionist, this did strengthen the strong schism between the Labor Parties and the Revisionist Zionism movement.[^150]
- Due to the increased immigration, the Arabs rioted against British public buildings in October of 1933. 26 Arabs and one British policeman were killed.[^150]
- There were still some continued efforts to make peace.[^154]
- In 1934, Ben-Gurion and Dr Magnes met with Auni Abdul Hadi, a leader of Palestinian Arabs.[^154]
- Ben-Gurion wanted to have a majority Jewish population in Palestine (he wanted to have an eventual 4,000,000 jews to 2,000,000 Arabs) with settlements on both sides of the Jordan.[^154]
- In exchange for allowing for Jewish independence, Ben-Gurion offered to help with arab independence and unity.[^154] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Not exactly sure what is meant by this. It seems to refer to the fact that there was British and French influence over at least some Arabic countries which the Arabs wanted to get rid of and establish their place.</span>}
- according to Ben-Gurion, At first, Abdul Hadi liked, the deal as he was very excited about Arab unity. However, after a little bit he lost enthusiasm.[^154]
- He asked about what guarantees would he have and if the jews would help the Arabs get rid of the British and French.[^154]
- Ben Gurion responded that they would figure out guarantees and that while the Zionists did not like a lot about what the British did, the British had helped sometimes and they didn't want to lose that help. as such, the jews would not fight.[^154] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I'm not sure what exactly is meant by this. Perhaps military protection? I assume what they didn't like about the British were the restrictions on immigration</span>}
- Ben-Gurion offered to help build up Arabic education, culture, and help develop Arabic countries.[^154] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">not sure wat that means</span>}
- Dr Magnes felt that this would unfortunately mean that the Arabs would have to sacrifice the Palestinian Arabs, but it would help the Arabs overall.[^154]
- Ben Gurion felt that the Palestinian Arabs would not be sacrificed as the Arabs, while they would be the minority in Palestine, would still be the majority when zooming out to the whole area (meaning, including all the Arabs in the surrounding lands).[^154]
- however, it was essential that the jews would be the majority somewhere. this was important as it would give a place for jews to be able to immigrate to from other countries and also give a place for jews to avoid discrimination.[^154]
- According to Ben-Gurion, Abdul Hadi agreed with both these points (that the Arabs would mot really be a minority and the jews did need a place that they were the majority).[^154]
- According to Ben-Gurion, Abdul Hadi said that the Arab masses did not feel such unity, but the intellectuals did view the Arabs as a singular people.[^154]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">The book does not discuss how the meating went, but it would seem that it didn't lead to an agreement.</span>}
- In 1934, 42,000 Jews immigrated to Palestine, mostly from Germany, Poland, and Romania due to rising antisemitism. This was the most in a single year.[^155]
- The Revisionist Movement tried to smuggle in several hundred illegal immigrants on boats.[^155]
- Most of these boats were stopped by the British navy.[^155]
- The British felt stuck in the middle: the Arabs wanted a total block on immigration, and the Jewish extremists wanted no limits on immigration at all. In addition, they also wanted to create a Jewish state. This was something that the mainstream Zionists may have wanted as well, but not something they were willing to openly say. (This is more than the "Jewish National Home" that was promised in the Balfour Declaration.)[^155] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't really understand the difference between the "Jewish National Home" and a Jewish state.</span>}
- The Jewish Agency employed only Jews in the major Zionist enterprises {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">don't know what is meant by that</span>}. This angered the Arabs.[^155]
- The Haganah smuggled more weapons into Israel. A crate of weapons broke open one time. The Arab dockworkers when on strike in protest of the imported weapons and other Arabs made demonstrations.[^155]
- In response, the Jewish Agency increased the land reserved for Arabs in the Huleh Basin.[^155]
- In 1935, 61,000 immigrants came to Israel, smashing the previous year's record.[^155]
- The British thought that this may have crossed the "economic absorption capacity" threshold (the Arabs certainly agreed to that).[^155]
- A British Royal Commission in 1937 led by Lord Peel said that this was not an issue, as increased immigration just increased the needs of the country and therefore increased the needs for work. (I assume that the book will talk more about this later.)[^155]
- Cultural life for jews in Palestine was blooming. In 1936 there were the "Jewish Olympics" in Palestine (Olympics with Jews from many countries). Many Jews illegally stayed after the Olympics were over. The Palestine orchestra was established. Many writers and poets were coming to Israel from many countries.[^156]
- Between 1934 and 1936, the Jewish population went from about 234,000 to about 384,000, which was from just over 20% to just under 30%.[^156]
- The Arabs started striking in protest on April 15 1936.[^156]
- In addition, Arabs killed 3 Jews.[^156]
- A small group called the Irgun Zvai Leumi (national military organization, also called the Irgun, or Etzel) {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">may have been part of the Revisionist Zionist movement or perhaps just lined up with their goals, although I don't know if the Revisionists would have totally been on born with the methods of the Irgun</span>} killed some Arabs in retaliation.[^156]
- The Jewish Agency denounced the killings by the Irgun.[^156]
- Arabs then started killing more Jews and destroying Jewish property.[^156]
- On May 7th, Arabs demanded an immediate halt on all Jewish immigration, an Arab majority government, and a ban on Jewish land purchases.[^156]
- Over a few months, thousands of acres of farmland was destroyed. Many Jewish communities had to flee, such as the Jews in the town of Acre.[^156]
- By October, 80 Jews, 140 Arabs, and 33 British policemen were killed.[^156]
- The Haganah established mobile patrols to launch preventative strikes against potential attacking forces.[^157]
- In May 1937, Britain sent a commission to investigate the cause of the unrest. Lord Peel was appointed chairman of the commission and Sir Horace Rumbold was appointed as his deputy.[^156]
- The commission visited many Jewish and Arab locations in Palestine.[^156]
- The Jews believed that the Peel Commission would recommend setting up two separate states.[^158]
- On one hand, Jews wanted an independent state. On the other, they were scared that if they didn't have a lot of land, they would be overrun by the surrounding Arabs.[^158]
- During this time, Jews came up with a new way to build settlements. They would work extremely quickly and at night. In a few days time (or even over just one night), they would have a settlement totally built.[^158]
- In addition, they would build a stockade around the settlement as well as a watchtower.[^158]
- In 1937, defense was a major focus of the Zionists. As such, there was considerable military training. Soldiers were trained to do night patrols and launch counterattacks on Arabic villages.[^159]
- The British worked together with the Jewish soldiers. They supplied the Jews with trucks and armored cars.[^159]
- Yitzchak Landsberg (who later changed his name to Sadeh) set up Jewish mobile patrols were in specific areas to defend specific settlements.[^159]
- When the passuk says "וירח את ריח בגדיו"[^160] (referring to when Yitzchak smelled the garments of Yaakov when he was about to give him the bracha) it means that Yitzchak saw the "traitors" (בוגדיו) of Klal Yisroel. This is a reference to Yosef Meshisa.[^161]
- When the Romans conquered the Beis Hamikdash, they wanted to send a Jew inside (as a ginny pig of sorts). They sent in Yosef Meshisa, who was a Jew but a traitor. The Romans told him that in exchange for going in, he could take one thing from the Beis Hamikdash.[^161]
- Yosef Meshisa went in and took out the menorah.[^161]
- The Romans didn't want to give him that (it seems they didn't expect him to take that) and they told him to go in again and take something else.[^161]
- At this point, Yosef Meshisa started feeling charata and he refused to go back in.[^161]
- The Romans tortured him to try to get him to go back in but he stayed firm and refused to go back in. He kept saying "woe is me that I angered my creator". Eventually the Romans killed him.[^161]
- This is what Yitzchak saw in Yaakov. Not all of the great tzadikim and talmidei chachomim. He saw this.[^162]
- Every Jew has a pure neshama inside of them. This neshama can't be tainted. It can be covered by layers of dirt, but the neshama itself can't be tainted. As such, a Jew always has the ability to "detonate". They can tap into the purity of their neshama, and in one moment, make a full turnabout.[^162]
- This is what Yitzchak saw when he was about to give Yaakov the brachos: the great potential and purity inside of every Jew.[^162]
- If someone wants to know how great the reward for doing mitzvos is, they just need to look at the aveira of the eitz hadaas.[^163]
- Adam did one aveira and look at how much death was brought to the world because of it?[^163] (Everyone who has died is because of this one aveira).
- Hashem's midda of doing good is more than his midda of doing bad. As such, if for an aveira one receives such a punishment, one can only imagine the good one receives for doing mitzvos.[^163]
- We see the power of every individual person. A random person by doing a mitzva can make such a difference in the world.[^162] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I am not sure what was being added over what the maareh makom already said. Maybe rebbi was just spelling it out?</span>}
- Avraham was standing in the beis hamikdash at the time of the churban.[^164]
- Hashem came to Avraham and asked him what he was doing there? Avraham said that he was there to plead on behalf of his children.[^164]
- He then proceeded to make different pleas on their behalf, like perhaps the aveiros were done by mistake, or perhaps only part of klal Yisroel were ovver. But Hashem rejected every one of them.[^164]
- At this point, Avraham put his hands on his head and started crying. He said "is there no hope for them?"[^164]
- Hashem said that there Klal Yisroel is like an olive tree. The purpose of an olive is at its end. So, too, Klal Yisroel will be able to return later. (In other words, Klal Yisroel is going to do teshuva later).[^165]
- This is the same Avraham who appreciated the greatness of everyone. When the three malachim came to his tent (and Avraham did not know what they really were) he wanted to be able to serve them as it was an honor for Avraham to be able to serve them.[^166][^162]
- This was because Avraham had a real understanding of the greatness of every person.[^162]
- And yet, this same Avraham, even he did not have the full understanding of the depth of that greatness. When Klal Yisroel was going through the churban and getting punished for their aveiros, Avraham didn't think that they could do teshuva. And yet, they were able to.[^162]
- We see this tremendous power of the purity of the neshama. This power is so great, that it was even beyond Avraham's understanding of how great it was.[^162]
- A man from Moav is not allowed to convert to Klal Yisroel because they did not give Klal Yisroel food and drink when Klal Yisroel left Mitzraim.[^167]
- However, a women from Moab is allowed to. This is because they were not supposed to go out to Klal Yisroel to give them food and drink because ofכל כבודה בת מלך פנימה .[^167]
- The daughters of Moav were not known for their greatness. And yet, they were able to feel this hargasha of כל כבודה בת מלך פנימה. (If they didn't feel it and just decided not to greet Klal Yisroel because of the same lack of empathy as the men, they wouldn't have been any better off.)[^162]
- We see how even non-Jews have a pure neshama that can feel very lofty and proper feelings.[^162]
- Rav Yochan Ven Messiah told his son to go hire some workers. The son did and as part of the deal, arranged to give them mezonos.[^168]
- Rav Yochanan ben Messiah told his son that he should not have offered mezonos (which, I assume is somewhat vague) as the workers are the sons of Avraham, Yitzchak and Yaakov. As such, even giving them a feast like Shlomo Hamelech had would be beneath their stature.[^168]
- As such, Rav Yochanan ben Messiah told his son that if the workers had not started working yet, he should attempt to rework the agreement.[^168]
- We see how great even random Jews are. It would have been beneath their stature to receive a feast of Shlomo Hamelech.[^162] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I am not sure what was being added over what the maareh makom already said. Maybe rebbi was just spelling it out?</span>}
- Before Lavan went on his way after confronting Avraham, he gave a bracha to his daughters.[^169]
- The possuk is showing us that the bracha of Lavan was going to make a difference as Lavan was a tzelem elokim and, as he was giving his children a bracha, he did it bichol nafsho.[^170]
- Rachel and Leah had Yaakov as a husband and Yitzchak as a father-in-law. Surely if they were in need of a bracha, they could go to one of these two people. How much could a bracha from a rasha like Lavan make a difference?[^162]
- We see the power of a tzelem Elokim. Every human has the power to affect the briah so profoundly. Even Lavan's bracha added something to his daughters, despite the great people in their lives.[^162]
- If one's answer is based and logical, it is okay to say, even if there are some docheks.[^171]
- This is especially true if one is answering questions that they don't have other answers for.[^171]
- While pashtus in lashon is defnitely a virtue, it is not the only virtue. There are other things that need to be accounted for when assessing if an answer is good or not.[^172]
- Link to footnote 83.[^173]
- The Peel Commission talked with David Lloyd George (the prime minister at the time of the Balfour Declaration and the creation of the Mandate) as well as Winston Churchill about the prospect of a Jewish State.[^174]
- George said that there should not be a state set up at the time, but at some point, if the Jews become the majority, there should be the creation of a Jewish State. At the same time, the concerns of the Arabs in Palestine must be accounted for.[^174]
- Churchill also said that at some point there should be the creation of a Jewish state if Jews keep going to Palestine.[^174]
- He felt that the situation for the Arabs in Palestine would improve with the influx of Jews. The Jews would create more usable land and more jobs for everyone.[^174]
- The Peel Commission was leaning toward creating two separate states.[^174]
- The reason for separate states was because "the root of the trouble \[was] a conflict of irreconcilable national aspirations".[^174]
- The report did note that partition would be difficult to carry out.[^174]
- The British thought that most Jews would not be happy with the agreement. They felt that the mainstream part of the Jews would not be violent about it but try to use political maneuvering to make sure that the suggestion did not take place. In addition, they wouldn't restrain those who did get violent as strongly.[^174]
- The British also thought that most Arabs would be in favor, although not the Mufti, or certain "dangerous elements".[^174]
- The Jewish State proposed by the Peel Commission was smaller than the Jews hoped for.[^174]
- The Jews didn't get the Negev, the Eastern part of the Sea of Galilee, or the Rutenberg Concession on the Jordan River (which contained a hydroelectric plant).[^174]
- Arabs were afraid that they would be overwhelmed and dominated by the Jews. At the extremely high rates of immigration that the Jews were migrating to Palestine in the previous years, it would only be a matter of a generation, or perhaps just a decade.[^174]
- To assuage this, the Peel Commission recommended a max of 12,000 Jews a year, over the next 5 years.[^174]
- Britain was to retain control of Jerusalem.[^174]
- The commission proposed partition as there was no way to make a government incorporating both sides.[^174]
- Neither side was happy with the thought that they would have less land then they hoped. The Arabs weren't happy as they used to have sovereignty over the entire Palestine. The Jews wanted all the land that they had as part of their historic homeland.[^174]
- The Zionist 20th Congress debated the Commission's proposal. They agreed to the concept of partition, but not the borders proposed by the Commission.[^174]
- The Arabs rejected partition altogether. They did not accept the idea for any Jewish State.[^174]
- Violence from the Arabs broke out again, against the Jews and the British.[^174]
- Some Jewish extremists were violent against the Arabs, as well, against the wishes of the Jewish Agency.[^174]
- Other Arab States put pressure on Britain to not go through with the suggestion of the Peel Commission.[^174]
- Because of this pressure, the plan of the Peel Commission was scrapped.[^174]
- Britain decided to try and appease the Arabs. They were going to strongly limit the amount of Jews that could immigrate to Palestine, as well as not give the Jews any land that they could have exclusive sovereignty.[^174]
- Despite the prospective for a Jewish State looking grim, Jews continued building settlements.[^174]
- New settlers would be given farming training in already established settlements before embarking on their own settlement.[^174]
- Chaim Weizmann and the Jewish Agency wanted to work with the British and denounced Jewish reprisals.[^175]
- Not everyone was on board with this, though.[^175]
- The Revisionists, led by Jabotinsky, held a World Congress in 1938.[^175]
- During the meeting, they decided that they were not going to let anything stand in their way from letting them set up a majority Jewish State on both sides of the Jordan. They also described the mainstream Zionist organization as traitors.[^175]
- This group would respond to attacks by Arabs with equally violent terrorism through their military branch, called the Irgun.[^175]
- 60,000 Jews immigrated to Palestine in 1936 and 1937.[^175]
- In the much of 1938, that number dropped to 3000 due to British restrictions.[^175]
- With immigration this low, there could never be a Jewish-majority state.[^175]
- Jews made up just under 30%.[^175]
- Arab violence continued into 1938.[^175]
- Nazis started ruling Austria in 1938, putting over 200,000 people of Jewish decent under Nazi control.[^176]
- A Revisionist was arrested and executed by the British in 1938 for shooting an Arab bus (nobody was killed). This was the first Jewish person executed. This angered the Jews.[^176]
- The Irgun would kill Arabs far more effectively than the Arabs killed the Jews.[^176]
- Davar and Haaretz (newspapers) as well as Zionist leaders and the Histadrut denounced the attacks of the Irgun.[^176]
- Antisemitism continued increasing in Germany, Austria, Poland, and Romania.[^176]
- The United States decided to adhere to its quota of 27,370 Jewish immigrants a year. Many other countries (like Peru and Britain) likewise were strict about quotas.[^176]
- Only the Netherlands (Holland), Denmark, and the Dominican Republic imposed no restrictions on immigration.[^176]
- The Haganah set up an organization to arrange for illegal immigration called the Mossad le-Aliyah Bet (Organization for Immigration B).[^176] 
- The American Palestine Fund was created in 1939 to help raise money for the Jews in Israel. There were already organizations in America raising money for Israel, but this put them all together.[^177]
- A new White Paper was issued by the British in 1939.[^177]
- It allowed for 100,000 Jews to enter Palestine over the coming 5 years.[^177]
- After that point, majority rule would be instituted. This would mean that the Arabs would get legislative rights. They made it clear that they would use these rights to put an end to Jewish immigration.[^177]
- This would make it impossible for there to be a Jewish State as the Arab majority would be secured.[^177]
- Also, Jews were not allowed to buy land except in specific restricted areas.[^177]
- There were about 445,000 Jews in Palestine in 1939, compared to about 1,500,000 Arabs.[^177]
- In response to the White Papers, the Jews continued creating more settlements.[^177]
- Many members of the Peel Commission were against the strict restrictions imposed by the 1939 White Papers.[^178]
- In 1939, Britain entirely suspended immigration to Palestine, in response to all of the illegal immigration.[^178]
- omething to keep in mind is that if you are procrastinating doing simething, there is a good chance that you are picturing negative scenarios about doing it. For example, if you need to make a public speech, you might be picturing yourself stumbling over words. This is going to maked it that you atually will fail at the task. For a few minutes a day (perhaps before going to sleep at night), imagine you doing the task successfully.[^179]
- Be willing to take breaks, even if a task is not totally completed. For example, if there is a lot of housework to get dine, set a reasonable time limit for yourself on how long you are going to work for. When you reach the time limit, take a break and do something you enjoy. Even if the housewirk doesnt get totally finished because of this, it's okay.[^180]
- Telling yoursekf you must do something will cause you to resist doing it. try to motivate yourself by the fct that you want to do it. perhaps write down the reasons you have for doing something and for not doing it. this may make it easuer for you to concretize why you want to do something.[^181]
- If people push you to do something, it makes it hard to do, even if it is the right thing to do. if you decide to do something and theb someone comes by nagging you to do it, you my immediately feel a resistance to do that very thing you decided you wanted to do. a way to ight this is to respond to the person something like, "Oksy, i will do this as i have decided that is the right thing to do", or "despite your nagging, im going to do this".[^182] (This sounds a bit immature to me.[^7])
- make a list of all the reasons you want to do sometthing. then, for a few mintes a day (perhaps before going to sleep at night) fantasize about having done whatever it is you want to do and you are enjoying these rewards. for example, if you want to quit smoking, imagine jogging on a crisp autumn day in the early morning (if that is something that you want to do). try to make it as lucid as possible.[^183]
- Sometimes, you might label yourself as a "procrastinator", someone who needs others to tell them what to do. To combat this, you can buy yourself a wrist counter and every time you do something against your label (like if you do something with little procrastination or because of your own decision), increment the number. Pretty quickly, you will see that your label is not true.[^184] (Perhaps keep track of the number each day. Seeing the number increase over time will also help.[^7])
- You aren't going to just "feel motivated", so don't wait for it to happen. You need to start doing things first, despite that lack of motivation. This will cause you to feel more motivated, which will lead to more action, which will lead to more motivation…[^185]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I think summarizing the specific strategies is the way to go.</span>}
- When someone criticizes you, this triggers thoughts in your dead that make you feel bad. It isn't the other person who makes you feel bad, it is you who makes you feel bad.[^186]
- You deal with those thoughts in ways that have been described earlier.[^186]
- By dealing with the thoughts, this opens one up to a more goal oriented approach.[^186]
- Solving problems and growing makes one feel better about themselves and also helps deal with criticism.[^186]
- Also, keep in mind that their criticism may not be fair or correct.[^186]
- A few steps to dealing with critics:
- step one: be empathetic. Try to get them to be specific about what you do that bothers the person.[^187]
- This helps make the attack less all-consuming (instead of attacking your whole character, they attack something specific), it helps remove the emotional element by showing that you actually care about they have to say and it removes the "me vs you" mentality by promoting a problem-solving mindset.[^187]
- Step two: Find some way to agree with the attacker. If you feel that your attacker is correct, then it isn't hard to find something to agree with.[^188]
- If you don't agree with your attacker, you can still agree with him somewhat. For example, if your attacker says that you don't care about them and you feel that this is not true, there probably is a little bit of truth in what they are saying. You probably could care more. If you attacker says that you are not good at your job, it is probably true that you can get better at your job.[^188]
- Remember to agree with your attacker (at least a little), don't be sarcastic or defensive, and make sure that you are saying the truth.[^188]
- This can be difficult to do, especially at first. Your natural reaction is to get defensive.[^188]
- One can practice doing this in their head (picture particular situations), on a paper (write down what the attacker says and some potential responses), or with a friend (have them attack you).[^188]
- This helps take the emotion out of your attacker, as well.[^188]
- Negotiation and Reconciliation: Establish your position tactfully but firmly.[^189]
- A way to do this is to admit that you might be wrong, as you of course make mistakes, and try to make it about an objective fact.[^189]
- For example, if someone says that you didn't pay for something, you can admit that you may have forgotten to pay and that you will check your credit card statement, or they can check their Zelle history.[^189]
- This works even when you know that they are wrong.[^189]
- In a situation when you know that they are right, then you can just admit that.[^189]
- Sometimes, the person may attack you about something relating to taste, like they don't like the way you dress.[^189]
- You can respond that you hear what they are saying and that there are certainly many people who agree with them, but this is what you prefer.[^189]
- If they keep going, you can keep repeating your defense: you here what they are saying, but this is what works for you.[^189]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">This is presented as a third step, but it seems to me more details about the second step</span>}
- Some Freudian phycological modalities will tell you that this is a bad idea. They will say that this will make your anger turn inward and the best way to deal with this is to confront the the person making you angry.[^190]
- If someone is heckling you in middle of a lecture, thank the person for their comments, agree that those points are important, encourage the person to do more research on the topic, and invite them to further discuss the issue with you after the lecture.[^189]
- Novaco Anger Scale: A way of measuring how much you can deal with irritation. The test gives a short description of some irritating situation and asks you to rate your level of irritability on a scale of 0 to 4. You the add it up you answers to get your score.[^191]
- Anger, like depression, is caused by your cognitive response to an event. Other people or events can't get you angry. Only you can get yourself angry.[^192]
- The cognitive distortions discussed before apply here, but there are a few that apply in particular:
- Labeling. You label the person you are dealing with. You view them as totally terrible. You may do this sometimes to defend your self-esteem (your not the loser; he is). Obviously, this is not true. The person you are dealing with is made up of good and bad things.[^193]
- Mind-Reading: You ascribe motive to the actions the person did. Obviously, you do not actually know what the person's motivations are, and many times, you are wrong (or at least exaggerated).[^193]
- Magnification: You make the situation bigger in your mind than it actually is.[^193]
- Should and shouldn't: You have a feeling of entitlement.[^193]
- Sometimes, getting a bit angry can be useful. For example, if your child runs into the street, you may feel angry at him. This can be useful for trying to scare him from doing it again. In these situations, one can usefully channel one's anger. You just have to be careful not to overdue it, and let it go when the situation is over.[^194]
- Tips for reducing anger:
- Develop the desire: First you need to feel like you actually don't want to get angry. Sometimes, one might feel that the other person deserves your anger. This makes it hard for you to get rid of your anger because you don't want to get rid of your anger. To develop your desire to get rid of your anger, make a list of the advantages and disadvantages you get from your anger, and then make a decision based off that. Chances are, the disadvantages outweigh the advantages.[^195] (Obviously, this is not done in the heat of the moment.)
- Examine the thoughts in your head. Write them down, and then write down responses to them. For example, you might be thinking something like: "My spouse is being so insensitive". You can respond, "perhaps my spouse doesn't realize. Sometimes people don't realize things, even when they should. That is perfectly normal". Or "how can he keep coming late when he knows it is so important to me" with "we all struggle with things. I wouldn't want to be judged harshly for the things I struggle with."[^196]
- You can also use the Daily Record of Dysfunctional Thoughts (as described earlier).[^196]
- Imagination: In your minds eye, you may be thinking about what you want to do to the person that is making you angry, or you are thinking about what they did to you. This continues to make you angry. This is important to realize. You can try to distract yourself to get this off your mind.[^196]
- We have certain beliefs about what should be. We feel that if we are nice to people, they should be nice back Obviously, it would be nice if they are, but many times this is not what happens. If this is something that you really expect, this can get you angry when it doesn't happen. Write down your "rules" and then a more realistic alternative to it. (This sounds like an extension of the expectation problem).[^197]
- Some people don't want to get rid of their anger because they think it is the only way to get what they want.[^198]
- This person needs to learn alternative ways to get what they want.[^198]
- One way is to try to respond positively to behavior one likes, rather than negatively to behavior one does not like.[^198]
- Dr. Mark K. Goldstein said that this was a major point in turning around struggling marriages.[^198]
- (Obviously, this is not the only way one can get what they want. This is just an example.)[^198]
- One can also use the disarming technique discussed earlier and be tactful, as discussed earlier. At the same time, one can remain firm about what it is they want. For example, if you hire someone to do a job and they didn't do it well, you can start off by complimenting them on the things they did well. When you point out what it is that you need redone, he may disagree. You can disarm him by finding truth in what he is saying, but firmly sticking with the fact that you need the job redone.[^199]
- Only use threats and ultimatums as a last resort. Even when you do, state them matter-of-factly ("I am sorry, but I cannot pay for this until it is redone") and only use them if you are willing to follow through (you really aren't going to pay for the job until it id redone).[^199]
- Try to be empathetic to the other person. This means to try and understand what they are feeling about the decisions they are making. This is allows one to avoid mind-reading mistakes (you think that they are doing something because they don't respect you, when in reality, if you think about it, you realize that they probably were just not paying attention, for example) and helps you avoid your feeling that things should be a certain way (you understand why the person did what they did and so you realize why they didn't do what you thought they should do).[^200]
- One way to try to develop this skill is with roleplaying. You be the person who is acting in an annoying way and have your friend ask you questions about your behavior. ("Why did you do this? Is it because you don't like this person?"). When you are actually forced to give answers for the other person, you can quickly see that your assumptions about why he is doing what he is doing were ridiculous.[^201
- It can be difficult to use these methods when dealing with anger, because anger, unlike depression, just explodes inside of you. You don't have time to think and before you know it, you are out of control.[^201]
- The way to deal with this is to practice in your head ahead of time.[^201]
- Try to think of a few situations that set you off and rank them. start at the easiest one and really picture the situation. You will begin to feel like you are in the situation a little bit and you will start thinking the thoughts you would think if you were actually in the situation.[^201]
- You can now work on responses to those thoughts, as discussed.[^201]
- In addition, picture yourself handling the situation using the tricks discussed. Picture yourself using empathy, or talking to the person causing you the distress and be tactful and firm, and use disarming, for some examples.[^201]
- Remember, you don't need to use all of the tricks. Use the ones that make sense to you in given situation.[^201]
- Imagine how in control and calm you feel by doing this.[^201]
- Remember that despite your efforts, this will probably not be fully successful, or even close, for a while. Don't fall prey to perfectionist thinking. It is okay if it takes time, and appreciate improvement.[^201]
- Remember that you can ask friends for situations that they notice that you get angry.[^201]
- Anger will almost certainly not get what you want in the long-term. Even in the short-term, what passes for "respect" or "love" isn't real.[^202]
- Many times you get angry as a defense to your self-esteem.[^202]
- Of course you are allowed to get angry. It's just that this may be a bad idea.[^202]
- A talmid has to be ready to work hard to understand the words of his rebbi. He has to be willing to forsake his own understanding. That is the primary job of a talmid.[^203]
- This can be especially difficult for bright talmidim. It may be extremely difficult to reign them in because they might be able to answer any question on what they are saying, but what they are saying isn't good. Yet they won't see it. They need to be trained more to be able to see it.[^204]
- Through the generations, we get less and less seichel. As such, we have to appreciate that the words of the earlier generations are full of wisdom that are beyond us and so our job is to work on the Torah of the earlier generations.[^205]
- When one does this, Hashem will give one the ability to understand the Torah.[^205]
- Shlomo Hamelech compares Torah to a search for precious jewels.[^206]
- Just like jewels are precious because of the tremendous work it takes to uncover them, so too, the words of Torah are precious because of the effort it takes to understand them. The work put in isn't a bad thing at all. It is what gives the Torah it's value.[^205] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Isn't Torah valuable because it is the knowledge of Hashem, regardless of the work put in?</span>}
- (Also see footnote 97 which brings down the end of this Chasam Sofer.)
- Guilt comes from thinking that you did something bad, and therefore you are bad.[^207]
- This can continue into feelings of depression, continue into thoughts that you are worried that people will find out about what you did (shame and anxiety) and that maybe the person who you wronged will retaliate (anxiety).[^207]
- Either of these two points (you did something terrible, and therefore you are a terrible person) can contain possible cognitive distortions:[^207]
- Maybe the incorrect actions you did were not so bad. You are magnifying how bad they are.[^207]
- In addition, you might be blaming yourself for the reaction of someone else, when you really did everything you could do. For example, if you criticize someone, but you do it gently and you really felt it was necessary, just because the other person receives it poorly does not mean that it was your fault.[^207]
- Just because you do something bad does not bad you a bad person. You do some good things and some bad things, just like everyone else.[^207]
- Once you get rid of the second point (you don't think you are evil), you will have feelings of remorse, which is a healthy recognition that you wrongfully hurt someone.[^207]
- Feeling that you should or shouldn't have done something based on knowledge you couldn't have had at the time also lead to guilt. you can rationally respond to the absurdity of this assumption.[^208]
-  Once someone feels guilty, they can end up in a vicious cycle. Because they feel guilty, they begin to think they are guilty (emotional reasoning) which leads to more feelings of guilt…[^208]
- In addition, one may try to berate themselves because they think they deserve it, and because they think that will get them moving.[^208]
- It turns out that the opposite is true. It drains one of their energy and makes it harder to be productive which further fuels one's guilt.[^208]
- You should ask yourself if you are feeling a reasonable level of guilt for what you did wrong, or is it over the top.[^209] (Perhaps one should ask a friend for their opinion if one isn't sure.[^7])
- Are you learning from your mistake and making a plan for what to do different, or are you just beating yourself up.[^209]
- Techniques:
- You can use the daily record of dysfunctional thoughts.[^209]
- You can write down your "shoulds" and then a more reasonable "should". It may be more reasonable to replace the shoulds with "it would be nice if". Also, some of your shoulds are based on unrealistic expectations. You feel like you should be able to stick to your diet. But why should that be? If you have not worked on you self control particularly, why do you think that you are naturally going to be able to act that way? Obviously this doesn't men that you are stuck never being able to diet. It means you should work on your self-control, and realize that until you do, it isn't reasonable to expect if yourself that you will be able to act with self control as often aw you expect yourself to.[^209]
- These feelings of guilt caused by "shoulds" make it difficult to accomplish your goal that involves self-control (dieting, smoking, etc.) because you start feeling guilty because you don't live up to your "should" ideal. This makes you want to engage in the activities you do for comfort (eating or smoking). If you mess up, you feel more guilty and so you want it more…[^209]
- If you are the type of person who feels guilty about things, chances are that people try and take advantage of you to do them favors.[^210]
- Constantly giving in to the other person is not good for you or for them, either.[^210]
- You have to be able to stand up for yourself.[^210]
- A few things to keep in mind when doing this:
	1. You ae not required to give in to the other persons demands; certainly not all the time.[^210]
	2. Try to use the disarming technique and be tactful.[^210]
	3. Don't buy that the other person is as helpless as they make themselves seem.[^210]
	4. The other person may get angry at you. Don't respond angrily to them.[^210]
	5. They may storm off or temporarily withdraw from you. Be willing to accept that.[^210]
	6. You may be expecting for the situation to explode far worse than will actually happen.[^7]
- You may want to peactice this with a friend, or by writing down some of the things the other person may tell you and then write down some of the ways you can respond.[^210]
- Sometimes someone complaining to you can make you feel guilty. Offering the person advice and help may not help in these situations. The person wants to complain.[^211]
- You should totally validate and agree with the person's complaints. This will get them to quickly stop complaining.[^211]
- An additional step you might want to take is to change the topic to something related to what the person said (after agreeing and validating). For example, if someone complains about their boss, ask them about their field of work.[^212]
- Because the Federal Reserve (the Fed) controls the money supply, the chairman of the federal reserve can be considered the second most powerful person in the US after he president.[^213]
- There is no exact definition of what the money supply is.[^214]
- Generally, currency, checking accounts, savings accounts, and money in money market mutual funds {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">don't know what this is</span>} are considered to be money as they are very liquid.[^214]
- The money supply can be broken down into three groups: the monetary base, M1, and M2.[^214]
- The monetary base includes bank reserves held at the federal reserve and currency.[^214]
- M1 includes currency and checking accounts.[^214]
- M2 includes everything in M1 plus savings accounts and money market mutual funds.[^214]
- The Federal Reserve has the most direct impact on the monetary base, but also some impact on M1 and M2.[^214]
- The money multiplier:
- When you make a deposit in a bank, it legally only needs to keep a certain percentage called the reserve ratio. In addition, the bank also needs enough money to give to people who want to make withdrawals. The rest of the money can be lent out.[^215]
- If the bank gets new money in deposits, this can make a multiplied impact on the money supply. For example, if the reserve ratio is 10% and someone deposits $1000 into the bank, the bank can lend out $900. If the person who borrowed the money deposits it into a bank (or he spends the money and the people he paid deposit the money in the bank), the bank ends up with $900 of new deposits. They can then lend out 90% of that, so $810. If the person who borrowed the money deposits it into a bank…[^215]
- The formula for figuring out the multiplier effect is 1/reserve ration. So if the reserve ratio is 10%, it is 1/.1 which equals 10.[^215]
- Using this multiplier effect, the Federal Reserve can add a little bit of money to the economy and have an outsized effect.[^215]
- Practically, banks hold more than the reserve ratio requires for a variety of reasons, such as loans not being particularly profitable, or if a bank is expecting an increase in withdrawals. This lowers the multiplier effect (because banks are lending out less money).[^215]
- Also, if people don't deposit the money back into the bank (meaning, they hold on to it by hiding it at home, for example), that also hurts the multiplier effect.[^215]
- During a recession, both of these things occur, so multiplier effect goes down. The Fed, therefore, has to do more to have an affect.[^215]
- A talmid needs to keep in mind that between yeridas hadoros, the fact that his rebbi has been learning and teaching Torah for much longer than the talmid has, and the fact that the rebbi learned from his rebbi, who was probably greater than the rebbi himself, his rebbi is probably correct about a pshat, even if it doesn't seem correct to the talmid at first. (This is especially considering the fact that the rebbi may be giving the shiur over in a way that is not the clearest on purpose, as discussed {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">in chapter 2. Add link</span>}.)[^216]
- How the Fed affected the economy before 2008:
- The Fed would buy or sell treasury bills from banks (called open market operations). This would increase of decrease their money in reserves, which affects the federal funds rate.[^217]
- The federal funds rate is the interest rate banks would charge each other to lend them money.[^217]
- Sometimes banks need to borrow money to allow its customers to make withdrawals or to make sure it has enough money to be above the reserve ratio.[^217]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't understand why this particularly affects the federal funds rate more than interest rates for other loans. This may be because treasury bills are short-term and banks would generally lend to each other for relatively short-term. As such, the federal funds rate is tied to the movement of treasury bills, but I am not sure</span>}
- When the Fed buys treasury bills, increasing the money supply, it is called expansionary open market policy. When it sells, it is called contractionary open market policy.[^217]
- The more money, the more banks can lend out which stimulates the economy. At the same time, more money means a higher level of inflation.[^217]
- The federal funds rate is correlated with other interest rates (mortgages, student loans, etc., but it is far from precise).[^217]
- The Fed also utilized its communication. When the Fed would announce a target for the federal funds rate, the rate would change even before conducting open market operations.[^217]
- In addition, the Fed affects the market with positive or negative messaging about the state of the economy.[^217]
- Beginning in the 2008 financial crisis, the Fed uses new methods to affect the economy.[^218]
- The Fed started doing something called quantitative easing. This is similar to open market operations, except instead of only buying treasury bills, the Fed would purchase or sell from a wide variety of assets, such as mortgages.[^218]
- This would cause a change in the interest rate in those particular assets, giving the Fed more direct control of specific types of loans.[^218]
- (I think this is because the Fed would potentially be buying some of the riskier assets. By doing this, this would decrease the risk for banks and so they could charge a lower interest rate, but I am not sure about this.[^7])
- This was particularly important because banks began holding on to a significantly higher percent of reserves since 2008. As such, simply performing open market operations had almost no affect on the economy because banks had enough money to make loans anyway.[^218]
- The Fed also started paying interest on money kept in the Federal Reserve. This encouraged banks to save more money in the Federal Reserve because the Fed is the most secure borrower. By adjusting the interest rate, the Fed is further able to control the economy.[^218]
- Another thing the Fed did is Purchase Agreements and Reverse Purchase Agreements. This means that the Fed would "lend" or "borrow" treasury bills from a bank at a certain rate. This would also affect the money supply.[^218]
- This is very similar to open market operations, except that it is much more short term. Open market operations require purchasing and selling of the actual treasury bill. This is more like a rental.[^218] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't see why this wouldn't suffer from the same problem as open market operations</span>}
- A talmid should not be worried that he won't be able to see the questions on his rebbi's pshat if he works hard on it. In fact, the opposite is true. The only way a talmid find questions and be confident that they are actually legitimate is if he works hard on his rebbi's Torah.[^219]
- We see this by Betzalel. Betzalel undoubtedly worked hard on to understand what Moshe told him (in fact, Betzalel was chosen to lead the construction because he would work hard in his learning[^220]). This did not stop him from realizing that he should make the mishkan before the keilim even though Moshe told him to do it the other way. We see that his efforts to understand what his rebbi Moshe was saying did not impede his ability to see where Moshe gave over the commandment from Hashem incorrectly.[^221]
- A talmid shouldn't be quick to go ask his rebbi questions. He should first put in effort to understand the Torah of his rebbi.[^222]
- The Chazon Ish said that the main growth in learning comes from understanding the alternative pshat given, and then weighing it against what you thought pshat is, and deciding which one makes more sense. As such, one has to make sure to listen and try to understand the alternative pshat.[^223]
- The Fed has a few additional functions as well:
- If people believe that a bank is insolvent (it has more liabilities than assets), people will panic and want to withdraw their money from the bank as soon as they can because they know that there won't be any money left for whoever comes last.[^224]
- Many times a panic can start from an untrue rumor. For example, a bank may have more assets that liabilities, but its assets are illiquid, meaning, they are going to mature into their full value over time, but can only be sold for much less than their value right now. An example of this is loans. A loan is worth money, but it is not clear exactly how much. It depends on the risk. It may be difficult to evaluate and, as such, if a bank had to liquidate a loan, it would not be able to get the full value of the loan.[^224]
- To prevent these panics, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation was created. They guarantee up to a certain amount of everyone's deposits. This helps prevent the mass panics.[^224]
- Usually, the Fed tries to only insure solvent banks and let the insolvent ones close. However, during times of extreme panic, the Fed may protect the insolvent banks as well. This is because there can be a domino effect, meaning, if banks start to fail, this can make the panic spread to other institutions. The Fed tries to prevent this by insuring some of these institutions.[^224]
- In addition, the Fed can also lend money to the bank so it can cover its withdrawals.[^224]
- In very unique circumstances, the Fed or the US government may take ownership of a company to prevent it from closing down, as happened with GM and AIG in the 2008 financial crisis.[^224] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know when the government decides to do this over loaning money</span>}
- The problem with these bailouts is that it incentivizes risk. A company that knows that it is going to be bailed out if it is in danger will take more risk because they can get all of the positives and pass off the negatives.[^224]
- This is the balancing act of the Fed when it comes to these things. On one hand, bailouts can prevent panics from spreading out of control (systemic risk). On the other, it encourages this risky behavior that may have to be covered by the government (moral hazard).[^224]
- To help with this the Fed imposes certain regulations on banks to not allow them to take certain risks. This comes with its own set of challenges. It does not allow the banking system to be flexible.[^224]
- Practically, there are a few difficulties for the Fed when it tries to stimulate the economy
	1. Quality of the data. Sometimes the data that the Fed is using to make its decisions isn't correct.[^225]
	2. Timing: It can take between 6 and 18 months for the Feds policies to affect the economy. The situation may have changed by the time these policies make an impact.[^225]
	3. Control: Some of the Feds policies rely on other participants. For example, when the Fed would perform expansionary open market operations to add more money to the economy, this assumes that the banks that received the money are going to lend it out and not keep it in their reserves. However, they may decide to do that, which would keep the money supply from increasing.[^225]
- If the economy is going through a temporary dip in Aggregate Demand (the AD curve shifts to the left) {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">add link</span>}, the Fed can try to increase the money supply to stimulate the economy. If the Fed undershoots, this will increase productivity somewhat, but the economy will not be back to the level it once was. If the Fed overshoots, this is going to cause high inflation in the long run. To get rid of the incflation, this can cause a temporary drop in GDP which leads to a recession.[^225]
- This may have happened in the 70s and 80s. The Fed stimulated the economy, but by the end of the 70s, there was runaway inflation. In the early 80s, inflation was lowered, but there was a recession. Unemployment was high.[^225]
- A talmid has to follow his rebbi (and the gedolim of earlier generations) in terms oh how to weigh svaros, as well as when it comes to paskening shailos as well. There is a gemara on shabbos 21b where a certain halacha was said to Abaye in the name of Rav Yirmiya and Abaye did not agree. When the same halacha was said to Abaye in the name of Rav Yochanan, Abaye did acept the pssak. We see an example where of an amora thinking a certain way about a certain issue, but willing to disregard his opinion when he found out that someone great disagreed with him.[^226]
- This is only when a talmid can understand where his rebbi is coming from. However, when a talmid has no understanding of what his rebbi is saying, even after putting the proper effort to understand him, the talmid should follow his own daas. (This is assuming that the talmid has attained a certain level of competency.)[^227]
- As such, a talmid has to think hard about whether he can hear where is rebbi is coming from or not.[^227]
- We see this in Rabbi Akiva Eiger, for example. At times, he will write a piece going one way about an issue, but then conclude אבל מה אעשה שהרי עומדים כנגדי דברי" מהרש"א" and follow the opinion of the מהרש"א. At other times, he will conclude a piece with "צ"ע" about a certain opinion that is against his own and not follow that opinion even regarding opinions of rishonim. Clearly Rabbi Akiva Eiger was going through this deliberation and so when he can hear where the person from the earlier generation is coming from, he will be willing to disregard his own opinion and when he can not understand, he will be willing to disregard it.[^227]
- If the economy is hit with a real shock (a decrease in long-term GDP), inflation increases and production decreases.[^228]
- As such, opposite courses of action are required. To deal with the inflation, the Fed may want to decrease aggregate demand by decreasing the money supply (this would shift the AD curve to the left). This, however, decreases production even further. The opposite is true about how the Fed deals with the lack of production.[^228]
- In the real world, real shocks and AD shocks are intertwined. If there is negative real shock (like Covid, for example), people are going to be more hesitant to buy things.[^228]
- Sometimes the Feds actions can cause disastrous harm. For example, the Great Recession.[^229]
- The economy was doing very well in the 1990s. There was a mild recession in 2000 to 2001 (the dot com bubble). However, even after the recession was over, unemployment remained high. To try to combat this, the Fed kept the federal funds rate very low until 2005. This encouraged people to borrow money and many did do this to buy houses.[^229]
- This led to the housing bubble. This means that the prices for houses was going up faster than the fundamentals could explain it (I think this means that the prices were going up because people kept expecting that they could sell the houses for more. However, people didn't really want houses to live in them at those prices. They wanted to sell them, and only people who thought that they could sell them for more were going to buy them.)[^229]
- The Fed began raising the Federal funds rate in 2005. As such, in 2006 housing prices began to fall.[^229]
- This led to the bubble popping, meaning, people began being unsure about whether the prices would keep climbing. As such, people were left with houses that they were unable to sell since people stopped believing that they could buy the houses so they could sell them for more.[^229]
- The Fed probably left the Federal funds rate too low for too long.[^229]
- It's hard to know when you are in a bubble. Everyone thinks that "this time is different". The problem is that sometimes, it is different.[^229]
- Nearly all economists at the time did not know how much the housing sector or the shadow banking system was tied to the overall economy.[^229]
- It is difficult for the Fed to deal with an asset bubble. The Fed can decrease the money supply but that decreases the overall AD, not just one asset.[^229] (I think that this may have been particularly true before 2008. I think quantitative easing is supposed to help with this. {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Add link</span>})
- Perhaps the Fed could have helped by creating regulations, or better enforcing the ones it already had.[^229]
- Sometimes, the Feds inaction can lead to disaster.[^229]
- During the Great Depression, the money supply fell by about a third. This led to a massive AD shock. The Fed should not have allowed that to happen.[^229]
- Because of these kinds of issues, some economists don't want to leave these decisions to the discretion of the Fed. They want the money supply to follow certain rules.[^229]
