- Milton Friedman suggested a constant 3% increase in the money supply (since GDP is 3% annually, on average). The problem with this system is that during a recession, velocity drops, which would cause a drop in GDP.[^229]
- Others suggest keeping AD increasing at a constant rate, meaning, that if V stays steady, M stays steady. If V increases, M increases. If V decreases, M decreases. The potential issue with this is that, at times (like the Great Recession), V falls extremely dramatically. As such, M would have to increase dramatically. Such action is unprecedented and it is not clear how the economy would react.[^229]
- Fiscal policy:
- The government sometimes create jobs for people or lowers taxes to help with an economic recession.[^230]
- During a recession, unemployment is higher and GDP is lower. The government can hire these unemployed people to reduce the unemployment and to increase the GDP.[^230]
- This creates a multiplier effect. The newly hired people go and spend their money in some business. The owner of that business now has more money which he goes and spends. In addition, the owner may have to hire new workers to accommodate the increased business.[^230]
- Hiring people during times of low unemployment doesn't do very much for the GDP because the government is mostly taking the people away from other jobs they would have been doing anyway (it may still be a good idea to hire them if the job the government wants to complete is deemed important enough).[^230]
- Expansionary fiscal policy (and monetary policy, as well, as previously discussed) is best used to combat AD shocks.[^231]
- During an AD shock, the AD curve shifts to the left, lowering the GDP (in the short-term).[^231]
- The government can increase spending or lower taxes (to encourage consumption amongst private citizens), moving the AD curve back to the right.[^231]
- There are a few problems with fiscal policy.[^232]
	1. Timing. It takes a long time to implement fiscal policy. By the time it is implemented, the situation may have changed.[^232]
		1. Recognition lag. You have to see that there is a problem.[^232]
		2. Legislative lag. A bill needs to be proposed which has to go through the house and the senate and all the budget meetings, writing up the bill, etc.[^232]
		3. Implementation lag. Actually making the plans to use the money. For example, if the government is going to do a lot of construction (which is the way it usually spends extra money), you actually need to make the plans, hire the companies, have the plans go through environmental review, etc.[^232]
		4. Effectiveness lag. Once all this happens, it takes time for the money being used on the projects to start making their way through the economy.[^232]
		- To counter this problem, there are automatic stabilizers which go into affect automatically. One example is progressive taxes. This means that the less money one makes, the less money they pay in taxes. This occurs automatically. No additional legislation or implementation needs to occur. This helps keep consumption up. In addition, people with less money are generally more likely to spend the money quickly, so it can get into the economy faster.[^232]
	2. Targeted. The stimulus should target those who need it most. If construction workers are struggling, the government should put extra money into construction. If retail workers are struggling, the government should put extra money into retail. The problem is that the government only has ways to spend money in a few specific fields (if restaurants are struggling, the government doesn't really have a way to hire a bunch of waiters).[^232]
	3. Magnitude. A large majority of the government's spending is non-discretionary (at least 80% if not more). In addition, government spending in 2015, for example, was over 3 trillion dollars total GDP was around 18 trillion. So government spending makes up only so much of the GDP. The 2009 stimulus bill (the largest since WWII) was 900 billion dollars over a few years, or roughly 2% of GDP. (Despite this, the stimulus bill is considered to have been helpful.)[^232]
	4. Temporary. As the economy adjusts and people are being hired again, it would be ideal for the people working on government projects to start getting jobs in fields that are now hiring again.[^232]
- The 2009 stimulus was an ideal situation for a stimulus because it was an AD shock, it was long-lasting (meaning that by the time the stimulus bill went through all of the timing issues discussed, the economic situation had not changed), and it was across the economy (meaning that there wasn't an issue with targeting because everyone needed money).[^232]
- Fiscal (and monetary policy, as well, I assume) are far less effective at dealing with real shocks.[^233]
- Fiscal (and monetary policy) are best used when there are underused labor and capital. In a real shock, labor and capital are being used. The problem is that the situation changed. Adding more money may help GDP a little but mostly it increases inflation.[^233]
- This is on addition to all the problems with fiscal policy previously discussed.[^233]
- Theoretically, government is supposed to increase spending during times of economic busts and save money during economic booms. Practically, though, governments don't do this. They always like to spend.[^233]
- This leads to the government increasing it debt.[^233]
- This is a problem for two reasons:
	1. This increases interest payments. Over time this becomes a larger and larger percentage of the national budget.[^233]
	2. People start getting nervous that governments aren't going to be able to pay their debts and so they pull investments from the country. This can really come to a head during a recession in such a country. People really start to lose confidence fast and pull out investments. This further hurts a country that is already suffering a recession.[^233]
	- This occurred in Argentina in 2002 and Greece in 2009 (amongst other examples).[^233]
	- There is no hard and fast rule but the idea is that the higher he debt and less credibility a country has, the more risky it is to keep spending.[^233]
- There is an additional problem with fiscal policy. There are other actors involved that may undo the fiscal policy. For example:[^234]
	1. Central banks. Expansionary fiscal policy can cause higher inflation (by shifting the AD curve to the right). A central bank might see this and decide to engage in contractionary monetary policy (shifting the AD curve to the left). These cancel each other out. This is called a monetary offset.[^234]
	2. Businesses. If government borrows money to engage increase its spending, this can increase the interest rate. This may companies more reluctant to borrow money and use it in investments. As such, the increased spending by the government can be (at least somewhat) countered by a decreased spending by businesses.[^234]
	3. Consumers. If the government cuts taxes to encourage people to spend more, people may instead decide to save the extra money if the expect an increase in taxes in the future. This is called Ricardian Equivalence. In reality, some people may think this way, although others do not. Even so, this means that some of the government's tax cuts will not get put back into the economy.[^234]
- It can be difficult to figure out all of these reactions. As such, at different times, the same response to seemingly the same situation can have vastly different results.[^234]
- Microeconomics:
- Opportunity costs: When you decide to do something, you have to invest time, money, attention, etc. Opportunity cost is the cost of using these things on this decision instead of doing something else.[^235]
- Tradeoffs: Most things have tradeoffs, meaning, there are sone good things, but some bad things as well. For example, renewable energy is better for then environment, but is more expensive and less consistent (at least, for now).[^235]
- Thinking on the margin: This is a useful way to examine a situation. It means that you examine the costs and benefits of a certain situation at a certain level. If the the benefits exceed the costs, you raise it and reexamine the situation. If the costs are greater, you lower it and reexamine. For example, wen you are watching a movie, you may raise the volume so that the movie is clear. You'll stop raising the volume when it starts hurting you ears.[^236] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I want a better example</span>}
- Sunk-cost fallacy: You examine previous assets which have already been used up and are irretrievable when making a current decision. For example, if you buy something to sell, you may not want to lower the price below what you paid for it, even if it is not selling. This is not an optimal decision. You should weigh the current costs and benefits and make decision based on that. The past costs don't factor in to the current decision. There, unfortunately, is a cognitive bias to factor that in, in many decisions. For example, if you start watching a bad movie, you may want to stay until the end, especially if you paid for the tickets. This is at least partially because of the sunk-cost fallacy. You should reassess the situation and decide if watching the rest of the movie is worth an hour of your time.[^236]
- Demand curve:
- ![[Pasted image 20241215010349.png]][^237]
- As price decreases, demand increases, and the opposite is true as well.[^238]
- In addition, as the price is lowered, the quantity demanded increases at an increasing rate. This means that a reduction of $100 to $95 will cause an increase in quantity demanded, but a reduction of $50 to $45 will cause a greater increase in quantity demanded.[^238]
- This is because as an item drops in price, there become more uses for the item that become available. As such, you have the increase in demand for the all of the uses before this drop in price, plus you have this new use which generates new demand. For example, oil can be used to make rubber ducks and fuel airplanes. If oil is starts getting expensive, you may use a different type of bath toy. However, there is no good alternative to go long distances to flying in an airplane, so if you want to get somewhere, you are going to have to pay for that increased price of oil. And the same is true in reverse.[^238]
- (I would assume that this is partially because as the price is lowered, the percentage change increases for a fixed amount. For example, 5$ is 5% of $100 but 10% of $50. As such, this would cause a greater increase in demand {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I'm sure I can find this online. Find a source.</span>}. This might be incorrect because the same can be said about the change in quantity, meaning, that at a lower quantity, the same fixed change makes a larger percentage change. As such, when the price is low, quantity is low, and so the a large change in the percentage of the price will also make a large difference in the percentage of the quantity demanded. Also, I think that there should be diminishing returns which works in reverse. For example, I may be willing to eat 4 slices of pizza if the price was low enough. However, I wouldn't eat more than that, no matter how low the price got. As such, I would imagine that the quantity demanded gets less and less the more the price decreases because there is less value to the increased quantity.)[^7]
- Every individual product has its own supply[^239] and demand curves.[^238]
- Supply curve:
- ![[Pasted image 20241215021020.png]][^240]
- Similar to the demand curve. As price goes up, more and more supply is generated.[^239]
- This supply increases at greater and greater levels, the supply increases faster and faster. The reason for this is that the greater the price gets, the more ways an item can be produced that would have been too expensive when the price was lower. For example, different parts of the world can produce oil but are different prices. As the price of oil increases, more and more places can be mined for oil and still turn a profit.[^239]
- (On the supply side, the percentage increase is the opposite. The difference between $50 and $55 is 10% and the difference between $100 and $105 is only 5%. As such, I would have thought that there is a bigger difference at the lower price points than at the higher. Also, diminishing returns should work the other way, meaning, the more money one makes, the less valuable additional money is and so the more money is needed to convince one to do more work.)[^7]
- Equilibrium:
- ![[Pasted image 20241215023059.png]][^241]
- Equilibrium is the intersection of the demand curve and the supply curve. This is the price at which the quantity demanded and quantity supplied are the same. This determines the price and the quantity sold.[^242]
- Market forces make this happen. If the price was higher, there would be more sellers, but they wouldn't have who to sell to, as the demand wouldn't be so high. This would create a surplus (meaning, there are now extra goods). This would encourage sellers to lower their prices until there are enough buyers to buy.[^242]
- If the price was lower than the equilibrium price, then there would be more buyers than sellers. As such, this would encourage sellers to raise their prices until there aren't enough buyers interested at the higher price.[^242]
- 2 ways to read a graph:
- Horizontal: At a given price, how many buyers would be willing to buy a product.[^243]
- Vertical: At a given price, what is the most amount of money you can for sure sell an item for.[^243]
- Consumer surplus:
- ![[Pasted image 20241215023858.png]][^244]
- The total amount that buyers would have been willing to pay more than the current market price. For example, if the price of oil is $20 but one buyer would have been willing to pay $50, his consumer surplus is $30, meaning, he would have been willing to spend an additional $30. If we add up all of the money that consumers were willing to spend but didn't have to because the market price was lower, we get the consumer surplus.[^243]
- This represents the amount that consumers gained (if consumers are willing to pay a certain price, it means that they think it is worth it to pay that price. And they are coming out at least a little ahead. If they have to pay less than that, they are walking away with a greater gain.)[^243]
- Graphically, this is the are under the supply curve but above the current price. If the supply curve is a straight line, we can calculate this like the area of any triangle.[^243]
- Demand curve shirt:
- ![[Pasted image 20241215030735.png]][^245]
- Demand can be shifter by a number of things, such as:
	1. Income. As income increases, the demand increases for most things. Some things, though, the demand decreases. For example, your demand for McDonald's might go down as your income goes up and you can eat at nicer places.[^246]
	2. Population: As the population increases, demand for things goes up because there are now more people demanding those things.[^246]
	3. Taste. Things go out of style and some thing become stylish. This shifts the demand curve for those things.[^246]
	4. Related goods. Some goods are related to other goods. Some are substitutes for each other, meaning, that while there are not the same, they are similar. For example, if the price of soy sauce started going up, people may just buy sweet sauce and spicy mayo. Some goods are complements to each other and if the demand for one increases, the demand for the other will increase as well. For example, ketchup and hot dogs. If demand for hot dogs increases, demand for ketchup will increase.[^246]
	5. Expectations. If people are expecting the price to go up or down in the future, or if they will have a sudden need for something in the future, that will affect the demand curve. For example, if there is a hurricane expected, the price for portable generators will increase.[^246]
- A shift means that a greater quantity is demanded at a certain price (as can be seen in the image. At point A 80 units are demanded and at point B, 115 units are demanded), and it means that at a certain quantity, people will be willing to pay more money (this would be shown by continuing the vertical line through point A up until it reaches the new demand curve) (horizontal and vertical methods of reading the graph {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">add link</span>}.[^246]
- ![[Screenshot 2024-12-15 091456.png]][^247]
- Change in quantity demanded vs change in demand.[^247]
- Change in quantity demanded means that the price changed, so there is a change along the demand curve.[^247]
- Change in demand is when the whole demand curve shifts, due to a change in one of the shifters, as discussed.[^247] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">add link</span>}
- The idea is that the graph shows the relationship between price and demand, assuming that all else stays equal. So, by definition, if the price changes, there is movement along the demand curve, but the whole curve doesn't move.[^247]
- And if something not on the graph changes (because of one of the shifters), that will affect the relationship between the quantity demanded and the price, meaning, that at the same price, more or less will be demanded (depending on if the shift increased to popularity of the item or decreased it).[^247]
- For example, if the graph says that at $3, 500 bottles of milk are demanded and at $2, 700 bottles of milk are demanded (so the demand curve goes through those two points), if the price changes from $3 to $2, the quantity demanded will change from 500 to 700, which are both points on the graph.[^247]
- If, however, milk becomes less popular, then the whole graph shifts to the left. So instead of there being a demand for 500 bottles of milk at $3 and 700 bottles at $2, now there is only a demand for 400 bottles at $3 and 600 at $2.[^247]
- The same concepts apply to the supply curve.[^248]
- One important note is that if there is an increase in quantity supplied, the curve shifts to the right and down. Reading the graph through the horizontal reading, this means that at a certain price, there are more units that are willing to be produced. Reading the graph through the vertical reading, it means that to produce a given quantity, sellers will be willing to take less money. (See image)[^248]
- ![[Pasted image 20241216021129.png]][^248]
- Producer surplus is similar to buyer surplus. The difference is that it is the area underneath the price and above the supply curve. This represents the gain of producers through a sale at the market price (See image).[^248]
- ![[Pasted image 20241216021317.png]][^248]
- The supply curve usually shifts because of a change in price in producing the good.
- Some things that can lead to a change in price are:
	1. Technological advances.[^249]
	2. Change in input prices (if the price of fertilizer goes up, the price of fruits goes up; if the price of labor goes up, the price of basically everything goes up).[^249]
	3. Taxes (increases and decreases) and subsidies.[^249]
	4. Expectations (If sellers think that the price of a product will go up in the near future, they may put some of the product in storage to sell when the price goes up, lowering the supply. Even if they are producing more now, the amount that is being sold is still lower).[^249]
	5. Entry or exit of producers into the market.[^249]
	6. Changes in opportunity cost (If producers can start making more money producing apples instead of pears, the price of pears will go up, because by producing pears, they are now not able to produce apples, assuming that pears and apples require the same assets to produce).[^249]
- Theoretically, equilibrium maximizes good exchanges and gets rid of wasteful exchanges.[^250]
- This means that at he equilibrium price, buyers are buying the good because it is worth more to them to buy the good than to have the money, and sellers are selling the good because they are producing it for little enough that they can make a profit selling it.[^250]
- At the equilibrium point, every buyer who is willing to pay the same or more than the price a seller is willing to sell for (meaning, buyers are willing to pay $50 or more for a good, for example, and sellers are willing to accept $50 for a good) is matched up. This leads to the most amount of productive exchanges.[^250]
- There are theoretically no wasteful exchanges. This means that if a buyer is willing to pay less than the price a seller is willing to sell for, there will not be a deal.[^250]
- A change in quantity demanded and quantity supplied are changes along the demand and supply curves, respectively, as discussed.[^251]
- A change in quantity demanded is caused by a change in supply (meaning, a shift in the supply curve) and a change in quantity supplied is caused by a change in demand (meaning, a shift in the demand curve).[^251]
- Vernon Smith was a Nobel-prize winning economist. He ran the following experiment to test whether equilibrium price and quantity would actually play out:[^252]
- He had two groups of people (buyers and sellers). He gave each person a card with a price.[^252]
- For buyers, this was the amount that they would be willing to pay for a product. For sellers, this was the cost of producing a product.[^252]
- Smith would pay the participants the differences, meaning if a seller had a "cost" of $3 and sold it for $4, Smith would pay the person $1. And the same for buyers; if a buyer had a "max price" of $4 and they bought something for $3, Smith would pay them $1.[^252]
- He let put these people in room had them talk to each other and try to buy and sell to make the most money.[^252]
- Here are the results:[^252]
- ![[Screenshot 2024-12-16 025549.png]][^252]
- On the left is the price and quantity predicted in theory. The price predicted is $2 and the amount of sales should be 5-6.[^252]
- The right is what actually happened. As can be seen, there were about 5-6 sales per period, and the price was around $2 for sales.[^252]
- Here is the price of oil aver a period of time:[^252]
- ![[Screenshot 2024-12-16 025818.png]][^252]
- As can be seen, the price of oil seems to follow the classic supply and demand shifts which affect the price as we would expect. For example, the Arab oil embargo in 1973 led to a decrease in supply, hence, an increase in price.[^252]
- Increased production of oil by non-OPEC countries beginning in 1981 increased supply, leading to the price going down.[^252]
- Beginning in 2000, there began being much more industrialization in China and India, leading to increased demand, hence, an increase in supply.[^252]
- In 2008 there was the Great Recession. Demand for oil dropped, causing a drop in price.[^252]
- Elastic vs inelastic.
- This just means how much quantity demanded changes when price changes.[^253]
- A flatter curve means a greater change (more elastic) and a steeper curve means a smaller change (less elastic) (see image)[^253]
- ![[Pasted image 20241216041637.png]][^253]
- The main factor that determines elasticity is how easy it is to replace the product if the price changes.[^253]
- A few points that determine that are:
	1. Substitutes. If there are substitutes available, it is relatively easy to switch, so even if there is a relatively small price change, you may decide to switch.[^253]
	2. Time span. It is relatively hard to switch products quickly. But over time, people learn to adjust. As such, things begin as less elastic but become more elastic the longer the price stays higher (or lower).[^253]
	3. Necessities vs luxuries. If something is more of a luxury, people will be more willing to go without it if the price changes. (This obviously depends on each individual person).[^253]
	4. Large vs Small part of personal budget. If something is a small part of your budget (like toothpicks, for example) even if the price changes radically, one probably won't notice, and even if they do, they won't really care. As such, it is inelastic. If something is a major part of their budget, they will try to look for deals or figure out other things they can do, hence it is more elastic.[^253]
- The back and forth between a rebbi and a talmid leads to the making the daas of the talmid more yashar.[^254]
- This takes time and can only be done by learning from a rebbi.[^254]
- A rebbi can use a talmid's question to get in to the talmid's way of thinking and be able to point out any potential mistakes in the way the talmid is thinking.[^254]
- A rebbi needs to have patience with his talmidim to be able to treat all of this talmidim's questions with respect, but this is an extremely important process of a talmid's growth.[^254]
- This is something that Rav Dovid was extremely good at. He had tremendous patience and treated every question from every talmid with importance.[^254]
- This love that Rav Dovid showed to his talmidim helped them overcome the pressures of the time against learning full time. (See footnote 2)[^255]
- For products that have inelastic demand curves, if the price is raised, the producer makes more money. The idea is that the price can go up significantly, while the quantity sold will not change very much.[^256]
- The opposite is true of elastic demand curves: if price goes up, this leads to a decrease in revenue for the seller, since a small increase in price leads to a large loss in quantity sold.[^256]
- This video[^256] also contains formulas for calculating the elasticity of demand which I decided to skip.
- Elasticity of supply:
- Similar to elasticity of demand.[^257]
- The factor that determines how elastic or inelastic the supply of a particular product is how much does the price to produce change as the quantity produced changes.[^257]
- Some determinants of this are:
	1. Constant (or decreasing) costs per unit versus increasing costs per unit.[^257]
	2. Short run vs long run. In the short run, it may be difficult to increase supply, even with an increase in price.[^257]
	3. Share of market for inputs. Some things do not make up a large share of the market for its inputs, while other things do take up a large share. For example, toothpicks do not take up a large share of the total market for wood. As such, if the supply of toothpicks increases, that won't raise the price of wood, so it is relatively easy to increase supply. Cars make up a large part of the global steel market. If supply of cars would increase, this would cause steel to rise in price, making it harder to increase the production of cars.[^257]
	4. How widescale the demand is. It is relatively easy to increase the supply for something if that increase is in a specific place. If the increase in demand is global, then it becomes much more difficult to satisfy the demand. You may have to utilize more expensive methods of production to be able to supply so much.[^257]
	5. Mobility of factors of production: If the things that go into producing something can easily be converted to producing something else, then even a small increase in price can cause a large decrease in supply.[^258]
- This video[^257] also contains formulas for calculating the elasticity of supply which I decided to skip.
- If the supply of a product is inelastic, if there is a shift in the demand curve to the right, then the price increases a lot, but the quantity doesn't change very much (see image)[^259]
- ![[Screenshot 2024-12-16 235453.png]][^259]
- The opposite is true for elastic supply curves (see image)[^259]
- ![[Pasted image 20241217000504.png]][^259]
- Housing prices have gone up so much because people are trying to move to big cities, and housing in big cities is inelastic (in other words, it is extremely expensive to build additional housing).[^259]
- This is because of a variety of reasons:
	1. Many big cities are near water and so there is a limited supply of land.[^259]
	2. Existing zoning laws make it difficult to build a house.[^259]
	3. Government officials that are needed to get a permit can be extremely slow.[^259]
	4. Even after that, there can be environmental committees, historical preservation committees, and neighbors.[^259]
- The people hurt the most by this are the people who could not get houses and now have to live somewhere else. The ironic problem is that they can't vote in any of the city's laws as they don't live in the city.[^259]
- Gun buybacks:
- Gun buyback programs are largely ineffective if done for a small group of people (like a town) in a large market.[^260]
- This is what happens:
- During a gun buyback, there is increased demand for guns because a new buyer has joined the market: the police.[^260]
- However, guns are extremely elastic, just like all other mass produced goods in a large market. Think of things like bread and gasoline. If the price goes up just a little bit, millions more loaves of bread and millions more gallons of gasoline can be produced.[^260]
- As such, a gun buyback in this market does not affect the price of guns, because, even though the demand curve shifts to the right, the supply curve is basically flat, so the point of equilibrium doesn't move up (see image).[^260]
- ![[Pasted image 20241217002848.png]][^260]
- As such, while gun buybacks will temporarily get guns off of the streets, in the long run, the price stayed the same so the same people who bought the guns originally will buy the guns again as there is no shift in the demand.[^260]
- This is in contrast to places that make it illegal to own guns.[^260]
- In such a place, guns are extremely inelastic.[^260]
- As such, when there is an increase in demand (meaning, the police join the market of buyers) then the demand curve's shift to the right increases the price of guns, causing less people to buy them (see image).[^260]
- ![[Pasted image 20241217003355.png]][^260]
- (I am not sure what I think about this. I think it is more complicated. Sometimes people won't buy the guns back. They don't want it anymore and would've gladly sold it if the could have. Another point is that this graph seems to assume that the police enter the market like any other buyer, meaning, that the demand curve totally shifts to the right. I don't know if that is accurate. Gun buybacks, I think, have specific prices for guns. As such, there is only a shift to the right beginning at that point, meaning, if if police are willing to pay $200 for a gun, this causes a large increase in demand for guns at $200, but there is no increase in demand for guns going for more. As such, if the amount they are willing to pay is above the equilibrium price, this will help raise the price, but only until that amount that they are willing to pay. And if it is less, then it wouldn't seem to do anything at all[^7])
- Commodity taxes (like sales tax)
- In terms of supply and demand, it doesn't matter if the tax is on the seller, or on the buyer. The price paid by the buyer, the amount earned by the seller, and the quantity sold are the same either way.[^261]
- When the tax is on the seller, the supply curve shifts up by the amount of the tax. This means that if it used to be that 100 sellers were willing to produce something for $10, if there is a new tax for $1, they will only be willing to sell it for $11 because they are losing $1 to taxes.[^261]
- When the supply curve shifts up, the equilibrium point shifts up and to the left.[^261]
- As such, the quantity sold is less, the amount that buyers need to pay is more, and sellers make less money (see image. Remember that sellers are not earning the money at point B. They earn the money at point B minus the taxes, which lines up with the original supply curve underneath point B).[^261]
- ![[Pasted image 20241217024631.png]][^261]
- When the tax is on the buyer, the demand curve shifts down by the amount of the tax. This means that if it used to be that 100 people would have been willing to pay $10 for something, if there is a new tax of $1 on something, the buyers are now only going to be willing to pay $9.[^261]
- When the demand curve shifts downward it moves the equilibrium point down and to the left, so the amount that sellers earn is less, the amount that is sold is more, and buyers pay more (see image). (Keep in mind that the buyer isn't paying the price at point B. The buyer is paying the price at point B plus taxes which lines up with the point on the original demand curve above point B.)[^261]
- ![[Pasted image 20241217024213.png]][^261]
- Another way of denoting this (without having to shift curves) is by driving a "wedge" into the graph (see image)[^261]
- ![[Pasted image 20241217024914.png]][^261]
- Point B represents a certain quantity demanded for at least the price at that point. Point D represent the same number of sellers who are willing to sell for the price at point B minus the taxes (this could be stated the other way as well. As was discussed, this does not make a difference).[^261]

- Rav Dovid was extremely patient with his talmidim. The "harshest" language he would use was "forgive me, but we can't just stay stuck on this one point".[^262]
- Even when the other talmidim were growing impatient, Rav Dovid would not desist from trying to explain the cheshbon to this talmid.[^262]
- If explaining it numerous times didn't help the talmid, Rav Dovid would try other things to try to get the talmid to understand. (I don't know what is meant by this.)[^263]
- When Rav Dovid would give tochacha, he would do it in a respectful way. He would tell the talmid "doing such is think is proper for a chashuv ben Torah like yourself."[^263]

- Many Jews immigrated illegally to Palestine during WWII, despite effort from the British to stop it. Britain spoke to some foreign governments which were helping the Jews and tried to get them to stop.[^264]
- Jews in Palestine kept building new settlements in Palestine in 1940.[^264]
- The Jews opposed the restrictions of the White Papers as well as the Germans. They enlisted with the allies, and also kept the illegal immigration.[^264]
- The Haganah improved. They became more organized and kept smuggling in weapons.[^264]
- Yigael Sukenik (who later changed his name to Yadin) was given an assistant leadership position.[^264]
- A small air force was developed, mostly of crop-dusting aircraft.[^264]
- Churchill tried getting Britain to start allowing the Jews to arm themselves.[^264]
- He thought it would increase the popularity of the British with the Jews, and it would free up British troops that were stationed in Palestine that they could use for the war.[^264]
- The suggestion was rejected avoid antagonizing the Arabs, especially because they wanted their help with the war.[^264]
- The British also decided to put the 1939 White Papers (limiting Jewish immigration (although it seemed that the British weren't doing much about that) and not allowing Jews to buy land in Palestine) into affect.[^264]
- Many Jews in Palestine felt that the land purchase restriction was a violation of the purpose of the Mandate.[^264]
- In 1940 the High Commissioner, Sir Harold MacMichael, decided to deport illegally arriving Jews to the British colony of Mauritius for the duration of the war.[^265]
- The Haganah accidentally sank the ship that was going to take the illegal Jewish immigrants, called the Patria, while it was being boarded.[^265]
- Because of the trauma to the passengers, they were allowed to stay in Israel.[^265]
- According to a British report, this incident didn't have much negative reaction from the Arabs.[^265]
- Other illegal immigrants weren't so lucky and they were deported to Mauritius.[^265]
- The conditions were purposely made to be harsh to discourage further illegal immigration.[^265]
- Britain then suspended all immigration from the end of 1940 through March 1941, and then again until September.[^265]
- These restrictions were going on when the killing of Jews really picked up during the Holocaust.[^265]
- Jabotinsky died in the United States at this time. He was an extremely large figure in the Zionist movement and so his death had a powerful effect.[^265]
- However, his Revisionists split off from main stream Zionism left a powerful rift between the two sides.[^265]
- Jews in Palestine had almost no involvement in the fighting in WWII. They fought a little with the Vichy French (the puppet French government set up by the Germans after defeating France).[^266]
- The most famous thing in the battles was that Moshe Dayan lost an eye.[^266]
- In 1940, Avraham Stern broke off from the Irgun after they were willing to make peace with the British to fight the Nazis. Stern felt that there could be no compromise with the British.[^267]
- He created the Lehi, also called the Stern Gangs.[^268]
- They committed many acts of terror[^268] and even tried to make peace with the Nazis and Mussolini to help overthrow the British and create a Jewish State (I have no idea why he thought the Nazis would want to do this).[^266]
- Stern was killed in 1942 but his terrorist movement carried on.[^266]
- Even though the Holocaust got worse, the British still did not allow illegal imigration into Palestine.
- Some Zionists, including David Ben-Gurion, made a conference at the Biltmore Hotel in New York.
- At the meeting, the Zionists decided that the Jewish Agency was the governing body of Palestine and made creating a Jewish State an official policy position, as opposed to just creating a Jewish National Home.[^269][^270] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't really get what the difference is</span>} This became known as the Biltmore Conference, or the Biltmore Program.[^271]
- Settlements kept being built through 1942.[^270]
- The Rema, regarding the halachos of giving kavod to one's rebbi, says that one's rebbi muvhak is one that teaches him iyun and gives him his sense of emes and yashrus.[^272]
- The Chasam Sofer says that the goal of a rebbi is to teach his talmid when to make extrapolations from one point to another and when not to. This can be extremely difficult as many seemingly similar points have minute differences which make an extrapolation incorrect. A rebbi does this by allowing the talmid to come to his own conclusion and then explain to the tamid how they are supposed to view the comparison.[^273]
- It is extremely important for the Torah leaders to be able to have a firm grasp on the dakei haemes as the depth of their understanding has a profound effect on the rest of the people. If they really understand the small minutia, there is a tremendous positive effect on the rest of the people, and the opposite is true as well.[^274]
- In 1943, Britain dropped the quotas for Jews in countries under the threat of the Nazis.[^275]
- A "passageway" through Turkey opened up. Jews would go to Turkey by ship and then take a train down to Palestine.[^275]
- Churchill pushed for a two-state solution.[^275]
- Ben-Gurion wanted the border of a future Jewish state to extend down to the Gulf of Aqaba.[^275]
- ![[Locator-map-Gulf-of-Aqaba.png]][^276]
- In 1944, the Irgun, led by Menachem Begin, wanted Jews to rebel against the British to try and set up a Jewish government in Palestine.[^275]
- However, they only attacked local police, not military installations, because they knew that it was imperative that Britain win the war.[^275]
- Al Husseini tried to convince the Germans to take some Arabs and train them to fight with the axis. However, this didn't end up happening.[^275]
- In 1944, members of the Stern Gang killed a member of the British government in Cairo named Lord Moyne.[^277]
- Lord Moyne was against Jewish immigration a few years earlier, although he was coming around.[^277]
- This lead to a tremendous outcry against terrorism.[^277]
- The attack, as well as the Irgun and Stern Gangs were denounced by Weizmann, Ben-Gurion, and other leaders.[^277]
- A list of 700 Irgun names was given to the British Mandate police who then went around arresting all of the people on the list.[^277]
- The Stern Gang were willing to give the assurance that they would not target Churchill.[^277]
- Some members of the British government were still opposed to the Jewish immigration to Palestine.[^277]
- Lord Gort, the new High Commissioner in Palestine, tried getting the Soviets to not let Jews pass through the Balkans.[^277]
- The Jews helped the allied cause. 30,000 Jewish troops fought for the allies. There were numerous spy mission parachutists dropped behind German lines as well.[^277]
- There were only 9000 Arabs who fought for the Allies, some of whom were from other Arab countries (Syria, Transjordan, and Lebanon). Many of these deserted or were discharged.[^277]
- This became a pro-Israeli talking point.[^277]
- In 1945, the Arabs created the Arab League, which a unification of Muslims in all of the Arabic lands, from North Africa, through the Middle East.[^277]
- An estimated 200,000 Jews died in the Soviet army over WWII.[^277]
- Rav Dovid was so machshiv the importance of a rebbi that sometimes, Rav Dovid would advice talmidim not to go learn in the great yeshivos of Europe because he felt that the talmidim there would not have a rebbi there.[^278]
- Although some talmidim were able to be successful even without a rebbi, Rav Dovid felt that this was something exceptionally rare.[^278]
- Rav Dovid tried getting the Chofetz Chaim to become the rosh yeshiva of the yeshiva in Radin when Rav Dovid was there so Rav Dovid could have a rebbi. While this didn't happen, the Chofetz Chaim did get Rav Naftoli Trop for this reason.[^278]
- Rabbi Akiva Eiger said that the purpose of learning is to hone one's sense of yashrus, even if this means that the person is going to have to sacrifice learning bekiyos.[^279]
- The person doesn't need to worry that perhaps they are going to argue on the words of a rishon somewhere that they did not see because a the words of the rishonim are yashar, and so a person trained in yashrus will be naturally avoid saying the opposite of the rishonim, even without realizing it.[^279]
- Rav Chaim Voloshiner said that the greatness of a gadol is entirely defined by his sense of yashrus.[^280]
- Who pays for most of the tax is determined by the elasticity of the supply and demand curves. Whichever side is more elastic will pay less of the tax (see image).[^281]
- ![[Pasted image 20241222005428.png]][^281]
- The idea is that if something is more elastic, a small increase in price causes a great drop in quantity demanded/produced and the opposite is true as well. So if demand is more elastic, the quantity demanded will drop relatively significantly compared to the supply, and so the price the sellers sell at will have to decrease significantly to lower the quantity supplied. The same is true for an elastic supply compared to demand.[^281]
- Labor is generally inelastic compared to those hiring labor. A laborer needs to work. There aren't many good alternatives. Demand for labor is relatively elastic. Labor can be replaced with robots or software, for example, or it can be outsources to other countries.[^281]
- As such, increased taxes on those hiring laborers, or on wages, will mostly be paid for by laborers. Studies have largely shown this.[^281]
- Taxes decrease from the consumer and producer surplus and give it to the government instead.[^282]
- However, some trades are not made as a result of the tax. This is called deadweight loss (see image).[^282]
- ![[Pasted image 20241222012311.png]][^282]
- The idea is that before a tax, certain trades would have occurred which was beneficial for both sides (by definition) which are now not occurring as those trades are not beneficial enough for the producers and consumers anymore.[^282]
- This loss doesn't benefit the producer, it doesn't benefit the consumer, and it doesn't benefit the government either as the government is not getting tax money on trades that are not occurring.[^282]
- The government needs to weigh its gain in tax revenue against this deadweight loss.[^282]
- Elastic demand curves (and presumably elastic supply curves) lead to more dead weight compared to government revenue while inelastic demand curves lead to less. As such, depending on the situation, there is a reason for government to tax inelastic products (see image). (Obviously, there are many other factors. For example, insulin is inelastic, but that doesn't mean it is a good idea to tax insulin.)[^282]
- ![[Pasted image 20241222014132.png]][^282]
- (I'm not sure if I agree with this. If a demand is elastic, it can mean that there are easy substitutes. If this is the case, the deadweight loss might be countered by an increase in demand for another product. For example, if the government taxes peanut butter, people may just start eating jelly more, assuming that it is similarly priced. While there may be some deadweight loss (people who like peanut butter but don't want jelly) that loss may not be significant)[^7]
- An example of this is a yacht tax in the early 1990s. The idea was that this was a luxury item bought by rich people, so it is a good idea to tax. What actually occurred was that the sale of yacht plummeted, presumably because rich people bought some other luxury goods, decided to wait and see what happens, or bought them in different countries.[^282]
- Meanwhile, thousands of workers were laid off.[^282]
- The government had to pay more in unemployment than it made from the tax.[^282]
- The tax was repealed after a few year.[^282]
- Some people learn with tremendous depth, learning until the point of עד שידו מגעת (ad sheyado magaas. Meaning, learning as in-depth as one can).[^283]
- They examine the exact lashon and try to get full clarity in the rishonim. Sometimes, these people may only cover on daf or even only an amud a week.[^283]
- This deep understanding of Torah can actually teach you about a wide variety of topics as many sugyos overlap with a variety of other sugyos, and so studying one in-depth teaches you about a whole plethora of sugyos.[^283]
- For example, the sugya of ירך שנתבשל בה גיד הנשה (yerech shanisbashel bah gid hanashe. A thigh that was cooked without removing the גיד הנשה first) is a small sugya in shaas but is relevant to at least nine different sugyos (see source for an exact list).[^283]
- Others try to cover ground, sometimes covering 30 blatt in a week.[^283]
- These people don't learn how to properly think. They don't develop their sense of yashrus and so they cannot properly makes connections from one sugya to another.[^283]
- The ability to make proper connections between topics is a main point in learning.[^283]
- The Rashba send a letter to the Rosh about the Rosh's virtues. The letter simply said that he was a בעל סברא ישרה (baal svarah yeshara).[^283]
- Also see Koheles 4:9-14 with the Seforno there.[^283]
- Subsidies:
- In terms of price and quantity, it does not matter if the seller or buyer receives the subsidy.[^284]
- When trying to calculate the price and quantity, we can use the same "wedge method" we used before by taxes, except this time, we come from the right (see image).[^284]
- ![[Pasted image 20241223092703.png]][^284]
- The logic is similar to before but the opposite. In the image, with a $1 subsidy, a seller who would have been willing to sell for $2.40 will be willing to sell to a buyer who would have only been willing to pay $1.40.[^284]
- As said before, it doesn't matter who the subsidy goes to. If the subsidy goes to the seller, he will be willing to sell the product for $1.40 because he is still getting $2.40. And if the subsidy goes to the buyer, he will be willing to pay $2.40, because only $1.40 is coming from him.[^284]
- Obviously, subsidy are not free. They come from taxpayers, and they are not cheap. They apply to every unit sold of this item (which is now further increased by the subsidy).[^284]
- This increased trade creates "deadweight loss". This deadweight loss is different than by taxes. This deadweight loss means that there are trades which are net losses, meaning, the buyer and seller and not gaining more than the subsidy.[^284]
- Adjusting the previous example a bit, if the seller will be willing to sell something for $2.30 and the buyer will be willing to pay $1.50 and there is a $1 subsidy, there will be a $.20 surplus between the consumer and producer (for example, the subsidy goes to the producer and the prices adjusts to $1.40, then each have a surplus of $.10, or if the subsidy goes to the consumer and the price adjusts to $2.40, there is once again a surplus of $.10 to the producer and consumer).[^284]
- Meanwhile, this is costing the taxpayer $1 to make this happen. This is called "deadweight loss".[^284]
- Like by taxes, the side that is inelastic reaps more of the benefits (see image).[^284]
- ![[Pasted image 20241223135831.png]][^284]
- When the supply side is relatively inelastic, this means that the an increase in price will not increase the supply much, so the small increase in supply is quickly eaten up by buyers who are willing to pay only slightly less than the equilibrium price before the subsidy. The opposite is true when the demand curve is relatively elastic.[^284]
- Subsidies increase the supply and demand. For this reason, subsidies are a good method for increasing trade of a particular commodity.[^285]
- For example, to increase employment of low-skill workers, Nobel Prize winning economist Edmund Phelps suggests introducing and expanding a wage subsidy.[^285]
- An important point about deadweight loss: Just because something is a "deadweight loss", that doesn't mean that it is overall bad. For example, in this situation, there are a of of positives that can come out of increasing low-skill labor employment. For example, in can decrease poverty, decrease payments from Medicaid and other social programs, decrease crime, etc. This may outweigh the "deadweight".[^285]
- This may be better than a minimum wage because while this increases the supply of labor, it decreases the demand for it, and hence, overall hirings.[^285]
- The government already has a wage subsidy called the Earned Income Tax Credit.[^285]
- As such, some economists who want to help low-wage workers, like Phelps, argue for the expansion of this program.[^285]
- For free-market economic systems, prices work as incentives.[^286]
- When the price goes up for something, this gets people to try to use less of something, or find a more inexpensive way to produce the thing.[^286]
- The increase in price has a long ripple effect through the economy, continuing to influence people throughout the economy.[^286]
- For example, in the 1970s the price of oil went up.[^286]
- This made the price of roses in the United States in the winter go up as most roses in the US were grown in greenhouses which took oil to heat up.[^286]
- This encouraged some people to buy alternate goods (buying chocolate for valentines day instead) or trying to find cheaper ways to sell roses. This caused entrepreneurs to try to pay shippers to ship roses from other countries to the US. This also caused an increase in what people were willing to pay for someone in these countries to grow roses.[^286]
- People acting in their own self-interest starting growing/shipping roses as the price for the roses increased high enough.[^286]
- This (as well as using alternatives to roses) lowered the amount of oil being used, allowing it to be used for other important things which may have not been able to stop using oil.[^286]
- What is fascinating is that there is no central authority making this happen. It occurs naturally by people responding to incentives.[^286]
- Products we use come from a wide variety of places. An iPhone contains parts and labor from all around the world. A supermarket has fruits grown in many different countries. To put all of these things together takes a tremendous amount of people coordinating together to make this happen.[^287]
- Markets create this coordination by getting many different and diverse people to act in their own self-interest toward accomplishing something.[^287]
- We always hear about conflict, but it is interesting to think about the coordination cooperation that is required to put so many things we use daily together.[^287]
- An example of seemingly mostly unrelated markets which in fact affect each other: If oil prices go up, that makes people look for alternatives to oil. One alternative is ethanol. Ethanol is produced from sugar in many places. This increases the demand for sugar. Increased demand for sugar increases the price of sugar. As such, the price of candy goes up. (And you can go on from there. Will people eat less candy? What will they use more, instead? How would increased demand on that new resource affect the economy? etc.)[^288]
- There are countless similar examples.[^288]
- The Great Economic Problem: We want to allocate out limited resources in the best ways possible. How do we do this?[^288]
- One way is in a centrally planned system. A single entity decides everything.[^288]
- Two problems with this:
	1. Lack of information. How do we decide which use of a resource is better. Even if we can decide between which of two particular products is superior, does that mean that this is the preferred way to use the resource? What if there are good substitutes for the superior product and none for the inferior product? And what about the substitutes for the substitutes? How do we even figure out how good a substitute is? Or all of the different ways a resource can be used? (And there are many additional problems that have nothing to do with substitutes.) There are simply too many variables to factor in.[^288]
	2. Lack of incentives. There are no incentives for people to tell the truth about their industry. If someone wants to use a certain product because it is slightly cheaper, they have no reason to tell the truth about if there is a good substitute, even if there is one. (This seems to just further the lack of information problem.)[^288]
- (This is in addition to other problems with central planning (like there is no incentive to work hard because you can't keep the profits anyway))
- The price system (free-market economies) solves the problem.[^289]
- When the price (set by the equilibrium) is above what someone wants to pay, they decide not to buy it. When the price is lower than what they are willing to pay, they are willing to buy it.[^289]
- This makes it that the people who put the most value in a resource get the resource, and those who do not need the resource as much, do not get it.[^289]
- All of the information that one needs about the value of a resource is wrapped into one metric: price.[^289]
- In addition, people have an incentive to follow the "metric". People are self-interested and so they want to follow what the "metric" tells them. This is "a signal wrapped up in an incentive".[^289]
- For example, when a price goes up, it tells people that a product is harder to produce and it also encourages them to look for alternatives unless they really need that product.[^289]
- The same is true of suppliers as well. When a price rises, it signals to suppliers that supply is scarce compared to the demand and encourages them to try to increase the supply.[^289]
- This is an argument in favor of price gouging after a disaster. The high price encourages suppliers to try to create that product for those that need it. Banning high prices removes this incentive for suppliers.[^289]
- Speculation:
- People attempt to predict the future price for some product. If they predict that the price will go up, they will buy some now and sell it at the higher price.[^290]
- This helps the economy because it stabilizes prices over time and it moves products from a time when they will be used for a relatively low value use, to the future when they will be used for relatively high value (see image).[^290]
- In the present, prices go up (due to decreased supply/increased demand from the speculators) but the the future, prices are lower than they would have been because the speculator sells his inventory, adding to the supply.[^290]
- One can speculate using the futures market.[^290]
- A future is an agreement between two people to trade a certain product for a certain price. If the price is higher than the agreed price, the one buying the commodity makes money because he buys the product at the agreed upon price and can now go and sell the product for the current market price which is higher.[^290]
- For example, two people (a buyer and a seller) agree to trade 1000 pounds of apples for $5000 in 2 years time.[^290]
- If the apples are worth $8000 on the market, the buyer can go and buy the apples from the seller for $5000 and sell them for $8000, pocketing the $3000.[^290]
- Practically, many futures contracts are not executed because the seller may noy have the commodity, or because the buyer doesn't want to deal with selling it. As such, the "losing" party will pay the difference. In the previous example, the seller pays the buyer $3000.[^290]
- (while this type of speculation can make you money, it doesn't transfer goods to the future, so it doesn't help with that.)[^7]
- Speculators have a strong financial incentive to be right. In addition, those who are wrong too much go bankrupt and have to stop speculating. These points keep the speculators market at least somewhat accurate.[^290]
- There was a paper published in 1984 called Orange Juice and Weather about using the oranges futures market to help predict Florida weather, and he found that it was helpful. {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Potentially more accurate than the weather stations. I am not sure if he said that</span>} This is because of the point previously mentioned: people had a strong financial incentive to predict the price of the commodity, which is strongly ties to the weather.[^290]
- While futures markets can be used to make predictions about highly related points (like orange juice and weather), there is still some noise (meaning, other factors which may make a difference about the orange juice crop, like fear of a worker strike, for example). Prediction markets cut out this noise, as they allow one to bet on the exact point that they want to bet on.[^291]
- Prediction markets or betting markets:
- People "trade shares" about predictions of real world events. People "buy shares" on whether a certain event will or will not happen. When the situation does or does not happen, the winners can cash out their shares, and the losers get nothing.[^291]
- For example, on the Iowa Electronic Market in 2008, people were able to buy "Obama shares" and "McCain shares". If their candidate won, the payout of these shares was $1 and the loser would get nothing. If you thought that Obama had an 80% chance of winning, you would be willing to spend up to $.80 on an Obama share. If the price was less than $.80, you would buy some shares. This would drive the price up by increasing demand for the shares. If the price was higher than $.80, you would sell Obama shares/buy McCain shares, pushing the price of Obama shares down or McCain shares up.[^291]
- When you have enough people doing this, the market tends to become more accurate. This is because people have financial incentive to try their best to be correct. People incorporate a wide swath of information. Also, if someone is consistently bad at making predictions, they will quickly run out of money.[^291]
- Betting markets on elections, for example, have been more accurate than polls.[^291]
- There are a wide variety of betting markets. You can bet on Oscar winners and things like that on the Hollywood Stock Exchange.[^291]
- Companies can use information found on betting markets to try and predict the future. For example, if a company makes a certain move and some bet on the company (like the EPS will be above a certain amount, for example) goes up or down, they can use that information.[^291]
- Betting markets have been quite accurate on these things. (See image for predictions on opening revenue for movies. There is a strong correlation between the prediction and what ended up occurring.)[^291]
- ![[Pasted image 20241224233008.png]][^291]
- Price ceilings:
- A Price ceiling is a maximum price allowed by government for a good to be sold or bought. It is illegal to charge above the price ceiling.[^292]
- This has five negative effects:
	1. It creates shortages.[^292]
	2. It causes a reduction of quality and level of service.[^292]
	3. It creates wasteful lines and search costs.[^292]
	4. It creates a loss in gain from trades (deadweight costs).[^292]
	5. It causes a misallocation of resources.[^292]
- A shortage is when the demand for an item exceeds its supply.[^292]
- A price ceiling causes this by forcing the price to be lower than the equilibrium price. This makes buyers have a high demand for the product, but sellers have low demand to sell it.[^292]
- In a free market, this is solved by buyers "bidding up" the price. This lowers the demand and increases the supply until we reach equilibrium. Because of the price ceiling, though, the price is not allowed to adjust upward (see image).[^292]
- ![[Pasted image 20241225233318.png]][^292]
- A loss in quality and level of service is also caused by this. This is because there is no motivation. Usually, you want to provide good quality and service to maintain customers. Now, there are more than enough customers, and so even with a loss in quality or service level, there is more quantity demanded than quantity supplied.[^292]
- (This lack of motivation due to a lack of incentive for profit is the reason for things like poor service at the DMV, and for terrible service in Russia under communism.[^292])
- Also, this leads to discrimination. In a free market economy, if a seller discriminates and refuses to sell to some people, they might not have enough buyers to sell to. With a price ceiling, a seller may not have to worry about this as they will have more than enough buyers, anyway.[^292]
- Lines and search charges.
- People compete for the limited supplies in a variety of ways when money is not allowed to be used to decide who gets a particular good. Sometimes, people will try to bribe those that have the good (with money or with favors), or use political or family connections to try and get some good.[^293]
- Sometimes, people will compete by "paying" with their time by waiting on line. People willing to wait long enough will be able to get the good. Those who are not, do not.[^293]
- Waiting on line is a bad form of competition. When buyers compete by using their money, the money goes to the seller. When buyers give up their time (by waiting in line), that does not go to the seller (see image).[^293]
- ![[Pasted image 20241226020202.png]][^293]
- Deadweight loss:
- There are trades which are beneficial for sellers and buyers which are not occurring due to the price ceiling. Some sellers would be willing to sell the product for a price above the price ceiling but below what the equilibrium price would have been and there are buyers who are willing to buy products at a price above equilibrium, but they are not willing to pay as much as those who need it the most. This mutual benefit is not occurring, hence a deadweight loss (see image).[^294]
- ![[Pasted image 20241226015325.png]][^294]
- Misallocation of resources:
- Price ceilings cause a misallocation of resources.[^295]
- If there is a high quantity and low demand for resources in one place and a low supply and high demand for resources in another, an entrepreneur may step in and buy the resource in the low demand location, bring it to the high demand location, and sell it there, pocketing the difference.[^295]
- This is good  for society as it brings resources from where they are not really needed to where they are.[^295]
- With a price ceiling in place, there is no motivation for the entrepreneur to do this because he won't be paid enough by those with high demand.[^295]
- This occurred in the oil shortage in the 1970s. Winter on the west coast was mild, leading to a low demand for oil for heat, but harsh on the east coast, leading to a high demand. However, there was no incentive to transport that oil. As such, some oil on the west coast went to heating up swimming pools, while on the east coast, some people didn't have enough oil to heat their homes.[^295]
- In addition, sellers have no incentive to try and find the buyer with the highest as the seller can't charge more anyway. As such, the seller may sell the product to however is easiest to sell to, or just sell the product randomly. Once again, those with the highest need are not getting a certain resource, even though they are willing to pay for it.[^295]
- Rent control:
- Rent control leads to the problems previously discussed.[^296]
- In the short-term, supply of apartments are pretty inelastic. There is only so much you can do with your apartment building in the short term. As such, the shortage that results from the price control is mostly due to the increased demand because of the lowered price.[^296]
- However, over the long term, apartments are more flexible. People can convert apartments into offices. In addition, people can stop building new apartments that they were going to build. Once that starts happening, the shortage of apartments affects many who would have been able to find an apartment if there was no price ceiling (see image).[^296]
- ![[Pasted image 20241226025013.png]][^296]
- Landlords have no incentive to upkeep the apartments because they will easily find new tenants, even if the apartment start falling apart.[^296]
- This is why price ceilings lead to the creation of slums.[^296]
- It can be extremely difficult to find an apartment when there is a price ceiling. As such, it takes a tremendous amount of time and effort to find one. This is a wasted search charge.[^296]
- Many people hold onto rent controlled apartments that they got a long time previously and now they don't need, but they hold onto it because the price is so low and so it is worth it. For example, some people may have a rent controlled apartment in New York, even though they don't live in NY. The price is so low it is worth keeping the apartment for when the person goes on vacation to NY. Meanwhile, you have families with many children crammed into tiny apartments, even though they are willing to pay more for a bigger apartment.[^296]
- Price floors create:
	1. surpluses. This means that there are more goods supplied than there are demands for that good.[^297]
	2. deadweight loss.[^297]
	3. unwanted increased quality.[^297]
	4. misallocation of resources.[^297]
- One common price floor is minimum wage. This is the minimum price that workers can "sell" their labor for.[^297]
- Price ceilings are more common than price floors. This is because price ceilings appear to help buyers, and there are more buyers than there are sellers. This is a good move for a politician.[^297]
- For the same reason, many politicians support minimum wage. This is a unique case where there are more "sellers" (workers) than "buyers" (employers).[^297]
- The "surplus" caused by minimum wage is better known as unemployment. People want to "sell" their labor but people aren't interested in "buying" it (see image).[^297]
- ![[Pasted image 20241226101045.png]][^297]
- Minimum wage only affects a handful of the labor market. 99% of workers earn more than minimum wage.[^297]
- Minimum wage mostly affects young and inexperienced workers. Even for these workers, though, about 94% of workers under 25 years old make over minimum wage.[^297]
- (I would assume that minimum wage also affect those making just above minimum wage. With minimum wage in place, these workers don't need to compete with other workers "undercutting" them by charging a smaller salary. This may allow them to ask for more money[^7])
- Small increases in minimum wage doesn't have much negative affect on prices or on unemployment, although small increases doesn't have much positive effect either.[^297]
- High increases in minimum wage would cause a lsrge amount of unemployment.[^297]
- Example 1: In 1938, congress passed the first minimum wage law. The wage was relatively low compared to the average wage in continental US. However, average wages in Puerto Rico were much lower and the minimum wage applied there as well. This led to enormous unemployment in Puerto Rico. Eventually congress exempted Puerto Rico.[^297]
- Example 2: France in the early 2000s had a relatively high minimum wage. They also had laws that made it hard to fire workers. As a result, firms were reluctant to hire young workers as they are relatively unskilled, and you don't really know how good of a worker they will be. In 2005 (when the economy was relatively strong), unemployment amongst those 25 years old and younger was 23%.[^297]
- There is also deadweight loss, meaning, that there are some potential employers and employees who would be willing to make a deal for less than minimum wage but are now not allowed to (see previous image).[^297]
- Yidias Hatorah means understanding the underlying logic for whatever it is that one is learning.[^298]
- A person who knows one masechta may be considered a talmid chacham,[^299] while someone who knows all of shas and may be considered an am haaretz.[^300] This is because of the point just mentioned: one's understanding of what learns is the main point.[^298]
- This is because without understanding the underlying logic, one doesn't know how to apply what they learn. Sometimes, a slight change in the situation can radically change what the halacha is. One can only realize this if they have a strong grasp on the underlying logic of the halacha.[^301]
- Price floors create unwanted increased quality.[^302]
- Usually, suppliers will compete with each other by offering lower prices.[^302]
- When they can't do that (because of the price floor), they will compete for customers by increasing the quality.[^302]
- While this may not be so bad, because the customer does receive the extra quality (as opposed to price ceilings and waiting on line as discussed previously), the customer would prefer to not receive the additional quality and not have to pay for it (see image).[^302]
- If there is a price floor, it is going to be above the equilibrium price (or there wouldn't be a point to making the floor). This means that customers want to pay less and are okay with receiving worse quality. If this were not the case, the equilibrium price and quality supplied would naturally move up on their own, without the price floor.[^302]
- ![[Pasted image 20241231011451.png]][^302]
- An example of this was the Civil Aeronautics Board (CAB) which regulated prices for airline flights between 1940 and 1985.[^302]
- One of the things they did was create a price floor which was above the equilibrium price.[^302]
- Prices for flights within states (which were not regulated by the CAB) were considerably cheaper than between states (which were regulated). This was even when the flights were the same distance.[^302]
- Initially, airlines raked in the additional profit.[^302]
- Over time, however, airlines competed by increasing quality (larger seats, non-stop flights, cocktail lounges on the plane, etc.)[^302]
- In addition, airlines made deals with unions to pay workers more (I am not sure exactly how this helped the quality. Perhaps they hired more workers or better ones?)[^302]
- In 1985, the CAR was disbanded. Prices immediately dropped and the airlines mostly got rid of all these extras. This was another indication that there was wasteful increased quality to compete for customers, meaning, that the customers preferred to not pay for the increased quality.[^302]
- This also leads to a misallocation of resources.[^302]
- Similar to what was just said, people may want low-cost airline carriers, but there are no resources moving there as they are not able to charge the low prices that the people want.[^302]
- As mentioned before, governments enact price controls because people think that it helps. Most people don't have a good understanding of economics.[^303]
- There are some positives to price ceilings. The advantage is that if you are able to get the product, then you pay less. The downside is that you may not be able to get the product as there will be a shortage.[^303]
- Similarly with minimum wage, if you have a job, this may help you, but you may not have a job.[^303]
- In addition, these policies are usually enacted to help the poor. However, they are not very targeted. Some wealthy people own rent-controlled apartments. Some people who are not poor (like students, or a young person living at home) have minimum wage jobs.[^303]
- As discussed before, subsidies may be more beneficial as they do not create there shortages and they are targeted (you give the subsidy based on income or something similar).[^303]
- The downside to subsidies is that they need to be paid for by the taxpayer. (This should be put with the discussion of minimum wage and wage subsidies.)[^303]
- Planned economies (such as communism) create price controls on all goods. This creates price ceilings and price price floors for different goods. This leads to all of the problems discussed earlier, namely:
	1. Surpluses and shortages.[^304]
	2. Poor quality and extra wasteful quality.[^304] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't really know why there was wasteful quality since people didn't really care about making money since they couldn't keep it anyway. Also, I assume that the poor quality wasn't because there were more than enough customers, but rather because people simply didn't care because they couldn't keep the profits.</span>}
	3. Lines and other search costs.[^304] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Once again, this may be because people didn't care about having normal service to distribute goods</span>}
	4. Deadweight loss.[^304]
	5. Misallocation of resources.[^304]
- Trade is good because:
	1. It allows people to exchange things they value less for things they value more.[^305]
	2. It allows for specialization which increases productivity.[^305]
- People don't need to take care of all of their basic necessities. They can get good at one thing, earn money from that, and use that money to buy the goods they need.[^305]
- This leads to a greater combined knowledge in a society.[^305]
- This is one of the advantages developed countries have over undeveloped countries. In undeveloped countries, people are largely focused on only a few industries (such as farming). This means that there is much less combined knowledge (since everyone mostly knows the same thing as everyone else).[^305]
- In developed economies, there is a much wider diversity in jobs so the combined knowledge is much greater.[^305]
- In addition, this allows for greater specialization. One topic can be divided into subtopics which can further be divided, etc.[^305]
- This is a reason for globalization. It creates a massive amount of combined knowledge (as you get the world's combined knowledge, not just one country's).[^305]
- People largely settled near bodies of water because it is easier to transport things over water, leading to increased trade.[^305]
- Even if one party is better at producing every type of good, it is still to the advantage of this party to trade. This productive party should produce whatever they are the best at and trade it to other people for other things.[^306]
- Example: Let's say that Lebron James is the best person in the world at ironing shirts. Should he iron his own shirts? He should not. Instead he should practice basketball (assuming that this makes him better and so he can earn more money) and pay someone else to iron his shirts, even though Lebron may be better than that person at ironing shirts.[^306]
- The idea is that Lebron has a high opportunity cost to iron his shirts. For him, ironing shirts comes at the cost of improving his basketball skills (which is a high cost for an NBA player). The person who he hired has a much lower opportunity cost.[^306]
- This is called comparative advantage. This means that this random person has an advantage in ironing shirts in that it comes at a lower opportunity cost than for Lebron (comparative means compared to the opportunity cost).[^306]
- Lebron is said to have the absolute advantage, meaning that he is better at both basketball and at ironing shirts.[^306]
- The idea is that you should produce what comes at the least opportunity cost and trade that for other things.[^306]
- If people get better at producing one thing, this benefits society as a whole because now it is worth it for people who produce that good to trade more of that good for other goods.[^307]
- (Specialization and comparative advantage are closely related topics. Specialization makes one good at producing something. This leads to comparative advantages.[^308])
- The Livush Mordechai there further says that the only way one can understand the words of the rishonim is with tremendous pilpul with talmidim.[^309] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I am not sure why this can't be done with chaveirim.</span>}
- This pilpul has to be focused on trying to understand the underlying svaros of the rishonim. It can't be focused on trying to say some big chiddush. This type of pilpul (meaning, the pilpul trying to understand the words of the rishonim) is what we see in the Ketzos and the Nesivos.[^309]
- Mishlei 12:8, as explained by Rabbeinu Yonah, talks about how a person who hears the truth but refuses to recognize it is scorned. This indicates that even such a person has the potential to straighten himself and see the truth (why would this person be scorned for something beyond his abilities?)[^310] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know how high of a level of yashrus this person can reach based on this passuk. However, since the passuk isn't clear, it would seem to be read that such a person is scorned in most situations that they refuse to recognize the truth. This would imply that a person has great potential to see truth.</span>}
- A similar point was expressed by Rav Chaim Brisker. A talmid asked Rav Chaim why it was particularly necessary for a talmid to have a rebbi once they reached the point that they could read and understand the Ketzos, Nesivos, Rabbi Akiva Eiger, and similar sfarim? The talmid could just learn those.[^310]
- Rav Chaim answered that the rebbi is needed to teach the talmid yashrus in svara. The only way this can be taught is through back and forth. Simply reading the Ketzos or similar sfarim will not get you this yashrus.[^310]
- Similarly, Rav Chaimalso said that a rebbi isn't someone who just presents nice pshat, but rather, he is someone who tries and straighten the way his talmidim think.[^311]
- A story illustrating this is said about Rav Dovid and the Chofetz Chaim. Rav Dovid and the Chofetz Chaim were learning a Tosafos together. The Chofetz Chaim told Rav Dovid to try and think of an answer for Tosafos's question. Rav Dovid thought of something and the Chofetz Chaim approved of the answer, praising Rav Dovid for it. Rav Dovid then thought of a "geshmak chiddush" type of answer. The Chofetz Chaim did not approve of that one.[^311]
- The Chofetz Chaim had such a strong yashrus in svara that he would be able to be mechavein to a train of questions and answers through generations. He would be mechavein to Tosafos's question on Rashi, the Beis Yosef's answer to the question, the Magen Avraham's attack on the Beis Yosef, etc.[^311]
- One only develops this sense of yashrus with a lot of work.
- Koheles Rabba,[^312] as explained by the Maharzu and Matnas Kehuna there, discusses how a person starts out learning by getting things wrong. He is then corrected.
