The person needs to work to the point of exhaustion and needs to withstand the feelings of discouragement (presumably, from getting constantly corrected) to be able to understand why the correct answer is correct and what he said is incorrect.[^313]
- Once WWII was over, Jews were hopeful that there would be a push to create a Jewish State in Palestine.[^314]
- However, neither the Labor Party or the Conservative Party in England were for really for a Jewish State in Palestine (even though Churchill himself was).[^314]
- The Irgun and Stern Gangs attacked British assets in Palestine. Now that the war was over, they started attacking military targets.[^314]
- According to J. W. V. Shaw, a British official part of running of Palestine said that the violence from the Jews was because they thought anything was justified to remove impediments to the Jewish State.[^314]
- The Labor Party in Israel also wanted to create a Jewish State (not just the terrorists).[^314]
- Despite previous failings with making peace with the Arabs, the Labor Party wanted to be able to at least live alongside the Arabs, rather than make the Arabs subservient to the Jews or kick them out.[^314]
- Jewish DPs wanted to go to Palestine. 100,000 visas were requested to allow this to happen.[^315]
- The British stuck to the 1939 white papers and didn't allow it as they did not want to upset the Arabs.[^315]
- Earl Harrison (a former official in the US government) wrote a report about the difficult conditions for Jews in the DP camps, and how they couldn't go back to the countries they left due to antisemitism (for example, several hundred Jews were killed in Poland by the Polish after the end of WWII).[^315]
- He recommended that the Jews be allowed to return Palestine.[^315]
- This became known as the Harrison Report.[^315]
- This had a strong influence on President Truman. He started pressuring Britain to allow the Jews to return to Palestine. The British remained firm through the beginning of 1946.[^315]
- Despite this the Anglo-American Committee was created. Its job was to examine the situation of the Jews in Europe and figure out a solution.[^315]
- Ernest Bevin, the British foreign secretary, became a face of the anti-Jewish immigration policy.[^315]
- Jews would largely make their way to Germany, Austria, and Italy and then try to take a boat to Palestine.[^315]
- As such, Britain asked the Italians to try and prevent the illegal immigration.[^315]
- Some describe Britain's efforts to try and prevent Jews from going to Palestine as an "obsession".[^315]
- Tension between Britain and the Jews ratcheted upward. Britain started sending Jews traveling illegally to Palestine, back to DP camps or even prisoner of war camps.[^315]
- The Irgun stepped up their terror attacks. In response, Britain killed any members of the Irgun they could catch.[^315]
- Asher Tratner, an unarmed high schooler was shot by a British officer for putting up anti-Britain posters. He was not offered treatment while in prison and later died from this wound.[^315]
- 40,000 Jews made their way illegally into Palestine between 1945 and 1948.[^316]
- Britain caught some of the boats. However, after detaining the people for some time, they released them into Palestine. (I think this shows that the British were perhaps not so anti a Jewish State.)[^316]
- The American Jewish Joint Distribution Committee (The Joint) helped Jews in the DP camps. Despite this, life in the DP camps remained bad.[^316]
- The Anglo-American Committee went through the DP camps, speaking to people. [^316]
- The vast majority wanted to go to Palestine. This was partially because of Zionist propaganda which convinced people to want to go to Palestine, but also because this actually was the best option. The DP camps were not a permanent solution, Britain and America were not opening its doors for the refugees, and there was tremendous antisemitism in the countries the Jews came from. Palestine was a place for Jews to be safe.[^316]
- The people in the camps disliked the people of the committee. They were British and so they were viewed as the "prison warden", keeping the people in the camps.[^316]
- Two boats with illegal immigrants were detained in Italy. The people on the boat refused to get off and went on a hunger strike. They threatened to sink the boat and commit suicide if people tried to force them off the boat.[^316]
- This tactic worked and the people were allowed to sail to Palestine. (This may further show that people weren't so strong against a Jewish State and Jews in Palestine.)[^316]
- A talmid shouldn't be discouraged by the difficulties brought by trying to understand the Torah. He should view this as the path to success, as this is the truth. There is no other way to understand the Torah.[^313]
- One can align his daas with the Torah to the point that even without an explicit source for something, they can be confident that they are correct. Rav Dovid (potentially from the Alter of Slabodka) would say that whenever the Rambam would say "יראה לי", he is saying that he doesn't have an explicit source for a halacha. However, these are his strongest points. When the Rambam quotes a source for a halacha, it is coming from one gemara. When the Rambam uses his koach hasvara, this is based off of the Rambam's understanding of shas.[^317]
- Even if one only learns one masechta, this koach hasvara will be strengthened, and it will help him with things related to other masechtos that he hasn't seen.[^317]
- We see the lengths that the gemara goes to to try and understand the words of amoraim that do not have an explicit source. The gemara goes through many ת"שs and is willing to be strongly madche the words of mishnayos. This is because of this point: if an amora has a certain understanding of something based on his koach hasvara which has been honed by learning Torah, this carries a lot of weight.[^318]
- A talmid has an important role to play. The talmid helps a rebbi gain clarity in the learning.[^319]
- Mishlei 20:5 (as explained by Rashi there) says that a rebbi has knowledge "locked away" and a talmid "unlocks" it.[^319]
- The talmid does this by asking questions. This forces the rebbi to have to concretize and clarify his knowledge.[^319]
- The Nesivos Hamishpat in his introduction to his sefer says that one needs people who he is greater than because by explaining things to his talmidim, he is forced get clarity in what he thinks by forcing him to focus on a discreet point.[^320]
- We see this from the Maharsha as well. In his peirush on maseches Shabbos, he says that he did not put his peirushim at the end of פרק כלל גדול in because he did not present them to the yeshiva. It seems that this is because of the point just mentioned.[^319]
- Taanis 7a (as explained by Rashi) says that the same way a small tree can spark a larger tree {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know what that means</span>}, a smaller talmid chacham can spark a larger talmid chacham through asking questions. The gemara further says that this is what is meant by מתלמידי יותר מכולם. The Rambam says similar.[^321]
- Rav Yehoshua Polick in his sefer נהורא דאורייתא, maamar 3 perek 8 says that one should take on many talmidim because talmidim, more than chaveirim, force one to gain clarity through their questions.[^321]
- in 1945, Britain's Home Secretary, Chute Edge, said that Britain would not accept large scale immigration because of concerns of housing, and taking jobs from British citizens, especially during demobilization.[^322]
- Madagascar (a British colony), was also ruled out as it did not have the infrastructure.[^322]
- In April 1946, the Anglo-American Committee told the British government to allow the Jews to go to Palestine. The British government continued to refuse.[^322]
- Due to this, the Haganah, Stern Gangs, and Irgun agreed to work together against the British. They called themselves the Jewish Resistance Movement.[^322]
- On one night in June they took out numerous roads and railway lines.[^322]
- In response, the British arrested thousands of people and closed Jewish Agency buildings. Many leaders of the Jewish Agency were arrested. Only Ben-Gurion, who was abroad, and Weizmann were not.[^322]
- This became known as Black Saturday.[^322]
- British officers discovered a massive secret weapons cache. In response, the Jews stepped up illegal weapon production.[^322]
- In July, 42 Jews were killed in Kielce Poland in what became known as the Kielce Pogrom. This caused Jews to further try and get to Palestine. About 130,000 ended up in German DP camps (as they were unable to continue through British-controlled Austria).[^322]
- On July 22nd, the Irgun blew up a wing of the King David Hotel. This killed 91 people, many of whom were just hotel employees and some of whom were Jews.[^322]
- This attack was condemned by the Jewish Agency and many others.[^322]
- This brought an end to the Haganah working with the Irgun and Stern Gangs.[^322]
- In July Britain decided to deport illegal immigrants to Cyprus. Between August 1946 and December 1947, 51,000 Jews were deported there, put into camps and watched by British guards.[^322]
- A British Cabinet Committee came up with the following plan (called the Morrison Plan after Herbert Morrison):[^322]
- Palestine would be divided into 3 zones. Britain would get 43%. Arabs 40%. Jews 17%.[^322]
- All 3 would be under British rule.[^322]
- Only people from Germany, Austria, and Italy, or children Eastern Europe would be allowed to emigrate to Palestine.[^322]
- America would have to pay for the transportation and food for 2 months.[^322]
- If this was accepted, Britain would allow the 100,000 visas.[^322]
- The Jews in Palestine rejected this as this did not give them enough land. As it was, numerous settlements would have been outside the Jewish zone.[^322]
- For most of 1946, Britain did not let up the restrictions, nor the containment of the Jewish Agency.[^323]
- While under this containment, the Jewish Agency arranged for the creation of new settlements in remote parts of Palestine.[^323]
- Most of these were in spread throughout the Negev, while a few were set up near the Lebanese border.[^323]
- On one night, 11 new settlements were created.[^323]
- Some Jews went to South America, the US, Canada, and Australia, but most wanted to go to Palestine.[^324]
- Ben-Gurion and Weizmann agreed that they would be willing to partition Palestine (assuming that the partition would be reasonably big enough) if that meant that Jews would have a place of their own and not have any restrictions on immigration.[^324]
- The 22nd Zionist Congress occurred near the end of 1946.[^324]
- Weizmann comdemned the British for their immigration restrictions, even during WWII when Jews were getting wiped out in Europe.[^324]
- However, Weizmann condemned terrorism and attacks on British assets.[^324]
- Many in the committee were strongly against this. They felt armed resistance was justified. This side was led by Ben-Gurion.[^324]
- The committee voted on this and by a slim majority, embraced Ben-Gurion and the path of armed resistance.[^324]
- This represented a shift in leadership from Weizmann to Ben-Gurion.[^324]
- Violence and retaliation between the Irgun/Stern Gangs and the British increased.
- The Arabs refused any sort of partition and demanded a total end to Jewish immigration.[^325]
- The British didn't want to offend the Arabs as many of the British colonies had large Arab populations.[^325]
- In addition, Britain realized that there was a lot of oil in the middle East and they did not want to offend the Arabs.[^325]
- They thought that if they give the Jews any land, they will try to expand. At the same time, the Arabs would certainly try to get rid of that Jewish State.[^325]
- Britain realized that the cost of running Palestine was not worth the reward, so at the beginning of 1947, they decided to leave the decision of what to do with Palestine to the UN.[^325]
- On March 1, 1947 an British Officers' Club was blown up. This was condemned by the Jewish Agency. (When the World Zionist Congress agreed to armed resistance, I guess they meant smaller acts? I am confused why they would condemn this if they weren't against using violence.)[^325]
- Ben-Gurion started preparing the country for war.[^325]
- At that time, the Jews had a supply of guns, machine guns, and mortars. However, they did not have heavy weapons, armored vehicles, troop transports, any aircraft, or naval units.[^325]
- With this lack of weapons and only a limited supply of manpower, the idea of taking on the Arab armies seemed daunting.[^325]
- The gemara in Baba Metzia (84a) discusses how Rav Yochanan became tremendously despondent after the death of Reish Lakish to the point that he started losing his mind and his fellow amoraim davened for him to die.[^326]
- This was because Reish Lakish asked many questions on Rav Yochanan's teachings, which led to a deeper understanding. Clearly, Rav Yochanan did not feel that he had another way to reach this same depth of understanding.[^326]
- As such, we see that in addition to the benefits to the talmidim of learning in this back-and-forth, give-and-take, question-and-answer format discussed before, there is also a benefit to the rebbi.[^326]
- There is a further benefit to the rebbi that he is pushed to learn to make sure that he has a clear understanding of what he teaching if he knows that he is going to come under fire from the talmidim.[^327]
- Protectionism: Government may try and "protect" a domestic economy from foreign producers. They do this by making quotas (only so much of a certain product is allowed to be imported) and tariffs (charging taxes on certain or all imported goods).[^328]
- The effect of global free markets is that it lowers the price, increases the amount of a good that is consumed, and decreases the amount supplied domestically (assuming that a foreign market could produce a certain good for cheaper) (see image).[^328]
- ![[Pasted image 20250102135549.png]][^328](The graph is working with foreign supply being unlimited at a certain price to make things simple, hence it is perfectly horizontal. The point is the same with a standard sloped supply curve)
- Tariffs decrease these effects. depending on how high the tariff is, these effects may still exist but be lessened, or not exist at all (see image).[^328]
- In addition, tariffs generate revenue for the government (see image).[^328]
- ![[Pasted image 20250102140113.png]][^328]
- Overall, tariffs create waste. They create deadweight loss, and they utilize supplies inefficiently.[^328]
- Deadweight loss is created by the fact that there are now useful trades that people would have made with the cheaper foreign supplier that they is now not worth it for them to make due to increased prices by the tariff.[^328]
- Supplies are overall used less efficiently because it would be more efficient to utilize the foreign suppliers (which produce the good for cheaper) and have the domestic producers go and produce something else.[^328]
- ![[Pasted image 20250102140530.png]][^328](In this example, the tariff makes the foreign supply equilibrium price equal to the domestic supply equilibrium price).
- The amount of waste that is generated can be calculated by simple geometry. Usually, though, the exact amounts need to be estimated so the exact amount can't really be known.[^328]
- Tariffs are bad for domestic consumers but are good for domestic producers.[^328]
- Overall, though, they are bad.[^328]
- (This is not discussing the fact that many times, if a country tariffs a different country, the other country will respond with tariffs of their own, which is bad for domestic producers.[^7])
- The UN began hearing arguments from the Jews and the Arabs about Palestine in May 1947.[^329]
- They established the United Nations Special Committee on Palestine (UNSCOP). To research the issue.[^329]
- The Arabs boycotted the committee as the committee wanted to find a solution for the illegal Jewish migrants. The Arabs felt that there shouldn't be any "solution" for these illegals.[^329]
- During this time, numerous boats with illegal immigrants were captured by the British Navy and the passengers were sent to Cyprus.[^329]
- One boat, the Exodus 1947, was ordered to take all of the migrants back to Europe. The migrants were ordered off the boat and onto a different one to make the return journey.[^329]
- Some members of the UNSCOP watched with horror as the migrants were beaten onto the new boat. This helped them make their decision.[^329]
- Ben-Gurion and Weizmann talked to members of UNSCOP. Their arguments consisted of:
	1. Palestine was the historic Jewish homeland.[^329]
	2. The Jews were a stateless people throughout their long history and so they were, in a sense, "owed" a land.[^329]
	3. They had nowhere else to go. There was still incredible amounts of antisemitism in Europe.[^329]
- The Arabs argued (in other contexts as they did not meet with UNSCOP) that it was not their responsibility to pay for Hitler's crimes.[^329]
- Weizmann said that Jews would be fine with partition as long as their was enough room.[^329]
- During this time, there was still violence between the Irgun/Stern Gangs and the British.[^329]
- In August, 1947, the UNSCOP proposed partition. A Jewish State would contain about 500,000 Jews and 400,000 and include the Negev, an Arabic State, which would contain over 700,000 Arabs and 10,000 Jews and include the Western Galilee, and Jerusalem and the surrounding towns would be under international trusteeship {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know what this means or why they did that</span>}.[^329]
- The Jews didn't like the fact that many Jews would not be in the Jewish State but they accepted. The Arabs rejected.[^329]
- Ben-Gurion realized that war was coming and readied the army. He wanted to defend the territory allotted to them, but also potentially conquer some more.[^329]
- King Abdullah of Jordan agreed to stay neutral as the king had some issues with the mufti, Al-Husseini. {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">It would be nice to know what these issues were</span>}[^329]
- The UN General Assembly debated and voted on partitioning Palestine.[^330]
- In the end, the vote was 33 in favor (mostly western countries), 13 against (mostly Arabic and Muslim countries), and 10 abstentions.[^330]
- At some time in the near future, the partition would go into affect.[^330]
- The Haganah organized and trained. Some people in the Haganah fought in WWII. Their veteran leadership was useful.[^330]
- This was technically illegal since the British still were in charge for the time being, so the Haganah had to do all this in secrecy.[^330]
- The mufti wanted all of the Jews to be driven out of Palestine, a belief he held for the rest of his life.[^330]
- A person needs to acquire middos before learning Torah. This is seen from many sources such as Pirkei Avos Perek 3 Mishna 9 and 17 (as explained by Rabbeinu Yonah), the hakdama of the Orchos Tzadikim, Ohr Yisroel Maamar 27, and Emunah Ubitachon (by the Chazon Ish) at the beginning of perek 4.[^331]
- Some arguments against free international trade:
	1. Decreases American jobs.[^332]
	2. Causes child labor.[^332]
	3. Some jobs need to remain domestic because of national security concerns.[^332]
	4. Some jobs have positive externalities.[^332] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Add link. It comes later. I don't really understand this argument</span>}
	5. US well being can be increased with certain jobs remaining domestic.[^332] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">This is vague. I should rewrite this</span>}
- Counters:
	- Decreases American Jobs:
		1. Trading with foreign countries gives them more money which they can then use to buy US-made products which increases American jobs. However, this moves jobs from fields that produce things that we import (generally low-skill products), to fields that produce things that we export (generally high-skill products).[^332] In addition, they may spend some of their money on American products, but some on other products.[^7]
		2. Overall, this leads to comparative advantage. The US is a relatively highly educated workforce and so it makes sense for the US to import low-skill products and produce high-skill products.[^332]
	- Causes child labor.
		1. Generally it is the poorest countries that use child labor. Not trading with them will really hurt those countries.[^332]
		2. As countries become richer, they stop using child labor (meaning, the percentage of the workforce which are children goes down. The absolute number is largely affected by the population of the country). Trading with these countries may help them get rid of child labor faster.[^332]
		![[Pasted image 20250127092612.png]][^332]
		3. Many times laws are ineffective and all they do is drive the child labor market underground. Children still work but get paid less for doing it.[^332]
		4. Other ways to combat child labor would be to help the economies of these poor countries, try to increase education levels, and try to incentivize parents to not send their kids out to work. (You can do both of these things by offering free meals for kids in schools.)[^332]
	- National security concerns.
		1. This is actually a good argument. However, it is somewhat limited in scope and is subject to much abuse (meaning, everyone claims that they are needed for national security).[^332]
	- Positive externalities.
		1. This argument is also good in theory, but it is difficult to figure out which industries and the ones with the positive externalities.[^332]
	- Increased well being.
		1. Other countries may retaliate with their own tariffs.[^332]
- Some situations lead to genuine sadness, such as losing a loved one, contracting a serious illness, becoming paralyzed after an accident, losing a limb, and losing a  job.[^333]
- These feelings are healthy and normal. This is not the same as depression.[^333]
- Some differences:
- Depression is caused by distorted thinking, does not resolve on its own, and leads to destroyed destroyed self-confidence and self-worth.[^333]
- Sadness is caused by what actually happened in a situation, is part of a normal, healthy process which resolves on its own, and doesn't hurt self-confidence or self-worth.[^333]
- There is a wholesomeness to healthy sadness. When you say good-bye to a loved one, for example, there is something meaningful in that moment of sadness. Depression is totally not like this.[^334]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I feel like I could use some more details than this.</span>}
- These powerful, negative situations can lead to depression. These can be difficult to uproot because they seem to be based on reality. It doesn't seem that there is a logical fallacy.[^333]
- Here are some things that a person may be making a logical fallacy about:[^333]
- Self-value/self-worth is not tied to productivity so society. A person a has a intrinsic self-value/self-worth. This is particularly useful for people after losing a limb, contracting an illness, or getting older. (I don't know how much people relate to this and so how much of a difference this will make to people. I feel like this is better for religious people.)[^333]
- One may be limited in what they can do now compared to what they were able to do before, but there are still so many things that one can still do. Instead of focusing on the negative, by using what one used to be able to do, or using what is "normal" as the reference point, one can not use any reference point and simply think about all of the things that one can do. This is the cognitive distortion of negative filtering. This can be particularly relevant after contracting an illness or losing a limb.[^333]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Perhaps when rewriting it may make more sense to group the negative situations (meaning, like put paralysis, disease, and loss of limb together, and then write some of the ways to deal with it, and then put loss of job and put some of the ways to deal with that, etc.</span>}
- Loss of job: You feel like you are worthless and have nothing to contribute. Many of the previous points deal with this (it seems that this particularly leads to many of the classic cognitive distortions). In addition, think about how you would treat another person. You wouldn't call them worthless and utter failures. This is because it is not productive (it isn't going to motivate them), and because it is not true. So don't do the same to yourself.[^333]
- Suicide of a close relative: People naturally blame themselves. They feel like they could have or should have done something different. However, this is always easier to realize in hindsight. You can't ever know what someone is thinking or feeling. In addition, you can't control what they are thinking or feeling. At best you can influence it. {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I feel like there are times when you really could have/should have known that the person was planning something and it was a rather egregious mistake to not come to that realization. Perhaps the response is the reason why you made the mistake, namely, you are only human and so you naturally are distracted, take the status quo (that nothing is happening) for granted, etc. Maybe this is a good rational response? I'm not sure</span>}[^335]
- Even after one recovers from their depression, there is more work to be done to make sure that one does not relapse.[^336] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I feel like the following points can be used as part of the "initial treatment" process, not just not prevent a relapse</span>}
- People have certain "silent assumptions". This means that a person values something and they are scared that they are failing at it.[^336]
- For example, let's say someone insults your driving and you feel depressed. You feel like he is right and you feel like a total loser. The classic CBT response would be to rationally respond "he's a random person. What does he know about me?" or "I am usually a good driver. Every now and then I make mistakes, just like everyone else". You focus on why the statement is inaccurate (or at least your interpretation is).[^336]
- We are going to change things up a bit. Ask yourself, let's say he is right, why does that bother you? There is something underneath.[^336]
- You get an answer. Then you try it again. You ask yourself "if *that* were true, why would *that* bother me?" You keep going at it until you get to the bottom (I assume this means until you can't go any farther. Something bothers you just because it does).[^336]
- You then respond to these "silent assumptions" with usual rational responses.[^336]
- Another way to find your silent assumptions is with the Dysfunctional Attitude Scale test, which can be found online, such as [here](https://themathersclinic.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/pdfs/Mathers-Clinic-Dysfunctional-Attitude-Scale.pdf) and [here](https://psytests.org/cbt/bdasAen.html). (There are a few slightly different versions but the point is the same.)[^337]
- This can be used to measure areas that can strongly negatively affect one's emotions, namely, approval (one's self esteem is based on other people's approval of them), love (one's self esteem is based on how much other people love them), achievement (one's self esteem is based on how much he/she accomplishes), perfectionism (self explanatory {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I guess most of these are?</span>}), entitlement (self explanatory), omnipotence (feeling that everything is one's own fault), and autonomy (feeling that one's emotions are because of outside forces, not oneself).[^337][^338]
- All of these categories will be explained.
- Approval:
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I feel like some of the following points have already been discussed in the book. I don't know if they need to be repeated or organized in a way to focus on approval</span>}
- For disapproval to be so powerful, you need to "buy in", meaning, that you need to think the other person is right about you.[^339]
- Step one is realizing that it is entirely possible that the other person is wrong.[^339]
- Step two is realizing that even if the other person is correct, that doe not mean that you are utterly worthless. At most it means you have a fault, just like everyone else, and it may not even mean that. It may mean that one time you were slightly inconsiderate and that's it, which, of course, everyone is from time to time.[^339]
- People may use your need for approval to take advantage of you.[^339]
- This need for approval may have been created inside of you because of some childhood incident.[^340]
- Responding rationally to that incident now, even though you were too young to have been able to then, can be helpful.[^340]
- For example, if someone's parent told him that he was no good and he would never amount to anything, they can realize now that their parent was overgeneralizing and, perhaps, that parent had something going on that day that caused them to overreact like that.[^340]
- (It seems like there is some omniscient component to the need for approval. Someone may criticize you by saying something like "why didn't you realize…" and this makes you think that you should have realized, when, in fact, it was quite reasonable that you did not realize.)
- Some people might not be so motivated to get rid of a particular emotional weakness they have (this point doesn't only apply to approval).[^341]
- One way to deal with this is to write down the pros and the cons of having this fundamental belief. If you are being honest you will probably quickly realize the cons far outweigh the pros.[^341]
- Also, think about some of the pros. Upon further reflection, you may realize that some of the pros are not actually pros at all, or at least not as complete of a pro as they originally thought. For example, your need for approval may drive you to do what people ask you, but perhaps you already have a lot on your plate and it would be better if you focused on the other things that you needed to do.[^341]
- Writing down a reworked version of a belief can be helpful. When it comes to approval, one might think something like "while it is nice to be approved by others, I can feel good about who I am regardless of what anyone else thinks". Look at the questions on the Dysfunctional Attitude Scale test that you agreed with to get some of your beliefs and rewrite them.[^341]
- One should try and write down all of the arguments they can think of against this secret belief. Don't write down things that you don't actually believe.[^341]
- We already discussed some verbal techniques one can use when dealing with someone who disapproves of you. One should employ the techniques used there.[^341]
- Sometimes, you can just be forthright and ask the person if they actually disapprove of you. The fact that they explicitly tell you that they do not is a powerful tool.[^341]
- If one is rejected by someone else, there are a few things to keep in mind:
	1. They could have rejected you because of some part of your personality didn't fit with them. This doesn't mean that you are a bad person or anything like that. You're loud and they want quiet, or you're quiet and they want loud, etc. This doesn't mean anything about the quality of person you are.[^341]
	2. Even if the person rejects you for something that is more objectively disliked (you lose your temper a lot), that still doesn't mean that you are a bad person. It means that you have a fault, just like anyone else. If you are willing to work on this fault and try to correct it, then it is on them for rejecting you anyway. This is something to think about to get yourself to not "buy" what the other person is saying.[^341]
- Despite all this, it is normal to feel down after getting rejected. This is perfectly normal. One thing you can do to try and recover is to allow yourself to grieve at a specific time every day for a specific amount of time (about 10 minutes should be enough). Think about how much you miss the person and let yourself cry or get angry or whatever. After the time is over, recompose yourself and carry on with your day.[^341] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I am not sure what I think about this. This feels like it is opening the door for depression to crawl in</span>}
- One trick to rid yourself of the need for others' approval is to carry a wrist counter {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I feel like this isn't something people are going to do. Maybe count on your phone instead? This  really applies whenever a suggestion with a wrist counter came up.</span>} and every time you do an action that you approve of (you do something nice, you do something you were pushing off, etc.) click the button. At night, see what number you are up to and record it. Keep doing this every day. After some time, you will begin to feel your own approval for yourself and you won't need others' approval.[^341]
- Obviously, approval feels good and disapproval stings. This is normal. We aren't trying to get rid of these feelings entirely. We just want to get rid of the need for and reliance on approval. {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">The same is true for the other things that will be discussed</span>}[^341]
- Even if one is granted extra intelligence and understanding, this won't help him understand Torah unless one has also developed proper middos, because Torah is made up of many finely detailed points.[^342]
- (See Emunah Ubitachon Chapter 3 Os 24 ד"ה ואין חכם בתורה, Igaros Chazon Ish Chelek 1 Os 3, Siforno to Devarim 32:15 ד"ה שמנת,, Rabbeinu Bechayei at the beginning of parshas Kedoshim, and Rabbeinu Bechayei on Shemos 18:20)[^343]
- Love:
- Start with tallying up the pros and the cons of this need for love (as discussed by approval).[^344]
- Keep in mind that being needy and dependent on people makes it harder to be loved. People aren't interested in clingy people.[^344]
- Some people have a belief that the only way they can be happy is if they are loved.[^344]
- To counter this false belief, think of things that you have and can do on your own that can make you happy. Sign up for an exercise class, go to college, make yourself a nice dinner, etc.[^344]
- In addition, actually go and do those things. You will find that you actually enjoy yourself and you can actually be happy without someone else. This will further dispel this incorrect belief.[^344]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I need to think about how this applies in frum circles. To a large degree, having a family is a major part of one's place in society (which, granted, is societally constructed) but is also a major part in the religion (having a family, having kids, etc. How do you combat this belief?)</span>}
- One trick is to make a chart. Write down some activity that you plan on doing and how much pleasure you think you will receive from doing it (meaning, on some scale, like 1-10). After doing the activity, write down how much you actually enjoyed it. As an added step, write down if you did the activity by yourself or with someone else. You may learn that you enjoy doing things by yourself more than you would have thought.[^345]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">This method was discussed before although I don't remember what it is called</span>}
- If activities are still not so enjoyable, it may be because you are ruining them with negative thoughts. Try to think about what thoughts you were having.[^345]
- You can use the double column technique (where you write down a negative belief and a rational response).[^345] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Perhaps just summarize the written techniques as discussed earlier?</span>}
- Generally, women are more concerned with love and approval than men are, and men area generally more concerned with achievement than women are.[^346]
- Achievement (work):
- Start with the pros and cons list.[^346]
- There are some pros. You will be able to get yourself to work harder.[^346]
- However, this comes at a cost. Your work will take over your life and you will lose other things you value (your family, for example).[^346]
- In addition, you are setting yourself up that if something happens (you get sick, or just get unlucky) your self-esteem will shatter.[^346]
- To try and fight this belief, think about how ludicrous it is to equate worth with achievement. People have inherent worth which has nothing to do with achievement.[^347]
- For religious people, one can argue that G-d put them in a role and they are judged on how they fulfilled that role, not anything else (although I am not 100% sure that this is true)[^7] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">link to haskafa</span>}
- Also, lot's of times the achievement is only in one specific area. Think to yourself how just because one accomplishes in one are does not define his overall self-worth[^347] (although perhaps one may respond to that by saying that the fact that one is accomplishing in any worthwhile area is enough to increase one's self-worth).[^7]
- You can use the double column to talk back to your negative thoughts equating your sense of worth with accomplishment.[^348]
- You can also use the pleasure predicting sheet (discussed by love) to try and see if you really need to be accomplishing.[^348]
- Important middos for learning:
- Emes:
- Pirkei Avos (4:5. As explained by Rabbeinu Yonah there) discusses how it is important for one to have a strong desire for the truth and be willing to sit for a days, and even years, to be able to clarify a small point, meaning, that one is driven by a desire to make sure that their understanding of is correct and there is no amount of effort that is too great to make sure that this happens.[^349]
- Even when there is nothing telling the person that they have some misunderstanding, they are still willing to toil to understand as best as they can to make sure that their understanding is correct.[^349]
- The difference between someone who wants to follow halacha (who is also a chashuv person for having that desire) and this person with nearly unquenchable desire for truth is considered a totally different level.[^350]
- The Pnei Yehoshua writes that he would sometimes spend many years on one sugya.[^351]
- Pirkei Avos (2:7 as explained by Rabbeinu Yonah there) says that anxiety over ones possessions will shorten one's life, while anxiety over Torah, which naturally should have the same effect, will lengthen one's days.[^352]
- It is clear from there that the level of anxiety for Torah one should have is at least the level that one has about their possessions.[^352]
- A talmid of Rav Naftali Trop said that Rav Naftali would sometimes give multiple pshatim in shiur on a topic. Each pshat he gave was beautiful and would have been praised by the gedolim in Europe. However, Rav Naftoli would keep working because for some reason or other, he didn't like what he was saying. He would keep going until he found a pshat he liked.[^353]
- This point is further seen from the fact that Rav Naftoli would only give one shiur a week and he would spend the the week working on the shiur. Yet, he focuses on one topic and doesn't incorporate much from shas. He was clearly using his extraordinary brain power to try and explain one point as bet as he could.[^353]
- the middah of Emes is also helpful when it comes to paskening halacha lemaaseh for oneself.[^354]
- A person has a lot of negios and that can blind a person from getting the halacha right.[^354]
- Even if the person has yirah, that may not be enough to fight these negios.[^354]
- If someone couples the yirah with this midah of emes, that can get them to overcome the negios.[^354]
- To clarify, the middah of emes is about emes for its own sake, not just about avoiding doing an aveirah (as that is what yirah is). A person with the middah of emes will even be careful about not doing aveiros by mistake because that would be the result of a distortion of the truth.[^355]
- See Ohr Yisroel maamar 29, the end of chapter 5 earlier in this sefer and Emunah Ubitachon Chapter 3 Os 30.[^356]
- The Ralbag in Parsha Yisro discusses how a judge needs to have a love for truth so they can judge honestly.[^357]
- Tochacha:
- A person needs to be able to accept tochacha.[^358]
- Rabbeinu Bechayei (beginning of parshas Shemos) says that the Torah is entirely tochacha.[^358]
- This seems difficult to understand as it appears that much of the Torah is not tochacha. There are many parts of the Torah that do not talk about issurim at all.[^358]
- It appears that this means that the Torah is full of wisdom that is actually beyond human comprehension and it is only because Hashem bestows upon us understanding that we are able to understand it.[^358]
- A person who is not willing to accept the wisdom of the Torah and straighten his daas along the yashrus dictated by the Torah will end up arguing on the Torah itself.[^358]
- See Ohr Yisroel Igeres 4 (mentioned before) that discusses this same point.[^358]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">To me this seems more along the lines of anivus, or the ability to admit that your wrong. Well, I guess that is what tochacha is.</span>}
- Perfectionism:
- Start out with the usual writing down the pros and cons of being a perfectionist.
- A perfectionist may feel that they can't enjoy some accomplishment unless it is perfect.[^359]
- Test this hypothesis. Write down a task you accomplished, how well you did it (on a scale of 1-10) and how much satisfaction you got from doing it (on a scale of 1-10). You may be surprised how little correlation there is between how well you did the task and how much enjoyment you got.[^359]
- One thing to keep in mind is that there is no such thing as perfect. You will never be good enough because you can't ever be perfect. This is a self-defeating position.[^359]
- Sometimes, perfectionism is coupled with anxiety, sometimes about specific situations. For example, one may want to wash their hands a lot, or double check all of the locks in a house after he knows that they are all locked.[^359]
- A person can make extreme focus on these particular situations and do not give in to the compulsion. You can write down how emotionally distraught you after some interval of time, and then after the next interval, etc.[^359]
- It is extremely difficult to do something purely lishma.
- Dovid Hamelech said that when Yishai was with his wife, he did not have totally pure intentions. Part of his intentions was his own hannah.[^360]
- Yishai was one of the few people who didn't do any aveiros his whole life. Clearly he was on a very high madreiga. Yet Dovid knew that even he wasn't totally pure when he would have tashmish because it is nearly impossible for a human to be able to do something totally lishma. Certainly we who are nowhere near this madreiga can have to realize that we can do things with a lot more lisheim shamayim.[^361]
- Tosafos asks a stirah between the gemara (Brachos 17a) which says that people should only do things lishma, and it would be preferable for a person to not have been created rather than do things shelo lishma, and the gemara (Pesachim 50b) which says that a person should do things shelo lisham because by doing things shelo lishma, they will come to do things lishma. (Tosafos gives his own answer but that is not what we are focusing on.)[^362]
- Rav Yisroel Salanter gives his own answer to this question. He says that the Brachos is referring to someone who is doing something not in line with how the mitzva is supposed to be performed. However, when someone does so the mitzva as one who is doing the mitzva properly would do the mitzva, this is valid and encouraged.[^363]
- This will lead to the inclination to do the mitzva shelo lishma to diminish. This is because there are times that the way the mitzva should be done runs in conflict with how the person's shelo lishma intentions push him to do it a different way. The person will be acting against his shelo lishma drives.[^363]
- Sometimes a person might feel guilty about feelings of doing things shelo lishma which can have negative repercussions. A person can try having this in mind (in addition to what was discussed earlier that it is nearly impossible for one to be fully lishma anyway).[^361]
- Hashem promised Avram a child and Avram didn't ask for a sign. This is different than when Hashem promised Avram Eretz Yisroel that Avram did ask for a sign.[^364]
- Obviously Avram had full emunah in Hashem and he knew Hashem would do what Hashem said. He asked for a sign regarding receiving Eretz Yisroel because he was concerned that maybe Klal Yisroel would do aveiros and then lose what Hashem promised them. By not asking, Avram showed a certain completeness in emunah, a certain total reliance and giving himself over to the word of Hashem that he did not show when it came to Hashem promising Avram about the land.[^365]
- We see what the highest levels of bitachon are. There is an idea that even thinking about and making cheshbonos about what Hashem promised is to some degree a lack of bitachon. On the highest medreigos, a person totally gives himself over in complete trust and doesn't think about what Hashem promised him.[^361]
- (This is interesting to me because at the end of the day, Hashem didn't promise it to Avram if there would be aveiros done. So was Avram simply forgoing having a child if he would do aveiros? Or did he have emunah in a way that was beyond what Hashem promised him? I am not exactly sure but I talked with Daniel Hirsch and he thought that pshat was something along the lines that simply having cheshbonos, even if they were true, can hurt one's emunah. To me this is interesting because I would think that he is simply viewing the situation accurately. Hashem only promised him this reward in most, but not all, situations. Avram would be giving himself over as much as Hashem promised him. Perhaps that is not the way humans work. There is a massive difference between 100% and 99%, even though the 99% is actually accurate. I am not sure about this and I need to think about it. Maybe a good way to think about it like if one's parents actually love them. It is healthy, normal, and probably necessary to simply think that they do (in most situations). Now, technically, you don't know this 100%. Perhaps this isn't true. But to try and carry that around inside your head, meaning, every time one's parents do something for them to think that almost certainly did it because they love them but maybe the don't sounds like an absurd and nearly impossible way to live.) ^9afec3
- Rav was learning Torah. He got so involved that he accidentally injured himself. A certain tzedoki was there and commented that this is similar to what Klal Yisroel did when they accepted the Torah. Hashem asked them if they want the Torah and without worrying about what was in it ad taking care of themselves, they immediately accepted.[^366]
- Rav responded that Klal Yisrael had faith in Hashem that Hashem wouldn't mislead them.[^366]
- Rav further added that the reason why the tzedoki was unable to see that was because he lived his life tricking people so he was unable to think of trusting someone else as anything short of foolish.[^366]
- We aren't talking about a normal being. We are talking about Hashem. And this is right after Klal Yisroel just went through Yetzias Mitzraim. They have every reason to have full faith in what Hashem is doing for them. Yet, the tzedoki as so blinded by the way he treats people that he is unable to see this.[^361]
- (I think rebbi might have also said that we see how one is supposed to fully give oneself over to Hashem and have full faith in him but I am not sure if he said this point.)
- (I came late to the shmuz and so I only heard the end of it. I am trying to extrapolate backwards about what the maareh makom was saying. Also, this should have gone earlier with the shmuz on Dovid.)
- When Yaakov was dying. He told Yosef to do with him a chessed shel emes and bury him in Eretz Yisroel. This is a chessed shel emes because Yosef couldn't hope to receive anything in return now that Yaakov would be dead.[^367]
- Obviously Yosef is going to do what his father asks him to do. Yaakov is trying to make sure that Yosef is going to take care of it in a purely lishma way.[^367]
- One would imagine that at such an intense moment with such great tzadikim, Yosef would certainly make sure to do this mitzva purely lishma, especially considering how much love Yosef had for his father. Apparently, doing something totally lishma is extremely difficult and even in this situation, Yaakov was concerned that perhaps Yosef would not be purely lishma.[^361]
- You can use the vertical arrow method (meaning, you ask yourself "am if that were to happen, why would that bother me?") for dealing with perfectionism.[^368]
- You can also challenge your belief that if you don't do something perfectly, things are going to be terrible. Try it and you may see that things didn't go so bad.[^368]
- Develop a process-oriented focus. Think of some reasonable steps you can take to accomplish a certain goal. Then try your best to follow that process. Grade yourself in your head on how well you were able to follow that process.[^369]
- Don't grade yourself on the result, Keep in mind that the result is beyond your control. For example, if you are trying to get a a certain job, you can interview and prepare your resume as well as you can, however, the final decision is decided by many factors, a lot of which are beyond your control (like if the boss has a friend who is interviewing for the job or the interviewer was in a bad mood on the day that you interviewed).[^369]
- Make yourself a schedule at the beginning of the day and stick to it. Set yourself only so much time per activity.[^370]
- Think about and write down the good things come from making mistakes, like how you can learn from them and grow.[^370]
- Perfectionists focus on all of the things that they did wrong. One thing to combat this is to carry a wrist counter (or phone) and click it every time you do something right. You may be surprised by how much you do right.[^370]
- Talk to a friend about something you messed up in. Usually, they will accept you despite your mess up. Seeing this will be beneficial. If your friend doesn't accept it, assert your right to make mistakes. Assert the fact that you are a human and you are putting in reasonable effort and despite that, mistakes will occur from time to time.[^370]
- Make a calculation. Compare how much you accomplish now with how much more you can accomplish by lowering your standards by a little bit. You might be doing things really well but it takes a lot of time that could be better spent doing other things.[^371]
- People become suicidal when they are depressed because they feel trapped. They feel like they are never going to be happy again. In fact, they feel like they were never truly happy in the past either.[^372]
- This is warped thinking caused by the depression.[^372]
- Assessing one's risk to commit suicide:
	1. Severe depression and feeling hopeless.[^373]
	2. Have attempted suicide in the past. Some people think that this is just someone trying to get attention or even a cry for help. Don't view it that way. This is a serious concern.[^373]
	3. Made concrete plans (the more concrete the more at risk one is).[^373]
	4. A lack of deterrents (you have a wife a children who you don't want to leave behind would be an example of a deterrent, for example).[^373]
- The more at risk one is, the more important it is for them to seek help from a licensed therapist.[^373]
- One doesn't need to be worried that by speaking things out, they will become more likely to commit suicide. When speaking with a therapist, the opposite is true. One will feel better by talking it out.[^373]
- Some therapists question their "right" to convince patients out of committing suicide. It is not a matter of "rights". It is a matter of trying to help people make the best decisions for themselves. And suicide is almost never the best decision.[^374]
- Suicidal desires are caused by mental distortions. You can recognize the distortion for what it is and fight against it using the methods discussed.[^374]
- One interesting technique is to role play. Have the patient be the therapist and the therapist should act like the therapist. The patient (now therapist) should talk to the therapist (now patient) about their problem and try to show the therapist the distortion in their thinking.[^374]
- It is important to keep in mind that your situation is not hopeless. Usually, depression disappears by itself. Also, there are many different modalities of therapy and medications and just because some have not yet worked, that does not mean that others are doomed to fail as well.[^375]
- Dealing with angry people:
	1. This is an opportunity to work on yourself. Keep that in mind.[^376]
	2. Sometimes, angry people will insult you. And sometimes those insults have at least a grain of truth in them. Use the disarming techniques discussed earlier. Ask them to continue their tirade until they get everything out and agree with at least in some way with what they say. Then respond in a matter-of-factly manner.[^376]
	3. Try to see the world through their eyes.[^376]
	4. Use the double-column technique to deal with lingering emotions.[^376]
- Dealing with ungracious people:
- People not expressing proper appreciation stems from the fact that you feel that they "should".
- You need to think about how it is their problem if they are unable to appreciate the good that you did for them.[^377]
- (It could be that one gets really bothered when someone doesn't appreciate something that they did because it makes them feel that they didn't actually accomplish anything. This can be especially frustrating after putting in a lot of effort to accomplish something. Keep in mind that what you accomplished may have nothing to do with this person's level of appreciation, especially if you know that this person is in general an ungrateful person. Also, try to think in a process-oriented. If you thought about what a person needs and you accomplished it in a reasonably good way, then you accomplished what you set out to do and you can feel good about that.[^7])
- Uncertainty that something bad has/will happen:
	1. You don't need to assume that the worst happened or will happen. That is your decision. Think about all the ways that it can/did end well (especially if it is actually true that it is probable that it did/will end well).[^378]
	2. Keep a process-oriented mindset. You put in a reasonable effort to make things go well and that is the only thing you can do.[^378]
- Chemistry of depression:
- (Note: This is from a book 50 years old. The research may have changed since then. This is useful to know what the theories were and to know what to check up.)
- There perhaps is a link between amines (chemical transmitters between nerves (neurotransmitters). Examples of amines include norepinephrine, dopamine and serotonin) and depression.[^379]
- Increased amines lead to more alertness and energy and decreased amines lead to less.[^379]
- Blood pressure meds lower one's amines (at least they did at that time).[^379]
- Depression sometimes runs in families, indicating a genetic component.[^379] (Or perhaps it is just correlated and is caused by other things, like a style of upbringing, or a certain family culture. Perhaps there was a study of siblings separated at birth to clarify this point.[^7])
- Nerves send messengers to each other in the following way:
- An electrical impulse travels down a nerve. It reaches the end of the nerve. There is small fluid-filled gap between this nerve and the next nerve known as a synapse. There are "packets" storing chemicals at the end of the nerve. When the electrical impulse reaches the end of the nerve, it releases the correct chemical, which traverses the synapse and reaches the next nerve, which triggers an electrical impulse in the second nerve. The chemicals are carried away and released through urine. The nerve needs to refill its stock of chemicals. It is thought that the nerves of depressed people don't restock these chemicals well.[^380]
- Mania is caused by extra amines.[^380]
- There are a few different types of antidepressants:
	1. Tricyclic antidepressants enhance the potency of the amines.[^380]
	2. M. A. O. inhibitors increase the level of amines.[^380]
	3. Lithium carbonate deals with extremes of moods. It isn't known how it deals with opposite moods. (At least it wasn't.)[^380]
	4. L-tryptophan is an amino acid part of amines, so it is a natural amine increaser. Also, it is fast-acting.[^380]
- Some antidepressants work for some people and not for others. It isn't (wasn't) known why this is. Just because some don't work for some people, others may work.[^380]
- You need to make sure that you take the "goldilocks" amount of an antidepressant. Too much is dangerous and too little won't do anything.[^381]
- It will almost certainly take at least two weeks for an antidepressant to kick in. At the same time, if the antidepressant doesn't work by 4 weeks, it is probably not going to work and it would make sense to move on and try something else.[^381]
- There can be some minor side affects that occur because of an antidepressant. Usually they will let up relatively quickly.[^381]
- However, some side effects are a sign that you are having too much of the antidepressant. Ask your doctor about what is normal.[^381]
- Tricyclic antidepressant have around a 65% chance of working for people trying it for the first time. This is considerably higher than 30% as is reported in a placebo group.[^381]
- Even after the depression leaves, one should stay on the antidepressant at least for some time and at least on some dosage, to avoid a relapse.[^381]
- M. A. O. inhibitors are particularly effecting on those undergoing unique depressions, such as phobias, anxiety, extreme anger, and OCD.[^381]
- M. A. O. inhibitors can cause a rise in blood pressure. As such one should be regularly monitored by their doctor.[^381]
- Lithium helps people with bipolar disorder and mania.[^382]
- This also takes at least 2 weeks from when you start taking it until there are positive effects.[^382]
- The longer you take it, the greater the effects are.[^382]
- Most people are prescribed this for life.[^382]
- If you have a history of mania in your family, lithium may help you even if you do not have mania yourself.[^382]
- With this as well there are normal minor side effects and some which indicate that you are taking too much. Ask your doctor about what is normal.[^382]
- L-tryptophan is an essential amino acid, meaning that your body needs it (to produce serotonin) but your body doesn't produce it. It has to come from what you eat.[^383]
- Most people naturally eat enough but some people do not.[^383]
- Studies on L-tryptophan have been mostly positive but a little mixed.[^383]
- It seems that it helps some patients, but it should be used in conjunction with therapy.[^383]
- One should not take an L-tryptophan supplement while taking an antidepressant.[^383]
- Some drugs which shouldn't be used:
	1.  Sedatives can cause lethargy which can increase depression.[^384]
	2. People become reliant on sleeping pills addiction after a few days and they need to keep taking more to fall asleep. In addition, they disrupt normal sleeping patterns. There are better alternatives to help with insomnia.[^384]
	3. "Pep pills", like Ritalin, create a high. Coming down from that high for depressed people can be bad. In addition, regular use can cause aggression.[^384]
	4. "Major tranquilizers", like Thorazine and Mellaril, should only be used for mania or hyperactivity.[^384]
- This isn't an exact science and some doctors may disagree with this.[^384]
- Anyone can take antidepressants but one needs to to be in conversation with a doctor.[^385]
- One shouldn't take multiple types of antidepressants at the same time as it is usually unnecessary and can be dangerous.[^385]
- If one tried many different types of antidepressants without success then in this situation it may make sense to combine multiple types of antidepressants.[^385]
- Therapy and antidepressants can be used together and can sometimes achieve better results.[^386]
- Myths about antidepressants:
	1. "I won't feel like myself". Antidepressants don't make you feel high or strange. In fact, many people finally feel like themselves after taking antidepressants.[^386]
	2. "These drugs are dangerous". Not if you have proper medical supervision.[^386]
	3. "The side effects are bad". They are usually mild.[^386]
	4. "The pills are addictive". They are not. You do not need to keep increasing your dose to keep achieving the same effect. You may need to keep taking a low dose even after the depression leaves to make sure that it does not come back. However, the side effects at this small dosage are negligible.[^386]
	5. "People are going to look down on me". Most people won't know. Your family and those who care about you will be glad that you are trying to make yourself better.[^386]
- People need to develop a love for others learning, as well, to be able to understand the Torah.[^387]
- In the days of Rebbi Shimon, a tremendously deep understanding of Torah was reached because of the love that people had for each other.[^388]
- The Torah is built on shalom and achvah and so there is a mystical connection between love for your fellows learning and understanding the Torah.[^387]
- (Also see Pirkei Avos 1:6 with the Rambam, Rabbeinu Yonah, and Pirush Hagra there.[^387])
- Another middah one needs is the desire for learning and understanding new things.[^387]
- There is a story with Alexander the Great that when he learned something new, he said he was a shoteh until then. This was out of his love and recognition of the importance of understanding something new.[^389]
- It is obvious that the more one loves learning new things, the more effort one is going to put into their learning.[^387]
- The Chazon Ish similarly writes about how integral to learning it is for one to have a desire to learn.[^390]
- (See Tiferes Dovid footnote 24.[^390])
- Even after the UN resolution to create a Jewish and an Arab state, the British were going to remain in control until the time of the transfer, which would not be for several months.[^391]
- The Jews were overjoyed with the news.[^391]
- The Arabs, local and abroad, were furious.[^391]
- Over the next several months, there were many Arab terrorist attacks on Jews in Palestine.[^391]
- There was a combination of coordinated groups (armies from surrounding Arabic states) and uncoordinated attacks.[^391]
- The first attack is considered to be an attack on a bus that killed five Jews.[^391]
- There were also many attacks on Jews in other countries. This occurred in Arab-controlled territories, like Egypt, but also non-Arab-controlled countries, like Britain. In many of these places, over one hundred Jews were killed.[^391]
- The British forces either did not want to or was not able to stop the Arab onslaught.[^391]
- There were many Jewish reprisal attacks, as well, from the Irgun. The Jewish Agency condemned these acts.[^391]
- The Haganah was mobilized for the main defense.[^391]
- The Negev was in a vulnerable position because they were isolated.[^391]
- Despite this, Ben-Gurion ordered that they not be abandoned as he felt that the Negev was important for the future shape of the Israel.[^391]
- Some reinforcements and supplies were given to help out.[^391]
- A person needs the middah of anava to be able to learn Torah.[^392]
- The Chasam Sofer talks about how if one's rebbi says something that they don't understand, they can't just decide that it is wrong, so throw it out and try to come up with a different understanding.[^392]
- The rabbeim of previous generations had tremendous mental abilities far greater than our own. If we don't understand something, it is because of the limits of our mental capacities. This realization of one's weakness compared to previous generations is the middah of anava.[^392]
- Therefore, a person has to be willing to work hard on his rebbi's Torah. They have to assume that there is an understanding to the words of his rebbi and only through working is he going to be able to uncover it.[^392]
- Mishlei compares Torah to silver and precious jewels.[^393] This is because the value of Torah, like these things, is that it is hidden and takes effort to uncover. This is important for a person to keep in mind.[^392] (This point had been discussed before.)
- Bzchus working on his rebbi's Torah, Hashem will grant him the understanding that he is looking for.[^392]
- Yosef left his garment in the hands of Eishes Potifar because it would have been a pigam to her kavod to take it. She got a grasp on it, so Yosef would have had to pull it out of her hands to take it away, which would be a pigam in her kavod.[^394]
- Eishes Potifar then made sure to be the first one to reach her husband to tell the story that would incriminate Yosef and leave her innocent.[^394]
- We see the level one needs to go to show kavod. A few tziurim:
	1. She was totally in the wrong here, meaning the reason why there would have been a lack of kavod was totally her own fault.[^395]
	2. She is clearly a rasha.[^395]
	3. It is Yosef's robe. He has a right to hold onto his stuff.[^395]
	4. By Yosef leaving it in her hands, he was opening himself up to what eventually did happen, namely that she would use the garment as evidence against him.[^395]
	5. How much of a lack of kavod is it to hold onto the garment? It doesn't seem like a serious pigam in her kavod, at all.[^395]
- We also see the sensitivity that people have. She just got rejected by Yosef. It would seem that that is the big gnai to her is. A small thing like having a robe pulled out of her hand does not seem like it is something that would register. Apparently it would have.[^395]
- People asked on this that perhaps it is "bietzem" a gnai, even if she doesn't feel it. See [[8531|Shmuess 8531]] where we seem to assume that the person receiving the gnai feels it. We don't take the "bietzem" assumption.[^7]
- We also see the power of first impressions. She made sure to go say the story first to make sure that she would be belived. Why would it matter if she went first or second? If the story is believable, then it is believable, and if it is not then it is not.[^395]
- Clearly, going first would have made a difference. Once Potifar and the bnei bayis heard the story one way, it would be harder to shift it and get them to believe the other way.[^395]
- Arabs committed acts of terror on the Jews while the Stern Gang and the Irgun committed acts of terror on the Arabs.[^396]
- Arabs attacked Jews heading to Jerusalem.[^396]
- The Irgun and Stern Gang attacks were condemned by the Jewish Agency.[^396]
- The Arabs Legion (part of the British forces) did not get involved in the terror attacks at first, although they eventually did (it seems that they didn't do very much, though).[^396]
- 33 settlements were excluded from the area of Jewish sovereignty given by the partition resolution.[^397]
- The Haganah felt that they would be stretched too thin to defend them and ordered that the settlers there should abandon the settlements.[^397]
- The people there refused. They felt they were protecting the Jewish area.[^397]
- The Haganah engaged in targeted reprisals, meaning, they would kill Arabs who attacked Jewish settlements. They gave out flyers and announced on the radio that they would be willing to make peace.[^397]
- There were times, though, that some of these reprisals killed innocent people, as well.[^397]
- The British were mostly bystanders during this time. However, they did get involved sometimes.[^397]
- In December 1947, they stopped some boats bringing illegal immigrants (there wouldn't be unlimited immigration until February 1948).[^397]
- There were also some battles that they took place in.[^397]
- In January, the Arab Liberation Army joined the fight and attacked a Jewish settlement. They were repulsed with help from the British.[^397]
- By mid-January, the death toll was 1069 Arabs, 769 Jews and 123 Britons, according to British figures.[^397]
- Some in the Jewish Agency, like Yosef Weitz, wanted to use the war to try and kick out Arabs who were living amongst Jewish settlements.[^397]
- The Arabs tried to attack some of the remote Jewish settlements.[^397]
- Piper Cubs were used to help defend some of the remote settlements with guns and grenades,[^397] and also helped with communication and some supplies.[^398]
- Arabs started recruiting some of the foreign soldiers to their side. Some British soldiers who abandoned the British army joined the cause and committed some acts of terror.[^398]
- Stern Gangs launched more retaliatory attacks against the British for this.[^398]
- The Haganah became more ruthless. They stopped only attacking the people who launched attacks on Jews. They attacked cities from where attacks were launched from.[^398]
- They didn't attack other cities and they would retaliate in a proportionate manner.[^398]
- Arabs left Jerusalem. The city was left nearly entirely Jewish.[^398] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">It sounded like they were forced out but this would be something to find out</span>}
- Ben-Gurion and the Haganah then permitted Jews to move into the abandoned houses in Jerusalem.[^398]
- Arabs were forced from their homes in other cities, as well, such as Caesarea. [^398]
- Arab Liberation Armies remained involved in the war.[^398]
- In March, a Provisional Council was set up.[^398]
- Later in the month, an Arab driver planted a car bomb in the courtyard of the Jewish Agency.[^398]
- The United Nations was having regrets about the partition. They started thinking that this was a bad idea. They were thinking about setting up a UN government over the region.[^398]
- The United States was in favor because they were scared of Russian influence. The Russians disagreed and were in favor of the Jewish state. They started selling weapons to the Haganah.[^398]
- The Jews held by the British in Cyprus began being trained.[^398]
- As the British were pulling out, the Jews came up with Plan D. (I seems that the British were providing some assistance to the Jewish defense.)[^399]
- The ideas were to:
	1. Conquer the land that was promised to the Jews under the partition.[^399]
	2. Conquer the land that had Jewish settlements that was beyond what was promised by the partition.[^399]
	3. Occupy any Arab village that was located in strategic locations.[^399]
	4. (Somehow this was also supposed to help create a more defensible I think but I am not sure how. Maybe creating the border around the promised area would have done this?)
- On March 22, Arabs captured the road leading to Jerusalem from the coast.[^399]
- The Haganah organized a massive counteroffensive (1500 people, which was by far the most of any operation) to recapture the road.[^399]
- This became known as Operation Nachshon.[^399]
- Weapons for the operation were smuggled in.[^399]
- There were some successes, such as the capture of the city of Kastel (which overlooked the road into Jerusalem) and other Arab cities, but generally the army got repulsed.[^399]
- Despite this, some supply trucks were able to make it to Jerusalem in early April.[^399]
- An Arab detachment was eventually able to recapture Kastel. However, during the battle, the leader of the Arab army was killed.[^399]
- In the disorganization that ensued, the Arab army abandoned the village and the Jews recaptured the city on April 9.[^399]
- On that same day, the Irgun massacred the Arab city of Deir Yassin. They murdered around 110 Arabs, including many women and children.[^399] [^400]
- Many Jews were horrified and the Jewish Agency expressed disgust this attack.[^399]
- On April 15, Arabs retaliated for Deir Yassin by killing 77 doctors, nurses, and patients on their way to Hadassah Hospital.[^401]
- The village of Mishmar Haemek was a major point in the battle for control of the road to Jerusalem. (should go earlier.)[^401]
- Druse soldiers helped the Arabs and Syria helped with some supplies.[^401]
- The British were leaving Palestine rapidly.[^401]
- There was no specific date given by the UN about when the British would be fully gone.[^401]
- Jewish and Arab leaders in Tiberias (Tiveria) kept things peaceful in the city.[^401]
- In April, the Arab Liberation Army cut off the Jewish Quarter.[^401]
- The Haganah and Palmach responded, citing Plan D (meaning, that this city was a strategic location).[^401]
- Due to the counterattack, the Arabs decided to leave.[^401]
- Beginning on April 21, Jews and Arabs fought for control of the city of Haifa.[^401]
- The Jews captured the city in 48 hours.[^401]
- During the fighting, some of the Arab leaders, Ahmad Bey Khalil, Ahmid Bey Azzadin, and Yunis Nafa'a left the city.[^401]
- This sowed tremendous uncertainty amongst the local Arabs and they decided to leave, despite the Jewish mayor of Haifa, the local Jewish population, and the Haganah trying to convince them to stay. (The Haganah handed out leaflets and did not allow its soldiers to loot the town.)[^401]
- The Arab Higher Committee in Damascus and the Mufti in Jerusalem tried to convince the Arabs to leave. They felt that there would be fighting in Haifa and so they wanted the Arabs out of the way.[^401]
- The Arabs that stayed were allowed to remain there until the present.[^401]
- On April 20, Arabs prevented a convoy from reaching Jerusalem, destroying many vehicles in the process.[^402]
- Yitzchak Rabin led troops to occupy the city of Sheikh Jarrah in the Jerusalem corridor but a nearby British army told him to leave.[^402]
- Throughout Palestine, Arabs were fleeing from their villages. The abandoned villages were then blown up by the Jews.[^402]
- The Jews decided that based on Plan D, Safed (Tzefas) and the surrounding areas needed to be occupied.[^402]
- Safed had a large Arab majority, but its location allowed it to harass the Jews in the Galilee Panhandle.[^402]
- ![[Pasted image 20250119235332.png]][^403]
- In addition, the Arabs started besieging the Jews in Safed and in Ramot Naftali (a village a mile south of Safed).[^402]
- The Haganah launched Operation Yiftach which was a counteroffensive against the Arabs in the area.[^402]
- The British abandoned fortresses as the left Palestine. These were located in key positions. The Arabs and Jews quickly tried to swoop in and claim them as they were leaving.[^402]
- Beginning near the end of April, the Irgun began working under the Haganah.[^402] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I think they may have been working alongside them before, but I am not sure</span>}
- The Jews also launched an attack on Jaffa.[^402]
- The British helped defend Jaffa. They were under pressure from the Arabs after the British "abandoned" the Arabs of Haifa.[^402]
- During early May, the Palmach launched Operation Matateh (Broom) to clear the Arabs out of the lands between the Sea of Galilee (also called Lake Tiberias or the Kinneret) and the Hula Valley.[^404]
- ![[Pasted image 20250120002248.png]][^405]
- This mission was to connect the Lower Galilee with the Upper Galilee, and to destroy enemy bases from which Arab attacks had been launched, destroy points of assembly for invading forces from east, and to join the lower and upper Galilee with a relatively wide and safe strip.[^406]
- This was part of Operation of Operation Yiftach.[^407]
- Safed fell on May 10. This was a powerful psychological blow to the Arabs. Many of them in the area abandoned their villages. This is what the Jews were hoping for.[^404]
- The Stern Gang was still launching attacks on British forces.[^404]
- A British officer told Chaim Herzog, a member of the Jewish Agency, that they had to get the Stern Gang under control or British forces would retaliate.[^404]
- Golda Meir was sent to Jordan to ensure they would stay out of the war. However, King Abdullah's hands were tied by Egypt, Syria, Lebanon, and Iraq.[^404]
- The king offered the Jews to be part of the Transjordan kingdom. They refused.[^404]
- On May 12, the Provisional Council met to decide if they accept a ceasefire being pushed by the Americans, or to declare themselves an independent state.[^408]
- If they declared themselves a state, the surrounding Arab countries would attack.[^408]
- If the agreed to the ceasefire, their plans for statehood may get pushed off indefinitely.[^408]
- They felt that their forces were at somewhat of a disadvantage to the Arab armies. They had more men and better equipment, like armored vehicles (although the Haganah did develop tactics to destroy them).[^408]
- They voted and decided they would declare the State of Israel on May 14.[^408]
- Gush Etzion, a cluster of villages between Jerusalem and Hebron, was under attack from Arabs since April. The villages fell on May 13 and 14.[^409]
- 
- ![[Screenshot 2025-01-20 234901.png]][^410]
- During the surrender at Kfar Etzion (one of the villages) the Arab Legion massacred 15 surrendered soldiers as well as civilians.[^409]
- Survivors from the villages were captured.[^409]
- On May 12 in the Jordan Valley, the Arab town of Beisan was abandoned by the 3000 Arabs that had lived there.[^409]
- On May 13, the Arabs formally surrendered Jaffa. 67,000 out of the 70,000 Arabs who lived there, fled.[^409]
- The Haganah took over Arabs suburbs in Jerusalem.[^409]
- On May 13, the Haganah captured Gezer, a city on the road between Jerusalem and Tel-Aviv.[^409]
- The cities of Bureir, Hulekeit, and Kaukaba would cause problems on the North-South road. As such, Plan D required that they be taken.[^409]
- This was accomplished on May 12 and 13. The inhabitants of the city fled.[^409]
- Nations in the UN wanted to put Jerusalem under UN rule. The Arabs wanted it under Arab rule, despite the Jewish majority there.[^409]
- David Ben-Gurion was appointed as Prime Minister and Minister of Defense by the provisional government.[^411]
- On May 15, all British troops (except for a small garrison) left Palestine.[^412]
- The Israeli Declaration of Independence said:
	1. The Jews need for a homeland, as was made obvious by the holocaust.[^411]
	2. Every Jew was welcome to come.[^411]
	3. Complete equality between races, sexes, etc.[^411]
	4. Appealed for peace with Arabs in the country and the neighboring Arab countries.[^411]
	5. Agreed to the UN charter (although Israel was not yet a UN member).[^411]
	6. The borders of Israel were not defined.[^411]
- America and the Soviet Union recognized Israel immediately after it was declared.[^412]
- Egypt bombed Tel-Aviv on the day of the declaration of Israel.[^412]
- Israel had almost no air force to start the war. They had the Piper Cubs and some Austers.[^412]
- The provisional council elected Chaim Weizmann as the first president.[^412]
- Weizmann, who was in America at the time, secured a $100 million loan for Israel from the US.[^412]
- There are 2 ways that people come to understand Torah. One way is natural, meaning, that dependent on one's level of intelligence and how much effort one puts into learning, they will understand the Torah. The other is a "gift", meaning that a person gains an understanding that is beyond his level of intelligence and effort that one put in.[^413]
