- For one to be zoche to this second level, one has to make themselves "מופקר לכל", meaning, they are willing to be "stepped on" and their pride doesn't flare up. They have a high level of anava.[^413]
- Receiving Torah through a "gift" is greater than receiving Torah through one's own efforts, because one's own intelligence is subject to mistakes. However, if one receives Torah as a "gift", they know that it is correct.[^414]
- Areas under Israeli control on May 15:
- ![[Pasted image 20250122150536.png]][^415]
- On May 15, the Syrian army started pushing into Israel from the Golan Heights. They overran a few Jewish settlements before being pushed back at the village of Degania (Deganya) with a few pieces of artillery that were rushed in.[^416]
- ![[Pasted image 20250121151447.png]][^417]
- Iraqi troops came into Israel a little farther south at the city of Gesher which they besieged.[^416]
- ![[Pasted image 20250121151745.png]][^418]
- Eventually, they were pushed back by the same artillery which was scrambled from Degania.[^416]
- Israel was under attack in the North, the Negev, and Jerusalem area.[^416]
- Israel's army numbered 36,500, which was a tremendous increase from the 4,500 they had at the time of the partition resolution 6 months previously.[^416]
- Israelis largely used Molotov cocktails and hand grenades at close range to deal with the enemies armored vehicles. If available, they would use artillery and the Projectile Infantry Anti-Tank (PIAT).[^416]
- The Egyptians moved in from the south, attacking the villages of Kfar Darom, Nirim, Yad Mordechai, and Negba.[^419]
- ![[Pasted image 20250121153759.png]][^420]
- ![[Pasted image 20250121153911.png]][^421]
- ![[Pasted image 20250121154130.png]][^422]
- ![[Pasted image 20250121154755.png]][^423]
- All four villages were tremendously outnumbered.[^419]
- Nirim, Kfar Darom, and Negba were able to repulse the attacks, at least for some time.[^419]
- Yad Mordechai was able to repulse the attacks for five days until it fell.[^419]
- During those five days, Israel was able to prepare defenses in Tel-Aviv and south of it.[^419]
- According to the Chasam Sofer brought down earlier, if one doesn't understand the words of his rabbeim, one needs to realize that their words are correct and he simply doesn't understand them yet. The person needs to work to the end of his ability to understand their words.[^424]
- If a person doesn't do this, this skeptical, disbelieving attitude will even extend that Hashem says.[^424]
- This is despite the fact that it is obvious to all that Hashem is infinitely more knowledgeable than we are and so it is obvious that Hashem is correct and we are incorrect.[^424]
- Despite this, without developing this middah of hachnaah by working on the Torah of one's rebbi that they don't understand, they will come to feel this way about the words of Hashem.[^424]
- (See sefer Chidushei Halev on Vayikra 19 19 for a similar idea.)[^424]
- This is actually the basis for the middah of yiras Hashem. It comes out that the same way one acquires Torah is the same way one acquires the middos of anava and yiras shomayim.[^424]
- It is also extremely important for a rebbi to work on his middah of anava.[^425]
- We see by Zecharia Hanavi that he did not properly feel the middah of anava. He viewed himself "above" the people. Because of this, the people rejected what he had to say and killed him.[^426]
- Now, Zecharia was a navi. This means that he obviously did attain anava to a very high level (or else he would not have been a navi).[^425]
- In addition, Rashi[^427] implies that the lack of anava in this situation was that Zecharia, due to his high position and the fact that it was Yom Kippur, didn't think that anyone would attack him for saying his nevua. This was a misplaced confidence. This certainly does not sound like an egregious lack of anava at all.[^425]
- Yet, despite this, Klal Yisroel completely rejected what Zecharia had to say and even killed him because of it.[^425]
- The first act of the Provisional Council was to remove any restrictions on Jewish immigration for the future, and retroactively (so all the "illegals" were now legal).[^428]
- Even during this time, new settlements were being built. Sometimes, nearby abandoned Arab settlements were destroyed for this purpose.[^428]
- The Egyptians attacked Ramat Rachel, a kibbutz south of Jerusalem. This location was vital to the defense of Jerusalem. The Jews were able to hold off the Egyptians and eventually push them back.[^428]
- ![[Pasted image 20250122141333.png]][^429]
- On May 24, the Jews launched an attack on Latrun (a village in the Jerusalem corridor) to break the siege on Jerusalem.[^428]
- ![[Pasted image 20250123024228.png]][^430]
- The attack was unorganized, the troops were poorly trained, and poorly equipped. The Arab Legion pushed them back.[^428]
- The Arab Legion attacked the Jewish Quarter in the old city with artillery. On May 28, the defenders were forced to surrender. The captured were taken Transjordan.[^428]
- This was a powerful psychological blow to the Jews and a major uplift for the Arab Legion.[^428]
- On May 29, the Jews were able to recapture the village of Acre (Akko). This reunited Nahariya with the rest of Israel.[^431]
- ![[Pasted image 20250122143308.png]][^432]
- Also on May 29, Israel got Messerschmitts from Czechoslovakia. Israel kept buying more planes for Czechoslovakia (more Messerschmitts, Mosquitos, and Spitfires) which were being flown to Israel. In addition, Israel bought some B-17 Flying Fortresses (the main United States bomber during during WWII).[^431]
- Egyptians were massing at the Arab village of Isdud (Ashdod) to launch an attack Tel-Aviv.[^431]
- On May 29 The Israelis were able to delay hold them off using the Messerschmitts (which weren't even tested yet). During the attack, one of the planes crashed and the pilot was killed.[^431]
- Boats were armed with some small pieces of artillery to become "battleships".[^431]
- Many Jews from other countries (like the United States and Britain) volunteered to join the army. These were known as Machal (Mitnavdei Chutz Laaretz). Many of these people were pilots and became a major part of the air force.[^433]
- The immigrants coming from detention camps at Cyprus became known as Gachal (Gyus (recruits) Chutz Laaretz).[^433]
- On May 29, the United Nations stopped allowing people of military age to immigrate to Israel.[^433]
- On May 31, Ben-Gurion officially declared the Haganah as the army of Israel. It became known as the Tzeva Haganah leYisrael (Tzahal, or IDF). Members of the Irgun and Stern Gangs joined.[^433]
- Foreign reporters in Israel asked Yigael Yadin how the battle between the Israelis and Egyptians in Isdud was going. He said that they had the Egyptians totally surrounded, which was a total fabrication.[^433]
- When the Egyptians heard this from the international press, the troops panicked.[^433]
- Israelis conquered Jenin but were then immediately pushed back by the Iraqis. The dead remained there for years before Jordan allowed the Israelis to come and bury them.
- ![[Pasted image 20250122151034.png]][^434]
- By the beginning of June 200,000 Arabs had fled from Israeli controlled territories (155 abandoned villages), 75,000 from Jaffa and Acre (which the UN allocated to an Arab State) and 40,000 from Jerusalem.[^435]
- Ben-Gurion wanted to resettle these villages with Jews.[^435]
- In the second week of June, the Israeli army conquered the area around Mount Tabor in the Galilee.[^436] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Add link to earlier map</span>}
- A second attack was made on Latrun on May 30, led by Mickey Stone (a US army WWII veteran). Once again, the Arab Legion repelled the attack.[^436]
- The situation in Jerusalem continued to get increasingly desperate.[^436]
- Ben-Gurion pushed for a third attack on June 9. However, an alternate route between Tel-Aviv and Jerusalem was found that did not go through Latrun. It went near the abandoned Arab towns of Bayt Jiz and Bayt Susin.[^436]
- ![[Pasted image 20250123021631.png]][^437]
- The path was quickly built to accommodate vehicles. It was eventually called the Burma Road.[^436]
- On June 10, the Syrians attacked and overran the village of Mishmar Hayarden. This cut off the panhandle of the Galilee.[^436]
- The village was later rebuilt and called Gadot.[^438]
- ![[Pasted image 20250123011617.png]][^439]
- On June 11, the UN was finally able to get the Arabs and Israelis to agree to a truce. It was agreed that the truce would last 4 weeks.[^436]
- The Israelis had conquered much land over the course of the fighting.[^436]
- Mickey Stone was killed after being misidentified as an Arab during the truce.[^440]
- Israel used the time of the truce to strengthen its position. The Burma Road was widened, the units from Cyprus were incorporated, and supplies were distributed.[^440]
- 876 soldiers and 300 civilians were killed between May 14 and June 11.[^440]
- The Syrians broke the truce after a week and attacked the newly established settlement of Kfar Hanasi. The attack was repulsed.[^440]
- ![[Pasted image 20250123013854.png]][^430]
- On June 19, a ship carrying weapons and Irgun troops landed in Israel. Ben-Gurion wanted the weapons and troops used for the Jerusalem Front, but the men on board refused.[^441]
- On June 20 and 21, Ben-Gurion decided to force the Irgun volunteers to capitulate using the army. The army fired on the boat. The boat was sunk, but the Irgun members survived. They were pardoned for their insubordination and were accepted into the army.[^441]
- During the truce, the UN mediator Count Bernadotte, tried to create a new partition with the Arabs getting most of the Negev and the Jews getting the Galilee. Jerusalem would go to the Arabs and the Jews in the city would have autonomy under the Arabs.[^441]
- Both sides rejected this partition.[^441]
- Anava is not only important for a rebbi when giving over tochacha. It is important whenever a rebbi is teaching anything.[^442]
- This means that a rebbi needs to can't be makpid on his kavod if a talmid acts out of line.[^442]
- A rebbi needs to develop his middah of anava to make sure that in these situations, he won't respond incorrectly.[^442]
- The Ramban[^443] says that when Reuven "slept" with Bilha, Yaakov did not kick Reuven out from being counted from amongst the shevatim because of his anivus.[^442]
- Even though Yaakov loved Reuven so much, without his tremendous anivus he would have made this mistake.[^442]
- Loving a talmid is not enough. The rebbi needs to have anava.[^442]
- A rebbi needs to be organized in his presentation of the material. The order the material is presented should make sense.[^444]
- We see the importance of this from the gemaros at the beginning of Nedarim and Yevamos. The mishnayos there give lists (in Nedarim, it is lists of kinuyim from nedarim and in Yevamos, it is a list of arayos) and the gemara immediately begins by trying to understand why the tanna presented these things in the order that he did. This is even before the gemara gets into the major sugyos that follow (in Yevamos, the sugya that follows is asei doche lo sasei).[^444]
- We similarly see this in Rabbeinu Bechayei[^445] who explains the reason why the Torah ordered the parsha of negaim the way it did.[^446]
- During the truce, there was some criticism of some of Ben-Gurion's decisions (such as the attacks on Latrun and the fight with the Irgun).[^447]
- To respond to the issues, Yigal Yadin suggested to create a War Cabinet so decisions would be made by a group rather than by Ben-Gurion alone. Ben-Gurion agreed to this.[^447]
- Kfar Darom was abandoned on July 8 after running out of supplies due to pressure from the Egyptians.[^448]
- The truce ended at midnight on July 9. Count Bernadotte asked the Arabs and the Israelis if they could both agree to extend the truce by ten days. The Jews agreed but the Arabs did not.[^448]
- Immediately after the time passed, the Israeli army conquered the hill which later became known as Mount Herzl (or Har Hazikaron). Shortly after, the Arab towns of Bayt Masmil (today, Kiryat Hayovel), Ein Karim, Malha, and Bayt Safafa, which were on the bottom of the hill, were captured. Some of these threatened a nearby railway line and were used as bases for attack against nearby Jewish suburbs.[^448]
- ![[Pasted image 20250123142610.png]][^430]
- The Israelis were not able to push the Syrains back across the Jordan due to the Syrian's air superiority and better artillery.[^449]
- Israel focused their offensive on the cities of Lydda (Lod), Ramle, and Ramallah. These cities threatened North-South roads and the road between Tel-Aviv and Jerusalem.[^449]
- Lydda and Ramle were taken but Ramallah was not.[^449]
- ![[Pasted image 20250123144832.png]][^430]
- During the fighting at Lydda, 250 civilians were killed in the crossfire.[^449]
- Ben-Gurion ordered the civilians of the city (50,000 in all) to be expelled. At least 335 died of exhaustion on the way.[^449]
- This gave the Israelis a strategic advantage and it forced the Arabs to deal with the refugees as well as clogged the roads, preventing the advance of the Arab Legion.[^449]
- Some Israeli leaders were opposed to this, such a Meir Ya'ari (co-leader of Mapam).[^449]
- On Jul 12, the Arab city of Ras al-Ain (Rosh HaAyin) was taken by the Israelis. The city sat on the water supply for Jerusalem (the Yarkon River) and was a major cause of the water shortage there.[^450]
- ![[Pasted image 20250123145719.png]][^430]
- The Egyptians in the Negev attacked Negba, Gal-On, and Be'erot Yitzchak in succession between July 12 and 15. All attacks were repulsed despite largely outnumbering the Israeli forces.[^450]
- On July 15, the UN ordered a ceasefire. The Arabs rejected this and fighting continued.[^451]
- Israeli forces from Negba pushed the Egyptians out of Kharatya.[^451]
- Israeli attacks on the cities of Iraq el-Manshiya and Faluja were pushed back, allowing the Egyptians to maintain a foothold in the Negev.[^451]
- The Israelis conquered the city of Nazareth in the north, cities between Tel Aviv and Haifa, and the cities of Tsora and Hartuv, located in the Jeruslam corridor.[^452]
- The villages around Nazareth surrendered as well.[^452]
- During the attack of Nazareth, there was some looting by the Israeli army. The army leaders issued an order to punish anyone caught looting.[^452]
- A third attempt to capture Latrun was pushed back.[^452]
- The UN forced a truce which came into effect on July 17 in Jerusalem and July 19 everywhere else.[^453]
- Just before the truce, an Israeli attack on the Damascus Gate failed.[^453]
- The Arab cities of Shilta and Deir Rafat (which threatened the Jerusalem Corridor) were taken. However, the Israelis were pushed back from attempts to take the village of Midya as well as hill 318. A fourth attack on Latrun was pushed back, as well.[^453]
- Israel used the second truce to lay a new water pipe to Jerusalem (to help with continued disruptions to the water supply from the Arabs), increased training and recruitment, bought weapons and supplies (mainly from the US, European countries, and Mexico. Czechoslovakia stopped selling during this time), and divvied them out.[^454]
- On September 17, Bernadotte was ambushed and killed by Stern gang members. This was because he proposed an Arab administration over Jerusalem.[^455]
- This attack was strongly denounced.[^455]
- Ben-Gurion decided to dissolve the Stern gang and Irgun over this. He arrested hundreds of Stern Gang members. He then issued an ultimatum to the Stern Gang and Irgun to hand in their weapons and not receive punishment for anything they had done until then. The Irgun and Stern Gang agreed.[^455]
- This set up the division between Ben-Gurion and Begin, and the Revisionists vs the Labor parties.[^455]
- He then dissolved the Palmach.[^455]
- In October, the National Palestine Council the provisional government of All-Palestine with Al-Husseini as the leader. This was supported by the Syria, Egypt, Lebanon, and Iraq.[^455]
- King Abdullah of Jordan didn't like this as he wanted more control of Palestine. He countered by creating the Arab Hashemite Kingdom which included Palestine and Transjordan.[^455]
- On October 15, Israel bated Egypt into breaking the truce by sending supplies through the Egyptian lines (which is something the Israelis were allowed to do). The Egyptians attacked the supply trucks and Israel renewed its hostilities.[^456]
- Egypt's position cut off Israel from supplying the cities in the negev.[^456]
- Israel tried pushing Egypt out of the city of al-Falujah, Beersheva, and Huleikat. Israel was successful at Huleikat (Helayqat) and Beersheva but not al-Falujah (which became known as the Faluja pocket).[^456]
- al-Falujah is located the point more Eastern point of the pair of points near Kiryat Gat. Heleikat is the more Western point.
- ![[Pasted image 20250127030117.png]][^430]
- The fighting decimated the crops of the local Arabs and so many ran away.[^456]
- The UN proposed another truce with soldiers going back to their original positions as they were at the end of the truce.[^456]
- Fighting resumed in Jerusalem but the Israelis were still unable to break through to the Jewish Quarter which was still under Arab control.[^457]
- The Israelis were able to drive the Arabs from some villages along the Jerusalem Corridor, including the village of Beit Shemesh.[^457]
- The Israeli government instituted martial law on its Arabic subjects. Arabs would require permits to travel and the Israeli government would be allowed to relocate Arabic settlements (which Israel would later do).[^457]
- Negative externalities:
- Some things which are consumed come with a cost to bystanders, that is, not the consumer or the producer (or at least not only by them).[^458]
- An example of this is superbugs, or bacteria that is resistant to antibiotics. Every time an antibiotic is used, the surviving bacteria become more resistant to that antibiotic. Over time, these bacteria become resistant to many different antibiotics. This is bad for society.[^458]
- However, this cost is not born by the consumer or producer (technically it is because the consumer and producer are part of society, but the point is that it doesn't particularly target them, like the price of consuming/producing the drug does).[^458]
- It is advantageous if the benefit of a product being consumed outweighs the cost to the consumer, producer, and society.[^458]
- However, this is not naturally incorporated into the market price. The supply cost is lower than it should be.[^458]
- This does not mean that anything with a negative externality should not be used. Rather, it just means that the benefit to the consumer should outweigh those costs.[^458]
- ![[Pasted image 20250127140219.png]][^458]
- (Deadweight loss in this diagram means that the overall losses outweigh the gains.)[^458]
- There are different ways of trying to adjust the market price to account for this social cost, such as Pigouvian taxes/subsidies, command and control economies, tradeable allowances, and Coasean bargaining. [^458]
- Positive externalities:
- The opposite of negative externalities. Gains to bystanders, not as much to the consumer or producer.[^459]
- However, the entire cost is born by the consumer and producer. As such, the demand for the item is below what it should be.[^459]
- ![[Pasted image 20250127165621.png]][^459]
- (Deadweight loss in this diagram refers to gains from trade which do not occur.)[^459]
- Command and control:
- Self-explanatory: the government says that you must do this and you can't do this. For example, in 2007, governments required washing machines to use less electricity.[^460]
- Problems:
	1. Rarely efficient. The goal in this case was to reduce electrical use. There are thousands of ways to reduce electrical use. What is the best way?[^460]
		1. It is impossible to know the best way overall.[^460]
		2. Each individual may have certain ways that work better for them. Forcing everyone to reduce electricity in one way forces people to reduce electricity in ways that are inefficient, meaning, they could reduce an equal amount of electricity by stopping to do other things that matter less to them.[^460]
		3. For some people, the use of electricity is extremely important and it would be better if they didn't reduce their electrical usage.[^460]
	2. Missing the target. Even in this situation, the goal isn't really to reduce electricity. The goal is to deal with climate change. It is difficult to pass laws that directly target the climate.[^460]
- Despite this, command and control is useful when:
	1. The best solution to a problem is clear.[^460]
	2. Flexibility is a bad thing.[^460]
- For example, smallpox was eradicated through command and control. People who had the disease were isolated and the communities were vaccinated. Allowing people to choose whether to be vaccinated or not would have allowed the disease to remain.[^460]
- Markets don't incorporate externalities well not because they are external, but because the rights in the situation are not clear.[^461]
- Examples:
- Pollution. If a factory produces pollution, who is hurt by it and how much are they hurt? Also, who pays who?[^461]
- Flu shot. If someone gets a flu shot and therefore doesn't spread the flu, who benefited from that and how much?[^461]
- Also, who pays who? Does the factory/person who does not get the flu shot have to pay others, or do factories/people who do get the flu shot get paid by others?[^461]
- These questions cause externalities to not be incorporated well. If these were clearly defined, market forces would come into play.[^461]
- (The video focused on Coase Theorem, but I like the way I presented it better.)
- Tradable allowances:
- A way to deal with negative externality issues is by having tradable allowances (also called cap and trade). This means that we give allowances for an entity (a company, usually) to cause a certain amount of a specific externality (like release a certain amount of pollution, for example).[^462]
- Companies have the right to buy and sell these allowances.[^462]
- This encourages companies to try and limit the amount that they create the negative externality. The companies that can efficiently remove the negative externality from their production can sell their extra permits.[^462]
- Meanwhile, the companies that cannot are forced to buy permits.[^462]
- This helps avoid the issues created by a command and control solution. It allows the free market to figure out the optimal amount that each company should reduce pollution.[^462]
- Additionally, this doesn't take tax money to implement.[^463]
- Also, private citizens can get involved in restricting the negative externality. For example, groups promoting green energy can buy pollution allowances and tear them up, not allowing anyone to have that allowance.[^463]
- How much allowance do we initially give out and who gets them is difficult to decide. One way to do this would be to have the government auction off allowances.[^463]
- The Clean Air Amendments of 1990 used this method to limit the production of SO<sub>2</sub> (a chemical in acid rain). It was highly effective. It reduced SO<sub>2</sub> production without sacrificing production of electricity.[^463]
- ![[Pasted image 20250127194644.png]][^463]
- This may be difficult to apply to CO<sub>2</sub>. SO<sub>2</sub> was a national problem. There is one government in charge. CO<sub>2</sub> is a global problem. It is much harder getting different countries to work together.[^463]
- (The video also explained the contrast between this a Pigouvian taxes/subsidies. When I learn about Pigouvian taxes/subsidies I should come back and watch this video.)
- Lots of inventions and developments are under-incentivized. This is because the creator doesn't get the externalities.[^464]
- For this reason, one can argue that the creators of the Covid vaccines were under-incentivized. The whole economy benefited greatly from the creation of the Covid vaccine.[^464]
- This seems against what people might think. People say that these companies are ripping people off.[^464]
- Placing a patent on a product limits the externalities by increasing the price to consumers. As such, this is not a good way to incentivize companies that produce a product with a positive externality.[^464]
- Helping these companies deal with the financial risk of rolling out this product earlier is a good thing to do.[^464]
- In a competitive market (meaning, a market for a product for which there are many sellers and the product is more or less the same between all of these sellers. This is in contrast to a seller working with a monopoly), any individual seller sells at the market price to maximize profit.[^465]
- You aren't going to sell below the market price, because people are willing to pay market price for your oil, and there are more than enough buyers for the little amount of oil that you sell as a proportion of the total market.[^465]
- You aren't going to sell above the market price because nobody will buy from you. They can just get the product at market price.[^465]
- Any individual seller cannot control the market price. They do not sell enough to influence the market price.[^465]
- Firms can control the quantity that they produce.[^466]
- The assumption is that firms try to maximize their profits (this is obviously not always true, but it is very often a major focus for a company).[^466]
- The revenue that a company earns is the amount of units produced multiplied by the market price (as that is the price that firms will sell their product for).[^466]
- Expenses come in two varieties: fixed cost and variable cost.[^466]
- Fixed cost are what you pay regardless of the level of production. For example, if you have a factory, even if the factory doesn't produce anything, you still need to pay rent on the land that the factory is on.[^466]
- Even if you own the land, there are still opportunity costs.[^466]
- Variable costs change as the quantity produced changes.[^466]
- Firms can increase their profits by increasing their production until marginal costs (the costs for producing an additional unit) equals the marginal revenue (the revenue for producing an additional unit).[^466]
- If the marginal cost is above the marginal revenue, a company can maximize profits by lowering production.[^466]
- The more units a company produces, the higher the marginal costs are. The more units that are produced, the harder it is to create an additional unit.[^466]
- ![[Pasted image 20250128010609.png]][^466]
- Average costs:
- Average costs are the costs per unit.[^467]
- At very few units, the average costs are high because of the fixed costs. If you divide the fixed costs per unit sold, the costs are high.[^467]
- As you produce more units, the fixed costs get divided to smaller and smaller amounts. As such, the average costs go down.[^467]
- At some point, though, the marginal costs rise faster than the amount that the fixed cost being divided per unit is shrinking. As such, the average cost starts rising again.[^467]
- ![[Pasted image 20250128093643.png]][^467]
- For example, if you drill an oil well, the average cost if you would only sell one barrel of oil would be really high. For one barrel of oil you had to drill a whole oil well. As you start producing more, the average cost starts falling. The cost of the oil well gets divided between all of the barrels of oil. As quantity increases, the marginal cost per barrel of oil rises above the current average price. This raises the average price.[^467]
- Profits are given by the following formula (which can be simplified): $$(RevenuePerUnit * Quantity) - (AverageCost * Quantity)$$[^467]
- As such, a company can only make a profit if the revenue per unit is above the average cost.[^467]
- Companies look at the long term when deciding to enter or leave a field. Even if in the short term a company can turn a profit, they won't enter the field because there are sunk costs (costs that you can't get back later). Conversely, a company in a field won't leave it, even if in the short term they are not making a profit. This is because there are costs with exiting a field, and also because they will have to pay to reenter the field later.[^467]
- Some products have rising costs per unit produced, some of constant costs, and some have falling costs (although this last group is rare).[^468]
- Products have rising costs per unit because the products necessary to produce them are somewhat limited. For example, oil is easily drilled in only certain countries. When it isn't feasible to keep creating oil well in these places, new oil well are created in places where it is harder (hence, more expensive) to get the oil.[^468]
- In addition, products made from other products that have rising costs themselves have rising costs. For example, gasoline is made from oil. Oil has a rising cost, so gasoline has rising costs.[^468]
- As such, the long run supply curve slopes upward (like the standard supply curve). This means that as the market price of a product increases, more sellers are willing to enter the market because they can still turn a profit, even though they may have high production costs.[^468]
- Some products have constant costs per unit. This is because they take up very little of the market for the inputs made to produce them. For example, pencils have mostly constant costs, as it doesn't take much wood, graphite, or rubber to produce a pencil. It doesn't have a large share of those markets. As such, even if more pencils are produced, the price will remain the same.[^469]
- In the short term, if there is an increase in the demand for pencils, the price will increase (by shifting the demand curve to the right). As people see this increase in demand, they get involved in the market, lowering the price back down to where it started (by shifting the supply curve to the right. It goes back to the original price because at this price, it isn't worth it for people to enter the market anymore, just like it wasn't before the increase in demand).[^469]
- As such, the long-run supply curve for such products is flat.[^469]
- ![[Pasted image 20250128152913.png]][^469]
- Some industries have decreasing costs per unit.[^470]
- This is usually caused by clusters of businesses in the same market opening the same location.[^470]
- You have a first business that picks a location to open a business. It organizes suppliers, shippers to ship out goods, and hires workers that learn the knowledge of the field.[^470]
- A second business wants to open in the same field. It may choose this location because it has all of these advantages (costs are lower). It also helps provide some more of the same advantage. It uses what the first firm has set up, and also sets up some new things.[^470]
- By the time a third, fourth, fifth, etc. firm want to open, this location is the prime location for this industry. Costs are really low.[^470]
- Obviously, there is a point that costs can't get any lower, even if more firms show up.[^470]
- This is true of things like Silicon Valley and Hollywood, and less known things, like Dalton, Georgia, US, which makes a high percentage of the worlds carpets.[^470]
- These types of curves are relatively uncommon.[^470]
- The "invisible hand" of the market keeps the costs for producing a certain quantity of a product as low as possible across firms in the same industry.[^471]
- For example, it is relatively inexpensive to drill for gas in the middle east, and relatively expensive to drill gas in Alaska. As such, companies in the middle east will drill for a lot of gas. However, at some point, marginal costs will rise until it is greater than the marginal revenue.[^471]
- Meanwhile, even though costs are higher, at certain quantities, a company can generate a profit by drilling for gas in Alaska. As such, companies will do this, until they reach a point that the marginal costs are higher than the marginal revenue.[^471]
- This breakdown is actually the most efficient way of producing that amount of oil. Production is increased wherever marginal costs are cheapest.[^471]
- Similarly, the value of resources across industries is maximized.[^472]
- If there is an industry that has a high profit level, people are going to want to create businesses in that industry. Conversely, businesses leave industries that have low profit levels.[^472]
- As such, out resources are put toward producing things that people want, and the right quantity for how high the demand is.[^472]
- For this reason, profit margins are somewhat similar across different industries. If they were considerably higher in one industry, people would flock to that industry.[^472]
- This is only true if:
	1. Prices are allowed to move to reflect the underlying demand.[^472]
	2. Companies are allowed to enter and exit different fields (as opposed to monopolies).[^472]
- To earn profits greater than this profit margin, you need to innovate. If you create something new, you can cash in (at least for some time).[^472]
- Monopolies:
- A company has a monopoly when it controls at least a large share of a particular market.[^473]
- This can be because:
	1. A company has a patent or some other government license that doesn't allow competition.[^473]
	2. A large company can produce something for cheaper than a small one. As such, it is difficult to enter the market.[^473]
	3. Exclusive access to an important input (ex there are only so many diamond mines in the world. If a company gets control of a good percentage of them, they will have monopoly on the diamond industry).[^473]
	4. If a company has some technological innovation, it may take some time for other companies to catch up. During that time, this company has a monopoly.[^473]
- Like in competitive markets, a firm maximized profits by selling until the point that marginal cost equals marginal revenue.[^473]
- The difference is that a company operating under a monopoly is able to control the market price, and hence, their marginal revenue.[^473]
- As the company increases price, quantity demanded drops. And as it lowers price, quantity demanded increases. A company maximized profits by finding that goldilocks price where demand is high enough and price is high enough.[^473]
- ![[Pasted image 20250129093717.png]][^473]
- For a linear demand curve, the marginal revenue curve will have twice the slope of the demand curve (I don't know why this is, but that is what it is).[^473]
- ![[Pasted image 20250129093817.png]][^473]
- As said before, the company will then maximize profits by selling until the point that marginal revenue is equal to marginal costs.[^473]
- - Profits are calculated like usual: $$(Price - Average Costs) * Quantity$$[^473]
- ![[Pasted image 20250129094346.png]][^473]
- The monopoly markup (the amount a company can charge more than would be expected if the market would be competitive) is higher when demand is more inelastic and lower when demand is more elastic.[^474]
- ![[Pasted image 20250129143427.png]][^474]
- Why medicine is so inelastic (and, therefore has such a high monopoly markup):
	1. If someone is dying, then there is no price they won't be willing to pay for a life-saving drug.[^474]
	2. If you are spending someone else's money, then you don't particularly care if the price goes up. When you get a medicine, your insurance usually covers it (or at least you think it is supposed to). As such, you don't really care if the price goes up.[^474] (This is a bit more complicated because, technically, your insurance prices do go up when the insurance has to spend more money on medicine.)[^7]
- Monopolies move gains from trade from the consumer to the producer, and create some deadweight loss, meaning, that there are potential mutually profitable trades that now will not happen.[^475]
- ![[Pasted image 20250130001301.png]][^475]
- Giving patents can help companies working in industries that have high development costs compared to marginal costs. In these industries, a company that develops something would have no way to earn their costs back. As such, there would be no incentive for anyone to develop things with high development costs.[^475]
- There is a balance between allowing competition to lower prices now to help with the dead weight loss (and other issues, like buyers with little money having to spend their life savings on life-saving drugs) and not disincentivizing companies to develop things in the future.[^475]
- Examples of these types of industries are pharmaceuticals, music, movies, and computer programs.[^475]
- Some economic historians believe that a lack of intellectual property rights is part of why there was little innovation until recent times.[^475]
- Another way to deal with this issue is with patent buyouts. A government "buys" the patent on something and annuls it. This opens the market to competition, driving down prices, and also keeps companies incentivized.[^475]
- A problem with this is that it falls on the tax payer to pay for this.[^475]
- Also, this system can be subject to corruption. Which patents does the government buy out?[^475]
- Another system is that the government offers prize money for developing certain technologies.[^475]
- It seems that this should also suffer from the same issues as the government buyouts, namely, it falls on the taxpayer and can be subject to corruption.[^7][^475]
- Another way to deal with this is price discrimination (selling the same product to different groups of people at different prices), which will be discussed.[^475]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I think that some of these points about prizes have been discussed before in a discussion about patents and subsidies. Perhaps these sections should be combined</span>}
- Price discrimination means charging different prices to different groups of people for the same good or service.[^476]
- A company will do this to maximize profits.[^476]
- With goods, there is the danger that someone will buy the good at the cheaper price and undercut the company and sell it to the group getting charged the higher price. A company has to prevent this from happening to be able to profit from price discrimination.[^476]
- Example, movies sell tickets for different prices to seniors as to young adults. This is to maximize profits. Young adults are more willing to spend more money on a movie than a senior.[^476]
- Rohm and Haas developed a plastic that had an industrial use (which had a lot of substitutes) and a slightly different one that had a use in creating dentures (for which there were few substitutes). They charged considerably more for the denture plastic than the industrial plastic. People started buying the industrial one and converting it to the denture one. To prevent this, Rohm and Haas spread a rumor that the industrial plastic was developed with some poisonous chemicals.[^476]
- Airlines charge more for last second ticker bookings (the person is probably inelastic. They need to go) than for early bookings (the person is able to make alternative plans if the tickets are too expensive).[^476]
- There are many more examples.[^476]
- Price discrimination can be a good thing (compared to a monopolist charging a fixed, profit maximizing price).[^477]
- This is because:
	1. This increases profits, even more than charging the single profit maximizing price. The single maximizing price charges some people less than they will be willing to pay, and charges others more. This can create incentive for companies to try and develop some product (like develop a drug).[^477]
	2. This increases the amount of profitable trades. Nobody is charged more than they are willing to pay.[^477]
- To be able to do this at a very individualized level, the company has to have a lot of knowledge about the consumer.[^477]
- A form of highly segmented price discrimination is financial aid from college tuitions. Colleges offer highly variable amounts of financial aid (or charge their students highly variable amounts of tuition, depending on how you look at it). Colleges are able to do this because they ask for a lot of financial information from the students when applying for financial aid.[^477]
- However, this does transfer all of the gains from trade from the consumer to the producer.[^7]
- Tying:
- Tying is wen a company sells one good for basically the cost price (the base good), and then sells another good with it that is required to make it work. This second good is bought in variable amounts, depending on how much you use it (the variable good).[^478]
- The variable good needs to be bought from the same company that sold you the base good.[^478]
- The company makes its profits by selling the variable good at a higher price than competition would suggest.[^478]
- Examples of this are printers and ink, and cell phones and service.[^478]
- This, in a sense, is price discrimination. Those that will need more of the good (they print more or they need more data) usually have a higher demand for the good so the more they will pay as they need to buy more of the variable good. Those that don't need so much usually have a lower demand and so they will pay less.[^478]
- By undercharging for the base good and overcharging for the variable good, as opposed to competitive prices for both, this allows people who don't want to use the good so much (they don't have a lot to print) to enter the market without paying prohibitively high costs for the base good.[^478]
- Meanwhile people who use the product a lot and have high demand are willing to pay more for their increased use.[^478]
- ![[Untitled design.png]][^479]
- Like price discrimination does, it gets rid of deadweight loss (people who otherwise would not have entered the market now can) and moves consumer surplus to producer surplus which can help a company deal with high R&D costs which they would not be able to cover without increased profits from price discrimination[^478] (although there may be some positives to allowing the consumer to keep the surplus).[^7]
- Bundling:
- A company may bundle a few products together and sell them to consumers.
- Examples of this are cable TV (with hundreds of channels), Spotify and Apple Music (with millions of songs), and Microsoft Office (which contains Word, PowerPoint, Excel, etc.).[^480]
- A company can maximize profits by doing this. If a company sells each product individually, then either they can sell it for cheap and have lots of sales but make little money per sale, or for a lot and have few sales but make a lot of money per sale. Either way, there is consumer surplus and perhaps deadweight loss.[^480]
- The demand for a bundle is much less variable. Everyone's demand is much closer to the mean.[^480]
- Example: Some people have a high demand for watching sports and a low demand for watching cooking shows. Some people are the opposite. Both of these people may have a similar demand for a sports-cooking show bundle.[^480]
- The company now can charge around the mean and get a lot of the consumer surplus while not leaving much deadweight loss.[^480]
- ![[Pasted image 20250130231102.png]][^480]
- Bundles are particularly effective when marginal costs are low. If a company needs to spend a lot of money on each item they add to a bundle, they need to really figure out which products they should  bundle together, which is difficult. When marginal costs are low, companies can easily create massive bundles.[^480]
- The usual effects (transfer of consumer surplus to producer surplus and limiting deadweight loss apply here apply here. In addition, products with little marginal costs very often have high fixed costs (like cable TV) and so producers are not going to enter the field unless they think they can recoup their money.[^480]
- People naively think that if 100 channels are $100, one channel is $1 and so people feel that they should be able to choose the channels they want. In reality, this is not right. If consumers can pick channels, the price per channel would go up.[^480]
- Companies demand more labor if the price of the labor is less than the profits they make from hiring the laborer.[^481]
- The more laborers an individual company hires in a given position, the less revenue each laborer adds to the company (diminishing returns).[^481]
- Some employees may work less when they get paid more.[^481]
- Still, there is an upward sloping supply curve and a downward sloping demand curve for labor, like by other things. This is because more people enter the industry which makes up for the amount less that people in the industry work.[^481]
- Janitors in first-world countries get paid more than janitors in developing countries. This is because janitors in first-world countries are worth more to the institutions that hire them. And this is because institutions in first-world make more money and so the difference between having a clean business and a dirty one makes a bigger difference.[^481]
- This point applies to basically any job. There is less demand for computer programmers in developing countries than in first-world countries because companies will make less profit from programs they use.[^481]
- In addition, there may be higher standards for cleanliness for companies in first-world countries than developing countries,[^7]
- In addition, first-world countries typically have a better educated work force and so there is a relatively low supply of people who want to be janitors compared to developing countries.[^481]
- Why are people with advanced education on average paid more than people who without?
- Four possibilities:
	1. People who are able to get through college have certain innate abilities (high IQ, hard workers, etc.). People with these abilities do better in the labor force. This would be true regardless of if they went to college.[^482]
	2. A degree is a signal to the job market that someone has these innate abilities.[^482]
	3. People actually learn things in college.[^482]
	4. You meet people and develop relationships in college.[^7]
- Indications to option 3 (over option 2):
	- Maybe to get a first job a signal is important, but over time, the people who are actually good employees will get sorted from those who are not.[^482]
		- Counter: Markets are somewhat inefficient.[^482]
		- Counter: A job is also a signal. Getting that first job is a signal to get a second job.[^7]
			- Counter: If you aren't good at the first job, a future potential employer can call your earlier employer and find out the truth. That won't function as a signal at all.[^7]
	- There are easier, less intense ways of inventing a signal. We can give tests.
		- Counter: It is hard to replicate what college shows. There is a lot of grit that is indicated by going to years of college. A test can't indicate this.[^482]
		- Counter: Now that the system is set up this way, it is extremely difficult to change it.[^482]
	- There are some concrete examples of additional education without an additional signal helping. In Scandinavian countries, the government passed a law requiring an additional year of college. The difference was not obvious on their resumes. Yet they still earned more years later.[^482]
	  Many "growth miracle countries" like China, Singapore, and South Korea) have invested heavily in education.[^482]
		- Counter: Countries in Africa have also invested a lot in education and it has not worked for them.[^482]
			- Counter: This is because of other factors, like bad institutions.[^482]
- Indications to option 2 (over option 3):
	- There are many jobs today that require a degree for which the person definitely did not learn anything about the job i[^482]n college (like a janitor, for example).[^482]
	  Similarly, to become a college professor, you need a college degree no matter how much you may know about a subject.
		- Counter: There are lots of skills one learns in college, not just the material. You learn how to work hard, submit to authority, make sure that you are where you need to be on time, etc.[^482]
		- Counter: We wouldn't feel comfortable with a self-study surgeon or driving over a bridge built by a self-study engineer.[^482]
	- Ask yourself what you would rather have: a Harvard education or a Harvard degree?[^482]
	- Students don't actually remember what they learned shortly after they learn it.[^482]
		- Counter: They still developed the critical thinking skills as well as other skills that they take with them. (This is really the same point as before. They should just be combined.)[^482]
	- There are some degrees that people get but don't end up working in that field (like art history). Even so, these people earn more than people without a degree at all.[^483]
		- Counter: They learned important skills (as said before).[^482]
	- There is a massive difference in salary between getting a degree a falling one credit short of getting a degree, but there is almost no difference in knowledge/skills.[^483]
- It seems to be some combination of things.[^7]
- Different workers (within the same economy) get paid different amounts because of levels of education and because what they are educated in.[^483]
- Over time, college educated people get paid an increasingly higher ratio compared to people with just a high school degree.[^483]
- ![[Pasted image 20250131190140.png]][^483]
- This is because:
	1. Computers have become an increasingly more important part of the economy. Learning computer skills (which one needs to do for a wide variety of majors) has become increasingly more important.[^483]
	2. Globalization. This allows educated people to access the global market. Since there are relatively few educated people in the world, these skills have become more valuable.[^483]
	3. On the flip side, less educated workers now have more competition to deal with. There are many uneducated workers in the world who now have access to the US economy.[^483]
- Jobs which have other benefits than the just the wages (they are more fun, more safe, etc.) have lower wages than other jobs with similar level of skills.[^484]
- As such, employers have an incentive to make jobs safer. Jobs which are unsafe need to pay more to get people to take those jobs.[^485]
- This is why jobs have gotten safer over time. Not so much because of increasing government regulations.[^485]
- This is only true when risks are known to the employee ahead of time. Otherwise, workers will not demand higher wages to take on the more dangerous job as they don't realize the danger.[^485]
- This is helped by workers' comp. Workers' comp is the government required insurance system that companies need to pay into for workers injured on jobs. As such, companies still have an incentive to try to maintain safe job conditions.[^485]
- This only works for injuries that occur on the job. Injuries that only occur later (like cancer from asbestos) are not protected by the workers' comp system.[^485]
- Lawsuits and government regulations are needed for these types of things.[^485]
- Over time, as these risks start to become known, companies do begin to have incentive to account for these risks.[^7]
- Unions usually raise wages for the industry that they are a union for. They do this by limiting the amount of people in a particular field, which drives wages up.[^486]
- For example, the AMA (American Medical Association) tried not allowing nurse practitioners to have the same allowances as doctors. They claim that this is for safety purposes.[^486]
- However, overall wages do not increase.[^486]
- This can be seen because countries like the United States and Switzerland, which have relatively low percentages of unionized workers (under 30%), have similar wages to European countries other than Switzerland, which have high rates of unionized workers (between 30% and 80%).[^486] (I think these numbers are from the early 2010s.)
- This is because workers who would have entered this field now have to go to other fields, driving down wages in those fields.[^486]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I thought that unions act like a monopoly and force a price floor, not that they limit the amount of workers in a particular field. Perhaps by forcing that price floor, the amount of workers that people want to hire in the field are limited? I feel like this may mostly transfer the surplus of a corporation to workers. I guess this doesn't seem to be the case because workers do not have higher wages on average in countries with unions than those without, as said earlier</span>}
- In addition, unions can sometimes gain so much power that they slow down the whole economy. In this situation, real wages drop.[^486]
- Unions may also create market inefficiencies. They may not allow the dissolution of required permits/licenses or other things like that which may not be necessary but create search costs, in an effort to keep people out of the industry.[^7]
- There are 4 types of goods: 
	1. Public goods: Non-rivalrous (multiple people can use the same good without an increase in cost) and non-excludable (everyone benefits from this good existing, even those that did not pay for it)[^487]
	2. Private goods: rivalrous and excludable.[^487]
	3. Club goods: Non-rivalrous and excludable.[^487]
	4. Common Resources: Rivalrous and non-excludable.[^487]
- Public goods:
- Ex: A nuclear weapons system. Whether you pay or not, the the whole country will (hopefully) not be attacked by nuclear weapons from other countries.[^487]
- In addition, for many of these things, the amount that an individual adds does not make a difference as to whether the good will be successful or not.[^487]
- As such, many people may decide to "freeride", which means take the benefit of the good without paying for it, by relying on other who do. The problem is that if there are enough of these "freeriders", then there actually won't be enough money to allow the good to be successful.[^487]
- A way of dealing with this is by using taxation and the government.[^487]
- However, this process doesn't have the "invisible hand" force that markets have. It uses the oftentimes messy political process.[^487]
- How much should we pay for these public goods? How should we produce them? These questions are not easily answered.[^487]
- In addition to the freerider problem, there is also a "forced rider" problem. This means that people who do not want a certain good are forced to pay for it. This is also something which should be avoided.[^487]
- One way public goods are monetized is through advertising. For example, radio is a public good. Companies make money from radio by selling ad time. Similarly, Alphabet (Google's parent company) treats Google like a public good. They don't exclude anyone from using Google. This is profitable to them because they also sell ads.[^488]
- Club goods:
- Ex: Wi-fi (up to a point). Cable TV.[^488]
- There is some inefficiently with these goods because some people may be willing to pay less than the price but are still excluded, even though the company stands to gain by including this customer (as it costs them next to nothing to add this person).[^488]
- Some goods have the option of being offered as a non-profit public good, a profitable public good, and a club good. (Ex: Wi-fi is sometimes provided by the government in some places, is offered for free in Starbucks and similar businesses, or is offered only if you watch an add or have a subscription to a company).[^488]
- Common resources:
- Ex: The environment, the amount of tuna.[^489]
- Individuals have no incentive to maintain common resources.[^489]
- The problem is that everyone loses out because of this as the resource is diminished.[^489]
- For example, an individual fisherman has no incentive to fish less to preserve the amount of fish there are. But because of this, the overall stock of fish dwindles. This is known as the "tragedy of the commons".[^489]
- Possible solutions:
	1. Command and control. Impose regulations on what one is allowed to do. This comes with the usual inefficiencies of command and control.[^489]
	2. Cultural norms. This is actually effective, but it can take a while to create these norms and they are mostly effective in smaller, stable groups.[^489]
	3. Create property rights. For example, government can create tradable allowances for a certain resource. This may be difficult to implement for resources which are shared by multiple counties (Ex: The environment),[^489]
- Asymmetric information and adverse selection problems:
- In some situations, one party knows more information than the other. (Ex: Health insurance. The person being insured knows more about their health than the insurance company. Used car sales. The car salesman knows more about the state of the cars than the buyers.)[^490]
- In these situations, the person without the information is not going to be able to offer better prices for the better product and worse for the worse product because they don't know which is which. Meanwhile, the person with the information can offer them the worst products. As such, the person without the information will have to assume that they are receiving a bad product.[^490]
- Because this is the case, it isn't worth it for the people with the quality products (people with the best health, or the most reliable used cars) to enter the market.[^490]
- Without these people, the average quality drops. As such, the person without the information has to offer a worse deal (someone buying a used car is only willing to offer less and insurance companies need to charge more). This forces the remaining people with the best quality product out of the market. This further lowers the quality which forces a worse deal, forcing more people out of the market, etc.[^490]
- Eventually there is no market except for the people with the worst products for which it isn't worth it for anyone to pay for (it isn't really worth it for anyone to buy the worst used cars or for insurance companies to insure people with the worst health).[^490]
- This is prevented from happening in a few ways:
	1. Have a third party ascertain information and give it to the party without the information (like Carfax for used cars, or a health checkup for people applying for health insurance).[^490]
	   Counter: This forces people with the worst health to pay the most money. If it is nearly guaranteed that they will have large bills, than insurance will basically be the price of the bill. People don't like this.[^490]
	2. The market doesn't always make sense. People who are healthy still buy health insurance because these are the same types of people who want to avoid risk.[^490]
	3. Lower the information that the side with the information has. For example, companies offer insurance to their employees. The companies don't know anything about the health of their employees. As such, even a company with healthier employees will not drop out of the market because they don't realize that they are overpaying.[^490]
	   Counter: People will lose their insurance if they lose their job. Also what happens yo people who retire?[^490]
	   Counter: There are some government programs that help with this (like Medicare).[^490]
	4. Command and control: Require everyone to engage in the market. For example the Affordable Care Act (Obamacare) requires everyone to have health insurance, even healthy people. This helps subsidize insurance for unhealthy people.[^490]
- Asymmetric information also leads to moral hazard. This means that the side with the information has an incentive to lie to the side without the information. (Ex: A mechanic has incentive to say that more repairs are required than actually are. The person paying for the repairs has no way of knowing if this is true.)[^491]
- This then encourages the person without the information to not deal with the person with the information if they believe that they may be taken advantage of, which can lead to problems (people not getting taking their car in for inspections).[^491]
- Solutions:
	1. Equate the information between the two parties.[^491]
	2. Align the incentive of side with the information to the side of the party without the information.[^491]
- Practically, both of these can be done through:
	1. Review sites.[^491]
	   Counter: This can be manipulated. (Ex: A person may get their friends to give his company good reviews. A college may encourage people to apply so they can reject people to lower their acceptance percentage to look more prestigious.)[^491]
	   Counter: A third-party reviewer (like Consumer Reports) won't be affected by this.[^491]
	   Counter: This information is non-rivalrous and, at least to some degree, non-excludable (even if you don't pay for the third party review, you can still get the information and use it). Like public goods {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">add link</span>} this creates freerider problems, causing an underproduction of these third-party reviews.[^491]
	2. Separate diagnosis of an issue from the person carrying out the repair. (Ex: Going to a doctor for a second opinion. Having a home inspector inspect your home (in states where a home inspector is not allowed to offer repairs)).[^491]
	   Counter: This can be manipulated. One group of people in a particular field may "work together" by always recommending the more expensive option. This way, they can be assured that when they are the person performing the service, they can know that the "diagnoser" will recommend an expensive option.[^7]
- Another solution to asymmetric information is for the party with the high information to convey a credible signal to the side without information.[^492]
- Example: Hyundai used to have a bad reputation. They started making cars with higher quality. However, they needed a way to let buyers know that their cars were made with higher quality. To do this, they offered a big warrantee on their cars. Buyers saw that Hyundai was putting their money where their mouth was and so they were able to see that Hyundai was actually making better cars. (Meanwhile, since the cars were actually better, this didn't cost Hyundai very much.)[^492]
- Example: A college degree (as discussed earlier).[^492]
- Example: An expensive engagement ring.[^492]
- Signals are not always so efficient (students need to jump through hoops to be able to get their degrees and waste time learning things that they don't need). They still do serve this role of passing information to the side that doesn't have it.[^492]
- Consumers buy things if the value (or satisfaction/happiness if you want to think of it that way) exceeds the cost.[^493]
- They make choices between what to buy and buy the things that give them the most value.[^493]
- Goods have diminishing returns. This means that every good of the same type that you buy, the less value that good gives you (for the most part). (Ex: One slice of pizza is good, two slices are better, but that second one doesn't add the same value as the first. Three slices are better but that third one doesn't add the same value as the second, etc.)[^493]
- When comparing different potential options, you may want to look at groups that chose each option and compare them. However, there is selection bias problems with this (correlation not causation). One group may have been more successful than the other because of other things unrelated to the choice they made. A group of people who have certain characteristics to make them more successful may be more likely to pick one option over the other, but the option itself is not what actually caused the success.[^494]
- Ex: Students that go to private college make more money than students who go to public college (on average). This may be because private college is better than public college. However, this may be because the students that want to go to private college are smarter, or were brought up with a greater focus on education.[^494]
- Randomized controlled trials (or randomized clinical trials when discussing medical research) (RCTs) are theoretically the preferred way to deal wit selection bias.[^495]
- In an RCT, subjects are assigned to either a treatment group, or a control group (a group not receiving the treatment).[^495]
- Due to the Law of Large Numbers, on average, the two groups are going to be nearly exactly the same.[^495]
- Example of the law of large numbers: The odds of getting 75% heads or more when flipping a coin 4 times is 31.25%. The odds of getting at least 75% heads when doing 100 coin flips is 0.0000282% (calculated using [this website](https://www.omnicalculator.com/statistics/coin-flip-probability)). The more coin flips you do, the more the results cluster around the expected result (which is 50% heads).
- Practically, these can be expensive or impractical. Also, sometimes the groups may not be randomly selected, which would ruin the experiment.[^495]
- The third truce started on October 22. [^496]
- By this time, Israel had made significant progress in the south, reconquering Yad Mordechai.[^496]
- This was immediately broken by Fawzi al-Qawuqji (also spelled Kaukji, the leader of the Arab Liberation Army in the north[^497]) who didn't hold himself beholden to the UN. His positions were located in the Upper Galilee by several cities, such as Manara, Misgav Am, and Meron. There were Syrian troops located there as well.[^496]
- The Israelis launched Operation Hiram to gain control of the territory part of the Mandate of Palestine. This operation successfully pushed the army back until the Litani River.[^496]
- ![[Pasted image 20250204225753.png]][^430]
- The Egyptian forces were besieged by the Israelis in Faluja.[^498]
- The Egyptians fought valiantly and were able to get supplies and soldiers past the Israeli army to Faluja to strengthen the forces.[^498]
- Yigal Allon, who was leading the Israeli forces there, was so impressed that he offered Sayid Taha (the Egyptian commander) the opportunity to be allowed to retreat back to Egypt. Taha, as a matter of honor, refused.[^498]
- On November 4, the UN demanded that a real ceasefire be reached and that forces should go back to there positions that they had held of October 14. The Israeli refused to return the the land they had pushed the Egyptians out of in the Negev (which was part of the Palestine Mandate).[^499]
- In Jerusalem, the forces there came to a truce. Jerusalem was divided in half, with a no-mans land between the two sides.[^499]
- There was disagreement between King Abdullah, Egypt, and Al-Husseini. King Abdullah had support of many Arabs, particularly in the West Bank, who wanted to become part of Transjordan.[^500]
- There was political unrest in Egypt due to the military setbacks in Palestine. The Muslim Brotherhood was behind a lot of this and assassinated members of the government. The government declared the Muslim Brotherhood illegal.[^500]
- The Israelis launched Operation Horev to push the Egyptians out of South West of Mandatory Palestine (south of Beersheva).[^501]
- A diversionary attack was launched on December 22 at night (which was repulsed). This, in addition to other attacks, convinced the Egyptians that the Israelis were focused on cutting off the road from Rafah to Gaza along the coast.[^501]
- The main attack was launched on December 25 along an ancient road from Beersheva to El Auja through Khalasa (Halsa), bypassing defenses along the main road that went through Bir Asluj.[^501]
- The Israelis took El Auja on the 28th and then pushed into the Sinai Peninsula, which was part of Egypt.[^501]
- ![[Pasted image 20250205232022.png]][^502]
- During this time, the Canadian government recognized the State of Israel.[^501]
- Management/leadership:
- Employees need to feel good about what they do. This is for the sake of the employee themselves, but also, it allows the employee to work at their maximum ability.[^503]
- Goals and performance metrics should be short and clear. (Goals should be about 250 words.)[^503]
- The manager and the employee need to discuss what these are to make sure that they are on the same page.[^503]
- They should then be written up and given to the manager and the employee.[^503]
- An employee shouldn't have more than a few goals at a time. That should be enough to encapsulate the job.[^503]
- The employee's job is the employee's job, not the managers job.[^503]
- Every so often (at least once a day), take a minute to review your goals (part of why it is important to have the goals written succinctly) and see if your actions are in line with those goals.[^503]
- With Israel pushing into Egypt, the UN called for a ceasefire on December 29.[^504]
- Egypt didn't agree as they wanted to hold onto Gaza and felt that their current position was precarious (I think). Egypt called on the Arab Legion and Iraq to attack Israel from elsewhere to relieve the pressure, but they refused.[^504]
- The British then gave Israel an ultimatum to retreat from Egypt or the British would get involved (due to the Anglo-Egyptian Treaty of 1936). Ben-Gurion ordered the troops there to leave. The US also demanded that Israel pull out of Egypt.[^504]
- The troops Yigal Allon and Yitzchak Rabin wanted to capture the town of El Arish to totally lock off the Egyptian forces in Gaza from Egypt. Ben-Gurion did not allow that. However, he did allow an attack on Rafah using the El-Auja Rafah road which was mainly in Egypt.[^504]
- The Israeli forces tried to get the Egyptians out of Rafah before the ceasefire kicked in on January 7, but they were unsuccessful.[^504]
- The Egyptians remained in Gaza and all the way up to Faluja, but the Israelis had been able to clear them out of the rest of the negev.[^504]
- Britain sent up some planes on January 7 to make sure that Israel was not is Egypt. However, Israel mistook these planes for Egyptian fighters and engaged with them, shooting some down.[^504]
- This, along with the fact that there were still some troops on the Rafah El-Auja road, which was in Egypt led to heightened tensions with the British. These were eased by the British releasing the last Jews on Cyprus to Israel.[^504]
- On January 20, the Israeli government decided to outlaw the purchase of non-kosher meat in Israel.[^505]
- The Israelis signed an armistice with Egypt in February and with Jordan in March.[^505]
- Just before a ceasefire (which came before the armistice) with Jordan was signed (while the mediators were talking) Israeli forces marched into Umm al-Rashrāsh (Eilat) without a fight and claimed it for Israel.[^505]
- ![[Pasted image 20250205235909.png]][^506]
- 6000 Jews immigrated to Palestine between March 1947 and the beginning of 1948, under the British Mandate with more started to arrive beginning in February, once there was no restrictions on immigration. After the war, tens of thousands of Jews immigrated to Israel, including the ones who were allowed out of Cyprus and into Israel.[^505]
- The first Knesset (parliament) was formed in late January 1949.[^507]
- Mapai (מַפָּא"י, מִפְלֶגֶת פּוֹעֲלֵי אֶרֶץ יִשְׂרָאֵל‎, Workers' Party of the Land of Israel, a left wing labor party) got the most seats (46 out of 120), Mapam (מַפָּ״ם, מִפְלֶגֶת הַפּוֹעֲלִים הַמְּאוּחֶדֶת, United Workers Party, similar to Mapai but more left wing) got 19, the United Religious Front (a mix of all religious Jews) got 16, and Cherut List (right wing party headed by Menachem Begin), gained 14.[^507]
- There were a few other parties, such as a Sephradic Jewry party, a Yemenite party, a communist party (called the Maki), an Arab party, and a party of ex-Stern Gang members.[^507]
- Ben-Gurion was the leader of Mapai and so he stayed as prime minister.[^507]
- He did not want to work the Cherut List or the Maki as he felt they were too right-wing and left-wing, respectively. This was something that he stuck to for nearly the whole time he was in office.[^507]
- The coalition contained Mapai, the United Religious Front, the Arab party, as well as a few others. The Mapam refused to work with the religious and so they refused to join the coalition.[^507]
- He wanted to make peace with other entities, particularly the Arabs, the UN, the US and the Soviet Union, a Labor majority in the government {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know what a labor party is</span>} and equality all people (men and women, Arabs, Jews, and Christians).[^507]
- Chaim Weizmann (representing Mapai) was overwhelmingly voted in as the first president.[^507]
- A cabinet was then formed with various ministers for different positions.[^507]
- Israel and Lebanon signed an armistice agreement on March 23, with Israel withdrawing from occupied Lebanon villages.[^507]
- Israel was also working on an armistice with Transjordan. There was an agreement made between Israel and King Abdullah despite some objections from the Transjordan Prime Minister over some villages in the Hebron Hills which would now be part of Israel.[^507]
- In May, the UN voted to accept Israel as a member state. The countries around Israel (Egypt, Syria, Lebanon, Transjordan, and Saudi Arabia) all voted against it.[^508]
- They also refused to turn the armistice agreements into peace treaties.[^508] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know what the difference is</span>}
- Syria and Israel made an armistice in July.[^508]
- The US pressured Israel to take in 200,000-300,000 of the Palestinian refugees (which were estimated to be between 700,000 and over 800,000. This was called the Lausanne Proposals). Israel refused to take in any as they were afraid of 5th columnists, as well as a public discontent and they felt that if they started to take some in, there would be more pressure for them to take more in.[^508]
- Israel did take responsibility for about 70,000 Arabs that had fled their homes but remained in Israel (internal refugees) or who had made their way back to Israel since the end of the war.[^508]
- The United Nations Refugee and Work Administration (UNWRA) was created in December 1949. They set up camps for the refugees in the surrounding countries.[^508]
- Israel passed legislation between 1949 and 1953 which made it more difficult for Palestinians to return to Palestine. Some of the laws allowed Israelis to take the abandoned property of Arabs. While this was for security purposes at times, there were times (and this may have usually been the case) that it was simply convenient to take the land to allow for settlements to expand.[^509]
- The Palestinians in the surrounding countries were not allowed to assimilate. They were forced to remain in camps to keep the Palestinian plight in the mind of the international community.[^509]
- Over 650,000 immigrants came to Israel between May 1947 and the end of 1951.[^509]
- Over 120,000 Jews came from Iraq, another 120,000 were Holocaust survivors (largely Polish) from DP camps, 45,000 from Morocco, Tunisia, and Algeria, 37,000 from Belgium, 45,000 from Yemen, as well as other places.[^509]
- Governments in some of these countries needed to be bribed to allow their Jewish subjects out.[^509]
- The Jews in Iraq had to leave behind their possessions.[^509]
- Jews in Iraq, Morocco, Tunisia, Algeria, and Yemen left because they felt like second-class citizens and were harassed in their home countries (sometimes, anti-Jewish riots and killings).[^509]
- Jewish extremists planted bombs in Iraq to further "encourage" the local population to come to Israel (it was thought that the Iraqi government or Iraqi extremists had planted the bombs).[^509]
- The Jews from Yemen were airlifted out, in a huge operation that became known as "Magic Carpet".[^509]
- The American Jewish Joint Distribution Committee (The Joint) helped organize many of these migrations.[^509]
- Israel had a population of about 600,000 when it declared independence and had to absorb all of these immigrants.[^510]
- Golda Meir, as well as others, attempted to raise money in America and Britain to build some sort of housing and provide food for these immigrants.[^510]
- Many immigrants were housed in tent cities, called ma'abarot, with poor living conditions.[^510]
- To help alleviate some of the problems, the army was utilized to grow crops.[^510]
- Within a decade, Israel was able to sort everyone out.[^510]
- This gave Israel poor PR. The immigrants to Israel were treated with as much dignity as Israel could muster. The Palestinians, meanwhile, were stuck in their refugee camps (I believe that they still are in refugee camps, like Jabaliya).[^510]
- Many of the immigrants were unskilled. They were taught how to plant.[^511]
- Jews were outnumbered by Arabs in the Galilee so the government made it a priority to establish new settlements there.[^511]
- Israel established a large reserve fighting force.[^511]
- The border with Jordan was drawn with some anomalies. The Arab village of Al-Tayyiba and Beit Safafa were included in Israel, while the city of Tulkarm was included in Jordanian territory, but the fields and railway lines were part of Israel.[^511]
- The Jordanians kept the refugees separate from the local population, even within a city, further exacerbating the refugee crisis.[^511]
- Many new Jewish settlements were created and many abandoned Arab towns were repopulated with Jewish immigrants.[^512]
- Israel argued that war created refugees. That was a fact of life and was perfectly normal for the time. In addition, Jewish immigrants from Arabic countries were in many cases mistreated. This was a population exchange (similar to what happened in Greece and Turkey in 1922).[^512]
- New settlements were set up in the Jerusalem Corridor to help strengthen it.[^512]
- Israel bought military boats from Canada to establish a navy.[^513]
- In July 1950, Israel passed the law of the Right of Return, which granted citizenship to any Jew that wanted to join Israel (with some exceptions, like criminals and Jews who converted out of the religion).[^513]
- More than 300 Arab villages had been systematically bulldozed while others were populated with Jewish immigrants.[^513]
- In 1951, UNWRA offered $100 million to try and resettle the Palestinians in the surrounding countries. This was rejected.[^513]
- In 1951, Israel built many settlements throughout the Negev. The land was inhospitable (the sand needed to be desalinated) but Jews were able to cultivate it with different agricultural methods.[^514]
- The kibbutzim were mostly Mapai with many Mapam, but, over time, Mapai became wary of communism, while Mapam stayed with it. As such, many kibbutzim split up.[^514]
- Ben-Gurion was never officially invited to the US as the US didn't want to hurt its relationship with the oil-producing Arabic countries (although Ben-Gurion did come unofficially).[^514]
- In 1951, Israel established its own aircraft industry, called Israel Aircraft Industries.[^514]
- There was a lot of political instability in the surrounding Arab countries in 1951.[^514]
- In March, the Prime Minister of Iran was assassinated by Islamic fundamentalists.[^514]
- In July, the Prime Minister of Lebanon was assassinated by Syrian extremists who saw Lebanon as a part of Syria.[^514]
- Also in July, King Abdullah was assassinated by a Palestinian because the king was showing too much concern for Israel.[^514]
- Israelis had almost no access to holy sites (the Kosel, I assume?), even though that had been part of the armistice agreement.[^514]
- In Egypt, King Farouk was deposed by a group including Anwar El-Sadat and Gamal Abdul Nasser. They were anti-West and pro-Soviet.[^514]
