- Members in the Israeli government felt that mass immigration was a positive for the country (it seemed that more skilled workers may have been coming to Israel by this point).[^515]
- In 1951, Israel had their government's second election. There were a few seat changes but for the most part, things were largely the same.[^515]
- The Mapam lost a few seats, and they would continue to do so until they dissolved.[^515]
- The religious party broke into different parties, but they gained less seats, so they rejoined together as the National Religious Party.[^515]
- They were in alliance with the Mapei for many years to come.[^515]
- Some people tried to push for a two party system, but that never came to fruition. The coalition system gave disproportionate power to small groups.[^515]
- The Agudat Yisroel party (the party for the religious Jews) {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I am confused by their relationship with the National Religious Party</span>}, led by the Gerrer Rebbe, did not approve of the country of Israel and none of their members were allowed to hold office that they had to swear in.[^515]
- Despite this, they were willing to work with the government.[^515]
- The Agudat Yisroel fought for and organized religious life, such as the creation of a separate religious school system, and military exemptions for people learning in yeshiva.[^515]
- New settlements were established in the Negev in 1952 as well.[^516]
- Despite being president, Ben-Gurion, as well as others, did not involve Weizmann in the government.[^516]
- Weizmann died in November 1952.[^516]
- There was a big debate in Israel about accepting reparations from Germany for their actions in WWII. Ben-Gurion and most of the Knesset were pro while Begin, his party Cherut, and others, were anti.[^517]
- Those against argued that:
	1. It was a disgrace for those who were killed. One person related that his son asked him, "how much are we going to get for grandpa and grandma?"[^517]
	2. They wanted nothing to do with Germany.[^517]
- There were riots in the streets during meetings about whether to accept this deal.[^517]
- Despite this, reparations were accepted. The deal was signed in September.[^517]
- 85% of the money went to Israel (as they accepted an outsized amount of the Holocaust survivors) while 15% went to Jews in other parts of the world.[^517]
- The $715 million Israel received was only slightly less than all of the money that Israel had raised since the inception of the state. This money was put to further development.[^517]
- In October 1952, Israel agreed to unfreeze the bank accounts of Arab refugees in Israeli banks.[^517]
- In 1952, the still new communist government of Czechoslovakian accused many Jewish members of the government of being saboteurs of the country. The accusation at least partially blames this on the fact that they were Jewish and that they were Zionists (even though many either had nothing to do with Zionism or were anti-Zionists). Israel itself was accused of trying to set this up.[^518]
- Two Israelis were even arrested in Czechoslovakia (and later released).[^518]
- Israel denied all of the allegations against itself and against the Jews in Czechoslovakia.[^518]
- This was the first of many times Israel would try to intervene for Jews of the international community.[^518]
- In 1953, Israel started establishing relations with some Asian countries, such as Burma, Japan, the Philippines, and Ceylon. However, because of Russia's friendship with Egypt and Egypt's dislike of Israel, these were shut down.[^519]
- Yad Vashem was established in 1953.[^519]
- In August, 1953, an Arab from Gaza killed an Israeli and injured his daughter.[^519]
- In response, Israel sent in a unit to respond, called Unit 101, in what would be called the Bureij Raid.[^519]
- They attacked the house where they thought was the base of infiltrators, the house of an Egyptian military leader in Gaza, Major Mustafa Hafez.[^519]
- Hafez was not home at the time and the attack led to death and injury of many civilians, many of whom were women and children.[^519]
- This attack was condemned by UNWRA.[^519]
- Israel denied responsibility, even though it was responsible. This may have been due to internal deception within the Israeli government.[^519]
- Israel tried to do some work on the Jordan River opposite Syria. Syria demanded that they stop. This was agreed to by a UN committee.[^520]
- A US diplomat was sent to make a deal between Israel, Jordan, Lebanon, and Syria, about water use in the area as well as other things.[^520]
- A beneficial deal was made for all sides, but Syria and Lebanon refused to sign it while Israel and Jordan did.[^520]
- Israel (in a new location) and Jordan was able to complete the irrigation projects. America helped supply some of the funding.[^520]
- This led to a somewhat growing relationship between Israel and Jordan.[^520]
- In the center of Israel, Jordanians would sneak over the border and cause problems. Between 1951 and 1956, there were 6000 recorded crossings and 400 Israelis killed.[^520]
- Israel sent Unit 101, led by Ariel Sharon, to the Arab village of Qibya to make it a "lesson" by causing "destruction and maximum killing". 69 Arabs were killed, mostly women and children. This became known as the Qibya Massacre.[^520]
- ![[Pasted image 20250209004114.png]][^430]
- There was tremendous international outcry.[^520]
- Israel tried to cover it up and claim that this was private citizens acting on their own.[^520]
- From this misstep, Israel decided to only target military locations.[^520]
- Israeli Educational institutes opened their doors to Arabs beginning in 1953.[^521]
- Ben-Gurion retired in 1953. This was in large part due to the stress of running a coalition government. Whenever members would drop out of the coalition for one reason or another, he would have to try and find new members for his coalition, and figure out how to cater to their demands.[^521]
- Part of Ben-Gurion's legacy was:
	1. The establishment that different government institutions would remain independent of each other (like the courts were independent).[^521]
	2. Unifying the military.[^521]
	3. Making the democratic institutions (like the Knesset), primary.[^521]
- Moshe Sharett, the previous foreign affairs minister, became the new Prime Minister in 1954.[^521]
- Moshe Dayan (as appointed by Ben-Gurion right before retiring) became Chief of Staff.[^521]
- Pinhas Lavon became the Minister of Defense.[^521]
- In 1954, a bus was attacked by Arab gunmen from Jordan. The Jordanian government denied responsibility.[^522]
- Israel issued a statement but could do little else after the Qibya Massacre.[^522]
- The Lavon Affair:
- Jews in Egypt, were arrested and imprisoned or hanged for sabotage in Egypt.[^522]
- These claims were actually true. These people were Israeli operatives. They were supposed to make it seem that British agents sabotaged things in Egypt to cause the British to slow their withdrawal of troops from Egypt (which Israel viewed as a bad thing).[^522]
- It was not clear who authorized this mission, which was debated in the Knesset. This hurt Mapai and Lavon, the Minister of Defense, resigned.[^522]
- Ben-Gurion was then convinced to come out of retirement as the Minister of Defense. Four months later, he became Prime Minister again, with Sharett remaining Foreign Minister.[^522]
- Armed groups of Palestinians (called the fedayeen) attacked and killed Israelis by crossing the Egyptian border during the winter of 1954-1955.[^523]
- Israel responded by destroying an Egyptian military base, killing 38 Egyptians and 2 local Arabs.[^523]
- The fedayeen, responded by sheeling Israeli settlements near the border.[^523]
- Israel was worried about potential backlash from the attack. The original plan was to kill far fewer Egyptians.[^523]
- There was relative peace in Jerusalem between Israel and Jordan. Commanders from both sides would talk to each other about different issues that would come up.[^523]
- King Hussein of Jordan was unpopular in Jordan and the Jordanians pushed for independence, particularly in the West Bank.[^523]
- Egypt was pro Jordanian independence and so tried to help that side. Israel liked the status quo and tried to help the king stay in power.[^523]
- Inn April 1955, there was a meeting between many Asian and African countries at Bandung, Indonesia. Prime Minister Nasser of Egypt met a Russian government representative. This eventually led to Egypt's procurement of advanced Russian weapons.[^524]
- In response, Israel started buying advanced weapons from France.[^524]
- In July 1955 was the third election, with Mapai staying in power and Ben-Gurion staying as Prime Minister. Once again, he formed a coalition with the religious party (now called the National Religious Front) and without Cherut.[^524]
- Including the religious party was partly for political expediency and partly because Ben-Gurion appreciated the significance of religion.[^524]
- Israel established its first nuclear reactor in 1956.[^524]
- In late August, Israel launched a raid on the Gaza strip.[^524]
- In response, there were numerous fedayeen attacks on Israel, on civilian and military targets.[^524]
- Rabbi Yitzhak Nissim was appointed as the Sephardi Chief Rabbi of Israel in 1955.[^525]
- He tried to bridge divides between the Orthodox and non-Orthodox by visiting various left-wing (largely Ashkenaz) kibbutzim.[^525]
- America had attempted to stem the growth of Russian influence by instituting the Baghdad Pact, whereby Turkey, Iraq, Pakistan, and Iran aligned themselves with the American defensive sphere.[^525]
- the Soviet Union attempted to avoid this block and shipped weapons to Egypt through Czechoslovakia.[^525]
- Initially, at the creation of the State of Israel, the Soviet Union tried to establish relations with Israel, but then they decided to take the Arab side of the Arab-Israeli conflict.[^525]
- Israel tried to establish relations with the US and create a defensive pact, but the US was not willing to do this, as they did not want to alienate the Arabic countries but told Israel that "the US will not abandon her".[^525]
- In October 1955, Egyptians crossed into the Israeli border and Israel launched a reprisal attack against an Egyptian military base in Kuntilla, killing 10.[^525]
- In response, Arabs crossed the border and killed 5 Israeli schoolchildren and their teacher in Kfar Chabad.[^525]
- In December 1955, Syrians opened fire on an Israeli patrol boat in the Jordan (this was a regular occurrence).[^526]
- In response, Israel attacked fortifications near the village of Nukeib. 6 Israelis and 43 Syrians were killed in the operation.[^526]
- ![[Pasted image 20250210141340.png]][^527]
- The French government "deplored" Israel's actions.[^526]
- Beginning in January, 1956, secret peace talks brokered by the US began between the Israel and Egypt.[^528]
- Nasser would allow Israel to use the Suez Canal, and wanted to work out a way to divide Jerusalem. He also accepted that Ben-Gurion did not return to government to cause issues.[^528]
- However, he worried that he was not able to contain the anger of the displaced Palestinians, who would keep attacking Israel. He felt that they needed to be dispersed and settled, but he was not willing to do that in Egypt.[^528]
- He also thought that the general public in Egypt would not be willing to accept peace with Israel, either. This was especially true because Egypt was getting stronger and Syria and Saudi Arabia were threating to fight Israel. In a short amount of time, they could unite a defeat Israel, which was something the public wanted.[^528]
- Ben-Gurion was extremely hopeful for peace as that was one of the main goals. However, he wasn't sure if Nasser was actually being serious.[^528]
- Nasser demanded that a chunk of the Negev until a few miles south of Beersheva be returned to Egypt. Ben-Gurion concluded from here that Nasser was not actually serious.[^528]
- Fedayeen attacks continues from Gaza.[^529]
- The UN instituted a ceasefire but it only lasted a few days. They didn't further pursue it after that.[^529]
- In April, 1955, Egyptian artillery opened fire on some Israeli settlements near the border. Six Israelis were injured and three killed.[^530]
- Israel responded by trying to destroy the artillery pieces with artillery of their own. This led to the death of 66 Egyptian civilians.[^530]
- Egypt then launched more artillery, but this did not injure anyone.[^530]
- (It seems from Israel: a History,[^530] that the Israelis were relatively safe because they had shelters in their basements.)
- America and Britain had promised to give funds for the construction of the Aswan Dam, which would generate electricity from the Nile River. However, after making some moves which seemed to befriend the Soviets, the US and Britain backed out.[^531]
- The Suez Canal was operated by British and French companies. In July, 1956, President Nasser nationalized it, meaning he took it over for Egyptian interests. He started collecting the taxes that these companies collected to help pay for the Aswan Dam.[^532][^530]
- Egypt then closed the canal to boats going to Israel, blockaded the Strait of Tiran (at the base of the Gulf of Aqaba), closing off Eilat, and economically boycotted Israel.[^530]
- ![[Pasted image 20250210233337.png]][^533]
- Egypt was getting more heavily armed by the soviets and Nasser started giving violent speeches calling for the eradication of Israel.[^530]
- Britain and France wanted to take back the straits with military action and in September, decided to bring Israel in on the mission.[^530]
- During this time, Israel beefed up their weapons imports from France to keep up with the Egyptians.[^530]
- During this time, their continued to be fedayeen attacks and Israeli reprisals.[^530]
- In September, there were multiple attacks from Jordanians on Israeli civilians (a group of archeologists and a group of women picking olives), killing 6.[^530]
- In September, Israel launched a reprisal attack against a police fort in the village of Husan, killing 37 soldiers and 2 civilians, and losing 10 soldiers.[^530]
- In response, killed another 5 Israeli civilians.[^530]
- In October, Jordanians killed and mutilated 2 Israelis. In response, Israel launched another reprisal attack against the Kalkilya police fort, killing more than policemen and soldiers (civilians aren't mentioned so it seems to be few) and lost 18.[^530]
- The reprisal raids seemed to be ineffective and Israel was losing soldiers to them (more were dying in reprisal raids than from the initial incidents that prompted the raids), so Israel decided to try something else.[^530]
- In response to attacks on them, Jordan called upon Britain and Iraq to honor the treaties that they had with Jordan and send troops in response to the Israeli attacks.[^534]
- Britain told Israel to not launch any more attacks or they would have to do as the Jordanians said.[^534]
- Iraq did send troops. Israel starting readying itself for a confrontation.[^534]
- In September and October, members of the Israeli, French and British government met in secret in Sevres, France, to discuss how to get the Suez Canal from the Egyptians (later called the Sevres Protocol).[^534]
- The French were having trouble with their colonies in Arab lands, who looked up to Nasser as a pan-Arab unifying figure.[^534] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Not sure if I understood this correctly. Need to look more into this.</span>}
- The British were worried that Egypt would block them off from using the canal.[^534] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Not sure if this is correct. Need source.</span>}
- This made things in Israel somewhat interesting. There were possibilities for Israel to be fighting against Egypt with Britain and France on Israel's side, or against Jordan and Iraq, with Britain supporting Jordan.[^534]
- During this time, Egypt starting massing troops in Gaza and the Sinai. According to Shimon Peres, Israelis felt that an attack was imminent.[^534]
- The plan to retake the canal was for Israel to launch an attack and "threaten" the canal, "forcing" Britain and France to step in to defend it.[^534]
- Israel was nervous as they did not want to upset the US, who was trying to work out a diplomatic solution to this problem.[^534]
- Israel also wanted to use this as an opportunity to take the Egyptian city of Sharm El-Sheik to open the Straits of Tiran.[^534]{<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Link to map of Straits of Tiran</span>}
- Israel began their attack in the Sinai on October 29.[^534]
- Israel started the campaign by destroying Egyptian communication and by dropping paratroopers near the Mitla Pass, 156 miles into the Sinai and only 45 miles from the canal. This attack took the Egyptians by surprise.[^535]
- ![[Pasted image 20250211091220.png]][^536]
- The next day, Britain issued an ultimatum to "both sides" to back off 30 miles from the canal (only Egypt was within 30 miles). Egypt refused.[^535]
- The US did not condemn Israel as Israel said they were doing to stop the fedayeen attacks, the blockade against Israel in the canal and the Straits of Tiran, the boycott, and the Arab military alliance against Israel.[^535]
- Aon October 31, an Egyptian ship (the Ibrahim Al Awwal) started shelling Haifa. The ship was driven off and later captured.[^535]
- France and Britain started bombing Egyptian Airfields.[^535]
- Ariel Sharon (who was commanding the paratroopers), asked to send a patrol in the Mitla Pass. The "patrol" he sent was fairly large and got tied down. He then had to send his remaining force in to free them.[^535]
- There was much backlash over this incident later, as the Mitla Pass was well defended and not necessary to the plan.[^535]
- Sharon was a great soldier with great battlefield instincts, but he would use deceit and wouldn't listen to orders well. People say that this is perhaps why he never became Chief of Staff of the armed forces.[^535]
- Between October 31 and November 2, Israel attacked and conquered Egyptian forces at Abu Ageilah, Rafah, and El-Arish, cutting off Gaza.[^535]
- ![[Pasted image 20250211092527.png]][^537]
- During this time, fedayeen attacks continued. However, there were almost no attacks from Jordan.[^535]
- Syria decided to join in with Egypt.[^538]
- Egypt launched bombing raids on Israeli cities, but they largely missed. Pilots were nervous to fly where they might get shot at.[^538]
- The Egyptian radio would talk about Egyptian "victories" and "successes", even when they lost.[^538]
- Israel captured many advanced Soviet tanks and other vehicles.[^538]
- Israel was able to push its way into Gaza. On November 2, the Egyptian governor of Gaza surrendered.[^538]
- The UN declared a ceasefire, which Israel accepted on November 3.[^538]
- Israel discovered that Egyptian soldiers carried Mein Kampf as part of their "standard gear".[^538]
- Israel sent an army down the west coast of the Guld of Aqaba. This met up with Ariel Sharon's paratroopers.[^538]
- On November 4, they were able to conquer the Egyptian force at Sharm el-Sheik, taking down the guns their overlooking the straits, which were largely the cause for the blockade.[^538] (I don't know what happened with the ceasefire, unless that only applied to the Gaza region?)
- On November 5, France and Britain landed troops and started taking over the Suez Canal. However, due to American pressure and Soviet threats, Britain, France, and Israel were forces to stop.[^539]
- ![[Pasted image 20250211145924.png]][^540]
- The American government condemned the attack and much of world opinion was against Israel.[^539]
- Golda Meir told the UN General Assembly that it was unfair that the fedayeen enjoyed "belligerent status" and "the rights of war", while Israel was forced to keep the peace.[^539]
- Israel agreed to withdraw on November 14, although the withdrawal did not happen until March. There was much disagreement about this decision in the government, particularly from Cherut.[^539]
- In exchange, a UN force was sent down to ensure that Gaza could not be used for fedayeen attacks against Israel and the straits and the canal were opened to Israel.[^539]
-  Egypt was allowed to return its military to Gaza, against Israeli wishes, even though Gaza was originally supposed to be independent under the mandate.[^539]
- The perception of Israel's strength changed dramatically because of this war.[^539]
- Israel strengthened its relationship with Britain, which had been frayed since the 1939 White Papers.[^541]
- During this time, Palestinian refugees were kept in terrible conditions in camps, to keep up the PR against Israel. Israel had managed to incorporate the refugees it had received into Israeli society.[^542]
- Egypt had also put back the big guns and fortress and Sharm El-Sheik.[^542]
- Egypt got back all of the land it lost.[^542]
- However, the blockades did stop and fedayeen raids stopped for the next 10 years.[^542]
- Israel established relations with many newly formed African and Asian countries, such as Ghana, Mali, Upper Volta, Madagascar, and Dahomey. Israel sent down agricultural and military experts, set up agricultural agreements, and helped build infrastructure.[^543]
- There was peace in Israel after the Suez crisis, but there was even more dislike for the Jews in the Arab world. 125,000 Jews immigrated to Israel, mostly from Egypt and North Africa.[^543]
- In January 1960, Syria started shelling settlements near the Golan Heights. Israel beefed up the military in the area.[^543]
- Syria and Egypt, who were still working together and now called themselves the United Arab Republic, mobilized their troops to their borders.[^543]
- Israeli reconnaissance did not catch the Egyptian mobilization for some time. When it was discovered, Israel mobilized troops to go down south.[^543]
- There was a standoff for some time, until things eventually calmed down.[^543]
- In 1960, Germany promised to keep giving financial assistance to Israel, even after the reparations were paid, as well as sell arms.[^544]
- There was discussion about having a "national spirituality", a "spiritual Zionism". Some people were fans of this.[^544]
- In 1960, Adolf Eichmann was captured and extricated from where he lived in Argentina. There was much rejoicing in Israel over this.[^544]
- His trial lasted from 1961 until 1962, where he was found guilty and hanged. He is the only person who has ever received the death penalty in Israel.[^544]
- The trial and testimonies of many survivors connected the youth with the holocaust.[^544]
- In 1960, new evidence came to light that Pinchas Lavon (the Lavon affair) was actually innocent. There were forged documents used in the prosecution.[^545]
- An inquiry was launched to look into the matter and ascertained that it was true.[^545]
- Despite this, Ben-Gurion wanted a full inquiry before acquitting Lavon.[^545]
- Lavon didn't like this and started leaking information to the public.[^545]
- To expedite the inquiry process, Levi Eshkol set up a committee to look into the matter and they to came out that Lavon was innocent.[^545]
- Still, Ben-Gurion was not satisfied. He felt that this was a matter of law, which required it to go to court. It was a matter of integrity and morality to him.[^545]
- His public behavior, though, was nasty, even to longtime friends, like Golda Meir. This made the public think that Ben-Gurion had a personal vendetta against Lavon.[^545]
- The more the public ridiculed Ben-Gurion, the worse and less articulate he became, leading to the public to be more convinced that this was personal.[^545]
- Ben-Gurion decided to resign from government over this.[^545]
-  In a secret meeting, members of government voted to fire Lavon from his role as the Secretary of the Histadrut, to appease Ben-Gurion. Ben-Gurion did return as Prime Minister, but he was still unhappy as he still felt that justice had not been served.[^545]
- An election was held and Mapai lost a few seats, but still had the most by far (42, to the Cherut's 17 (who were second most)). Mapai still refused to include Cherut and still worked with the religious party.[^545]
- Ben-Gurion lost many friends because of this.[^545]
- In 1961, Yigael Yadin and a team of archeologists found a letter addressed to Bar-Kochva, as well as many other items.[^546]
- This caused much excitement amongst even secular Israelis, who still had a connection to Judaism.[^546]
- Yigal Allon was Minister of Labor from 1961-1968.[^547]
- He improved infrastructure (like roads), improved labor courts (which dealt with disputes between employees and employers) and helped create factories.[^547]
- Political parties kept changing. In 1961, a new party, called the Liberal Party, was formed from merging the General Zionist and Progressive parties. They were for private economies and small government involvement.[^547]
- This lined them up with Cherut (not exactly sure why although I see why they are both on the right) who they eventually merged with, forming the Gachal Block (although it seems that they remained unique parties).[^547]
- The Progressives broke away and recreated their own party.[^547]
- This Liberal party joined the Likud party in 1977.[^547]
- A census was held in May 1961. There were over 1.9 million Jews and almost 250,000 non-Jews.[^548]
- Arab villages were mostly in the Galilee and the "Triangle". Many Arabs lived in villages that were part Jewish, part Arab, like Haifa, Jaffa, and Lod.[^548]
- ![[Pasted image 20250211165940.png]][^549]
- Israeli Arabs did not serve in the army, although Druse, Circassians, and Bedouin did.[^548]
- There were numerous regulations against Arabs:
	- Regulation 109: Permitted arrests of people in forbidden areas.[^548]
	- Regulation 110: Permitted police supervision over people for a year.[^548]
	- Regulation 111: Permitted military commanders to put people in administrative detention.[^548]
	- Regulation 124: Permitted house arrest.[^548]
	- Regulation 125: Permitted military commanders to close certain areas.[^548]
- Over time, these were reduced. In 1962, these were removed from Druse, and in 1966, they were totally removed (although they could be reenacted by head of the military).[^548]
- The Left was generally anti these regulations as they felt it was undemocratic, although Ben-Gurion was for them. Levi Eshkol, when he was Prime Minister, relaxed them.[^548]
- Various educational and other facilities were opened for Arabs in 1962. In addition, there were numerous Arab political parties to vote for in the 1962 election.[^548]
- Near the end of 1962, Ben-Gurion reached out to President Tito of Yugoslavia to help broker peace between Israel and its neighbors. Tito eventually responded in April 1963 that he would not.[^550]
- A few days later, Egypt, Syria, and Iraq signed a treaty to free Palestine. Jordan, notably, did not sign.[^550]
- Israel and Jordan engaged in talks that had some success.[^550]
- In response to the Soviet Union, its allies, and the Muslim countries, Israel attempted to make a close connection with the US which flourished under President Kennedy. The first weapons from the US were sent to Israel under President Kennedy.[^550]
- Egypt started developing missiles, with the help of a few German scientist.[^551]
- Israel protested to Germany that they stop this. The head of the German Parliament assured Ben-Gurion that the perception of German involvement was overblown (which was the truth).[^551]
- As such, Ben-Gurion decided to do nothing, especially considering the flourishing Israel-German relations. There was much backlash from the political right, who wanted Ben-Gurion to take a harder stance against Germany.[^551]
- Despite this, the Mossad independently decided to threaten and kill the suspected German scientists, called Operation Damocles.[^551][^552]
- The plot was exposed when Mossad agents threatened the daughter of one of the scientists in Switzerland. She reported this to the local police, who arrested the agents.[^551]
- This was considered an international scandal for Israel. Israel publicly denied these claims, saying that they only used "peaceful methods".[^552]
- (Personally, I don't see what was wrong with what Israel did. I don't see why they don't have a right to kill weapons scientists for an obvious hostile enemy. Perhaps they shouldn't have threatened relatives of the scientists, but isn't that better than just killing the scientists outright?)
- The head of the Mossad, Isser Harel, was forced to resign.[^551]
- Despite the issues for Israel, the German scientists left Egypt and Egyptian missile development fell apart.[^553]
- In June 1963, Ben-Gurion resigned from being the Prime Minister. He was replaced by Levi Eshkol.[^551]
- In January 1964, Syria suggested that displaced Palestinians be allowed to come together and take back their homeland.[^551]
- Jordan allowed this new group, called the Palestine Liberation Organization (PLO), to meet in East Jerusalem. Egypt allowed the PLO to use the Sinai and Gaza.[^551]
- The goal of the PLO was to "attain the objective of liquidating Israel", as it says in its founding manifesto. To accomplish this, they created the Palestine Liberation Army.[^551]
- The PLO was to receive funding from the surrounding Arab countries.[^551]
- In 1964, Syria tried to divert the Hasbani River. This river was one of the tributaries that led to the Jordan. Diverting it would harm Israel's water supply.[^551]
- There was discussion amongst Israeli political leaders about what to do. Eventually, Syria solved the problem by attacking Israeli civilians and soldiers near the Golan Heights, to which Israel responded.[^551]
- Despite these skirmishes, the construction continued.[^551]
- Israel did not want to cross the border to deal with this. Instead they trained soldiers and experimented with different firing techniques and munitions until eventually, Israeli tanks were able to hit the Syrian construction equipment at a distance of 2.5 miles, causing them to stop.[^551]
- Syria wanted to retaliate, but Egypt convinced them to bide their time.[^551]
- In January 1965, the Movement for the National Liberation of Palestine (Fatah, meaning "victory") was created by Abu Ammar, who would later be known as Yasser Arafat.[^551]
- Fatah commandos snuck into Israel to commit terror attacks. At first, Syria did not allow them to sneak in from their border as they were nervous about an Israeli response.[^551]
- In May 1965, Eli Cohen, an Israeli spy, was hanged in Syria.[^554]
- Because of his espionage, Syria broke off relations with West Germany because they were friendly with Israel. There was also increased anti-Israel sentiment.[^554]
- Fatah continues to launch attacks on Israel. In May, Israel responded by sending in paratroopers to destroy Fatah bases to deter Fatah.[^554]
- This was ineffective. 2 days later, Fatah planted bombs in Israel (although they did not kill anyone).[^554]
- Ben-Gurion, from his retirement, brought up the Lavon affair once more. He wanted a full investigation into who had given the original order for the mission.[^554]
- Eshkol, as well as others, wanted this to remain behind them and pushed against this. The matter went to a vote. The decision was made to not look further into the matter.[^554]
- In anger, Ben-Gurion left Mapai and formed his own party, called רְשִׁימָת פּוֹעַלֵי יִשְׂרָאֵל (Rafi or רַפִ"י). Joining him were Moshe Dayan, Shimon Peres, Chaim Herzog, and Teddy Kollek, as well as others.[^554]
- This was a left-wing party which ran on the idea of politics without the infighting and self-interest.[^554]
- Mapai merged with another left-wing party called Achdut Haavoda to form the המערך לאחדות פועלי ארץ ישראל, Alignment for the Unity of the Workers of the Land of Israel, or the Alignment.[^554]
- In the 1965 election, the Alignment had the most seats by far, with 45. Rafi, which hoped to challenge the Alignment, only mustered 10 seats. The name Ben-Gurion, which Rafi hoped to ride to victory, did not carry the same power that it once did.[^554]
- The situation for the Bedouin in the Negev was not good. Those that fled during the War of Independence were not allowed back, and even those in Israel were shuffled around the Negev.[^555]
- Israel offered money in exchange for the land, but the Bedouin considered it to be less than the actual value of the land.[^555]
- In addition, the laws against the Arabs, which mostly ended in 1963, landed until 1965 in the Negev.[^555]
- Despite this, many Bedouin have joined the Israeli military.[^555]
- In the 1990s, Israel tried forcing the Bedouin into cities by banning goat grazing, the Bedouins' main source of livelihood. Eventually, the Bedouin were able to pressure the Israeli government to rescind the law.[^555] (I don't know what this is doing here in the book.)
- Israel's biggest ally in 1966 was France, from whom it bought much of its air force. However, relations with Britain started to warm up, and relations with the US had already started improving a few years earlier.[^556]
- In 1966, there were 17 Fatah raids into Israel. (There may have been other raids from Arabs not in Fatah. The book didn't exactly say.) These killed military personal and civilians.[^556]
- Israel responded with some reprisal attacks on Fatah and Syrian sites.[^556]
- Israel also appealed to the Syrian government and the UN, but neither of them said anything.
- The Arab governments united together. They declared that if Israel would attack any one of them, they would all get involved. The Soviet Union said it would back them up.[^556]
- Israel wanted to launch an real attack on the Arab countries. They were scared of Syria's military, so they decided to attack Jordan.[^556]
- In November, Israeli tanks rolled into Jordan to blow up buildings in Samu, from which Fatah attacks had come. On loudspeakers, they told the inhabitants to leave, which they did, before beginning to blow up the buildings.[^556]
- While they were doing this, a Jordanian brigade showed up and started fighting the Israelis. Both sides then brought in their air force to help.[^556]
- There is much disagreement about the casualties and destruction, ranging from 15 soldiers and 3 civilians killed, to "dozens" of soldiers killed, and from 41 houses, to 125 houses and other structures, including a school.[^557] One Israeli soldier was killed.
- The reactions to this were:
	- Jordanians were more set against Israel (instead of being cowed).[^557]
	- Distanced King Hussein from Israel (and he was the most moderate ruler of the Arabic countries in the area).[^557]
	- Jordanians were upset at their king for not doing more to stop this.[^557]
	- Egypt was upset at Jordan for having such incompetence. This would lead to Egypt bringing officers to Jordan to train their army.[^557]
- The UN finally got involved and condemned the Samu Raid.[^556]
- ![[Pasted image 20250212163331.png]][^430]
- Other Arab countries expressed remorse at not having come to Jordans defense.[^556]
- Syria realized from this (and from relatively weak retaliation from Israel to later raids) that Israel was scared of taking on Syria.[^556]
- For the first few months of 1967, Syria bombarded Israeli settlements in the north with artillery from the Golan. Israel would respond with air attacks on these positions.[^558]
- In early April, Israel sent tractors into a demilitarized zone to cultivate the land there. They had the military ready as they thought there may be some resistance to this.[^559]
- The Syrians started firing artillery at the tractors. Israel responded by firing from tanks and artillery of their own on these positions.[^559]
- Things eventually escalated to an air battle. 6 Syrian planes were shot down in view of the Syrian populace in Damascus.[^559]
- This further increased tensions.[^559]
- The Arab countries and the Soviet Union claimed that Israel was preparing for war. In particular, the Soviets sent Egypt a report on massing Israeli troops near Syria. This report was false. (This will be discussed a little more later.)[^558]
- Fatah continued their raids, now crossing in from Syria.[^558]
- On May 13, Nasser (who was emerging as a leader of pan-Arabism) started massing the Egyptian army in the Sinai, from where many would head to Gaza.[^558]
- Egyptian radio blared out how Egypt was ready to "plunge into a total war that will end Israel".[^558]
- At the Israeli day parade, they did not display their weaponry to try and keep things a bit calm. However, this backfired. Israel was accused of not having military vehicles at the parade because they were massing them on the border for an invasion of Syria.[^558]
- On May 16, Egypt demanded that the UN Emergency Force (UNEF) who were in Sharm El-Sheik and the Gaza Strip to keep the peace, should leave (as they were on Egyptian land, only with Egyptian permission).[^558]
- Both Israel and Egypt thought that the commander would run this decision by the UN, but he did not. He decided to leave, which he largely did by May 20.[^558]
- UNEF was only permitted to be in the territory of a country if given permission. As such, when Egypt pulled their permission, they had to leave Egypt.[^560]
- There was a lot of criticism of the decision to leave, but it seems that legally, and practically (UNEF couldn't take on the Egyptian) there was no other option.[^561]
- The commander of UNEF asked Israel if they could have permission to station in Israel. Israel refused,[^562] as it had done since the the beginning of their deployment in 1956.[^563] Israel didn't want UNEF in its territory as it felt that UNEF would potentially monitor and hinder its troop movements.[^564]
- On that day, Israel called up their reserves.[^558]
- Israeli leaders met to talk about what they should do. They were hopeful that maybe the Arabs wouldn't attack. They decided that if Nasser would close the Straits of Tiran again, that would mean that he meant business.[^558]
- On May 21, Levi Eshkol gave a speech where he discussed Israel's desire not to go to war. During the speech, he fumbled some words. Nasser took both of these as a sign of Israel's weakness and unpreparedness to fight.[^558]
- On May 22, Egypt blockaded the Straights of Tiran.[^558] This was a big blow to Israel as 90% of its oil came through Eilat, which was now blocked off, causing delays in oil delivery.[^565]
- The next day, senior Israeli officials met.[^566]
- Egypt had gathered its forces in the Sinai, but Jordan and Syria had not yet moved their troops.[^566]
- The Arabs populations of all three countries were in the streets pushing for war.[^566]
- Israeli officials were divided if they would be able to win a way. Rabin thought they could, but there would be high casualties.[^566]
- The US told Israel not to do anything for 2 days until diplomacy was tried. Israel thought they didn't have anything to lose by waiting, so they agreed.[^566]
- Israel declared to the world that they viewed the closure of the Straits of Tiran as an act of war.[^566]
- The Secretary-General of the UN flew to Egypt to intervene. That failed.[^566]
- An Israeli official flew to France. The president him that Israel should make sure not to be the first to shoot. He didn't view the closing of the straights as an act of war.[^566]
- Britain and the US were more on Israel's side. They wanted to force Egypt to open the straights and were even prepared to drive warships through to secure this.[^566]
- In a meeting on May 26, the US implored Israel to wait while America would try and arrange this international flotilla.[^566]
- Nasser kept giving even more incendiary speeches where he called for the destruction of Israel.[^566]
- The Soviet Union backed them up and as a member of the security council, made it known that they would veto anything against Egypt and Syria.[^566]
- By May 28, it seemed that America was potentially going to be able to put this international task force together. Canada and the Netherlands were on board. As such, Israel decided to keep waiting.[^566]
- In addition, Israel didn't want to go against the US and hurt their relationship.
- Many Israeli civilians (around 24%) were not in favor of the waiting. Distrust of the government was mounting, especially for the families of the men called up for reserve duty.[^566]
- Egypt and Jordan were, in general before this time, not getting along. But on May 30, Jordan and Egypt signed a defense pact.[^566]
- Troops from Iraq started coming in at this time, as well.[^566] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I am confused where they went. On page 377 it says they went to Egypt but on page 380 it says they went to Jordan.</span>}
- The international flotilla was not proceeding. America had asked 80 nations but only Canada and Denmark were willing to join. As such, America started relenting on restraining Israel.[^566]
- Israel established a national unity government, meaning, the coalition brought everyone on, including Cherut and Menachen Begin, and the Rafi party. This allowed Moshe Dayan (a former Chief of Staff) to be put in a position of leadership.[^566]
- Israel had 2 plans: one to take Gaza and then "trade" that to Egypt for peace, and another to go take the whole Sinai. Israel, chose the second.[^566]
- On June 2, Nasser threatened that any Great Power that would get involved would be banned from using the Suez Canal and they would  not longer receive Arab oil.[^566]
- According to Martin Gilbert:
	- Arab armies:
		- Egypt: 100,000 soldiers and 900 in the Sinai, with another 104,000 in the west Sanai, ready to go.[^566]
		- Syria: 75,000 soldiers and 400 tanks on the Golan Heights.[^566]
		- Jordan: 32,000 soldiers and 300 tanks, with another 150 tanks from Iraq.[^566]
		- Total: 311,00 soldiers and 1,750 tanks, and 700 aircraft.[^566]
	- Israel: 264,000 soldiers, 800 tanks and 300 aircraft.[^566]
- According to Michael Carrol:
	- Total Arab army: 643,000 soldiers, 2700 tanks, and 1090 aircraft.[^567]
	- Israel: 300,000 soldiers (mostly reservists) 800 tanks, and around 400 aircraft.[^567]
- There is much disagreement about the likelihood of an Israeli victory. Martin Gilbert says that Israel thought it could win, but it would come at a tremendous cost.[^566] US intelligence thought that Israel could "maintain internal security, defend successfully against simultaneous Arab attacks on all fronts, launch limited attacks simultaneously on all fronts, or hold on any three fronts while mounting successfully a major offensive on the fourth."[^567]
- By June 3, it was clear that the US task force was not going to come together, and that the US would not be against Israeli action.[^566]
- Egypt wanted Israel to attack first, and then respond with their own attack.[^566]
- On June 4, Israel decided that they were going to launch the attack. The exact decision of when and what was made between Eshkol and Dayan alone. They decided to launch an attack on June 5 at 7:45 am.[^566]
- Israel famously took out 180 Egyptian planes, mostly on the ground, and took out runways and equipment.[^568]
- This attack was a tremendous gamble. Israel only left 12 planes behind to defend its own airspace.[^568]
- Jordan had launched some artillery fire, but Israel told them that if they stopped, Israel would not invade their territory.[^568]
- Nasser called King Hussein and told him that most of Israel's air force had been destroyed and pressured him to take advantage of the situation. King Hussein agreed.[^568]
- Iraq, Syria, and Jordan launched their own planes against Israel. Most of these were shot down and Israel was able to destroy many Jordanian and Iraqi planes on the ground.[^568]
- In total, 400 Arab planes were destroyed. Although there were another 280 planes, they were not a factor in the war.[^568] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know why. Maybe because runways were destroyed?</span>}
- This destroyed Arab moral and strengthened Israeli moral.[^568]
- Israel decided at first not to attack in the Old City but Jordanians kept firing into the area controlled by the Jews.[^568]
- As such, Israel launched a pincer attack to cut off Jerusalem from Jordan. The northern force took French Hill and Ammunition Hill and the southern force took the government house. The Israeli air force kept Jordan from reinforcing. The Jordanians fought valiantly but the Israelis were able to overrun them.[^568]
- In the Sinai, Israel took Abu Ageilah and El Arish, cutting off the Gaza Strip and threatening the whole Sinai peninsula.[^568]
- Many pushed Moshe Dayan to push the Syrians off of the Golan Heights, but he refused to, as they had not launched attacks from there. He didn't want to start wars on multiple fronts and he was afraid that Russia would get involved.[^568]
- He felt that the most important task was getting to Sharm El-Sheik to end the blockade. The Soviet Union was calling for a ceasefire and he didn't want that to happen before Sharm El-Sheik was taken.[^568]
- Israel put together a force to take Sharm El-Sheik on June 7. However, Egypt had already begun falling back to the Suez Canal. Israel took Sharm El-Sheik without a fight.[^568]
- On June 7, Israel decided to go into the Old City and take it, taking back the Jewish Quarter[^568] and the Hebrew University on Mount Scopus, Lost in 1948.[^569]
- There was a scare in Israel for some time. They attacked a ship they had originally thought was Egyptian, but then they were led to believe was actually Soviet. They were concerned that the Soviet Union was getting involved.[^569]
- It turned out to be American and they were willing to overlook the incident.[^569]
- The UN ordered a ceasefire to begin at 10 AM on June 7. Nasser refused to accept this. He felt that the terms of the ceasefire should include a return of the Sinai to Egypt. He fought for another day until it was clear that his forces were not going to be able to reconquer it. By this point, Israeli forces were at the Suez Canal.[^569]
- In all, 15,000 Egyptian soldiers were killed, the West Bank (including East Jerusalem) and the Sinai were in Israeli control.[^569]
- Syria, Egypt, and Jordan all were willing[^569]
- On June 9, Israel decided to attack the Golan Heights to drive off the Syrians who had launched many attacks on villages in the north from there. The Syrians fought hard for that day, but then starting pulling back that night to defend Damascus as they were scared that Israel would continue there (which was only 40 miles away. Israel had no such plans).[^569]
- Syria had refused to allow their Jewish population to emigrate out of the country.[^569]
- On June 10, Israel was able to take the city of Quneitra (Kuneitra), the biggest city in the area. The civilian population and military had abandoned it.[^569]
- ![[Pasted image 20250213150749.png]][^570]
- The ceasefire began at 6:30 on June 10.[^569]
- Israel had to deal with the massive amount of land that it gained (Israel tripled its size) and the one million Arabs that lived there.[^569]
- Israel had lost 777 troops; far less than had been anticipated.[^569]
- Decision making for Egypt was divided between Nasser and Field Marshal Amer (the military was largely independent of the rest of the government), with Nasser being more in charge.[^571]
- There was a also the Supreme Executive Committee of the Arab Socialist Union which played a part. There was a parliament as well, but they didn't convene after May 14.[^571]
- Nasser valued advice from Foreign Minister Mahmoud Riad, but the Foreign Ministry was not listened to very much.[^571]
- Nasser viewed the US, Britain, and Israel as his enemies, as he viewed them as imperialists. His main enemy was the US.[^572]
- This was particularly true during Lyndon Johnson's presidency, due to US arms sales to Israel and because (he perceived) the US took advantage of Egypt's economic crisis in 1965. This was also thought that the US was behind worldwide events and the reason for their interventions. He also thought that the US had plans to assassinate him, although the US almost certainly did not.[^572]
- He did not believe that the US would get involved as they were dealing with the fallout of the Vietnam War.[^572]
- Nasser, in public and private discussions hated Israel. One reason was because he thought they wanted to expand.[^573]
- Egypt's military expressed in private and public that they could defeat Israel (potentially, quite easily).[^573]
- In truth, Egypt had fallen behind in the arms race with Israel since their economic recession in 1965.[^573]
- Israeli intelligence knew that Egypt needed more time to rearm.[^573]
- It is not clear what Nasser thought about Egypt's military strength against Israel. Some statements (private and public) imply Egypt was more powerful. Others imply Israel was more powerful.[^573]
- A way to explain the contradictory statements is that Nasser thought that the Arabs, when divided, were weaker than Israel when it had Western support (many believed Israel only won in the 1956 Sinai Campaign because of British and French support). However, if the Arabs were united and Israel was alone, the Arabs would be able to easily win.[^573]
- Egypt was informed by the Soviets in May 13 that Israel was massing troops on the Syrian border. This report was particularly compelling because of:
	- Its level of detail.[^574]
	- Israel had less troops by its Independence Day parade.[^574]
	- This was conveyed through multiple channels from the Soviets.[^574]
- Egypt knew by May 16 that this report was false (they sent their own people to investigate) but by then they had already ordered troops to the Sinai. Recalling them would have looked foolish.[^574]
- These troops were to dissuade Israel from aggressing Syria, as is apparent from the fact that they took defensive positions, and because the troops were proudly displayed, rather than snuck into position. A similar actions was done by Egypt when tensions between Israel and Syria were raised in 1960.[^574]
- At this point, Nasser did not think that war was likely.[^574]
- Egypt continued to build troops in the Sinai after this point, despite the lack of Israeli buildup, because they still thought that Israel was going to try something because of:
	- Perceptions they already had of Israel.
	- Aggressive statements by Israeli officials.[^574]
	- They thought the Israel-US relationship was strengthening and the US would direct Israel to attack.[^574]
- Egypt also thought that they Soviets would support them.[^574]
- Nasser may not have initially wanted the UNEF troops to be fully expelled. He may have wanted them to remain in Sharm El-Sheik, but there was a mistranslation in the letter given to the UNEF secretary. Once this happened, though, he decided to go with it. This is indicated by:
	- Nasser told U Than (UNEF secretary general) not to appeal UNEF's decision to leave to the UN General Assembly.[^575]
	- General Fawzi (who was a friend of Nasser and working for him), told the UN on May 17 to pull their troops from Sharm El-Sheik.[^575]
- Amer seemed to want UNEF out as he suggested this previously and because he pushed to station his troops in Sharm El-Sheik even before the UN troops left.[^575]
- Nasser, Amer, and other members of the Supreme Executive Committee decided to close the Straights of Tiran on May 22 to Israeli ships, and to strategic materials going to Israel.[^576]
- It is not clear if this meant that Egypt was pushing for war.[^576]
- Against:
	- Only 5 Israeli ships had gone through the Straights of Tiran.[^576]
	- On May 24, Nasser said that Egypt thought Israel did not consider the port in Eilat important.[^576]
- For:
	- This threatened Israeli oil and other imports from Asia and Africa.[^576]
	- Limited Israel's deterrent capabilities.[^576] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't know what this means. What could Israel do through the straights?</span>}
	- Israel had made it very clear that they considered this a reason to go to war.[^576]
	- Israel had gone to war over this same issue in 1956.[^576]
	- By May 19, Israel told a Soviet representative that Israel would not go to war if Egypt didn't blockade the straights.[^576]
	- According to some at the meeting deciding to close the port, Sadat said that this would mean war (later Sadat said that he thought it was between 50%-80%, at that time).[^576]
- Sadat may have closed the straights because of pressure from the Jordan and his own citizens.[^576]
- Some say that Sadat felt that he had to close the straights because he needed to put have soldiers in Sharm El-Sheik, and he had to put soldiers there because of the dismissal of the UNEF soldiers. (He definitely was not going to allow it to be undefended).[^576]
- However, just because there were soldiers there does not mean that they had to blockade the straight. Also, the decision to blockade the straight was made at a meeting of Egyptian leaders (including Sadat and Amer). As such, it seems that Nasser wanted to close the straights, despite knowing the risk.[^576]
- This seems to be because he thought that Egypt could take on Israel if it did not have help.[^576]
- He thought this because Eshkol sought peace in speeches and private channels. (Why would Israel seek peace if they could take Egypt on?)[^576]
- It seems that there was confusion in the Egyptian army about if there purpose was offensive or defensive. Amer may have had a plan to conquer the Negev, or at least Eilat and the surrounding area to connect Egypt with Jordan, but Nasser canceled them (hence, leading to the confusion).[^577]
- Nasser wanted Israel to attack first because to avoid negative political backlash. The military wanted to attack first but gave in to Nasser.[^577]
- Nasser did make some efforts (or appearance) of de-escalation. He was willing to put a two-week moratorium on the blockade and have the ICJ decide if it was legal.[^577]
- He also scheduled a date for the Egyptian vice-president to meet with the US.[^577]
- However, these were not major concessions. It seems that the was doing this to gain time to consolidate his army. In addition, Israel was not able to remain in a mobilized state for so long. Delaying helped Egypt and hurt Israel.[^577]
- Nasser also took some actions to increase tensions. He made a defense pact with Jordan on May 29, as well as used aggressive rhetoric. He did condition aggression on an Israeli attack first, but he also said that the displaced Palestinians needed to be fought for.[^577]
- Nasser gave many contradictory statements in late May and early June.[^577]
- He may have not really cared so much one way or the other. If no war came, it was a political victory and if there was a war, he believed there would be a military victory.[^577]
- At some point he thought that the Soviets would fight with him, but he soon after realized that this was not going to be the case. However, he did know that the Soviets would keep the US from getting involved.[^577]
- He may have thought that Israel was not going to get attack because of the fact that the US was not going to get involved and he viewed Israel as a tool of the US. However, it does seem that an Israeli attack was not ruled out.[^577]
- Egypt sent numerous flights over Israel without resistance. This indicated to Egypt that Israel was weak and didn't want to fight.[^577]
- The US told Israel not to attack. This was also interpreted as a sign of Israeli weakness.[^577]
- Eshkol stumbled over some words in one of his speeches, also indicating Israel's weakness (this was mentioned before).[^577]
- (It might make sense to put all of the indications of Israeli weakness together.)
- The US ambassador to Egypt thought that Nasser believed that Egypt could win against Israel without American interreference. Other foreign officials stationed in Egypt got the same impression that Egyptian leaders believed they would win against Israel.[^577]
- Some reasons why Israel decided to go to war:
	- The Egyptians were massing there forces and the longer they waited, the more entrenched they would be. This would increase Israeli casualties.[^578]
	- Also, the international community would force a ceasefire quickly (as actually happened). If Israel couldn't get through the Sinai quickly, they wouldn't be able to Sharm El-Sheik to get the straights open.[^578]
	- The Israeli economy suffered under extended mobilization.[^578]
	- The military thought that the Egyptians would attack first and, if they did, may be able to land a decisive blow against Israel. (The political part of the government went with the intelligence department's take that Egypt was much weaker than Israel, which was proven correct by the events of the war).[^578]
- The Israeli military pushed to attack. The other government ministers wanted diplomacy to try and play out, and to make sure that allies wouldn't be upset at them for attacking.[^578]
- The political part of the government wanted to wait and the military part wanted to attack. This created tremendous tension between the members of the different parts of the government.[^578]
- Eshkol made Dayan the Minister of Defense, something that he, as the Prime Minister, was until he gave it over.[^579]
- An arrangement was created so that the new minister of defense couldn't begin wars or take drastic actions on his own without consulting the Prime Minister and the non-military part of the government.[^579]
- However, Dayan did exactly this when he decided to attack the Syrians on June 9. On the evening of June 8, there was a government meeting where it was decided not to attack Syria (which Dayan himself was strongly supportive of at the meeting). The next day, Dayan went against this decision and ordered the attack.[^579]
- Dayan specified 3 goals for the forces in the Sinai:
	- Take the Gaza Strip.[^579]
	- Don't threaten the Suez Canal.[^579]
	- Take the Northern Sinai until El-Arish.[^579]
- There was debate about taking the strip. Some thought that there was no point. Dayan himself was worried that Israel would become responsible to deal with the refugees. However, the prevailing thought was that Gaza threatened the nearby villages.[^579]
- Dayan didn't want to threaten the Suez Canal because he was afraid that the Great Powers would not like this and force Israel out. In the end, Dayan changed his mind.[^579]
- Israel was willing to return the conquered lands from the Six Day War to the Arab countries in exchange for peace.[^580]
- After the war, Israel was ruling the West Bank. Dayan wanted to have a more hands off type of approach. There would be little Israeli interference and there wouldn't be Israeli secret police.[^580]
- This was in contrast to the way Israelis treated the Palestinians during the War of Independence. Arabs were not driven from their homes.[^580]
- This was also in contrast to the way that the Jordanians had treated the place. Under Jordan, the West Bank was a "second class" location and not developed.[^580]
- Israelis thought that they would be "kind occupiers".[^580]
- There was no timetable for how long the occupation would last.[^580]
- Israel allowed for the West Bank to appoint an Arab to be in charge of administration duties (named Hamdi Ka'anan), even though he was not pro-Zionist.[^580]
- However, Dayan didn't want to allow the West Bank to have full independence because he was afraid that the PLO would take charge. Other government leaders disagreed.[^580]
- In November 1967, the UN passed Resolution 242. This resolution called for:
	- Israel to withdraw from occupied territories.
	- Peace between all countries in the region.
	- A resolution to the refugee problem.
- The resolution did not say that Israel had to withdraw from "all", or "the" occupied territories. Technically, if Israel withdraws from *some* of the territory than it had fulfilled the first point.
- This point is contentious for numerous reasons.
	- The French translation does say "the" and potentially, the French translation carries legal weight as it is a UN "recognized language".[^581] However, the UN voted on the English language version, and this was proposed by Britain (an English-speaking country) so perhaps only the English version matters.[^582] In addition, this may have simply been a translation error.[^581]
	- Representatives of other countries, many not involved in the conflict, interpreted it to mean "all". However, that is their opinion and not what the resolution says.[^583]
	- The resolution also discussed the fact that a country can't gain land through war. In that context, it is argued that clearly the word "all" or "the" is implied.[^584]
	- Even without the word "all" or "the", it is clear that this is what it means. An example of this is in the following sentence: "Dogs must be kept on the lead near ponds in the park." The sentence clearly means *all* dogs, not *some* dogs. In addition, the resolution also calls for freedom of navigation through waterways without saying the word "the" or "all". According to the same reading, this would mean that Arabs only need to provide freedom of navigation through some waterways.[^585]
	- It clearly means all land because otherwise, that line in the resolution is meaningless.[^7]
	- Drafts that included the were "the" were rejected by the UN council.[^586]
- Israel and the Arab countries disagreed about whether lands should be returned as part of the negotiated peace, or if Israel would first withdraw and then peace would be negotiated.[^587]
- The Arabs in Israel voted for the Rakach party (רק"ח Reshima Komunistit Chadasha, meaning, "New Communist List") since they were pro-Arab. They wanted to return the West Bank immediately and viewed terrorist activities of the PLO as legitimate.[^588]
- The Land of Israel Movement was started in August, 1967. They didn't want to return any land at all. Begin and other right-wingers joined the movement.[^588]
- Nasser was going to resign after the war, but did not due to popular demand.[^588]
- The Soviet Union sent over new weapons to Egypt and people to train the Egyptian army.[^588]
- Egypt sunk an Israeli ship 13 off the Egyptian coast (which is international waters). In response, Israel destroyed oil refineries along the Suez Canal, forcing thousands of Egyptians to have to flee their homes. There were many other fights along the Suez.[^588]
- The fighting between Israel and Egypt (and the other Arab countries to a lesser extent) between 1967 and 1970 became known as the War of Attrition.[^589]
- In March 1969, Israel finished construction of fortifications along the Suez, called the Bar-Lev Line.[^588]
- Yehoshua was chosen as the leader for Klal Yisroel after Moshe because he put tremendous effort into his learning from when he was young and had a good reputation amongst people. Because of this, he was chosen over other great people alive at the time, such as Pinchas and Elazar.[^590]
- Din (meaning, monetary court cases) are extremely integral to the Torah. This is why they are discussed right after Matan Torah (in Parshas Mishpatim).[^591]
- As such, a judge has to spend time[^591] and put in much effort[^592] to make sure that they get the correct din. Some things may come up that the judge did not originally realize and only after thinking about it does it occur to him.[^593]
- A person has to keep teaching new talmidim. Even once someone gets older and they have already taught many talmidim, a person needs to teach more because even if the talmidim one has already taught are successful, one can't know if that will remain.[^594]
- The rabanan established gezeiros and takanos to make sure that people don't accidentally come to violate the halachos of the Torah. This is based off the passuk in Vayikra (18 30) וּשְׁמַרְתֶּ֣ם אֶת־מִשְׁמַרְתִּ֗י, which the gemara in Moed Katan learns to mean to create dirabanans to protect the Torah.[^595]
- I am not precisely sure what the difference about how to read the resolution (meaning, whether the resolution should be read as if it said "all" or "the" or not) makes. Either way, Arab countries were obviously not going to accept peace with Israel keeping most of the land, even if that satisfied the letter of the law (according to the Israeli reading) and it seems that Israel was willing to give up nearly all of the land in exchange for peace.[^7]
- Perhaps this made a difference because Israel wanted to make some small changes to the border. Reading the resolution Israel's way would give Israel more strength to stand on. Also, perhaps reading it Israel's way would allow Israel to deal with the other aspect that the Arabs were pushing, namely, that Israel withdraw first and then negotiate peace. Reading it Israel's way can allow Israel to claim that it satisfied the condition to withdraw from occupied territory and so now peace dealings can happen (even if within those peace dealings, Israel would give up the vast amount of the occupied land).[^7]
- Egypt and Syria responded to Israel with the Khartoum conference and the three 'no's: No peace, no negotiation, no recognition.[^596]
- According to Walter Eytan (an Israeli political leader) Israel invested in the infrastructure of the newly acquired territories and allowed for local elections of village and city councils.[^596]
- According to Eytan, the economies of Israel and Palestine flourished because of new trade between the two and Arab unemployment plummeted.[^596]
- Dayan allowed for Arabs of the West Bank to Travel to Jordan to sell their goods.[^596]
- The Palestinian Arabs were still strongly anti Israel.[^596]
- The Labor Party and the Histadrut became weaker during this time due to less creation of new settlements and a rise of businesses.[^596] I think this means that the Labor Party and the Histadrut pushed socialism (at least to some degree). This works well in small, tight-nit communities were everyone works together to take care of the community. Once people start going to big cities and creating their own companies, they become anti-socialist.
- In March 1968, a school bus was damaged by a land mine, killing two school children. In response, Israel attacked the Jordanian village of Karameh, where the Arabs who did the attack came from.[^597]
- In December, the PLO attacked an El-Al plane. In response, Israeli commandos blew up 13 planes on the ground in the Beirut airport.[^597]
- In April 1968, Jews started moving to Chevron. They created a village on the outskirts called Kiryat Arbah.[^597]
- Dayan liked the idea of creating Israeli villages between the Arab ones, to break up the continuity. (He was still opposed to setting up Jewish villages inside Arab ones as he was in favor of the hands off approach).[^597]
- Dayan wanted to incorporate the West Bank into Israel.[^597]
- Yigal Alon, the deputy Prime Minister wanted to only keep bits of the land that was taken for security purposes. He felt that if Israel were to stay true to its values and be democratic, it couldn't hold onto the massive Arab population in the areas it now occupied.[^597]
- The allon Plan called for Israeli control of:
	- In the West Bank:
		- The Jordan Valley and some area to its west (with a wide path around the area of Jericho to connect the West Bank with Jordan).[^597]
		- The new village of Kiryat Arbah.[^597]
		- East Jerusalem.[^597]
		- Gush Etzion.[^597]
		- ![[Pasted image 20250220022043.png]][^598]
	- In the Sinai:
		- The west bank of the Red Sea, down to Sharm El-Sheik.[^597]
		- Rafah and the surrounding area.[^597]
	- The Golan Heights:
		- Most of the Golan Heights.[^597]
- The West Bank would be connected to Jordan, and Gaza and the West Bank would be connected by a highway.[^597]
- allon felt that it was important for Israel to maintain a military presence in these places as well as to build settlements there.[^597]
- allon also called for Arabs of the West Bank and Gaza to demilitarize.[^597]
- While the allon Plan was not formally accepted by Israel, new settlements were built in these places until 1977. The Arabs did not accept this plan.[^597]
- Torah Shebiksav and Torah Shebaal Peh were given to Moshe at Har Sinai. This is clear from the fact that Torah Shebiksav is not clear about many things it mentions. Clearly there was more given that clarified these points.[^599]
- Example: The Torah doesn't discuss causing damage to a fellow person. The whole source of for the laws of damage is from לא תגזל. Obviously, there is a greater explanation to what is liable for than just these 2 words.[^599]
- Example: The Torah (Devarim 17 8-13) discusses the Kohanim adjudicating cases based on their exact details. These details which affect the the law are not discussed in the Torah Shebiksav.[^599]
- The religious in Israel put great importance on settling the whole land of Israel, including the West Bank. They created many settlements there.[^600]
- The divide between the religious and the secular Israelis began at this time (I don't get why).[^600]
- Levi Eshkol met with President Johnson in February 1968 to arrange for the purchase of American F-4 Phantoms to combat Egypt's rapid military rearmament. An arms race between Israel and Egypt, sponsored by the US and the Soviets respectively, was born.[^600]
- France had placed an arms embargo on Israel since the Six Day War, so this deal with the US was important.[^600]
- Levi Eshkol wasn't well and so Golda Meir was chosen to be the next Prime Minister, even though she wasn't well, either. Originally, she planned on being Prime Minister for one year until the next election cycle could choose someone else. (Obviously, this is not what ended up happening.)[^600]
- Dayan and Allon had a feud based on different views of how to treat the occupied territories (Allon wanted to give back most of the captured lands, while Dayan did not) as well as personal disagreements.[^600]
- Eshkol died in February 1969 and Golda Meir was appointed as Prime Minister.[^600]
- Yigal Allon helped run the government a lot since Golda Meir wasn't well and he was deputy Prime Minister. As such, people though about making him Prime Minister. However, he turned down the offer as he did not want to butt heads with the other political leaders.[^600]
- Golda Meir wanted to make peace with the surrounding Arab countries. They, however, did not want to make peace with Israel. Nasser said that land lost by war must be regained through war.[^600]
- Golda Meir even offered to personally go to Egypt and meet with Nasser there, but this was rejected.[^600]
- There were many military skirmishes along the Suez Canal. The Israelis leveled the city of Ismailia with artillery and air strikes.[^600]
- Abba Eban wanted to make peace with the surrounding Arab countries. However, he did not seem tolerant of the Arabs in the West Bank. He seemed to want to annex the West Bank. He felt that Arabs should be fine with the other Arabic countries.[^600]
- Golda Meir similarly said that the Palestinians were not a people.[^600]
- Despite this, Palestinian nationalism was rising.[^600]
- In December 1969, Israel decided to escalate the conflict to try and deter the Egyptians.[^600]
- In January 1970, Israel launched a massive bombing missions on military bases north and south of Cairo, and sent some planes 80 miles into Egypt. They also launched air attacks on terrorist and military bases in Lebanon and Jordan.[^600]
- Since the Six Day War, the air forces of the surrounding Arab countries were not able to compete with Israel, with many of them being shot down.[^600]
- Israel's victory in the Six Day War created feelings of Jewish pride amongst Soviet Jews. From 1970 to 1972, the Soviet Union started letting Jews leave the country. Around 45,000 Jews immigrated to Israel in that time.[^601]
- In June 1970, the US brokered a ceasefire between Israel and Egypt.[^601]
- The Right-Wing Gachal did not want to give up anything in the ceasefire. According to Golda Meir, they also thought that despite not listening to the US, they could "demand" for the US to continue to supply them with weapons. They resigned from the unity government when the ceasefire was signed. (I don't know what Israel had to give up in the ceasefire that got Gachal so mad, unless they didn't want to make any deal at all?)[^601]
- About 600 soldiers and 125 Israeli citizens had been killed in the war.[^601]
- President Nixon accepted Golda Meir with warmth when she visited the US.[^601]
- Golda Meir argued that the original British Mandate had included Israel and Jordan and called for one Arab state and one Jewish State. According to that view, this had been achieved. There was one Arab state: Jordan.[^601]
- Meir felt that another Arab state would simply be a springboard to launch terror attacks.[^601]
- At the same time, she also strongly desired peace.[^601]
- In September 1970, the PLO hijacked four international airliners and tried to overthrow Kind Hussein in Jordan. This became known as Black September.[^601]
- King Hussein killed around 2000 PLO fighters, as well as several thousand more civilians.[^601]
- This led for the PLO to leave the West Bank and more to southern Lebanon, which they took over.[^601]
- Between the end of the Six Day War and March 1971, 120 Israeli citizens and 180 Israeli soldiers were killed by PLO terror attacks. In response, Israel killed over 1800 terrorists in Israel.[^601]
- Yasser Arafat called for the destruction of Israel.[^601]
- There were also terror attacks on Jews outside of Israel. More airliners were taken. In Munich, February 1970, Jews in an old age home were killed.[^601]
- The mesorah of Torah was transferred from rebbi to talmid from Moshe until our times. It was written down in the forms of the Mishna and Gemara, neither of which can be edited.[^602]
- A person needs to take time and put in effort when judging cases. Mistakes are easy to make. In addition, sometimes a thought might hit someone that they did not initially think of. A person who does not do this and makes a mistake is considered negligent, even if the person put in an honest effort.[^603]
- The reason why Pirkei Avos discusses this by din is because din is extremely important. Practicing proper din is the way one gets to know Hashem {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Rabbeinu Yonah discusses practicing din, but perhaps he means learning it? Meaning, when one learnsthe halachos of dinim, perhaps he "understands Hashem" (as much as a person can do that)?</span>}.[^603]
- The importance of din is shown in the Torah. The Torah discusses din right before  matan Torah (וְשָׁפְט֣וּ אֶת־הָעָם֮ בְּכׇל־עֵת֒ (שמות יח כב)) and right after (וְאֵ֙לֶּה֙ הַמִּשְׁפָּטִ֔ים אֲשֶׁ֥ר תָּשִׂ֖ים לִפְנֵיהֶֽם (שמות כא א)).[^604]
- A person should teach many talmidim. This is in line with Beis Hillel as opposed to Beis Shamai. Beis Shamai was more of an elitist. Beis Hillel was was much more open. He felt that you never know which talmidim you teach will end up being successful, so it is important to teach as many as possible.[^605]
- This is actually that Hillel himself experienced. The gemara discusses how all of Hillel's talmidim came to see him when he was on his deathbed. Rav Yochanan ben Zachai, because of his anivus, waited outside. Hillel asked his talmidim if they were all there. They responded that they all were except for the least great of them. Hillel told his talmidim to bring him in as he was destined for greatness (which is what happened).[^606]
- (This story seems a bit difficult to understand how it proves the point of Rabbeinu Yonah. Rav Yochanan Ben Zachai was not really a "bottom of the shiur" person. He was a tremendous gadol who hid his greatness. As such, how does one see from here that even one's "lowest" talmid can be destined to be greater than the "top guys"? Perhaps the idea is that even though Hillel realized Rav Yochanan be Zachai's greatness, his talmidim, who were also great people obviously did not. As such, a rebbi has to realize that he may also be missing his talmid's potential (although this is a situation where the talmid is hiding his own greatness. I don't know how one can see from here how that one's talmid may have untapped potential.))[^7]
- Walter Eytan wrote an article in the Jerusalem Post in 1972 about how Israelis would not want to give up the West Bank, but would be willing to do so for peace.[^607]
- He also pointed out that Israel purposely did not annex the West Bank for this reason (that they can return it in exchange for peace).[^607]
- In May 1972, Japanese gunmen working for Palestinian terrorists killed 27 people at Lod airport (later known as Ben-Gurion airport).[^607]
- In September 1972, the Israeli Olympic cycling team was killed in Munich.[^607]
- Shorty after, an Israeli agriculture government official was killed by a letter bomb.[^607]
- Israel made settlements in the Golan Heights and in the Jordan River Valley. The Israeli settlements were not placed near Arab settlements.[^607]
- (According to the book, the Jordan River Valley was largely unpopulated and a desert. I think I have seen elsewhere that it was very fertile, which would make sense as it is a river valley.)
- Israel still continued with its hand-off approach, letting Arabs locals elect their own mayors and control their education.[^607]
- There was some mixing of Israelis and Palestinians, but for the most part, they had little to do with each other and this didn't really lead to much goodwill. A small minority of people warned that occupation can't lead to goodwill.[^607]
- Despite this, acts of terror were not so common, either.[^607]
- There was segregation with the Sephardim. The Ashkenaz had the government positions, the better jobs, and the better places to live.[^607]
- Through the army, the young Sephardim became Israeli.[^607]
- According to Walter Eytan, the economic situation for the Arabs in the West Bank and Gaza by 1973 had improved greatly since Israel had taken over in 1967. There were even sometimes labor shortages rather than unemployment. Around 40,000 Arabs worked in Israel.[^608]
- Despite this, Arabs wanted their own country.[^608]
- The Labor Party was divided on whether to make the occupation of the West Bank, the Sinai, and Gaza, permanent or not. Dayan (as well as the National Religious Party, which was part of the Labor party) pushed for it. He wanted a city with a port built in the Sinai near Rapha, and land in the West Bank should be allowed to purchased by private Israeli citizens.[^608]
- Pinhas Sapir (the Finance Minister), Yigal Yadin, Yigal Allon, and Abba Eban were against a more permanent settlement. They felt that this would require Israel to give voting rights to the Arabs, which would radically change the nature of the country. However, they were scared that if Dayan left the party, the Labor Party would lose control of the government.[^608]
- A compromise (called the Galili Compromise) was reached. The port of the potential city in the Sinai was not agreed to. The city was. Land in the West Bank was mostly not permitted to be sold.[^608]
- Israel was not worried about war. They were confident that they could easily destroy the Arab armies if they attacked. Israel was planning on cutting out over half of the spending on the military over the next few years. An inexperienced commander (named Shmuel Gonen) was put in charge of Israel's southern forces.[^608]
- In early September, there was a air battle between Israel and Syria. 13 Syrian planes were shot down to one of Israel's. When Syria and Egypt mobilized troops after that, Israel didn't think anything of it.[^608]
- In October, Libya started sending in "terror squads". Zaire broke off relations with Israel.[^609]
