- The Arab League {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">some sort of unified Arab organization. Need to look this up</span>} put pressure on the Soviets as well as Europeans countries not to allow Soviet Jews to enter Israel.[^609]
- In October, Palestinian terrorists killed some Austrians and took some Soviet Jews hostage who were traveling through Austrian refugee camps to Israel. In exchange for their release, Austria closed down the refugee camps. The UN refused to get involved as the Soviet Jews were not "refugees", as they had a place they were heading (Israel). (It seems that they were left stranded in Austria but I am not entirely sure.)[^609]
- Syria and Egypt ramped up their troop movements and Russia officials evacuated Syria (they were no longer in Egypt). Israel started getting nervous, but only called up very limited reserves.[^609]
- Before the war, Syria had 1400 tanks ready in the Sinai. Israel had 170.[^609]
- The Egyptians and Syrians launched a surprise attack on October 6 at 2:00 PM, on Yom Kippur, with Egypt quickly gaining the east bank of the Suez Canal.[^609]
- The plans were kept secret even from the Arab military commanders. Nearly all didn't know there would be an attack until that morning.[^609]
- Later, the Agranat report discussed the mistakes of the Israeli military for not seeing the attack coming. Some things the report mentions:
	- Egypt was running military exercises. However, some Israeli military personnel thought that this might be a coverup for a planned attack (which they were correct about). This report was not given to the military command.[^609]
	- The Soviets pulled warships away from Egypt.[^609]
- The head of the Mossad told the military in the early morning of October 6 that Egypt would attack later that day.[^609]
- Dayan refused to launch a preemptive strike and was cautious about calling up reserves as he was nervous about US opinion. He didn't want Israel to look like the aggressors.[^609]
- Despite this, the Chief of Staff started full mobilization as 10:00. Because men were all in shul or at home (rather than working all over the place), this process went faster than it otherwise may have.[^609]
- Egypt launched a massive assault on the Israeli forces on the Suez, with 240 planes, 2000 artillery pieces, and 8000 soldiers. The Israelis had 16 forts manned by over 400 soldiers. Despite being outgunned, they fought extremely hard, causing casualties and delaying the Egyptians.[^609]
- ![[Pasted image 20250223234044.png]][^610]
- The gemara learns out that Hashem wants us to create safeguards around the halachos of the Torah from the words וּשְׁמַרְתֶּ֣ם אֶת־מִשְׁמַרְתִּ֗י (ויקרא יח ל).<span style="visibility:hidden">l</span>[^611]
- Keeping these dirabanans is a greater show of love than keeping the halachos themselves. If one keeps the halacha but not the dirabanans around it, it shows that one is not particular about violating the halacha. Keeping the dirabanans shows that one does care. (Obviously, this is not referring to someone who only keeps the dirabanans and not the actual halacha itself.)[^612]
- As such, one can achieve tremendous growth through being mindful of these dirabanans.[^612]
- At the same time, the Syrians launched their attack in the Golan Heights. They heavily outnumbered the Israelis there.[^613]
- The Israelis there fought hard and were able to slow them and inflict massive casualties.[^613]
- Within the first 24 hours, the Syrians made it most of the way across the Golan Heights. That was the farthest they made it during the war.[^613]
- ![[Pasted image 20250223234138.png]][^610]
- Dayan spoke confidently on the radio about how Israel would easily be able to push the Arabs back and told Jordan that they better not get involved this time around. An English reported there noted that Dayan did not look as confident as he sounded.[^613]
- Syrian artillery shells damaged many Israeli settlements all over the north.[^613]
- By midnight that night, Israel mobilized 200,000 reserves. The Syrians had 300,000 and Egypt had 850,000 (although most of them stayed back to defend Damascus and Cairo from an Israeli counterattack).[^613]
- Much of the Israeli equipment was not maintained and did not work.[^613]
- By the morning of October 7, about 100 tanks Israeli tanks on the Suez or which had been rushed there remained. 200 had been destroyed. However, more tanks were being brought in.[^614]
- On October 8, Israel launched some counterattacks against Egypt, but these were unsuccessful.[^614]
- Counterattacks against Syria were more successful but Israel was suffering heavy casualties.[^614]
- Israeli radio presented both of these as going better than they were.[^614]
- Dayan was extremely pessimistic about the war to the point that Golda Meir did not allow him to go on the radio to talk to the Israeli public. Instead, she had General Aharon Yariv give the press conference.[^614]
- Israel wanted to push into Egyptian and Syrian territory. They wanted to "teach the Arabs a lesson".[^614]
- Israeli warplanes hit targets in and around Damascus.[^614]
- By the end of October 8, Israel was totally pushed off of the Suez Canal and were set up 3-5 miles away.[^614]
- Algeria, Morocco, Uganda, and other countries gave some military or moral support to the Arab countries. Australian Jews came to help with civilian tasks and American Jews quickly raised $100 million.[^614]
- There was worry that Jordan would get involved in the war, but they decided against it.[^615]
- The Russian anti-aircraft weapons held the Israeli air force in check. They were mobile and had radar-guided missiles.[^615]
- By October 10, Israel had pushed Syria back to the initial ceasefire line. Dayan argued against pushing into Syria because he did not want to get too close to Damascus as this would potentially pull the Soviets into the war. He was outvoted, although Israel was cognizant of that point. They decided to advance 12 miles in Syria (Damascus was 30 miles from the border).[^615]
- There was a lot of pessimism in Israel as they realized how much and how powerfully the Soviets had armed Egypt and Syria. Even as the war continued, the Soviets Union continued to supply Egypt and Syria.[^615]
- A judge should deliberate when making a judgement, even if seems to be obvious to him. This is because a judge (assuming he is a good person, which he probably is), may feel compassion for one party or the other, for a variety of reasons. It is important for the judge to take his time to judge the case honestly and put aside these feelings. For that reason, it is important for the judge to take his time.[^616]
- Similarly, the gemara[^617] says that a judge has to wait for both parties to be present before being presented with the arguments. This is because we don't want to judge to feel for the person who is present. It will be difficult for the judge to remove these feelings and judge the case honestly.[^616]
- This doesn't just apply to matters of din. One should always respond to things only after consideration.[^616]
- This is one of the problems with anger: it gets a person to respond hastily without thinking.[^618]
- When teaching, this is also particularly important. When a person is asked a question in learning, they should think about it before responding. It is easy to respond quickly and make a mistake.[^616]
- On October 11, Israel began their counterattack on Syria. Syria was able to slow down the Israelis. By the end of the day, Israel was around 6 miles into Syria.
- On October 12, a big Soviet supply of arms landed in Syria.[^619]
- Arabs put pressure on King Hussein to join the fight, but he did not want to after losing the whole West Bank and East Jerusalem in the Six Day War after attacking Israel.[^619]
- Iraq contributed 15,000 troops to the Syrian front.[^619]
- On October 13, Egypt made a push for the Mitla and Gidi Passes. This led to a massive tank battle with over 2000 tanks used (more than in nearly any battle in WWII). Israel defenses were able to hold back the Egyptians. Egypt lost over 250 tanks while Israel lost ten.[^619]
- David Elazar (Israeli Chief of Staff) decided to take advantage of this victory and counterattack.[^619]
- On October 12 and 13, Israel made gains into Syria. They were able to destroy against many Iraqi units sent their way. A commando unit deep inside Syria destroyed roads to keep Iraqi units from coming, while ambushes were able to destroy and push back many on the front lines.[^619]
- While Jordan did not want to get involved in the war, it would defend Damascus if Israel would get close.[^619]
- The Soviet Union was worried about Syria. They sent in a tremendous amount of weapons, stationed naval units to defend Syrian towns on the water, were potentially going to bring in the air force to defend Damascus, and supplied Algerians with tanks to take to the fight.[^619]
- Israeli decided against shooting town transport planes as they didn't want the Soviet Union to join the war.[^619]
- Israeli troops were able to make it 12 miles into Syria.[^619]
- On October 13, President Nixon started sending military supplies to Israel.[^619]
- By October 14, Israel had lost over 650 soldiers.[^620]
- On October 15, Israel made a push to try and cross the Suez Canal. Israel constructed a nearly 200 foot bridge to span the canal and pushed it toward the canal with tanks.[^620]
- There were many Egyptian forces that needed to be cleared out before getting the bridge to the canal. Both sides suffered heavy losses and Israel was not able to get their main forces to the Canal.[^620]
- Despite this, a small force was able to reach the canal and cross in middle of the night. Within a few hours they were able to establish a three mile bridgehead.[^620]
- Food was starting to run low in Israel as all able-bodied men and nearly all trucks were on the front.[^620]
- France had sold planes to Libya which they sold the Egypt which were being used against Israel.[^620]
- Britain tried being neutral but this hurt Israel more. They stopped selling Centurion tanks to the "belligerents" but the only nations that had bought them were Israel and Jordan, who wasn't fighting. Britain also continued training Egyptian helicopter pilots. They also did not allow for American planes to resupply at a British base in Cyprus (although Portugal helped with that).[^620]
- SAM-6 units on the west of the Suez were a problem for the Israeli air force, creating a "protective bubble" up to 20 miles into the Sinai. The Israeli forces on the west of the Suez started targeting these units.[^620]
- The Egyptian forces on the east of the Suez were defending a location known as the Chinese Farm (a Japanese military training center before 1967).[^620]
- By October 16, the small force on the west of the canal had done some damage without sustaining many losses. In addition, the Egyptians lost the Chinese Farm.[^620]
- Sadat (who was president. Probably should have put down when that happened) went to the UN to try and get a ceasefire.[^620]
- Israel blamed the Soviets for causing this war by giving the Arab countries such military strength. This allowed them to go to war rather than negotiate.[^620]
- According to Israel, the Soviet Union had sent nearly 5000 tanks to Egypt before the war had started, in addition to other weapons. This is in addition to the vast amount of weapons brought in once the war began.[^620]
- Israel did not want to end the war yet. They felt that the Arab countries needed to be taught a harsher lesson to make sure they would not attack again.[^620]
- Britain pushed for Israel to give back the land taken in the Six Day War.[^620]
- In the Golan, Israeli forces beat back attacks from Iraq and Jordan at Tel El-Mal and Tel El-Alaliekh, respectively. An attack that night from Syria caused serious Israeli casualties but it was driven off. Israel was still 18 miles from Damascus.[^621] (I couldn't find these 2 places on Google. Maybe the spelling is way off?)
- Egypt counterattacked on October 17, but that was fought off.[^621]
- The prefabricated bridge was placed across the Suez and operational by the night of October 18, although Egypt continually attacked it.[^621]
- There was much displeasure with the Israeli government, particularly amongst the soldiers, considering how unprepared Israel had been.[^621]
- Syria, Jordan, and Iraq launched counterattacks on October 19, but these were beaten off. Israeli troops halted before the city of Sasa.[^621]
- ![[Pasted image 20250225003946.png]][^430]
- Fatah had been attacking Israel from Lebanon since the war began, although this did not make much of a difference in the war. Lebanon itself remained neutral.[^621]
- On October 19, Russia and the US agreed to urge both sides to a ceasefire.[^622]
- On October 19 and 20, fighting was starting to slow on both fronts as the Arab armies were thinning. Israel consolidated its forces on the west of the Suez, amassing a large force.[^622]
- Much of the Egyptian force was stuck on the eastern side of the Suez.[^622]
- On October 20, another counterattack on the Israeli forces in Syria failed. On October 21, Israel retook the observation post at Mount Hermon, although it took a lot of casualties.[^622]
- On October 21, Golda Meir agreed to Henry Kissinger (US Secretary of State) to a ceasefire.[^622]
- This saved the Egyptian third army which was surrounded on the east of the Suez and poised to be destroyed. This angered many Israelis but Kissinger felt that Sadat needed to be able to save face.[^622]
- The UN Security council passed UN Resolution 338 which called for a ceasefire at 6:52 on October 22, based on the positions of the armies at that time. Israel took advantage of the time before the ceasefire went into effect by advancing their positions.[^622]
- However, fighting did not stop at that time. On the Egyptian front, both sides blamed the other for starting the fighting, although Israel took advantage to press forward.[^623]
- Fatah openly declared that they would not stop fighting (although their attacks had only killed 20 people the whole war). The president of Uganda urged the Arabs not to accept the ceasefire.[^622]
- Israeli forces reached the Ismailia-Cairo road and were only a few miles south of Ismailia, as well as other roads leading to Cairo.[^622]
- On October 24 at 7 AM, the ceasefire mostly went into effect.[^622]
- Israel attacked the town of Suez after this ceasefire and was repulsed, taking losses.
- UN observers began arriving to the Suez front that night.[^622]
- Israel lost 350 tanks in the Golan, although 250 were able to be repaired. Syria lost 1150, Iraq lost 100, and Jordan lost 50. Of these over 850 were repaired. Israel lost over 700 troops in the Golan and Syria lost 3500.[^624]
- In total, Israel lost over 2500 troops.[^624]
- America had supplied Israel with tremendous financial and military aid. This would continue after that.[^624]
- Third World countries broke off ties with Israel. This was despite efforts made by Israel to create relationships with these countries.[^624]
- This war also broke Israeli confidence and gave the Egyptians confidence, even though, Israel had overall won a military victory.[^624]
- OPEC (led by Saudi Arabia) began to flex its muscle by creating an oil embargo on countries that had helped Israel. They pushed countries against Israel.[^624]
- One should have many talmidim to make sure Torah is not forgotten,[^616] and because of the concept of מָה עֵץ קָטָן מַדְלִיק אֶת הַגָּדוֹל, אַף תַּלְמִידֵי חֲכָמִים קְטַנִּים מְחַדְּדִים אֶת הַגְּדוֹלִים.<span style="visibility:hidden">l</span>[^625] (This seems to refer to the concept of וּמִתַּלְמִידַי יוֹתֵר מִכּוּלָּן as those are the next words of the gemara.)
- One should take council with his talmidim when answering questions, as this will help one find the answer. The gemara[^626] discusses how Rav would do this.[^616]
- One should speak in a measured way, not too little and not too much. This is especially true when teaching Torah. If one speaks too little, others won't be able to understand him. If one speaks too much, people will start to tune him out.[^627]
- In addition, different situations call for different ways of saying things (sometimes leaving some things out, and others, saying things in full) and person has to be aware of that.[^616]
- A person should put safeguards on anything that he does to make sure that he doesn't accidentally come to doing an aveirah.[^616]
- Peace talks began in Egypt. The Arabs basically wanted Israel to return to its pre-1967 borders with Israel remaining on the east of the Sinai down to Sharm El-Sheik.[^628]
- Israel wanted to basically maintain the whole Sinai (giving up the eastern bank of the Suez) and that's it.[^628]
- In December, the peace talks moved to Geneva (called the Geneva Peace Conference). Egypt and Jordan agreed to go but refused to sit with and talk directly with Israel. Syria refused to go. The PLO wasn't invited.[^628]
- The Syrians refused to give over a list of Israeli POWs they had.[^628]
- Almost immediately after the meeting started, Israel had elections (which were supposed to have been in October). The Labor Party lost a little and the right (now forming an alliance called the Likud) was in second. The Likud didn't want to negotiate at the Geneva Peace Conference. This may have hurt them in the elections as the Israeli public was traumatized by the war and desperately wanted peace.[^628]
- Golda Meir had a hard time putting a cabinet together as many wanted a unity government. Eventually she got her Labor-led government.[^628]
- The Agranat Report was published in October, causing outrage against the Labor leadership. Golda Meir and Moshe Dayan were forced to resign (despite not being implicated in the report). Yitzchak Rabin became Prime Minister and Shimon Peres became Minister of Defense.[^628]
- In January 1974, Egypt agreed to Israel and allowed Israel to stay a few miles off the Suez.[^628]
- Syria continued to refuse to join the conference. They started shelling the Israeli forces in the Golan. The said that their goal was to keep the pressure on Israel to force them to keep reserves mobilized to hurt their economy.[^628]
- In addition, Syria started massing a small multinational force in its borders, featuring North Korea, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, Morocco, and Cuba.[^628]
- In May, PLO terrorists killed schoolchildren in Maalot, after killing a family on the way. This was done by one of the moderate factions of the PLO.[^628]
- At the Arab summit in October 1975, the PLO was declared the sole representative of the Palestinians. In November, the UN voted them as an observer to all UN meetings, declaring the rights of the Palestinians to "national independence and sovereignty" and to return to "their homes and property".[^629]
- A few days before this, Yasser Arafat gave a speech where he said that there can be no compromise with Israel and Israel must be destroyed.[^629]
- The PLO lobbied for Zionism to be considered racism by the UN. A resolution was eventually passed. Many western countries voted against it, but the Arab countries, Soviet allies, African countries, and a few other outvoted them.[^629]
- Some point out that while Israel's constitution does call for a Jewish state, it does also state that Arabs should be treated equally. The constitution for numerous Arab countries, like Pakistan and Saudi Arabia call themselves Islamic states in their constitutions.[^629]
- In 1975, negotiations with Egypt continued. They failed in March. President Ford stopped sending aid to Israel because of that. However, they were successful in September. The conditions were:
	- Israel withdrew to the east of the Gidi and Mitla passes and withdrew from Abu Rudeis oil fields. UN and American troops were stationed in the middle.[^630]
	- An agreement not to block non-military ships.[^630]
	- An agreement for Egypt to sell Israel oil at reasonable prices.[^630]
- In December 1975, a group called Gush Emunim was formed. They wanted to settle the whole West Bank. This was because of a mix of nationalistic and religious reasons.[^630]
- At first, Israel resisted. Eventually, with help from people on the right (like Ariel Sharon), and because of the negative sentiments toward Israel from the world (the Zionism is Racism thing. The government didn't want to fight against its own people at such a time) compromises were made. Slowly but surely, these compromises increased, allowing them to do more and more.[^630]
- Rabin later regretted having ever given in to the demands.[^631]
- Until this time, settlements were nearly entirely on the areas outlined by the Alon plan.[^630]
- In March 1976, 6 Arabs were killed by the IDF when Israel tried to confiscate their land to create Jewish developments (compensation was promised).[^630]
- In Arab cities, Arabs elected many leaders who were pro the PLO. This angered the Likud.[^630]
- In 1976, a crossing point between Israel and Lebanon was created at Metula (known as the Good Fence). Many Arabs came to Israel through this crossing for medical treatment.[^630]
- In late June, hijackers took an El-Al plane. they landed it in Libya where they let off all non-Jewish passengers, and then brought the plane to Entebbe, Uganda.[^631]
- They demanded a release of Fatah members in Israeli prisons in exchange for the hostages.[^631]
- Israel conducted a military operation to get the hostages out. It was highly successful. One woman who was at a local hospital during the raid was left behind and later killed. 3 civilians and 1 of the commandos were killed. The commando was Yoni Netanyahu, brother of Benjamin Netanyahu.[^631]
- This showed Israel's strength and weakened terrorism.[^631]
- In December 1976, cutting edge aircraft (F-15s) from America were brought to Israel. A ceremony was held for the occasion on the day they landed, which was on Friday. The ceremony ran into Shabbos.[^632]
- The religious parties were upset by this and instigated a vote of no confidence. Rabin responded by reforming his government without the National Religious Party. This weakened the Labor party's hold on the government. However, this also got rid of the pressure that the NRP would put on the government.[^632]
- Rabin was challenged within his own party by Peres, but Rabin slightly beat him out.[^632]
- There was much dislike between the two for many years, until they would eventually make up.[^632]
- There was still strong sentiment against the Labor Party because of the failings of the Yom Kippur War, as well as some financial scandals by Labor members of the government. In addition, the NRP joined the right. They felt that the left didn't care about religion enough.[^632]
- Hashem gave Moshe Torah Shebiksav, Torah Shebaal Peh, Neviim, Kesuvim,[^633] Targum,[^634]  and Kri Uksiv[^635] and everything was passed through the generations.
- Neviim and Kesuvim were not allowed to be written down until the time came. The neviim of those times wrote them down with ruach hakodesh.[^636] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Was this not passed down to the masses? How did Klal Yisroel ever do chataim if they already knew the neviim? Also, why did they need to be written with ruach hakodesh? Why couldn't they just have been written as they had been passed down?</span>}
- Targum was forgotten and then rediscovered.[^634]
- In February 1977, Rabin and Sadat were both keen on making peace.[^637]
- In March, Rabin met with newly-elected President Jimmy Carter. Rabin was fine giving back nearly all of the Sinai for peace with Egypt. He just wanted a drop of Israeli control near the Straights of Tiran. (He was fine giving back Sharm El-Sheik.) He was not okay giving up the Golan Heights. He was okay with significant withdrawals from the West Bank, but not back to pre-1967 borders. He was also not okay with a purely Palestinian state (he was okay if they would be under Jordan.)[^637]
- Carter wanted a near complete withdrawal from the Wests Bank and he wanted the Palestinians to have their own state.[^637]
- Rabin had a bank account in America from his time as ambassador in the US. This was illegal once he moved back to Israel. When news of this account broke, it was considered a financial scandal (despite the fact that the account was seemingly never used for anything corrupt and it was more a matter of forgetting to close it). The political pressure forced Rabin to resign. Peres took over as Prime Minister temporarily until elections a few months later (where Peres was the Labor candidate).[^637]
- A new political party called תְּנוּעָה דֶּמוֹקְרָטִית לְשִׁינּוּי (Democratic Movement for Change. ד״ש or Dash) was created by Yigal Yadin and others. This was considered a centrist party. Their focus was on helping disenfranchised Israelis, such as many Sephardim, as well as some political reforms. In the election in 1977, they got 15 seats (which was pretty impressive for a new party).[^637]
- Labor was finally toppled. The Labor party only 32 seats, compared to Likud's 43. Likud formed a coalition with Dayan's party (even though he was Labor), the Agudat Yisroel and the National Religious Party. Begin was now Prime Minister.[^637]
- Ever since then, the right has been in control of Israeli politics.[^637]
- He decided to keep many members of the government on board even though they were largely Labor.[^637]
- Begin did not want to give back any land in the West Bank and he wanted to continue building settlements there. In public he said that an independent Palestine may allow for Soviet forces to be landed there and used against Israel.[^638]
- The US asked Israel if they would be willing to accept a step-by-step withdrawal with security arrangements. Begin did not accept.[^638]
- Begin was also not willing to withdraw from the Golan Heights. However, he was willing to make substantial withdrawals from the Sinai.[^638]
- Begin particularly pursued peace with Egypt as they were the main Arab power in the region. Making peace with them would severely weaken the Arabs. In addition, he thought that making peace with any of the Arab would keep American pressure about the Palestinians and the settlements off his back.[^638]
- Israel wanted to work with Jordan to deal with the West Bank, but they refused. The other Arab countries wouldn't accept them taking control of the West Bank. King Hussein told Israel that a full Israeli withdrawal from the West Bank was necessary.[^638]
- America and the Soviet Union were pushing to set up another Geneva Peace Convention to deal with all of the issues in the region.[^638]
- Meanwhile, Begin started pushing to meet with Sadat, without the other Arabic countries. He did this without American knowledge.[^638]
- The PLO fought against Christians in southern Lebanon. Israel came to help out using American weapons, against an agreement with the US that Israel would only use the weapons for self-defense and would notify the US before taking military action. When the US threatened to halt future weapon deliveries to Israel, Israel backed down.[^638]
- Some of the settlements set up in the West Bank were done without the approval of the Israeli government (so the government claimed).[^638]
- The Dash Party joined the right coalition. For two years, funds were allocated to Dash's goals of developing slums and making places better for the disenfranchised (called Project Renewal).[^639]
- There was some progress. Some neighborhoods had been upgraded. However, after two years a new Finance Minister was chosen who decided to use the money to build new settlements in West Bank.[^639]
- Dash was against this and threatened to withdraw from the coalition, but they never did as a withdrawal would not break the coalition anyway.[^639]
- Rabin's memoirs were published in 1979. He described a lot of underhanded ways that Peres had treated him. There was much political backlash against Peres for this. (Peres was still in charge of the Labor coalition.)[^639]
- Rabin also described how Ben-Gurion was totally fine with 50,000 Arabs getting kicked out of Ramle and Lod. Rabin claimed that violence was sometimes used to get the Arabs out. Yigal Alon (who was there as well) denied that violence occurred (I find that hard to believe. Of course the Arabs resisted.)[^639]
- In November, the PLO fired rockets into Israel from Lebanon, killing a woman. Israel responded with a air raid.[^639]
- On November 7, Sadat publicly declared that he would be willing to travel to Israel to try and make peace. Some in the Israeli government thought that there was some sort of trick, but Begin publicly responded that Israel would gladly greet him.[^639]
- Sadat came to Israel on November 19, 1977. He greeted and was greeted warmly by Israeli politicians and the Israeli public, many of whom lined the streets waving Israeli flags.[^639]
- Sadat opened with a speech calling for Israel to withdraw from all Arab lands, but he emphasized that he did not want to go war anymore.[^639]
- The US was heavily involved in the peace process.[^640]
- Dayan and Begin later traveled to Egypt to continue peace talks. However, there was an impasse as Egypt insisted on Israel giving up the Arab lands and giving independence to the Palestinians, and Israel refusing to do so. The peace talks stalled for months.[^640]
- During this time, more settlements were created and expanded in the West Bank.[^640]
- A new group called Peace Now, urged Begin to make any sort of deal.[^640]
- In March 1978, the PLO hijacked a bus (after killing a woman on the seashore that they landed on). In the fight that ensued, 39 Israelis on the bus were killed.[^640]
- Israel responded by sending a force into Lebanon that went up until the Letani River, killing many PLO members and camps in the process.[^640]
- The troops stayed there for many months until UN troops took their place (called the United Nations Interim Force in Lebanon (UNIFL)). After this, the border with Lebanon and Israel was peaceful (Lebanon was relatively peaceful with Israel, other than the PLO and Fatah).[^640]
- Christians were fighting against Syrian influence in Lebanon (called the Lebanese Front). Israel provided them with weapons, training, and reconnaissance.[^640]
- In September, Sadat and other Arab leaders met with Begin, Dayan, and other Israeli leaders at Camp David. President Carter moderated the negotiations. Jordan, Syria, Saudi Arabia, and the Palestinians boycotted the meeting.[^640]
- The agreement was not an actual peace treaty, but a framework for one. Many integral details needed to be worked out later.[^640]
- Israel agreed to eventual Arab autonomy in the West Bank and agreed to the 'legitimate rights' of the 'Palestinian people'. This was the first time Israel acknowledged the Palestinians as a unique entity.[^640]
- The agreement called for normalized relationships between Israel and the surrounding countries (which seemed like a longshot as none of them seemed interested).[^640]
- Israel agreed to totally withdraw from the Sinai and give up all of the settlements and air force bases there. The US agreed to build new airfields in the Negev in Israel and set up early warning stations in case Egyptian forces were building up. (I thought they already had this?)[^640]
- Sadat did not agree to demilitarize the Sinai, but he did agree to leave a large buffer before Israel.[^640]
- Economic boycotts on Israel were lifted. Many companies didn't do business with Israel out of fear of getting boycotted by the (far larger) Arab countries.[^640]
- Jerusalem was talked about but, basically, the agreement came down to Israel saying that Jerusalem was indivisible and the Egyptians saying that it need to be part of the West Bank, without changing anything.[^640]
- Begin went to the Knesset to have them vote on the deal (even though he did not have to do this). The Knesset overwhelmingly voted in favor, although people were uneasy with different parts of it. Some didn't like giving up all of the settlements in the Sinai. Others were concerned about the part of Palestinian autonomy.[^640]
- Two new political parties were formed in opposition to the deal: Brit Ne'emanei Eretz Yisroel and Tehiya (Tehiya was more to the right of Begin. I don't know what the other one was. I am a little confused by it.)[^640]
- Many Israeli politicians didn't think that the deal would last, but it did.[^640]
- However, the issue of Palestinian autonomy wasn't really revisited. Despite this, the rest of the deal was acted upon by both sides.[^640]
- After the agreement, Israeli settlers continued settling the West Bank (there were times that they would pressure the government. One time, a group occupied a building in Hebron until the government agreed to allow them to create a settlement).[^640]
- The final negotiations occurred in March 1979. Egypt agreed to sell oil to Israel for a reasonable price for the next 15 years. The US promised that they would sell oil to Israel after that if Israel couldn't buy oil elsewhere.[^640]
- The deal was signed in Washington on March 26, with Sadat, Begin, and Carter signing the deal.[^640]
- In December, Begin and Sadat won the Nobel Peace Prize.[^640]
- The same way Moshe gave over Torah Shebaal Peh to Yehoshua, he transferred over Nevua to Yehoshua, as well. Moshe's level of nevua was unique that allowed others to gain nevua from him.[^641] (רבינו בחיי sounded like he was using the transfer of Torah interchangeably with the transfer of nevua. Perhaps we see how much Torah and nevua are linked).
- Many settlements continued to be built in the West Bank and Gaza, some near heavily populated Arab areas.[^642]
- In 1980, Begin passed a Basic Law (something like a constitutional amendment for the US) that Jerusalem would not be divided and would remain the capital of Israel.[^642]
- In 1980, immigration from Ethiopia started increasing.[^642]
- In 1984, Israel ran a secret airlift operation called Operation Moses to get Jews from Ethiopia.[^642]
- Between 1972 and 1985, over 14,000 Ethiopians had immigrated to Israel.[^642]
- The Dash Party disbanded in 1981 as they realized that they couldn't accomplish their goals.[^642]
- Elections were held in June 1981. Labor got all of the seats from Dash, but still came up short of Likud, losing 48 to 47 seats.[^642]
- In October 1981, extremists assassinated Sadat because he made peace with Israel.[^642]
- The peace between Israel and Egypt held but was already a "cold peace".[^642]
- Israel withdrew from the Sinai. Soldiers had to drag Israeli settlers away.[^642]
- There was a fight between Israel and Egypt about a small tourist village called Taba. Both sides claimed that it was on their side of the border. In the end, Israel capitulated and gave it up to Egypt who allowed Israelis to visit the town.[^642]
- Judges need to take their time when coming up with their psak as law is of vital importance. This is seen from the fact that the Torah says ואלה המשפטים right after the עשרת הדברות. The 'ו' (vuv, meaning 'and') connects the laws (which are discussed in פרשת משפטים) with the עשרת הדברות.<span style="visibility:hidden">l</span>[^643]
- This is also important because by the judges taking a while and deliberating the case, the litigants are more likely to accept the psak.[^644]
- There is a point when it is too much and the judges need to be careful about that as well. (Rashi on the gemara in Shabbos says that this is referring to when the judges already have a psak, but they are just delaying in giving it for whatever reason. I am not sure if this is what the רבינו בחיי is saying, or if he is saying that they are still deliberating, but at some point, enough is enough).[^645]
- Having a lot of talmidim increases overall Torah knowledge as, simply, the more people who work on finding pshat, the greater the understanding of Torah there is.[^646]
- In addition, the Torah knowledge of the talmidim themselves is increased by being talmidim.[^646]
- In addition, one shouldn't stop producing talmidim as one doesn't know if the talmidim one produced will continue to be successes and maybe only new talmidim will be successful.[^646]
- Israel administered the West Bank harshly and kept building settlements there.[^647]
- In 1982, Peace now held demonstrations against these policies. Soldiers who were in the West Bank gave first-hand accounts, such as:[^647]
- A soldier had Palestinians in the West Bank write their identification number on their arms. That day was Holocaust Remembrance Day. The soldier didn't realize the callousness of his actions.[^647]
- Many times soldiers clashed with women and children. They would throw rocks at the soldiers who responded with their rifles.[^647]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't have a feel for what specifically happened</span>}
- This led to a demonstration of 80,000 people in Tel Aviv.[^647]
- Israel opened investigations into the matter and convicted some people.[^647]
- The National Religious Party pressured the government to stop allowing El Al to fly on shabbos.[^647]
- This was the biggest religious push until that point and was a precursor for religious-secular relations.[^647]
- In June 1982, the PLO started shelling Israeli settlements in the north from Lebanon. (Nobody was killed)[^647]
- At the same time, a Palestinian gunman severely injured an Israeli ambassador in London. (The man was not from the PLO but this was hidden from the cabinet members.)[^647]
- Before this, there were other shelling attacks as well as terrorist units that would cross into Israel.[^647]
- On June 5, Ariel Sharon got approval from the cabinet to launch a small offensive to clear out the PLO to help defend the north. Sharon said that troops would not go more than 25 miles into Lebanon and would only be there for 2-3 days. This was called Operation Peace for Galilee.[^647]
- However, Sharon actually wanted to push to Beirut to clear the PLO out of its headquarters which were there. He also wanted to establish Bashir Jemayel, a Christian Phalangist (who was pro-Israel. Phalangists are also called Maronites), as the president of Lebanon to make peace with Israel after this was over.[^647]
- (I think the Phalangists were part of the Lebanese Front, or whatever that Christian group that Israel supported was called.)
- Israel made quick progress, nearly reaching Beirut and destroying many PLO bases in the process, in the first few days, before meeting some resistance from some of the towns.
- Israel also clashed with Syrian troops who were stationed in Lebanon, although neither country formally declared war.[^647]
- The UN security council passed a resolution on June 6 demanding Israel withdraw.
- Israel agreed to a ceasefire to go into effect in June 11 with the Syrians, but not with the PLO.[^648]
- By June 13, Israel reached Beirut, surrounding the city with the help of the Phalangist forces over the next few days.[^647]
- Syrian and Lebanese forces held positions in the towns of Aley and Shtoura.[^647]
- Peres said that Begin was not made aware by the military that they were attacking Beirut.[^647]
- By June 20, Israel had control over a stretch of the Beirut-Damascus Highway.[^647]
- Israel made plans to attack West Beirut. An Israeli brigade commander named Eli Geva requested to be replaced rather than lead his unit in the attack.[^647]
- Israeli troops described how many buildings and houses in the cities they fought through were destroyed.[^647]
- Some cabinet members wanted the Palestinians in Sidon (refugee camp) to be expelled. This did not sit well with the army. Commands like this led to Israeli soldiers forming the movement Soldiers Against Silence.[^647]
- On July 3, Peace Now organized a 100,000 person march in Tel Aviv against the war. The government responded by organizing a bigger counter-march in favor.[^647]
- Sharon tried (and failed) to get papers who were talking against the war to be censored.[^647]
- On July 4, Rabin put forward a plan to cut off the water supply of Beirut. Israel continued to bombard the city for the next month, trying to dislodge the PLO forces.[^647]
- This was the most highly televised war up until that point in time. It really hurt Israel's image on the world stage, especially when they struck a hospital.[^647]
- An American envoy named Philip Habib was able to negotiate a ceasefire between the sides. The PLO agreed to leave Beirut.[^647]
- Chazal made safeguards around the mitzvos to make sure that we don't come to accidentally violate them.[^649]
- (Brings down Brachos 5a that the whole Torah, Shebaal Peh, Shebiksav, etc. were given at Har Sinai, as brought down in Rabbeinu Yonah)[^650]
- Hashem gave over the Torah to Moshe with the machlokesim that are in the gemara ("Elazar, my son, said this, and Yehoshua my son said that"). This is a definition of Eilu vaeilu divrei Elokim chaim (namely, that both sides of a machlokes were given over at Har Sinai).[^651]
- (Neviim and Kesuvim were given to Moshe but were not to be written down until later using ruach hakodesh, and that is why they are called Divrei Kabalah (as they came down from Moshe) as Rabbeinu Yonah said.)[^650]
- (Targum was also given, forgotten, and rediscovered, as Rabbeinu Yonah said.)[^650]
- (There is a discussion about the mesorah of trop and nikudos, but I don't understand what he is saying.)[^650]
- In September, the US released the Reagan Plan, which called for Palestinian self-governance of the West Bank and Gaza. Begin refused as he did not want to give up control of the West Bank.[^652]
- On September 14, Jemayel was assassinated by Muslims in Lebanon. This enraged the Phalangists.[^652]
- Israel stationed troops in west Beirut (which was largely Muslim) to protect them from retaliation.[^652]
- In revenge of the assassination, the Phalangists killed 1300-3500 Muslims in the Sabra and Shatila refugee camps between September 16-18.[^652][^653]
- While Israel was not directly involved in the massacre, they were indirectly involved by not protecting the Muslims and by shining lights into the camps at night. Israel claimed that they thought that the Phalangists were going to target soldiers.[^652]
- There was tremendous outrage in Israel over this. Peace Now organized a march of 400,000 people (10% pf Israel's total population) in Tel Aviv. This was supported by the political left.[^652]
- A potential support to G-d's existence is the fact that humans have the ability to be random. Humans have free will and don't follow specific rule.
- Computers are not able to be truly random. Computers get random numbers from various inputs (sometimes measuring physical phenomenon). The computer may use the amount of nanoseconds according to the exact system time when the computer looks for the random number, or may measure the noise produced by the fan, or measure some aspect of nuclear decay in an unstable particle.
- The computer may then take these numbers and combine them using some algorithm. Then the computer gives you a result.
- This isn't truly random. While practically these are impossible to predict, theatrically, they can be predicted if someone knew every aspect that generated the specific input.
- Counter: Maybe humans are also really just following a set of super complex rules.
- This would seem to be the case, at least sometimes. Humans are not good at being able to truly generate random numbers. For example, if someone flip a coin 100 times, there will usually be certain outcomes, such as how many times you will get 2 heads (or tails) in a row, how often you will get 3 heads (or tails) in a row, as well as other things.[^654]
- A person (or computer program) that knows these rules can look at 2 lists, one generated from 100 real coin flips, and one generated from a person picking heads or tails 100 times at random, and guess which one is which with a solid degree of accuracy.[^654]
- Possible counter: I believe that quantum physics may involve truly random phenomenon. (I need to look into this.)
- The Israeli government issued the Kahan report analyzing the massacre.[^655]
- The report said that Israelis were not directly involved in the killings.[^655]
- However, it criticized Yehoshua Sagi (leader in the intelligence department), Sharon, and Begin, for not attempting to protect the Muslims from the obvious potential slaughter. This was certainly true once the Phalangists actually did start massacring people.[^655]
- This was the most controversial war amongst Israelis themselves by far. There were many protests and counterprotests during and after the war. [^655]
- Sharon resigned from Defense Minister but stayed on in the cabinet. Sagi resigned from Military Intelligence.[^655]
- In terms of the military results, Israel dominated, particularly the air force which destroyed over 80 Syrian planes to just 1 Israeli plane.[^656]
- Even if one has heard the same shayla numerous times, he should think about it again before issuing a psak.[^657] (Is this because by learning the sugya again, you may come to a different conclusion, or is it that by analyzing the shayla, you will realize that there are points of difference that can change the psak. Also, I assume that it depends on the kind of shayla. I don't think poskim go over the halacha every time someone brings in a milchig spoon that was in the fleishig pot…)
- (Have lots of talmidim to increase their chochma, like Rabbeinu Yonah.[^658] I am a bit confused because they both bring down the gemara of "I learned from my rabbeim, more from my peers, and most from my students". This doesn't sound like we are talking about the students' chochma.)
- Due to international pressure, Israel withdrew from Beirut.[^659]
- There were peace negotiations between all the parties which called for foreign powers to leave the country. Syria refused to leave, and so Israel stayed as well.[^659]
- Israel occupied southern Lebanon up until the Awali River and also the Jebel Barouk (a mountain 15 miles from Beirut) with 30,000 troops. Many Israeli soldiers were against the occupation (60 went to prison for disobeying orders).[^659]
- Syria occupied the Beka valley with 40,000 troops.[^659]
- There were also 10,000 PLO members still in Lebanon, even though the leadership left.[^659]
- An American and French peacekeeping force was stationed in Lebanon as well.[^660]
- This is all in addition to the Lebanese army.[^659]
- Settlements in Gaza and the West Bank increased. The government pushed this by offering monetary subsidies for people who would move to these areas.[^659]
- By 1983, there were 20,000 settlers in the West Bank and Gaza.[^659]
- The Labor party was against this and removed the subsidies when they were in power in the 1990s. They wanted to possibility for peace to remain viable.[^659]
- Many settlers were racist against Arabs. There were many violent confrontations between the two.[^659]
- There was talk of annexing the West Bank but this was not popular.[^659]
- The argument in favor was that this would give Israel strategic depth.[^659]
- The arguments against were that the Palestinians couldn't be given voting rights because they would change the electorate too much, but they couldn't be denied voting right as that would apartheid.[^659]
- The PLO continued terror attacks in Israel.[^660]
- All this pressure pushed Begin to resign. He lived out the rest of his life quietly (partially because his wife died at around this time as well).[^660]
- Yitzhak Shamir became Prime Minister in October 1983.[^660]
- Muslims attacked the American and French peacekeepers in Lebanon, including the bombing of a base in Beirut, killing nearly 250 soldiers.[^660]
- Shamir didn't want to withdraw Israeli troops until security arrangements were agreed upon. The Labor party wanted to withdraw immediately.[^660]
- In April 1984, there was a vote of no confidence. As such, general elections were held in July.[^660]
- The parties came out deadlocked. Neither was able to form a coalition. As such, a rotational National Unity Government was formed. Peres became Prime Minister with Shamir as Deputy Prime Minister for 2 years, when Shamir would take over.[^660]
- Rabbi Meir Kahane's Kach (כך) Party was active in the 70s through 90s. They were very anti-Arab, wanting to transfer (with force if necessary) all Arabs out of the Israel, the West Bank, and Gaza. However, they were not popular (the most votes they ever had was 1.2% of the Israeli population).[^660][^661]
- In 1988, Israel passed a law banning racist parties (such as Kach).[^660]
- Kahane was assassinated in 1990.[^660]
- Right at the beginning of Peres's Prime Minister tenure, Israel was undergoing massive inflation (as high as nearly 450% in a year).[^662]
- In June 1985, Peres cut government subsidies and fixed prices and wages (called the Economic Stabilization Plan). This led to the recovery of the economy.[^663]
- Israel pulled out of Lebanon in 1985, maintaining a 3-4 strip in the south for security.[^663]
- Before pulling out, Israel trained and armed the Christian army there (called the South Lebanese Army).[^663]
- Also in 1985, Israel traded 1150 Palestinians in Israeli prisons for 3 soldiers held captive by a terrorist organization (not the PLO). Israel wanted to maintain the idea that they would not abandon their soldiers.[^663]
- In September 1985, the PLO killed 3 Israelis. Israel responded by bombing the PLO headquarters in Tunis, killing over 70 PLO members and Tunisians.[^663]
- Anatoly (later changed to Natan) Sharansky was a Refusenik (a Jewish Soviet who pushed for Jews to be allowed to leave the country). Because of this, he, and others, were put in jail.[^663]
- Due to tremendous internal and international pressure, he was freed and allowed to go to Israel in 1986.[^663]
- Over the next few years, continued pressure (such as a 400,000 person march in Washington arranged by Sharansky) caused the Soviet government to start letting Jews leave the country.[^663]
- In September 1986, an Israeli nuclear scientist named Mordechai Vanunu revealed the strength of Israel's nuclear arsenal to reporters (to try to end nuclear weapons).[^664] Until that time, it was unknown whether Israel had nuclear weapons. The truth was that they had nuclear weapons for 2 decades by that point.[^665]
- He was arrested and sentenced to 18 years in prison. 11 of those years were spent in solitary confinement.[^666] His sentence was not pardoned despite international pressure.[^665]
- In 1987, Jonathan Pollard was arrested for spying on the US for Israel and sentenced to life in prison.[^665]
- In October 1986, Shamir became the Prime Minister.[^665]
- In April 1987, Peres (who was still part of the government) tried to negotiate a peace deal with Jordan involving the UN and US (called the London Agreement). However, Shamir and Likud did not want to give up land and torpedoed the deal.[^665]
- A group called Ateret Cohanim raised money for the purpose of buying Israel from the Arabs as they felt that there was a religious obligation for Jews to rule all of Israel.[^665]
- In November, a member of a terrorist group snuck into Israel and killed 6 soldiers in a base. This was followed by a Jew being stabbed to death a few days later in Gaza City.[^665]
- At least some of the earliest tanaim were on the level of receiving nevua, if it still existed in their time.[^667]
- Moshe received the Torah directly from Hashem, not from a malach.[^668]
- (Brings down Brachos 5a)
- Torah, Mishna, Halacha, Agadah, Gemara, and psakim that talmidei chachomim were destined to issue were all taught to Moshe.
- Torah Shebaal Peh is an explanation of things which are mentioned in the Torah. Examples:
	- The Torah says not to do "melacha" on shabbos.
	- The Torah mentions yibum and chalitza.
	- The Torah mentions shechita.
- Other things which are not referred to in the Torah but are part of the mesorah are called halacha leMoshe misinai.
- In a sense, Torah Shebaal Peh is valued more than Torah Shebiksav. People are given harsher punishments for denying Torah Shebaal Peh than Torah Shebiksav, and the Medrash talks about how Torah Shebaal Peh is more dear to Hashem than Torah Shebiksav.
- Originally, Torah Shebaal Peh was not to be written down. However, because it was being forgotten, the tanaim learned from the possuk Eis Laasos LaHashem that they should write it down.
- Between 1983 and 1987, Arab resistance to Israeli rule increased. No weapons were used at first, but that changed over time. In addition, Arabs went on strike and roadblocks to Israeli soldiers. Children were encouraged to participate.[^669]
- The situation for Palestinians in the West Bank and Gaza was poor. Many were still in refugee camps.[^669]
- There was a line from Rabin to "break their bones". The full context was that it a soldier should break the bones of a protester rather than kill him.[^669]
- Within 2 months in 1987, over 50 Arabs were killed. Hundreds more were injured. Many were beaten. Israeli courts did take action against soldiers found guilty.[^669]
- The Arabs created their own party called the Arab Democratic Party which demanded an independent Palestine in the West Bank, East Jerusalem, and Gaza, and supported the PLO.[^669]
- The UN Security Council condemned Israel over their treatment of the Palestinians. Israel ignored this.[^669]
- On (Israeli) Independence Day in 1987, police officers tortured 4 Arab civilians. They received one year or less of jail time.[^669]
- In February 1988, Richard Murphy, a US representative, got representatives of Israel, Jordan and Palestine to agree that by 1991, parts of the West Bank would be autonomous and Israel would withdraw its troops.[^669]
- Right after this was agreed to, Hamas was created. They were against any form of Jewish State. They were willing to use violence against Israel, and against Arabs who did not agree with them.[^669]
- They also gave out humanitarian aid and were against schools going on strike as they were pro education.[^669]
- In April 1988, Israel assassinated a PLO leader.[^669]
- Chaim Herzog (President of Israel) was concerned about the violence against the Palestinians.[^670]
- In 1988, Hamas published its charter which called for no Jewish State, Jihad against Israel and "degenerate elements" amongst the Arabs (like drug dealers).[^670]
- They were largely supported by Iran.[^670]
- In November 1988, Likud won a close election again. It formed a coalition of right wing and religious (like the National Religious Party and Shas) parties. The religious parties were large enough to swing the balance of power to the left.[^670]
- Some smaller left wing parties came together to create the Meretz Party, which was pro peace.[^670]
- B'Tselem (an organization that reported human rights abuses by the Israeli military) was created in 1989.[^670]
- Many Palestinians who wanted to leave Gaza or the West Bank were rejected from leaving, even though they were allowed to leave. An organization called Hamoked was created to help with this.  Permission to leave started being granted more consistently.[^670]
- In 1987-1989 Israel didn't allow classes on many schools days in the West Bank and Gaza, from kindergartens to universities. According to one estimate, schools were closed for 175 days out of a possible 210 in a year.[^671]
- Israel claimed that the schools were closed for security purposes. Others thought that Israel simply wanted to not allow for any sort of organization, particularly, not any nationalistic organization.[^671]
- The Israeli public was largely indifferent during the intifada.[^671] (The book presents one person's opinion. I don't know if this is accurate based off things previously and soon to be said.)
- Jews started being allowed to leave the Soviet Union. Nearly all of them left with visas to go to Israel. However, around 80% went elsewhere (mostly the US).[^671]
- Israel petitioned the US to stop allowing the immigrants in (although there was a lot of debate about this). The US started listening in 1989 because it was costing them too much money to keep bringing in immigrants.[^671]
- Beginning in 1990 and continuing over the next few years, hundreds of thousands of Soviet (and former Soviet Union, once it collapsed) refugees went to Israel.[^671]
- Around 20,000 Ethiopian Jews came to Israel in the early 1990s. However they were the victims of discrimination. In addition, gedolim did not accept them as Jews without undergoing a geirus.[^671] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">This is not to imply that gedolim came to this psak because of racism. This is just pointing out the fact that the Ethiopian Jews felt ostracized.</span>}
- In May 1989, Israel proposed a four part plan to the US to lessen hostilities:
	- Strengthen the Egypt-Israel peace.[^672]
	- Officially make peace with the other Arab countries (technically, Israel was still in a war with some after only signing an armistice in 1949).[^672]
	- Resolve the refugee problem.[^672]
	- Allow for elections in the West Bank and Gaza (which was the main point).[^672]
- However, Israel refused to deal directly with the PLO.[^672]
- In the first stage (which was to last 5 years), elections would be held and there would be some degree of self-governance. Israel would be in charge of local security, foreign affairs, and matters relating to Israel.[^673]
- After that, Egypt and Jordan would take charge of Gaza and the West Bank, respectively, in conjunction with the elected representatives.[^672]
- This became known as the beginning of the peace process.[^672]
- The US and Egypt were largely on board with this plan. They each released plans to build on this.[^672]
- The Palestinians rejected it as they wanted total independence. They issued leaflets called Leaflet 40 which called for Palestinians to kill Israeli soldiers and settlers, and any Palestinian "collaborators".[^672]
- Israel continued its iron fist policies. There was some public outcry against this.
- In May, soldiers used pages of the Koran as toilet paper. They were reprimanded for this.[^672]
- Israel ordered the military to keep settlers from damaging Arab property in the West Bank.[^672]
- Students in a yeshiva near Nablus killed a 13 year old girl, for which they were sent to court.[^672]
- In October, Israel allowed troops to fire on masked individuals without knowledge of whether they were involved in an incident.[^672]
- The Israeli Palestine Center for Research and Information was started to help create peace.[^672]
- Jews used to shop in the Arab bazars. They largely stopped during the intifada.[^674]
- On June 22, an Israeli Professor was stabbed to death as part of an initiation into the PLO.[^674]
- On July 6, a bus passenger grabbed the wheel and drove a bus into a ravine, killing 16.[^674]
- Following that, 24 Jews were arrested for attacking Arabs.[^674]
- On July 28, Israeli forces went in Lebanon and captured a Sheikh who was also heavily involved with Hezbollah.[^674]
- Shamir kept delaying the peace process by refusing to meet with lists of Palestinians proposed by James Baker (the US representative under George H. W. Bush involved in the process).[^675]
- The Labor Party was upset by this and in March 1990, dissolved the government in a vote of no-confidence.[^675]
- However, they were not able to establish their own Labor government as they were not able to sway the religious parties, who all sided with the right (partially because Rav Shach urged them to side with the right). As such, the right wing government stayed in control.[^675]
- In April, Arafat called on the Palestinians to kill any Jewish immigrant. This was at a time of tremendous immigration.[^675]
- In 1989, around 13,000 Soviet Jews came to Israel. In 1990, over 185,000 came. In 1991, around 150,000. This made up more than 5% of Israel's total population.[^675]
- In August 1990, Saddam Hussein, leader of Iraq, invaded Kuwait, kicking off the Persian Gulf War.[^676]
- He agreed to withdraw if Israel and Syria would withdraw from Lebanon and Israel would withdraw from the occupied territories.[^676]
- The US back Israel's decision to not withdraw.[^676]
- The leader of the Arab Democratic Party supported the Iraqi invasion.[^676]
- A US-led coalition made up of many Arab countries fought against Iraq.[^676]
- Iraq launched scud missiles at Israel trying to get Israel to join the war. However, the US urged Israel not to as the Arab countries in the coalition would not fight with Israel against other Arab countries. Israel agreed.[^676]
- 4000 apartments in Israel were destroyed and several hundred were injured, but only one person died. There was a big psychological effect as Israel thought that the missiles may contain chemicals and Israeli civilians (Jews and Arabs) were given gas masks to protect themselves.[^676]
- The US gave Israel some Patriot missile batteries to deal with the scuds. It is unclear if they helped.[^676]
- Palestinians cheered on the scud missiles as they flew overhead.[^676]
- According to Israeli numbers, by September 1991, 1225 Arab had been killed. Around 700 were killed by Israeli soldiers of which around 80 were 14 years old and younger. The rest were killed by other Arabs. 13 Israeli soldiers were killed.[^676]
- In October 1991, Israel met with a Palestinian-Jordanian coalition, as well as Syria and Lebanon in Madrid, Spain. This was coordinated by the US and the Soviet Union.[^676]
- More meetings were held in Washington, and then in various cities in different countries.[^676]
- Israel agreed to individual autonomy for the people of Palestine but not the land. The Palestinians rejected this.[^676] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I don't get what the Israeli side means.</span>}
- According to B'tselem, during the intifada 2000 Arab houses and 120,000 were destroyed. On average, 14,000 were in Israeli prisons at any given time. 4500 Arab were tortured.[^676]
- In December 1991, Shamir asked the US for a $10 billion loan to help with the former Soviet Union refugees. The US refused as they were already giving Israel $3 billion a year for weapons, and because they didn't want the money to be used house the immigrants or anyone else in the West Bank or Gaza.[^676]
- Labor won elections in June 1992. Shas party switched to the left under the advice of Rav Ovadia Yosef. Rabin, who defeated Peres in taking control of the Labor Party, became Prime Minister.[^676]
- Rabin wanted to actually make peace with the Palestinians.[^676]
- Right away, Rabin made decisions to try and pursue peace.[^676]
- The army wanted to go after some Palestinians in a school. Rabin did not grant permission.[^676]
- Within a week, Rabin put a freeze on all settler construction in the West Bank and Gaza. The right came out against this.[^676]
- Rabin had meetings with the president of Egypt and the US. The US decided to give Israel the $10 billion loan.[^676]
- In August, Israel canceled deportation orders on 11 Palestinians. A few days later, 800 Palestinians were released from Israeli prisons.[^676]
- Peres became the foreign minister in the government and he worked together with Rabin to try and make peace.[^676]
- In September, Britain agreed to end its arms and oil embargoes on Israel in place since the Lebanon War.[^676]
- With the fall of communism, travel to Eastern Europe was allowed. Many Jews began visiting the "old country". The March of the Living was started.[^677]
- Hamas and Palestinian Islamic Jihad (PIJ) were against the peace process as they didn't want any compromise. They were gaining popularity amongst the Palestinians as they also provided humanitarian aid and educational opportunities.[^677]
- In December 1992, Israel deported over 400 Hamas a PIJ leaders into Lebanon. However, Lebanon didn't want them either so they were forced to make a camp on the Lebanese side of the border.[^677]
- The media covered the difficult plight of these deportees. Some were very old. Lebanon didn't allow the Red Cross to travel through its lands to give aid.[^677]
- The UN Security Council issued resolution 799 demanding Israel let them back in. Israel ignored this.[^677]
- Israel admitted that there were some who were mistakenly deported.[^677]
- Israel and the PLO started having secret meetings near Oslo. Norway helped broker the deal. This is while the Madrid and post-Madrid talks were ongoing.[^677]
- Abu Ala'a, the PLO Finance Minister, proposed a 3 part plan:
	- Israel would withdraw from Gaza in 2-3 years. Egypt (and others) would then help run it. Meanwhile, there would be ongoing discussion about the West Bank.[^677]
	- Economic aid would be given to both the West Bank and Gaza (similar to the Marshall Plan after WWII).[^677]
	- Israel and Palestine economies would work together to increase prosperity.[^677]
- This plan was similar to what the Israelis had in mind.[^677]
- In January, 17 of the deportees were allowed back to their homes. 101 deportees were given the same offer shortly after. They rejected it to keep on the international press attention.[^677]
- According to Israel, 2/3 of Palestinians killed by the IDF were in situations that did not threaten the life of the soldiers. Israel's policy was for soldiers to aim for the legs in such situations. However, the soldiers did not follow the policy.[^677]
- Many times, children were sent to throw rocks at the soldiers.[^677]
- The negotiators at Oslo had more power than the negotiators at the post-Madrid negotiations.[^677]
- In February, Peres presented the Ala'a's plan, adding that this did not preclude eventual Palestinian autonomy (which the Americans wanted) and gave more details on economic cooperation.[^677]
- Between December 18 (the day of the deportations) and March 18, 10 Israelis had been killed by Palestinians and 30 Palestinians by Israelis.[^677]
- In 1992, 11 Israeli soldiers and 11 Israeli civilians had been killed. Israeli soldiers killed 100 Palestinians. 220 more Palestinians were killed by fellow Palestinians (mostly Hamas and PIJ).[^677]
- The talks in Oslo were progressing well. The negotiators for Oslo were more flexible than those in the post-Madrid negotiations.[^677]
- The negotiators at Oslo wanted to go with Gaza first because Israelis didn't really like being there anyway as there were many terrorist attacks.[^677]
- Part of the flaw of Camp David was that the five year timer agreed upon only began once negotiations were complete. In Oslo, the timer was to begin while negotiations were ongoing.[^677]
- Israel offered to give over Jericho in addition to Gaza to start.[^677]
- Ala'a told Israel to decide what was necessary for security in the West Bank (but "don't declare the whole West Bank a security area").[^677]
- The handover of Gaza of and Jericho was set to happen within 3-4 months of the signing of the Declaration of Principles, which happened in September 1993.[^677]
- The Israeli right first opposed this but eventually joined in.[^677]
- Arafat signed a letter denouncing terrorism and urging Palestinians to normalize relations.[^677]
- The US, now under Clinton, made a big celebration for the signing which happened in Washington.[^677]
- "One does not make peace with one's friends. One makes peace with one's enemies". Rabin in his memoirs.[^677]
- Hamas and PIJ were religious fundamentalist groups while PLO was secular. There was almost no hope of making peace with Hamas and the PIJ. Rabin, therefore, planned on strengthening the PLO over Hamas and the PIJ.[^677]
- Extremists on both sides tried to destroy the peace process.[^678]
- In February 1994, an Israeli named Baruch Goldstein killed 29 Arabs in the Mearas Hamachpeila.[^678] In retaliation, two Hamas members killed 13 people and injured 85 in bus bombings in April. After the first Hamas attack, Israel responded by closing the border to Palestine and arresting 340 Hamas members.[^679]
- According to Dr. Iyad Sarraj (a Palestinian psychiatrist), every Jew is an enemy, the suicide bomber goes to paradise and gets forgiven for all of his sins.[^679]
- Arafat broke off communications for a few weeks. However, Rabin continued to pursue the deal and eventually Arafat came back.[^678]
- Final negotiations took place in Bucharest and Cairo. May 4 was set to be the day that the deal would be signed. Late at night on May 3, Arafat started making small territorial demands, after the months of meticulous negotiations.[^678]
- Egyptian President Mubarak suggested Israel give up a small piece of land in Gaza it wanted to retain and Arafat was appeased.[^678]
- During the nationally televised signing, Arafat refused to sign part of the map near Jericho. After much cajoling (including from the President Mubarak) he finally agreed. He was showing that even though he agreed to the deal, he wasn't happy with it.[^678]
- The Cairo Agreement created a new group called the Palestinian Authority (PA) to take charge of Gaza and Jericho during the 5 year interim period. They were in charge of internal affairs. Israel remained in charge of external security and foreign agreements. Arafat became president of the PA and moved to Gaza.[^678]
- Within a few days, Arafat called for Jihad to liberate Jerusalem which shocked the Israeli public.[^678]
- Within 2 weeks, Israel pulled their soldiers out of Jericho and Gaza, following the agreement.[^678]
- 14 Israeli settlements with 5000 people were allowed to stay in Gaza. They were not under the PA.[^678]
- UN forces were placed in Hebron and Gaza to keep the peace (called the Temporary International Presence (TIP)).[^678]
- Israel started working on peace with Jordan. Jordan had 3 demands:
	- Figure out the water situation.[^678]
	- Return of the land.[^678]
	- Return of the settlers to their homes.[^678]
- Israel was willing to agree to the first two and Jordan was willing to accept.[^678]
- Economic cooperation between the two countries was organized.[^678]
- In July, Jordan signed an official peace treaty to end the war from 1948.[^678]
- A border crossing just north of Eilat and Aqaba was opened for the citizens of both countries.[^678]
- In October, an Israeli soldier names Nahshon Wachsman was kidnapped by Hamas. They offered to trade him in exchange for 200 Hamas prisoners.[^680]
- Rabin refused to negotiate with terrorists and attempted a rescue. However, Wachsman and a commando were killed in the attempt. This put a pause in the Palestine-Israel negotiations.[^680]
- Negotiations with Jordan continued. Rabin met with Jordan King Hussein in Jordan on October 16. Over the night they worked out a line between the two countries, agreed on how to divide the water, agreement to help Jordan with its international debt, as well as other things.[^680]
- On October 19, another suicide bomber killed over 20 on a bus.[^680]
- The Israeli right (such as Benjamin Netanyahu) discussed how the peace process was behind the attacks as it placed the PA in charge of dealing with Hamas in their territory. The left worried that this kind of talk would further encourage Hamas attacks.[^680]
- Israeli soldiers mistook a Palestinian for the person who built the bus bomb and killed him.[^680]
- Israel set up camps and other initiatives in Russia, encouraging them to come to Israel. In 1994, 60,000 Russians did.[^680]
- Israeli support for the peace process dropped from over 60% at the time of the signing of the Declaration of Principles to around 50%.[^680]
- Rabin decided to close off travel between Israel, and the West Bank and Gaza to stop the attacks. This made 50,000 Palestinians who worked in Israel unemployed.[^680] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I thought the border was already closed?</span>}
- On October 26, the Israel-Jordan Treaty was signed.[^680]
- {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">Don't have the lands agreed to be given over clear.</span>}
- On October 29, 2500 Israeli, Arab, American, European business and political leaders met at Casablanca to discuss business ventures in the area. A major topic of discussion was the water supply which had to be organized.[^680]
- This focus on business would help maintain relations and the peace.[^680]
- In January 1995, a suicide bomb killed around 20 soldiers and a civilian in Beit Lid. PIJ called the victims pigs and monkeys.[^681]
- Arafat took harsh measures and arrested many in Gaza.[^681]
- Rabin confided in John Major (Britain's Prime Minister) that he was most nervous about terror attacks and a lack of economic stability in the Palestine and Jordan derailing the peace process. He also asked for help with Jordan's debt.[^681]
- Major thought that economic stability would help with terror.[^681]
- In the early 90s, a lot of foreign aid from the West was being sent to eastern Europe and Russia, so less was able to be sent to the Middle East.[^681]
- Gazans were split. Some wanted to try and make peace. Others did not.[^681]
- Peres met with Arafat in Gaza on July 4 to try and continue the Peace Process. This became known as Oslo 2. Israel agreed to make a phased withdrawal of troops from most of the West Bank. The West Bank would then be under complete PA rule, other than Israeli settlements.[^681] {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">I think the agreement happened on that day. I don't think that all the negotiations went down on that one day.</span>}
- Details needed to be hammered out, which took another 3 months. Pressure was placed on Rabin to go faster, but he had always been cautious and continued to do so here. Many issues needed to be worked out, such as the parameters of the military withdrawal, the setup of Palestinian policing, the release of Palestinian prisoners, transfer of territory, and the protection of Jews in Hebron, particularly, the Mearas Hamachpeila. This last point proved to be a sticking point as Jews and Muslims considered it a holy place.[^681]
- On July 24, 6 Israelis were killed by a bomb.[^681]
- In 1994 and 1995, Israel transferred control of many aspects of civilian life to the PA. There were a few sticking points, such as Hebron, water resources, if Palestinians in East Jerusalem would be able to participate in Palestinian elections, and the release of over 1000 prisoners.[^681]
- The rights opposition to the deal grew in intensity.[^681]
- In September, Peres and Arafat met in Taba and finalized a deal there. The land was divided into 3 areas:
	- Area A (3% of the land containing around 30% of the Arab population) contained 7 large Arab cities (including Hebron, except for the Jewish part). This would be put under Palestinian control.[^681] Palestine would be in charge of internal security while Israel would be in charge of dealing with external threats and defense against terror attacks.[^682]
	- Area B contained smaller Palestinian villages (around 440 of them. Slightly less than 25% of the land).[^683] Joint Israeli and Palestinian control. Security would be joint responsibility[^683] and Palestine would be in charge of civil responsibilities.[^684] Israeli troops were to withdraw from Area A and B shortly after the agreement.[^685]
	- Area C (Around 75% of the land containing around 5% of the Arab population) the rest of the West Bank, mostly, the Israeli settlements (with 140,000 settlers).[^681] Israel was to withdraw its military within 18 months to specific locations in the West Bank.[^686] Transfer of sovereignty would occur later.[^687]
- Arabs were to have freedom of movement within the West Bank and between the West Bank and Gaza.[^688]
- On September 28 1995, Rabin and Arafat signed Oslo II in Washington.[^681]
- The right was extremely anti this. Many right wing supporters talked about assassination. The left urged for the tone to be brought down but this was ignored by the right. The right pointed out that the left was riding on the Arab party in the coalition for support.[^681]
- The Knesset debated the deal on October 5-6. With a slim 61-59 majority, the vote passed.[^681]
- During the meeting, tens of thousand of right wing supporters rallied outside of the meeting.[^681]
- A follow up meeting to Casablanca happened at Amman in October. Egypt and Jordan announced that they would remove trade barriers with Israel. Qatar offered to sell natural gas to Israel.[^681]
- On October 28, there was another anti Oslo rally in Jerusalem. Netanyahu was the main speaker.[^681]
- On November 4, Peres and Rabin had a pro peace rally. Near the end of the rally, Rabin was shot and killed.[^681]
- Rabin's killer was a student at Bar-Ilan University named Yigal Amir. He was extremely against the peace process.[^689]
- Peres became Prime Minister on November 4.[^689]
- The funeral was well attended. King Hussein, President Mubarak, and President Clinton came (all gave eulogies, as well). Qatar, Oman, Morocco sent high government officials. Over 80 countries had representatives there.[^689]
- It was decided that it would be best for Arafat not to come. He came to console Rabin's widow a few days later, his first trip into Israel.[^689]
- In January 1996, Yahya Ayyash, an explosive maker for Hamas, known as The Engineer, was killed by Israel.[^690]
- In February, two suicide bomber killed 26 Israelis.[^690]
- Arafat's advisor condemned the killings.[^690]
- In March, another suicide bus bomber killed 13 more.[^690]
- The next day, another suicide bomber killed 18 more in Tel Aviv.[^690]
- Peres set up specific crossing points for vehicles traveling from the West Bank and Gaza into Israel and told Arafat that he better tighten up control of Hamas.[^690]
- The PA allowed solidarity meetings in Gaza for the death of Yahya Ayyash and Arafat consoled his parents. This did not go over well with Israel.[^690]
- Despite the death of Rabin, Peres still went through with the agreement and withdrew Israeli troops from Area A except for Hebron and the whole Area B.[^690]
- Tanaim overlapped with the last of the neviim. Rebbi Dosa Ben Hurkinus[^691] and Rebbi Tzadok[^692] discussed where Chagai sat when giving over certain halachos. Rebbi Dosa lived after the churban at the times of Rebbi Akiva[^691] [^693] and Rebbi Tzadok lived during the churban.[^694] (It seems that Chagai lived a very long life.)
- Israel, Egypt, the US, and many others, had a meeting in Sharm El-Sheik, about how to stop terrorism.[^695]
- By this time, 100 Israelis had been killed and 500 injured in terror attacks.[^695]
- Libya and Iran were the large backers of terrorism.[^695]
- Peres tried working out peace with Syria. He was willing to trade nearly all of the Golan Heights for peace with Syria.[^695]
- Because of growing support for Likud, Peres called for elections to see if he still held a mandate from the people to pursue peace.[^695]
- Some shelling from terrorists in Lebanon injured 3 Israelis. In response, Israel responded with Operation Grapes of Wrath. (This was right before the election.)[^695]
- Over 100 Lebanese civilians were killed, including a blown up ambulance and a civilian shelter. Israel received worldwide condemnation and pulled out. Likud decried that fact that Peres gave in to international pressure.[^695]
- Netanyahu ran for the Likud on the slogan of "peace with security".[^695]
- Religious parties back Netanyahu.[^695]
- There was a change in the voting system: Prime Minister and party were selected separately. As such, the party with the most votes would not necessarily have the Prime Minister.[^695]
- The election was in May 1996. Labor got slightly more seats, but Netanyahu won the Prime Minister vote (by less than 1%). He made a coalition without Labor.[^695]
- Religious parties claimed that the supreme court was biased against them. The court denied it.[^695]
- Peres continued fighting to make peace.[^695]
