- Netanyahu delayed the peace process as Arafat was not upholding his end of the bargain. He wasn't doing enough to combat terrorism. (According to the right.)[^695]
- Netanyahu opened a tunnel near the Temple Mount that Peres had been in negotiations with the Palestinians about. This sparked anger in the Palestinians. 15 Israeli soldiers and 56 Palestinians were killed in fighting afterwards.[^695]
- Clinton invited Netanyahu, King Hussein and Arafat to Washington to try and keep things moving but it was not successful.[^695]
- In January 1997, the Hebron Agreement was signed. 80% of the city was transferred to Palestinian control. In addition, Israel agreed to withdraw troops from Area C (except for specific locations) by August 1998. The original deal had called for the withdrawal by September 1997.[^695]
- Once the dayan knows the din, it is wrong for him to wait before delivering it.[^696]
- (Mentions the medrash that even if someone has already seen a specific case, one should reexamine it.)[^697]
- Dayanus is mentioned as the thing to take ones time because it is complex and important. It is the "main part" of the Torah. (Brings the medrash that mishpat is mentioned before and after aseres hadibros.)[^697]
- A dayan becomes a partner with Hashem in the creation of the world.[^698]
- One should take one's time when delivering  any teaching.[^697]
- By 1997, 400 Israelis had been killed in Lebanon since the end of the Lebanon and over 1400 injured. Many Israelis were pushing to find a way to pull out without sacrificing security.[^699]
- In March, a Jordanian soldier went rogue and killed 7 Israeli schoolgirls. King Hussein personally went to console the families.[^699]
- The next day, Netanyahu decided to begin a construction project in the neighborhood of Har Homa (in southern Jerusalem). This angered the Arabs there and was condemned by UN (except for the US and Micronesia). This violated the Oslo Accords that called for no changes in Jerusalem until a compromise was reached.[^699]
- In response, the PA threatened any Arabs who sold land in the Har Homa area to Jews with death (which they carried out a few times).[^699]
- Arye Deri (head of Shas Party) made a deal with Roni Bar-On. Deri promised to vote for Bar-On as Attorney General. In exchange, Bar-On would acquit Deri (who was under investigation).[^700]
- This was exposed and Deri was indicted. People thought that other members of the government should be indicted, including Netanyahu, but this was not the case. Netanyahu accused the left-wing media of trying to undermine him.[^700]
- In May (I think) 1997 The government passed a law formally only recognizing conversions done by Orthodox rabbis. The left strongly protested this.[^700]
- Many Mizrachi Jews lived in the poor areas of towns. Organizations were created to try and integrate them into mainstream society.[^701]
- On Memorial Day in Israel, everyone in the nation is supposed to stop and stand to pay their respects for those who have been killed.[^701]
- In 1997, many religious Jews ignored this. This was in retaliation for the national disrespect for halacha, like shabbos.[^701]
- Rabbi Lau (Chief Rabbi of Israel) denounced this because it created a strong rift.[^701]
- In June 1997, religious Jews attacked non-religious Jews at the Kotel. Soon after, a pub (called The Gotham) that was open on shabbos was attacked.[^701]
- In 1997, Israel had a population of 5.7 million. Around 80% were Jews, around 15% Muslim Arabs, 3% Christian Arabs, 2% Druze, and less than .1% Armenian. 1.8 Arabs lived in the West Bank and Gaza, mostly Muslim.[^701]
- In the summer of 1997, it was revealed that Israel was not renewing visas for Arabs living in Jerusalem. The was done when the "center of life" changed out of Jerusalem. The reason for this was to try to clear the Arabs out of West Jerusalem.[^702]
- Building in the West Bank continued. Subsidies were offered for communities in the West Bank (this was a policy that began under Shamir and was canceled under Rabin and Peres) .In a speech, Netanyahu said that he was not willing to limit Israel to pre-1967 borders.[^702]
- On June 10, Haredi rioters attacked conservative Jews, Arabs, and the police sent to stop them.[^702]
- In June, the right wing considered giving some autonomy in the West Bank. 40% of the land (nearly all of the land where Arabs lived) would be ruled by the PA. They would not have statehood (I don't know what the difference would be) and wouldn't share a border with Jordan.[^703]
- The land that Israel would retain would mostly be along the Israeli and Jordanian borders, with a strip of land connecting them. Israel would also have some east-west highways running across the Palestinian territory. Israel would retain control of the aquifers in the region. Israel would also have a buffer around Jerusalem. They would keep East Jerusalem. This was largely based on something called the Baily Plan.[^703]
- Israel wanted the land along the Jordanian border for defense purposes (similar to the Alon Plan).[^703]
- The Palestinians were against this but the government didn't discuss it with them. They were going to force it upon the Palestinians.[^703]
- This was a major shift from Oslo which offered 80% of the land.[^703]
- Jerusalem Mayor Ehud Olmert led the bulldozing of Arab houses in Jerusalem due to a lack of permits. The left said that permits were being withheld from Arabs.[^703]
- In July 1997, suicide bombers killed 16 Israelis (including some Arabs) in Mahane Yehuda. Israel responded by cutting off funding for the PA (which had been agreed to at Oslo. Israel paid for 60% of the PA budget) and stopped allowing Palestinian workers into Israel.[^703]
- The right said that the PA was not fulfilling its agreement to root out terrorism and they were not going to give money when there were Israelis getting killed. The left claimed that the PA was fighting against terrorism.[^703]
- Some people blamed the attacks on the poor situation in Gaza/the West Bank.[^703]
- Russia sold missile technology to Iran (which would potentially give them ballistic missiles). Israel asked the US and UK to intercede.[^704]
- In fall 1997, Israelis started moving into homes purchased in Rad al-Amud, a nearly entirely Arab area close to the Dome of the Rock. This further escalated tensions.[^704]
- Under US pressure, Israel agreed to move the families out and move some yeshiva students (with security) there instead.[^704]
- Shortly after, Netanyahu announced an expansion in the Etzion bloc. The US expressed their dislike about this. Israel said that this would stop when the PA took stopping terror more seriously.[^704]
- In September, the Mossad attempted to assassinate a Hamas leader in Jordan. The agents injected the poison but they were caught. King Abdullah (who was afraid of a Hamas uprising in his country[^705]) demanded that Israel give over the antidote and free Sheikh Ahmad Yassin. Because of US and Jordanian pressure, Israel agreed.[^706][^704]
- In May 2000, Israel withdrew from Lebanon due to internal pressure. They attempted to reach an agreement with Lebanon and Syria on security prior to the pullout but were unable to (Syria refused to talk at all).[^707]
- In July 2000 Clinton, Ehud Barak, and Arafat met in Camp David.[^707]
- During the Camp David negotiations, tensions were heightened when Arial Sharon came with police to the Al-Aqsa Mosque to stop alleged actions that were destroying ancient artifacts. 4 Arabs were killed in the protests that followed. This kicked off the Second Intifada.[^707]
- Israel offered 100% of Gaza and 91% of the West Bank, with some land swaps.[^707]
- Jewish and Arab parts of Jerusalem would be under their governments' control.[^707]
- Refugees would get integrated into the country that they were currently in or go to on of many different countries which offered to accept many of the refugees (Canada offered to take in many of them).[^707]
- Late in the negotiations, Arafat insisted that all refugees and their descendants be allowed to return to their original homes, many of which were part of Israeli cities. Israel said that taking in this many Palestinians would drastically change Israeli society and refused.[^707]
- Some (including Israel's Foreign Minister at the time) blame Israel for the failure of the negotiations. They say that Israel:
	- Demanded too much land (9% of the West Bank. They viewed it from the perspective that the starting assumption was that the Palestinians get the whole West Bank).[^708]
	- Israel demanded control of the Al-Aqsa Mosque area.[^708]
	- Palestinian refugees were not going to be allowed back to their homes.[^708]
- Clinton and other American leaders involved in the negotiations blamed Arafat.[^709]
- Talks were continued in January 2001 at Taba. However, they had to be broken off before much progress was made due to elections in Israel.[^707]
- Sharon won the elections in February by a landslide and became Prime Minister.[^707]
- Since the 30 Years War beginning around year 1600, there has been a Great Power conflict every 50 years or less. Arguably, this has been happening since ancient times (although it becomes harder to define what a Great Power is).[^710]
- The two exception to this are the times between the French Revolutionary/Napoleonic Wars and WWI (1815-1914) and now (1945 to present).[^710]
- Great Power conflicts kill millions of people and divert extreme amounts of resources. Even the winning side loses due to the scale of destruction on all sides.[^710]
- Sharon, in his opening speech, came off as much more pro peace than he had seemed previously (although he talked about security in the speech a lot as well).[^710]
- The Second Intifada continued. In 2001, Over 200 Israelis were killed in attacks. A large majority were civilians.[^711]
- Iran gave money to families of suicide bombers.[^711]
- Israel responded with a policy of "targeted assassinations" where they killed terrorist organizers in the West Bank and abroad.[^711] The civilian/militant ratio was 1:1 or better.[^712]
- Most of the Israeli government was in favor of this policy (such as Rabin's daughter who was part of the government), although some were against as it just bred more hatred of Israel. The US was not pleased with the policy.[^711]
- In March and April 2002, there were a string of suicide bomb attacks. Israel responded with Operation Defensive Shield. In this operation, Israel reoccupied all Arab towns in the West Bank, particularly, Jenin, which was known as "The Martyrs' Capital" to Palestinians.[^711]
- There was a fight between Israeli soldiers and Arab soldiers there. Arabs claimed that Israel led a massacre in Jenin and killed over 500 civilians. Amnesty International, Human Rights Watch, and the UN denied this. The UN put the death toll at around 50, with most being soldiers. There was no evidence that Israel purposely targeted civilians found.[^711]
- In 2002, around 350 Israelis were killed and close to 1500 people were injured.[^711]
- In June 2002, President Bush issued his Road Map for Peace which would occur in phases.[^713] This can be summarized as:
	- End the violence.[^713][^714]
	- Halt settlement activity.[^713][^714]
	- Reform Palestinian institutions (like have elections, and replace Arafat).[^713]
	- Give economic aid to Palestine to help build the economy.[^713][^714]
	- Figure out other issues, like water usage.[^713][^714]
	- Resolve the refugee problem.[^713][^714]
	- Create peace between Israel and the surrounding Arab countries.[^713][^714]
	- Establish borders for a Palestinian state (including figuring out Jerusalem).[^713][^714]
- This was supposed to occur by 2005.[^713][^714]
- Beis Shamai[^715] and Rabban Gamliel[^716] thought that Torah should only be taught be the best talmidim. Beis Hillel[^715] and Rebbi Elazar[^716] thought it should be taught to the masses. Out mishna paskens like Beis Hillel and Rebbi Elazar.[^717]
- Different gemaros[^718][^719] talk about how teaching Torah to unworthy talmidim is bad. However, this only refers to talmidim who actively fight against the Torah, not talmidim who are simply lacking certain middos/not the most intelligent.[^717]
- One should make sure to teach Torah to many talmidim and one should keep teaching talmidim, even when one is older and already has many successful talmidim as one can't ever know who is going to be successful and who won't be.[^717]
- Arafat appointed Mahmoud Abbas as the Prime Minister of the PLO in March.[^713]
- By the end of April, the Road Map was released to the public.[^713]
- In June, Abbas, Sharon and Bush met in Aqaba. Arafat was not invited. He was viewed by the US and Israel as not wanting peace.[^713]
- Israel released 100 Palestinian prisoners. Abbas pledged to end terrorist organizations.[^713]
- Over the next few days, Bush met with numerous heads of other Arab countries.[^713]
- Hamas was against the deal and pledged to continue their violence. On the day that Bush left, 2 Israelis were found dead. A few days later, 4 Israeli soldiers were killed. Israel then attempted to assassinate a Hamas leader but missed and killed 2 bystanders. Hamas responded with a suicide bomb that killed 17 Israelis.[^713]
- In late June, Hamas, PIJ, and other terrorist organizations announced a 3 month ceasefire. The PLO announced a 6 month ceasefire. This was done on condition that Israel release Palestinian prisoners (which was not part of the roadmap). Israel agreed. In addition, Israel pulled troops out of North Gaza and Bethlehem. The US gave $30 million to the PA.[^713]
- Despite this, over 180 Israelis and around 600 Palestinians were killed in 2003.[^720]
- In March 2004, Sheikh Ahmed Yassin, a paraplegic and Hamas leader was assassinated. Israel was condemned for this by much of the world. America was concerned about how this would affect the peace process.[^713]
- There was much outrage in the Arab world and this increased Hamas's popularity.[^713]
- In April, Abdel Aziz al-Rantisi, the next head of Hamas was also killed.[^713]
- Women and minors were used in suicide bombings.[^713]
- In September, an Israeli woman was killed. Israel responded by demolishing 20 Arab homes in Khan Yunis. A rocket killed 2 Israeli children. An Israeli airstrike killed 34 Palestinian civilians. 2 more Israelis were killed. 3 Palestinian collaborators were killed by fellow Palestinians. 5 Israeli border police were indicted for abuse.[^713]
- In November, Arafat died. Abbas officially took over the PA.[^713]
- By September 2004, 1000 Israelis had been killed. Around 2000 had been severely injured. Over 2700 Palestinians had been killed. Around 1200 had been planning or carrying out acts of terror. Over 1500 were not.[^713]
- Military spending as a percentage of all government spending was about 90%. In the 1600s, this started dropping until about 50% by the French Revolutionary/Napoleonic Wars. After that, though, it has continued to drop until about 14% today. This has allowed governments to actually spend money to help the people.[^721]
- The peace wasn't because there were no potential conflicts that could have flared up. It was simply that governments were more scared. They appreciated the price of failure. The French Revolutionary/Napoleonic Wars can be considered a world war.[^721]
- 3 different schools of thought about how to achieve peace: The Realists, the Idealists (or Liberals), and the Marxists.[^722]
- Realists:
	- The world is naturally in anarchy.[^722]
	- States act rationally.[^722]
	- If given incentives for peace/incentives against war, there will be peace.[^722]
	- War is about power, meaning, a powerful country can and will take a weak country.
	- The way to prevent this is by making sure that strong countries can't take advantage of weak countries. This is done by making sure that strong countries are not near weak countries, and by making it clear to all countries that an act of aggression will elicit a united response from many other countries.[^722]
	- (I think that the point about anarchy explains the next few points.)
	- Political revolution and upheaval is a bad thing that contributes to war.[^722]
	- As such, secret police and restrictions on free speech should be used to prevent this.[^722]
	- All wars are bad (I think because they have a potential to spiral out of control).[^722]
	- The goal is no wars at all (although this is be unreasonable).[^722]
	- The era after the French Revolutionary/Napoleonic Wars was full of realists.[^722]
- Idealists (Liberals):
	- Political difference between two countries is what creates wars.[^722]
	- Some have used a goal of political alignment as a basis for war.[^722]
	- They are more hopeful and think that war may be able to be totally ended.[^722]
	- The French Revolution can be described as an explosion of Liberalism. Anyone who did not support the new French Republic was an enemy.[^722]
- Marxists:
	- War is a product of economic inequality. Reducing this inequality leads to peace.[^722]
- Realists see trouble when a strong state is next to a weak state. Liberals worry when a closed state is next to an open state and Marxists worry when a rich state is next to a poor state.[^722]
- All of these can be true at the same time.[^722]
- In September 2002, Israel decided to build a wall between Israeli and Arab areas in the West Bank and between the West Bank and Israel. The wall was 20+ feet tall, had electronic monitoring stations, and barbed wire.[^723]
- 7000 acres of Palestinian land was put on the Israeli side of the wall, as well as over 400,000 Palestinians (most in Jerusalem). This made life difficult for those Palestinians as some were cut off from schools and work. Entrances through the wall were far apart.[^723]
- The wall was effective. As the wall was being built, suicide attacks dropped. By December 2004, suicide bombings had dropped over 80%.[^723]
- In June 2004, Israel decided to pull the settlers in Gaza out. There were over 8000 settlers. Each settler was promised $200,000+ and were potentially arrested if they didn't leave.[^723]
- Sometimes, kids would be used to launch rockets into Israel. On October 6 or 7, 2004 (source wasn't clear) 2 boys ages 13 and 14 were killed by Israel when launching a rocket.[^723]
- In February, Sharon and Abbas agreed to a ceasefire at Sharm el-Sheik. In March, Israeli forces pulled out of Jericho and Tulkarm and opened up an entrance in the wall next to Tulkarm.[^723]
- Numerous organizations were started to try and promote peace such as:
	- Seeking Common Ground (started in 2007. Had Arabs and Israelis visit schools/orphanages in Israel and Palestine).[^723]
	- New Israel Fund (started in 1979).[^723]
	- Project COPE (started in 2002. Had Israeli and Arab breast cancer patients meet and talk and provide moral support for each other).[^723]
	- Peres Center for Peace (started in 1996. Created joint soccer leagues, helped with Palestinian farming in Gaza, and organized business meetings between Israelis and Palestinians (the Palestinian economy was in shambles)).[^723]
	- Saving Children Project (Palestinian doctors worked and got training in Israeli hospitals to take their skills back to Palestine).[^723]
	- Peace in Sight (started in 2003. Palestinians patients received treatment at Hadassah Hospital).[^723]
- Continued Israeli military activity strengthened bad feelings and hurt the economy.[^723]
- Other members of Likud (such as Netanyahu) disagreed with Sharon's withdrawal from Gaza.[^724]
- In January 2005, Hamas pledged an informal ceasefire with Israel. 9 months went by without an suicide attack.[^724]
- In October (a month after the Israeli pullout of Gaza was complete), the President of Iran Mahmoud Ahmadinejad called for the destruction of Israel. At the time, Iran was trying to create a nuclear bomb.[^724]
- To counter, Israel set up a satellite to monitor Iran (and Syria).[^724]
- Sharon pledged to pull out of the West Bank as well. Netanyahu and much of Likud were against this.[^724]
- Because of this, in November, Sharon resigned as head of Likud and made his own party called Kadima ("forward"). Kadima wanted to create a two-state solution. Members of Likud (such as Ehud Olmert) and the Labor Party (such as Peres) joined the coalition.[^724]
- The Labor Party pulled out of a coalition with Kadima to force elections. Before elections could happen, though, in January 2006, Sharon suffered a stroke and fell into a coma.[^724] He would never recover and died in 2014.[^725]
- Olmert became the Acting Prime Minister. In January, Olmert said that Israel would give back much of the West Bank and would give Palestine autonomous rule even before negotiations were finished if there would be peace.[^726]
- In February, Olmert had 6 illegal houses in Amona demolished.[^726]
- In elections for the Palestinian Legislative Council in January, Hamas won a majority of seats (76/132) by a landslide.[^726]
- Ismail Haniyeh (a Hamas leader) was appointed Prime Minister.[^726]
- The US, Russia, EU, and UN, demanded that Palestine accept Israel's right to exist and give up armed resistance to continue to receive aid. Hamas refused and aid from the US, the EU, and Israel was cut off.[^726]
- Fighting broke out between Hamas and Fatah.[^726]
- In the end, Hamas agreed to form a unity government with Fatah. The government accepted the conditions, although Hama still did not.[^726]
- (The following is the DoD Law of War Manual. This is the US interpretation of the Laws of War. It obviously is not binding on other countries so I am not sure if it is relevant. However, may be a pretty good base for international law.)
- Principles of war are more general than laws of war. As such, they can be interpreted differently. They can be used to help interpret and apply laws and can be used when there are no specific laws.[^727]
- Principle of Military Necessity:
- Measures needed to defeat the enemy as quickly and efficiently as possible.[^728]
- This principle allows for destroying military targets and subduing an enemy (killing, capturing, etc.).[^729]
- This cannot be used to violate the laws of war. Laws of war were constructed to account for military necessity. However, some laws explicitly call for exceptions in situations when something is deemed necessary or allow a party to delay doing something if it is a military necessity to wait.[^730]
- It is somewhat difficult to define what military necessity is. A few principles:
	- One may make a decision based on the overall goal of winning the war as efficiently as possible, not accomplish a specific objective.[^731]
	- Some say that armies should attempt to do the minimum necessary. One should capture the enemy rather than wound, or wound rather than kill. However, this does not reflect customary international law, nor the DoD law. (I think that if all things are equal, one should try to capture rather than wound and wound rather than kill. However, the threshold for deciding that the risk of losing the target or risk to the attacking force is too great, is a low bar.)[^731]
	- Attacking enemies, not giving enemies the chance to surrender or warning, and taking POWs are generally all legal actions one can take in war (subject to specific laws). [^731]
	- The general assumption is that these acts are necessary unless it is clear that they are not.[^731]
	- Obviously, a decision one makes can't be judged on information that one didn't have yet.[^731]
- In the Israeli elections in March, Kadima won and was able to make a coalition with the Labor Party, Shas, and others, and excluded Likud. Olmert became Prime Minister.[^732]
- In May, Abbas demanded that Hamas accept Israeli borders. Hamas refused. This led to Abbas consolidating his position in the West Bank with Hamas fighting and taking over Gaza.[^732]
- The Israeli army entered Gaza to help Fatah.[^732]
- In June, Gilad Shalit was captured.[^732]
- Hamas demanded that Israel release all female prisoners and minors. Israel refused and upped their forces in the Gaza Strip. Abbas tried to get Hamas to release Shalit but it did not work.[^732]
- Hamas largely launched rockets into Israel. In July, Hezbollah started launching rockets, as well. Israel responded by blockading Lebanon, bombing the Beirut airport, and striking military targets.[^732]
- Hezbollah captured 2 Israeli soldiers, Ohad Goldwasser and Ehud Regev.[^732]
- Hundreds of Hezbollah soldiers as well as hundreds of civilians were killed. Israel accused Hezbollah of using human shields.[^732]
- The UN denounced Israel and said they were using disproportionate force and demanded a ceasefire. Israel rejected this as rockets were still coming.[^732]
- Israel mostly fought using airstrikes rather than a ground invasion.[^732]
- In late July, Israel bombed an apartment with nearly 60 civilians in Kfar Kana from which rockets had been launched. This led to increased outrage against Israel.[^732]
- On August 14, UN Security Council Resolution 1701 (a ceasefire) came into effect. Within a week, Israeli troops pulled out of Lebanon.[^732]
- The Lebanese Army and 15,000 international troops occupied the southern part of Lebanon to keep Hezbollah quiet.[^732] However, Hezbollah was not dismantled.[^733]
- Around one million Israeli civilians had slept in shelters, or were forced to leave the area. Nearly 120 Israeli soldiers and around 40 civilians were killed. 270 Hezbollah fighters and over 1000 Lebanese civilians were killed.[^732]
- Human Rights Watch and other NGOs said both sides had committed war crimes.[^732]
- Israel commissioned a report on the war by Judge Eliyahu Winograd (the Winograd Commission).[^734]
- Arabs in Israel made less money, had worse jobs and were housed in worse neighborhoods. Arab educational institutions received less State funding.[^732]
- In 2006, there were numerous scandals in the Israeli government. In addition, prisons for Israelis were in terrible condition.[^735]
- Fatah and Hamas fought in Gaza. Hamas also continued firing rockets into Israel, which brought Israeli strikes.[^735]
- Hapoel Tel Aviv (an Israeli soccer team) created a soccer league for Palestinian children to be trained by Israeli coaches to try and normalize relations.[^735]
- The Winograd Commission was extremely critical of Olmert, Defense Minister Amir Peretz (who was also the head of the Labor Party), and Chief of Staff Dan Halutz. They were criticized over the ineffectiveness of Israel's army which was blamed on poor planning.[^735]
- Halutz resigned and Peretz lost in the following Labor Party election to Ehud Barak, who then replaced him as Defense Minister.[^735]
- The report also highlighted the fact that many people did not enroll in the military or left service early. Israel passed legislation to deprive these people of state funds.[^735]
- A organization called the Portland Trust started building projects in the West Bank to try and help with the housing and the economy, and foster Palestinian-Israeli relationships.[^735]
- Hamas harassed or killed many Muslim moderates in Gaza.[^735]
- In June, Abbas declared a state of emergency, cut off ties with Hamas and removed Hamas from the PA government, making Salam Fayyad the Prime Minister (the Finance Minister and former Vice President of the World Bank. Highly regarded by the West). The EU, US, and Israel started giving money to the PA.[^735]
- Olmert, Abbas, King Hussein, Egyptian President Mubarak, and Tony Blair (who was no longer the UK PM, and was now the Middle East envoy of the Quartet (US, EU, Russia, UN)) had meetings together about creating a Palestinian state. In June, Israel released 250 Fatah prisoners and gave $500 million in tax revenue that was meant for the PA.[^735]
- Some settlers were removed from Hebron. Soldiers that tried to stop the removal were court marshaled.[^735]
- Barak was against this peace deal. He thought that the West Bank was necessary for security. He did not want to get rid of the checkpoints.[^735]
- Peres was the President and he was very much in favor of peace.[^735]
- In August, PA police rescued an Israeli soldier who accidentally drove into Jenin. This led to further hope that Abbas could lead Palestine to peace.[^735]
- In August, 2007, Olmert agreed to dismantle over 25 illegal settlements.[^735]
- The Israeli Supreme Court has forced the building of the Wall to circumvent Palestinian villages.[^735]
- In a meeting the Annapolis, Maryland, Israel and 15 Arab states met to discuss the creation of a Palestinian state, figure out a solution to the refugee problem, Jerusalem, and the Israeli settlements.[^736]
- In December (shortly after the meetings) Fatah policemen murdered an Israeli settler. This didn't sit well with Israelis. Hamas was launching missiles as well. Despite this, Olmert and Abbas agreed to keep meeting.[^736]
- In December, Olmert released 400 Palestinian prisoners in Israel (most of whom were convicted terrorists).[^736]
- A US-led commission was agreed upon to decide when steps in the Road Map were completed and to indicate to move on to the next step.[^736]
- Arab states, along with the Portland Trust, would build up the economy in the Palestinian State.[^736]
- One goal was to weaken Hamas's influence (partially through the PA's setup of the economic assistance).[^736]
- Olmert and Abbas made offers to each other between 1%-6% Israeli control of the West Bank, with nearly equal sized land swaps in Israel. Jerusalem would be divided, with the Israeli and most Arab parts rules by their respective governments and partially by an international committee.[^737]
- Olmert wanted to Abbas to sign on the day the map was proposed (not sure why). Abbas refused as he wanted time to review the map.[^738]
- The deal broke down as Olmert faced corruption charges in Israel and eventually he was forced to resign.[^738]
- (Mentions how keeping dirabanans shows one's care for the mitzvos more than keeping the mitzvos themselves.)[^739]
- (Mentions that because dirabanans keep the Torah safe, Hashem has a special love for dirabanans.)[^739]
- Rebbi Tarfon said Krias Shema while laying down, rather than sitting like Beis Hillel holds (and the way we pasken). The gemara says that he was "chayav misah" (doesn't seem to be literal). (It seems that this is because he didn't have proper appreciation for the words of Beis Hillel.)[^740]
- Rebbi Akiva nearly died because he wasn't given enough water to wash before eating bread (a dirabanan).[^741]
- The rabanan didn't reveal all of the reasons behind why they made certain gezeiros. This is because they were afraid it wouldn't be transmitted correctly, or perhaps it would be, but people would try to do things which one can easily make a mistake and be nichshol (for example, one is allowed to carry in reshus harabim less than 4 amos, but the rabanan didn't want to say it because they were afraid people would try it and accidentally carry too far).[^742]
- One Minute Praising:
- When someone begins working for the company or starts a new project, keep a close eye on them (physically, or have them write what they are doing).[^743]
- When they do something positive, go over to them and tell them. Catch them doing something right.[^743]
- Be specific. Tell them exactly what they did right and how it made you feel.[^743]
- Perhaps add some physical contact (arm around shoulder, handshake, etc).[^743]
- Make sure to not be affected by other things going on in the company. Even if other things aren't going well, make sure the compliment remain the same.[^743]
- This doesn't need to take very long.[^743]
- By doing this, this allows you to be straight with your employees when they don't do something well. You are always straight with them.[^743]
- Over time (as the project continues or the person is in the company for some time), you can back off of this somewhat.[^743]
- An employee can and should praise themselves for a job well done (particularly experienced employees who may receive less of it from the manager).[^744]
- When someone makes a mistake, the manager should come in right when he hears about it. Don't wait around.[^744]
- Be straight about what the mistake is and how it made you feel.[^744]
- Let it sink in for a few seconds. This whole process shouldn't take more than a minute.[^744]
- The reprimand should be entirely about the action that was done, not about the nature of the employee.[^744]
- After this, give praise. Explain that you know that the employee can do better and that you have higher expectations. Also, perhaps give physical touch (arm on shoulder, touch their arm, etc).[^744]
- Employees should be warned ahead of time that you are going to be straight with them.[^744]
- Be consistent. The reprimand should be the same regardless about what else is happening in the company.[^744]
- It is important for a manager to laugh at his or her own mistakes and not get eaten up by them. This sets a good example for the other employees when they make a mistake.[^744]
- "One minute" doesn't literally mean one minute (although it often does).[^745]
- Managers need to have the mentality to invest in their people.[^745]
- People thrive on having goals and having clear feedback about them. That is why it is important to provide this. Think about bowling without pins. That, obviously, would not be fun.[^745]
- Some managers don't want everyone to succeed as they have to give performance reviews for the employees under them and it would look bad for the manager if everyone did well.[^745]
- Managers need to realize that all of their employees can do well if given the right direction.[^745]
- Even when you hire talented employees (or your employees get trained in well), you still need to go over goals with them. They need to know what to do.[^746]
- It is important for goals to be written short. People need to read them and reread them and need to check their actions against their goals. If it is too long, nobody is going to look at it.[^746]
- When we train animals or children, we give them rewards for taking incremental steps. 
- First, we give them a reward for taking a small step in the right direction. We stick at this for a while.[^747]
- Eventually, we stop giving them a reward for this and wait until they can complete another small step. We stick at this for a while.[^747]
- Eventually, they can accomplish the total goal.[^747]
- Obviously, adults are not animals/children but the concept is still the same.[^747]
- Some managers only give reward when the employee does the whole thing. Even worse, managers may punish the employee for not getting to the whole goal. This just demoralizes employees.[^747]
- There is very little reprimanding of new employees. Instead, we review the goals.[^747]
- One Minute Reprimands work because they are immediate. Some managers "store up" mistakes, or only discuss mistakes in a yearly review report. This can hurt the employees ego which gets them demotivated and they won't want to improve from the criticism. In addition, with so many things brought up, they get overwhelmed and don't review what mistakes they made.[^748]
- Start with the reprimand and then give them the praise. (I think that now people say to do the "sandwich": compliment, reprimand, then compliment).[^748]
- The manager needs to make sure that the employee feels that the manager cares about him and wants him to succeed. This is especially important when having physical contact with the employee to encourage them. If he doesn't feel this connection, the employee will feel uncomfortable with touch.[^748]
- Only talk about behavior, not the person.[^748]
- Don't give reprimands based off hearsay. Only give reprimands on things you see.[^748]
- Making goals starts good behavior. Having consequences maintains good behavior.[^748]
- Be upfront. Tell your employees that you are going to do this reprimand routine.[^748]
- If you aren't sure that you are going to be a perfect One Minute Manager, you can be upfront with your employees but that you will try. Then, you actually need to try.[^749] You have to be open to criticism from your employees.[^7]
- Even though you are getting people to do what you want, they don't feel manipulated. Manipulation is when you secretly get people to do things they don't want. Since you care about the employee and you are upfront, they don't feel manipulated.[^748]
- This system of using the Uruk King List and starting Alexander's reign in 318 allows explains why year 311 is so significant. It is the year that he dies and the whole empire is divided four ways (as discussed in Daniel (11:4)).[^750]
- One Astronomic Diary (BM 34616) says that year 302 is year 4 of a king's reign. According to conventional history, year 302 is year 10 of Seleucus I.[^750]
- If we start in year 318 and follow the Uruk King List, we end up in year 4 of Antigonus (see table). (I assume that secular historians would say that this is just a mistake.)[^750]
- Based on the evidence presented, it seems that a mistake crept into the King Lists. The diaries were then changed to reflect the king lists.[^750]
- The Astronomical Diaries are the source that Darius III's last year was 331 BC. And there is no source that mentions any king ruling Darius and Alexander. As such, it seems that that the Diaries back to the Persian period were adjusted.[^750]
- There are relatively few tablets from somewhat near the end of the Persian period.[^750]
- The first 100 years of the Greek Empire after conquering the Persian's also has very little writing that survived. This is despite the fact that it is documented that there was a lot written at the time.[^750] (This will be discussed later.)
- The Torah shebiksav was given over to all shevatim (Moshe wrote a sefer Torah for each shevet on the last day of his life). The mesorah for Torah Shebaal Peh was carried through the leaders, as the mishna says. (Obviously others knew Torah Shebaal Peh as well. I think it means that the leader was the final authority on it. As opposed to Toah Shebiksav that you can just look at the Sefer Torah.)[^751]
- The mesorah of Torah is extremely important because Torah is beyond human logic. We are not able to figure it out. The only way we have the Torah is through the mesorah.[^752]
- A person should establish many talmidim to be able to talk over psakim with them,[^753] because they will ask questions and sharpen the rebbi's understanding (ומתלמידי יותר מכולם),[^754] and because having more talmidim increases קנאת ספרים תרבה חכמה.<span style="visibility:hidden">l</span>[^755]
- Judges should first try and reach a compromise between the parties. When that doesn't work, the judge should discuss the case with many talmidim (which he should make sure to have) to arrive at the correct din. After, beis din should create safeguards to make sure that people don't violate the din. (This is an understanding of how to read the mishna as one long piece of advice.)[^756]
- Discrepancies:
- According to Seder Olam (written 2nd century CE), Alexander conquered the Persians in year 318 BC. According to Claudius Ptolemy (in 2nd century CE), this happened in year 331 BC.
- According to Ezra 4:24, the Beis Hamikdash was built in the second year of the reign of Darius the Great. According to Seder Olam, Darius was defeated 34 years later by Alexander. As such, it is clear the the Beis Hamikdash was built in year 352 BCE (34 years before Alexander conquered the Persians in year 318). As such, the Hamikdash lasted 420 years (as it was destroyed in year 69 according to Avodah Zarah 9a. Also, there is no year 0. We go from 1 BCE to 1 CE).[^757]
- According to conventional chronology, Darius the Great began his reign in year 519 BCE (making the Beis Hamikdash built in year 520 BC) and Alexander conquered Darius III instead, in year 331. This was 189 years.
- According to conventional history, the Beis Hamikdash lasted 589 years (from the second year of Darius the Great in 520 BCE until 70 CE. There is no year 0).[^757]
- The 420 years of the Beis Hamikdash is also recorded in Tosefta Zevachim 13:3, Yoma 9a, Eruchin 12b and Avodah Zara 9a.
- The 420 years (vs 589) can be calculated in other ways:
- Method 1:
- There were 5 leader between the Shimon Hatzadik (the last of the Anshei kneses hagidolah) and when Hillel began leading the people (he was the 6th).[^758]
- Hillel began his leadership 100 years before the destruction of the Beis Hamikdash.[^759]
- The Anshei Kneses Hagidolah were only around for a short time at the beginning of the Beis Hamikdash. This is discussed in the rishonim,[^760][^761] and implied from the fact that the Zugos (which came after the Anshei Kneses Hagidolah) received a tradition from the neviim (who came before the Anshei Kneses Hagidolah).[^762]
- According to conventional history, Hillel would have began his leadership in the 489th year of the Beis Hamikdash. This would mean that there were 5 leaders lasting 489 years, an average of nearly 82 years of leadership each. This is unlikely.
- Rashi says that Shimon Hatzadik led for 40 years after the Anshei Kneses Hagidolah. However, that would still leave around 75 year averages for each leader.
- According to the Jewish calculations, Hillel started his leadership in year 320 of the Beis Hamikdash. That would leave an average of 64 years per leader or, if we take Rashi's opinion, 56 years. This is much more reasonable.
- (I am not  sure what to make of the fact that they seemed to live much longer in those days. Rebbi Akiva lived until he was 120 year sold. Also, therse calculations assume that the Anshei Kneses Hagidolah didn't last for nearly any length of time. However, even if we add a few years, I don't think it will make much of difference.)
- In 1917, the monarchy in Russia (the Romanoff Dynasty) fell and were replaced by the Bolsheviks, led by Vladimir Lenin.[^763]
- A civil war broke out for control of the country. The White Army wanted to return Russia to the way it was (it was a mixed group but it had more upper class people) and the Red Army of the Bolsheviks (which was largely peasants) which was led by Leon Trotsky.[^763]
- There were other armies as well. Poland, Lithuania, Ukraine, and Georgia (and perhaps others) were all fighting mostly against the Red Army. [^763]
- Western powers (like the US and France) and Japan sent armies to fight against the Red Army. This backfired as it increased nationalistic feelings and strengthened the Red Army so they pulled out.[^763]
- There was a famine in Russia during this time as well.[^763]
- By 1923, the Red Army won and the Bolsheviks were in charge.[^763]
- Joseph Stalin had risen up the Bolshevik ranks.[^763]
- They set up the Central Committee with Joseph Stalin on it. However, they gave him the seemingly unimportant role of secretary.[^763] (Not sure why)
- However, Stalin used this position to gain power by selecting people loyal to him for high positions.[^764]
- Russia had many ethnic minorities. As such, it was decided to make countries where these people largely were. These countries were all semi-independent and semi under Russian control. This was called the USSR (The Union of Soviet Socialist Republics).[^763]
- Lenin was sick and died in January 1924.[^765]
- Before Lenin had died, Stalin convinced Trotsky to take a vacation. Trotsky (who suffered from illnesses as well) agreed. When Lenin died, Trotsky (who was the main competitor with Stalin to take over) wasn't there. As such, the new leader was chosen without him there. Trotsky largely became irrelevant.[^765]
- In addition, Stalin, through his secretary position, had already set himself up with much support to take over, which is what happened.[^765]
- Lenin had some free market and some collectivization (socialism).[^765]
- This was a major point of disagreement amongst the Communist Party (the Bolsheviks turned into the Communist Party). Some wanted total socialism and others were fine with the way it was.[^765]
- The USSR started gaining international recognition in the 1930s and was allowed to join the League of Nations in 1934.[^765]
- Some in the USSR felt that there needed to be a "global communist revolution" for communism to be truly safe. As such, the USSR supported communist parties and groups in capitalist countries.[^765]
- At the same time, the USSR needed trade agreements and good relations, so they kept the support secretive and put on a face of being friendly with the capitalist countries.[^765]
- Method 2:
- Yochanan Kohen Gadol (the father of Matisyahu the Chashmonai) lived at the same time as Yehoshua ben Prachia and Nitai Ha'Arbeili.[^766]
- Yehoshua ben Prachia and Nitai Ha'Arbeili were the 3rd generation leaders after Shimon Hatzadik.[^767]
- The Chanukah story (which Matisyahu was a part of) happened near the end of the Greek Empire or around 385 years after the Beis Hamikdash was built.[^768] (I Googled it and saw that Matisyahu Chashmonai's death, happened in year 166 BC,[^769] which would only be about 350 years after the construction of the Beis Hamikdash, counting from year 520 BCE as previously mentioned).
- Even if we assume that Yochanan Kohen Gadol lived 85 years before the Chanuka story, that would still mean that he (along with Yehoshua ben Prachia and Nitai Ha'Arbeili) lived 300 years after the building of the Beis Hamikdash (or 265 according to my cheshbon).[^768]
- Yehoshua ben Prachia and Nitai Ha'Arbeili were the 3rd generation of leaders after Shimon Hatzadik. Even with Rashi's opinion that Shimon Hatzadik led after the end of the Anshei Kneses Hagidolah, that would still mean that there are 260 (or 225) years with only 3 generation of people in charge. That sounds implausible.[^768]
- According to Seder Olam there are around 140 years during this time. This is much more reasonable.[^768] (If you assume that Matisyahu died in 166 BC, then there are 186 years from the building of the Beis Hamikdash (in year 352 BC) until the death of Matisyahu, or 146 according to Rashi).
- Method 3:
- Shimon Hatzadik met Alexander the Great.[^770]
- According to conventional history, Alexander came to Eretz Yisroel in 332 BCE (before having defeated the Persians).[^771] This would be about 180 years after the Beis Hamikdash was built. Shimon Hatzadik would have had to live to an incredible age, and we don't have any sources for this.[^768]
- According to Jewish history, Alexander defeated Darius 34 years after he built the Beis Hamikdash, which means that Alexander came to Eretz Yisroel in less than that.[^768]
- Method 4:
- Ezra Hasofer's father was killed around the end of the First Beis Hamikdash.[^772] That would mean that Ezra was at latest born around that time.
- There were 70 years between the destruction of the first Beis Hamikdash and the building of the second.[^773]
- Shimon Hatzadik was the Kohen Gadol after Ezra[^774] for 40 years[^775] (this might be what Rabbi Hool meant that we know that Shimon Hatzadik led for 40 years because I don't think Rashi says it).
- As we know that Shimon Hatzadik met Alexander, that would mean that Ezra was alive at most 40 years before this meeting (meaning, assuming that Shimon Hatzadik met Alexander at the end of his life).[^776]
- According to conventional history, Ezra would have to have lived from around 586 BCE (when the first Beis Hamikdash was destroyed according to conventional history[^777]) until 292 BCE (40 years before Alexander came to Eretz Yisroel), around 300 years.[^776]
- Method 5:
- Chagai, Zechariah, and Malachi were alive at the time of the building of the second Beis Hamikdash and died around the beginning of the Greek era.[^778] (I think they were alive during the destruction of the first.)
- According to conventional chronology, the Greek era began around 188 years after the building of the second Beis Hamikdash. This would mean that they would all need to be at least 180 years old.[^776]
- According to the Jewish tradition, the Greek era began 34 years after the construction of the second Beis Hamikdash.[^776]
- See Emunos Videi'os perek 8 pg 259-260, Maayanei Hayeshua pg 288-289 and 375-376, Sefer Hayuchsin Maamar 1 and 6, Tzemach Dovid part 2 pg 186-191, and Jewish History in Conflict by Mitchell First (Also, somewhere in the beginning of Berrel Wien's book on Jewish History on that time he has a footnote the mentions something that discusses this topic.)
- Sources for conventional chronology:
- Ptolemy's Canon/Royal Canon. A king list from the neo-Babylonians, Persians, Greeks, Romans, and Byzantine. It is known that Ptolemy (70-165 CE) had this list. It is believed that he got this information from a Greek named Hipparchus (2nd century BCE).[^779]
- It gives the following lengths of reigns (the years these occurred is given as assumed by conventional chronology):[^779]
	- Cyrus 9 years (538-530 BC)
	- Cambyses 8 (529-522 BC)
	- Darius I 36 (521-486 BC)
	- Xerxes 21 (485-465 BC)
	- Artaxerxes I 41 (464-424 BC)
	- Darius II 19 (423-405 BC)
	- Artaxerxes II 46 (404-359 BC)
	- Oshus 21 (358-338 BC)
	- Arses 2 (337-336 BC)
	- Darius III 5 (335-331 BC)
- Ancient historians:
	- Herodotus (c. 485-425 BC). Writes about Cyrus through Xerxes. (This has been referenced by Plato and Aristotle.)[^779]
	- Thucydides (c. 460-400 BC). Writes about Artaxerxes I and Darius II (and references previous kings).[^779]
	- Xenophon (c. 428-354 BC). Writes how Cyrus the Younger tried to overthrow Artaxerxes II and failed, gives a first hand account of Greece during his lifetime, and references previous Persian kings.[^779]
	- Diodorus Siculus (1st century BC). Wrote a universal history, including information on Arses and Darius III.[^779]
	- Plutarch (c. 46-120 CE). Wrote biographies on many Greeks and Romans, but also included some information on the Persian kings.[^779]
- Literary Works:
	- The Persians. A Greek play written by Aeschylus (525-456 BC) about wars between Greeks and Persians under Xerxes.[^779]
- Elephantine Papyri:
	- Documents dated under Persian rulers. Some people are found on multiple documents, so it allows for figure out the order that things happened.[^779]
- Archeological Dig in 1993 in Egypt:
	- Uncovered documents dated under Xerxes, Artaxerxes I, and Darius II.[^779]
- Royal Inscriptions:
	- Inscriptions in palaces, such as in Persepolis (a Persian capital), dated from Xerxes through Artaxerxes III.[^779]
- Cuneiform Tablets:
	- 5000 tablets dated between Cyrus and Darius III have been found. They nearly perfectly line up with the conventional chronology (for example, nearly 1000 tablets have been found dated for all 9 years of Cyrus' reign. One has been found dated to the 11th year (which historians assume is a mistake)).[^779]
- Astrological Texts:
	- Around 1300 ancient Babylonian texts dated from around 780 BCE through the Common Era have been found. These were dated based on astronomy (where stars, planets, the sun, and moon were). As such, we get a precise date when things were (scientists can figure out when the stars, etc, were at those positions described in the diaries).[^779]
- According to Jewish tradition, Alexander the Great began ruling the land of Israel 34 years after the Beis Hamikdash was built. They then created a world empire that lasted until around the time of the Chashmonaim, 174 years later.[^780]
- There isn't necessarily a conflict between most of the evidence previously given and the Jewish Tradition. If the Persian Empire continued under the Greeks (rather than before as assumed by conventional history), then there may have been the 10 Persian kings as discussed before, even though Alexander defeated the Persians just 36 years after Darius the Great began his reign (34 years after the Beis Hamikdash was built, which happened in the 2nd year of Darius the Great).[^781]
- This would still be in conflict with the astrological diaries, which give relativelt precise dates, not just an order of rulers and a length of their reigns, and the Royal Canon which places the Greek rulers after the Persian rulers.[^781]
- Alexander married the daughter of the Persian king he defeated, so he may may have decided to allow them to continue under Greece.[^781]
- The USSR economic plan was to collectivize the farms, sell the extra grain, use that money to improve heavy industry and energy production, and then produce consumer goods and the like. This collectivization happened in 1928-1929.[^782]
- Between 1929 and 1932, the kulaks (the relatively wealthy peasants) were largely arrested, deported, or killed. This was done to help with the farm collectivization.[^783] However, these people were the most productive. By getting rid of them, production dropped dramatically.[^782]
- Nobody had incentive to work hard because anything extra was just taken away {add link to problems with central planning/communism}.[^782]
- There was a lot of lying and misinformation. For example, the 1937 census said that around 12 million people were missing, so they took another one and faked the numbers.[^782]
- The government didn't listen to what the people beneath them were saying. They trusted the best predictions.[^782]
- The world had entered the Great Depression. Other countries were not particularly interested in buying grain from the Soviet Union, so they had to sell more and more to make the same money.[^782]
- This led to massive famines which killed between 10-15 million people (largely in Ukraine).[^782]
- This cowed the peasantry. They had no will to rebel.[^782]
- (I think the significance of the next point will be explained later.)
- Nearly all of the sources that say that the Greek Empire occurred after the Persian Empire are Greek, or went through Greek hands.[^781]
	- Herodotus, Thucydides, and Xenophon were Greek.[^781]
	- Most of the Astronomical Diaries were form the Seleucid era (312-65 BC). Some of the ones from earlier times were copies of earlier texts by Greeks. It is reasonable to assume that all of the astronomical diaries passed through Greek hands.[^781]
- There are no first-hand records of Alexander the Great. What we know about him is from much later sources.[^781]
- The post-Alexander history we have is all from Greeks.[^781]
- Stalin wanted to further consolidate power. Between 1936 and 1938, the Soviet Union underwent the Great Purge.[^784]
- Anyone who could challenge Stalin was arrested and put on trial for fake crimes and inevitably found guilty. This was partially in response to resentment about collectivization and the treatment of the peasants.[^784]
- This largely targeted those in power, although minorities (such as Germans and Poles) were also targeted because of fear of disloyalty.[^784]
- Many people who had supported Stalin and helped him gain power were killed.[^784]
- These trials showed the Soviet people and foreign powers that there were enemies within the state, but Stalin waws taking care of them. (It seems that many thought that these trials were legitimate.)[^784]
- Between 700,000 and 1.2 million were killed. Most were executed but many were sent to gulags (forced labor camps).[^784]
- Further evidence from Tanach that Alexander defeated the Persians much earlier than conventional chronology alleges:
- Daniel had a dream which was interpreted to mean that Media and Persia would conquer the world. Then, a Greek king (seemingly, Alexander) would come along and defeat Persia. However, the Greek king would suddenly die and his kingdom would be divided into four parts.[^785]
- During the 3rd year of Cyrus (according to Rabbi Hool. I didn't look into what the passuk says), a malach tells Daniel that there would be 3 more kings for Persia followed by a fourth who would be defeated by the Greek king.[^786]
- It is clear that there were only 4 Persian kings before Alexander (I am confused by the word "more" in the malach's nevuah. It sounds like after Cyrus there would be another 4 kings, making 6 in total, including Daryaveish Hamadi). This refers to Cyrus, Achashveirosh, and Daryaveish. The fourth king is either a Median (Daryaveish Hamadi, maybe? Rashi doesn't say) or a different king named במבישה, a son of Cyrus, who ruled before Achashveirosh[^787] (Bardiya? He ruled before Darius and after Achashveirosh, assuming Achashveirosh was Cambyses).
- The Uruk King list lists Cyrus, Cambyses, and Darius on the front. On the back, it has Ni din-Bel, Darius, Alexander, (and the continues with more Greek kings).[^788]
- The list is missing all of the kings that, according to conventional chronology, occur after Darius the Great. This fits with our theory.[^788]
- Historians respond that they were all originally there but the stone broke off (if you look at a picture of the stone, the bottom is broken off and the writing goes down to the bottom of what we have[^7]).[^788]
- The king before Darius III was not name Ni din-Bel. It was Arses. There is an explicit papyri that says the date that the kingship was passed from Arses to Darius III (so there was no time for a king in between). Arses went by multiple names. None of them were Ni din-Bel.[^788]
- However, Darius the Great was preceded by a king who went by the name Ni din-tu-Bel (also known as Nebuchadnezzar III). This seems to be who the Uruk King List is referring to (meaning, both times it mentions Darius, it is referring to Darius the Great. I don't know why it would say Darius twice. And I don't know why the second time would put an additional king before him. And the second time says his reign was 5 years, in line with conventional chronology, and not in line with assuming he was Darius the Great).[^788]
- Historians assume that this was either a Babylonian revolutionary (this would be the only reference to this king. There is no other reference anywhere else), or Ni din-tu-Bel, mistakenly recorded before the wrong Darius.[^789]
- In August 1939, the Soviet Union and Germany signed a non-aggression pact.
- Russia and Germany had been enemies. Germany was fascist and strongly anti-communist.[^790]
- However, the Soviet Union did this because:
	- They were also strongly anti-capitalist (hence, against the West).[^790]
	- They never liked the west after they tried to help the White Army in the Russian Civil War.[^790]
	- The Soviet Union was excluded from the Munich Agreement. This made it seem to Stalin like Western countries didn't respect the Soviet Union. In addition, he was afraid that the Western countries would force the Soviet Union to give up territory to Germany if Germany started being aggressive.[^791]
	- The soviet Union hoped to be able to take over some Eastern European countries (like Poland, Romania, and Finland). Germany was the only country that would naturally pose a threat to these moves.[^790]
- Stalin knew that long-term peace with Germany was impossible. They were too opposed to each other ideologically. However, he though that Germany wouldn't betray him because he was giving them vital resources (like wheat and oil).[^790]
- In addition, he thought that Germany wouldn't want to fight a war on two fronts.[^792]
- This came as a shock to the western world and people in Russia (Russia had portrayed Germany and Hitler to be evil).[^790]
- Nearly right after the non-aggression pact, Germany invaded Poland. Shortly after that, the Soviet Union invaded the eastern half, Estonia, Latvia, and Lithuania. The Soviet Union had a score to settle with Poland and massacred many of the army officers.[^792]
- In June 1941, Germany invaded the Soviet Union. The Soviet Union had made some preparations,[^793] but was still caught off-guard as troops were largely disorganized. Stalin had been warned by his intelligence, as well as the intelligence of numerous other countries, but he mostly dismissed the warnings.[^792]
- In the first few months, Germany made quick progress. They got within striking distance of Moscow.[^794]
- The Soviet Union was able to pull out massive factories from the west, load the machines onto trains, set them up farther east, and then produce materials needed for the war (a tractor factory in the west was converted was shipped to the east in this way and turned into a tank factory).[^794]
- The Soviet Union through everything it had at the Germans and were able to stop them at the Battles of Kursk and Stalingrad. However, this came at a cost of several hundred thousand soldiers[^794] (the Battle of Kursk is considered the largest battle in history[^795]).
- In 1943, Churchill, Roosevelt, and Stalin met. There was no formal pact but they unofficially agreed to defeat Germany.[^794]
- Despite the Great Purge, Soviet citizens still felt tremendous loyalty to their country and were willing to fight and die.[^794]
- In February 1945, British, US and Soviet leaders met at Yalta to decide what to do about Germany after the war (it was clear by that point the Germany would lose). It was agreed that Germany would be demilitarized and Naziism would be destroyed.[^794]
- In May, the Soviet Union captured Berlin.[^794]
- It is generally easier for older people to perfect their middos because at that point they naturally have much less desire for pleasures in this world.[^796]
- Also, older people generally have more chachmah. (I am not exactly sure why this is. Probably simply because they spent a lifetime gathering information. See source.)[^797]
- The Soviet Union has their army all over eastern Europe and so he sets up all the countries there as Soviet satellite countries, or fully annexes them into the Soviet Union.[^798]
- ![[Pasted image 20250325200638.png]][^799]
- As such, the Soviet Union emerged from WW2 with a lot of power.[^798]
- Churchill was suspicious of the Soviets and he didn't like all of the power they were getting. He drew up a plan called Operation Unthinkable to try and free up some of these countries but this was not acted upon because the Soviets were too powerful and the western soldiers had a positive view of the Soviet army (after being allies for the previous years) and didn't want to fight them.[^798]
- In March 1946, Churchill (who was no longer Prime Minister) gave a speech where he talked about Soviet influence over eastern Europe and that the west should be worried about it. He compared the Soviets to an "iron curtain" (hence, the speech became known as the iron curtain speech).[^798]
- At that time, Truman was still trying to keep the UK-US-Soviet alliance going. This threw a monkey wrench into the whole thing (although it was falling apart anyway).[^798]
- The western countries gave money to European countries to rebuild after the war. They offered money to the eastern European countries as well. This was refused as the Soviet Union didn't want western influence on its satellite countries. Instead, it created the Molotov Plan, which was basically the Soviet version of the Marshall Plan.[^798][^800]
- Check this out: https://www.reddit.com/r/badhistory/comments/eq2k6c/alexander_the_great_defeated_darius_i_and_the/ and this: https://www.livius.org/sources/content/mesopotamian-chronicles-content/bchp-4-alexander-and-artaxerxes-fragment/
- Cuneiform Text BM 36613:[^801]
- \[1]  \[_illegible_]

\[2] ……………………… the enemy with fire  ………………

\[3]  ……………… and the troops ………………

\[4]  ……………… iron of his own hands the debris from the wall of ………

\[5]  ……………… were removed. On that day \[_nth_] ………

\[6] \[month ? year _x_]th of [Arses](https://www.livius.org/articles/person/artaxerxes-iv-arses/), son of [Ochus](https://www.livius.org/articles/person/artaxerxes-iii-ochus/), who is called Artaxerxes

\[7]  \[_some people of some houses_] entered the temple of Anunitu inside the city of [Sippar](https://www.livius.org/articles/place/sippar/)

\[8]  ……… for Alexander, the king, they were mourning. You Babylonians

\[9]  ……… \[_verb_] The \[_lacuna_] and the houses to the possession of Esagila and the Babylonians returned

\[10]  ………………  Esagila they will rebuild. The army ………………

\[11]  ……………… few troops ………………………………

\[12]  \[_broken off_]
- The middle lines sound like they are saying that someone named Arses, son of Ochus, also called Artaxerxes was mourning Alexander and gave a speech to Babylonians there.[^802]
- This seems to be referring to Artaxerxes IV, who was the son of Ochus and whose real name was Arses. In addition, there were no later Persian kings named Artaxerxes.[^802]
- According to conventional history, Artaxerxes IV died 12 years before Alexander.[^802]
- Some suggest that this refers to different things. the first half refers to events in Artaxerxes IV's reign and the second half refers to events in Alexander's reign.[^802]
- However, the text does seem to be referring to one narrative. In addition, Artaxerxes IV is referred to in present tense, not past tense.[^802]
- According to our chronology, this would refer to Xerxes, who may have also gone by Artaxerxes. Darius the Great's name (who was the father of Xerxes) was Ochus. (I don't know if Xerxes went by Artaxerxes. Also, I don't know if his name was Arses).[^802]
- The Uruk King List either is damaged or there was a mistake in the writing of it. When it mentions Darius the Great's rule, it says 5 instead of 35 (just need to add in the 3 before the 5).[^802]
- Immediately following WWII, the Soviet Union did not want to fight the west as it did not have an atomic bomb and the West did. However, in 1947, they successfully developed an atom bomb and were thinking about fighting with the rest of Europe.[^803]
- This did not happen in part because of the Chinese Civil War in which the communists under Mao Zedong were victorious, creating the second communist great power. (I am not sure how this stopped a potential war).[^803]
- This led to the Korean War, the first major proxy war of the Cold War. (Not exactly sure how the Chinese Civil War led to the Korean War but they are neighbors so I assume that had something to do with it).[^803]
- The Korean War ended in 1953.[^803]
- In March 1953, Stalin died. He didn't leave over his wishes of who should take over.[^803]
- People thought that Georgy Malenkov (a Soviet politician) or Lavrentiy Beria (head of the secret police and the Soviet atomic project). These people were well educated.[^803]
- Nikita Khrushchev, although poorly educated, was much more dynamic and he was able launch a coup and seize power.[^803]
- Some of his political rivals (like Beria), were executed. Others (like Malenkov) were given positions in far flung places, where they wouldn't have much power.[^803]
- Khrushchev did not like Stalin. He publicly stated that Stalin committed terrible crimes and created a cult. This may have also been personal. Khrushchev's son committed a crime and was sentenced to death. Khrushchev begged Stalin for some leniency, but he refused.[^803]
- The Alexander Chronicle (BM 36304) Obverse:[^804]
- 3' … his thr\(one) they removed him. Be
  4' … as his name they named and Alex(ander) and his troops
  5' … (with) his few (troop)s from the troops…
  6' … he killed/defeated. The Hanean troops, his troops wh(ich)
  7' … (from) Babylon to Darius, the King of Kings.
- This seems to be talking about Alexander defeating Darius.[^804]
- Darius the Great was referred to as King of Kings, not Darius III.[^804]
- Line 13 (not quoted) talks about a holiday to the Babylonian god, Bel. According to conventional chronology, Alexander's victory over the Persians was around 200 years after the fall of Babylon. It doesn't seem likely that their gods were really worshipped.[^804]
- If we assume Alexander defeated Darius the Great, then the fall of Babylon was far more recent.[^804]
- Next piece of evidence:
- Far more texts have been found from the reigns of Cyrus, Cambyses, and Darius the Great, than the other rulers.[^804]
- Cyrus and Cambyses had around 1000. Darius had around 2000.[^804]
- Of the next 6 rulers, none have 500, and 4 had less than 130.[^804]
- Babylon also wrote relatively little about the latter Persian empire.[^804]
- According to conventional history, the Persian empire was still largely the same (for most of the latter rulers, anyway, at least I think so. I am not sure about this. I think at some point they started showing signs of weakness).[^804]
- According to out version, this makes sense. Persia became a vasal of Greece after Darius.[^804]
- Next:
- There is very little archeological evidence of Greek rule during the Seleucid period in Persia, while there is a lot in Babylon and Syria.[^804]
- According to conventional history, the Greeks conquered the whole Persian empire, so there should be a lot of archeological evidence across the empire.[^804]
- The lack of evidence suggests that the Greeks conquered Babylon and Syria and largely left the Persians alone.[^804]
- Next:
- There is evidence (collections of papyri and documents) of Persian rule over the whole Egypt through Darius the Great. After him, though, there is evidence only in southern Egypt (mostly around the city of Aswan). There is no evidence that  any Persian king after Darius visited Egypt.[^804]
- There is much evidence (Oxyrunchus papyri and numerous temples) for Greek rule in Egypt.[^804]
- This indicates that the Persian kings beginning with Xerxes had only limited influence in Egypt. This makes sense with the idea that they became a Greek vasal around this time, and Greece started making inroads into Egypt.[^804]
- Next:
- According to conventional history, Xerxes took over immediately following Darius the Great.[^804]
- Darius died at some point in his 36th year of rule (not counting his ascension year). One tablet is dated to the 12th month. 2 other tablets say he was still ruling in the 9th month.[^804]
- However, other tablets dated to Xerxes ascension year (which is presumably the year that Darius died) are dated to the 8th month.[^804]
- There was no coregency either as texts say that Xerxes ascended after Darius died.[^804]
- Sort of next:
- Leap months were added to the calendar every few years. This was standardized under Artaxerxes II, but rulers going back to Darius the Great followed the same pattern.[^804]
- Leap months were added in a 19 year cycle in years 3,6,8,11,14,17, and 19 (just like the Jewish calendar).[^804]
- This pattern holds true, nearly perfectly, for every king and goes from king to king. The only exception is from Darius to Xerxes. There is a gap in the pattern of a few years (Darius's last year is year 12 and Xerxes first year is year 16).[^804]
- Both of these points indicate that there was a gap between the rules of Darius and Xerxes. This makes sense if the empire was in disarray after being defeated by the Greeks.[^804]
- Sort of next:
- Darius went by the title "King of Babylon". Xerxes dropped that title. And Artaxerxes I dropped more titles after that.[^804]
- Sort of next:
- There are reliefs in the palace at Persepolis. The relief shows Xerxes and it was created after the death of Darius, but it does not call Xerxes, king. This further indicates a gap between the rules of Darius and Xerxes.[^804]
- The Astronomical Diaries do not allow for such a gap. The Astronomical Diaries leave precisely 21 years between the end of the reign of Darius and the beginning of the reign of Artaxerxes I. We know that Xerxes reigned for 21 years as there are numerous texts double-dated for Xerxes 21st year and Artaxerxes ascension year.[^804]
- Many other Astronomical Diary entries clearly indicate that there is no gap (numerous entries discuss a specific year of a rulers reign and when that was in history. Looking at this in conjunction with the length of reigns of various rulers that have been certified by numerous tablets throughout their reigns).[^804]
- This would indicate that there was a massive rewriting of the Astronomical Diaries.[^804]
- The Torah was given over to the Anshei Kineses Hagidolah exactly as it was received at Har Sinai.[^805]
- The way people think is fickle. Sometimes, a person will be sharp and on the ball. Other times, a person will be prone to mistakes (and it seems that they may be able to tell when they are which, at least sometimes). As such, one needs to review a case multiple times (הוו מתונים בדין) and have many people think about the case (העמידו תלמידים הרבה) to ensure that the case has been examined with the proper mental level.[^806]
- (Mentions the point that taking time when deliberating a case helps the disputants accept whatever verdict the judge comes out with as it shows he took proper care.)[^806]
- People need to teach and provide physical needs for many talmidim for a long time while they are in yeshiva.[^807]
- Germany surrendered in May 1945. The rest of Europe celebrated the end of the war.[^808]
- However, Europe and the Soviet Union were destroyed by the war. In Europe, over 40 million people were displaced not including Germans[^809] and around 40 million were killed.[^810]
- In addition, the infrastructure was destroyed as well.[^808]
- Food and water was scarce in many countries in Europe. Destroyed train tracks made in difficult to transfer goods. Germany gave out coupons to its citizens to wait in ques to receive basic necessities.[^808]
- The Soviet Union reached Berlin first so they were in charge until it was later agreed to be divided. On camera, the Soviets were kind occupiers and helped the people. However, not on camera, the Soviets attacked the civilians (over 2 million women were attacked) and took German heavy industry and factory equipment and sent it back to the Soviet Union on trains.[^808]
- On the surface, the allies all got along. Under the surface, tension was brewing.[^808]
- Germany was divided between the US, Britain, France, and Soviet Union. Berlin was similarly divided, even though it was in the Soviet zone.[^808]
- Germans moved on from Hitler and Naziism as they focused on survival.[^808]
- The allies were still suspicious of the Germans and told their soldiers not to fraternize. The soldiers on the ground largely disregarded this.[^808]
- There were around 11 million German and Italian prisoners of war. The allies divided up the prisoners and put them to help rebuild the allied countries.[^811]
- They were barely paid. Many had to get rid of mines and some died because of it.[^811]
- Germany lost much of its male population between 18 and 40 years old. As such, women had to do much of the rebuilding of Germany, such as getting rid on the millions of tons of debris.[^811]
- Millions of displaced people passed through Germany on their way back home. They pillaged and destroyed things on the way.[^811]
- Holocaust survivors also took revenge on their captors. In Dachau, all of the SS guards were killed. In one camp liberated by the Soviets, they allowed the prisoners to do whatever they wanted to the German guards for 24 hours.[^811]
- Over 10,000 concentration camp survivors died after being liberated due to disease and eating food too rich for their starved bodies.[^811]
- The allied were concerned about the spread of disease and so they set up checkpoints where they sprayed people with DDT to kill lice.[^811]
- The UN Charter was signed in June 1945, with the goal of not allowing the repeat of such a great war.
- The United Nations Relief and Rehabilitation Administration (UNRRA) helped distribute supplies in Europe. The money and supplies were largely from the US.[^812]
- The UNRRA also helped organize the transportation to bring people back home. For many, there was a joyful reunion between them and their families that they hadn't seen in years.[^813]
- However, captivity and war changed people which led to a spike in divorces.[^813]
- In addition, many people had lost their families and communities. There was nothing for them to come back to. This was particularly true for the Jews.[^813]
- Pogroms and other acts of antisemitism continued. The worst pogrom was the Kielce (a town in Poland) Pogrom in July 1946. The pogrom was committed by soldiers and civilians (including women). Over 40 Jews were killed and between 40 and 80 were wounded.[^814][^813]
- This led to Jews to further leave Eastern Europe.[^813]
- In July 1945, the leaders of the US, UK, and Soviet Union meet at Potsdam, Germany.[^813]
- At the meeting, Stalin drops hints that he would like to conquer much of the rest of Europe.[^813]
- Stalin doesn't think much of Truman.[^813]
- There are communist groups in many countries in western Europe, which the Soviet Union wants to try to leverage. On the side of the west, they have atomic weapons.[^813]
- German across Europe are humiliated and sometimes killed.[^813]
- Several hundred thousand Nazi collaborators are brought to trial. The biggest one in the Nuremberg Trial, in which 21 Nazi leaders are put on trial. This began in November, 1945. After a year of hearings and 16,000 pages of transcriptions, they are found guilty. Some are sentenced to life in prison, while others are executed.[^813]
- However, many Nazis are able to escape justice.[^813]
- In October 1957, the Soviet Union launched the Sputnik satellite into space. This was the first man-made object to be put in orbit.[^815]
- The US tried to catch up to the Soviet Union and created NASA in July 1958.
- The Space Race was important on a cultural level. It showed the brilliance and technological capabilities of the US and Soviet Union. In addition, it was also a quasi arms race. This showed each of the countries rocket capabilities.[^815]
- On May 1st 1960, an American U-2 spy plane was shot down over the Soviet Union and the pilot captured. May 1st (May Day) is a Soviet holiday and so this was a insult for the Americans to try and fly a plane over the Soviet Union on that day.[^815]
- This also represented a turning point. U-2s would fly too high to be shot down. However, the Soviet Union missile technology was improving and their missiles were now able to reach the U-2s.[^815]
- This hurt relations between the Americaas and the Soviet Union. Eventually, relations were repaired when the US traded a captured Soviet spy for the pilot.[^815]
- In April 1961, Soviet Yuri Gagarin became the first man in space. The US publicly congratulated the Soviet Union for this accomplishment. Privately, though, they were furious.[^815]
- Since one can (and often does) make mistakes in psak, one needs to make a fence around the Torah to make sure that they don't accidentally violate the Torah.[^816]
- One needs to hold to the mesorah. Don't let people change it. Part of the reason for having many talmidim (and presumably deliberating for some time when coming to a decision) is so that they can work to find the truth by examining pilpulim people come up with to say that something is muttar or assur.[^816]
- In February 1959, Fidel Castro became Prime Minister of Cuba, officially making it communist.[^817] The US tried to overthrow him, including the Bay of Pigs invasion in April 1961. These all failed.[^818]
- The Soviet Union started taking a harder stance against the west by building the Berlin Wall and putting nuclear weapons on Cuba.[^819]
- East German leaders put pressure on him to take a more firm stance against the west. In addition, many East German people would sneak into West Berlin, which didn't look good for the Soviet Union.[^819]
- America had nuclear missiles in Italy and Turkey, very close to the Soviet Union.[^819]
- One thing Khrushchev did was build the Berlin wall to keep the German people under Soviet occupation from leaving. This was a bad PR move. It didn't look good that the Soviet Union had to build a wall to keep people from leaving.[^819]
- They also started sending nuclear missiles over to Cuba to:
	- Help defend them from further American aggression (like another Bay of Pigs invasion).[^819]
	- As a counter to America's missiles in Italy and Turkey.[^819]
