- Khrushchev was under pressure from China who was becoming the leader of the socialist world. They were critical of Khrushchev's leadership. This led to him taking a harder stance against he west.[^819]
- This was discovered by the Americans in October, as a tremendous surprise.[^819]
- Khrushchev was using extreme language, threatening nuclear war.[^819]
- There was tremendous tension between the two sides. This was arguably the closest that the world came to nuclear war.[^819]
- In the end, the Soviets pulled their missiles from Cuba and the US pulled their missiles from Turkey.[^819]
- Both sides claimed that their governments did an excellent job pursuing peace.[^819]
- Arguably, the Soviets "won" the crisis, as it forced the Americans to pull their missiles from Turkey, while the Soviet Union was back to how they were before putting the missiles on Cuba.[^819]
- A person is supposed to learn Torah biiyun to better understand Hashem so one can have Yiras Hashem and Ahavas Hashem. a person needs to think about this, though. Learning on its own is not enough. (I think that this is referring to learning mussar.)
- Moshe was able to learn the whole Torah in its totality from Hashem.
- By the time it came down to the Anshei Kneses Hagidolah, though, the Torah was being forgotten. That is why they instituted 3 points of the mishna.[^820]
- The chachamim were charged with giving malkus beyond what the Torah says to keep people from violating the Torah.[^821]
- In October 1964, Khrushchev was forced to step down for the Soviet leadership.[^822]
- This was partially because of a failed plan of his which led to food shortages, and partly because of pressure by Leonid Brezhnev who wanted Khrushchev out so he could succeed him, which he did.[^822]
- Brezhnev grew up in a working family and he later joined the military.[^822]
- He forced a more centralized economy (similar to Stalin), but he also allowed for a black market, which the government turned a blind eye toward, in exchange for the loyalty of the people.[^822]
- Khrushchev was not executed or imprisoned. This was unique for the Soviet Union (usually transfers of power resulted in executions and banishments).[^822]
- There was a lot of corruption in the government under him.[^822]
- He put an emphasis on the military, and particularly on the missile program. In the 1970s, the Soviet Union surpassed the US for amount of nuclear missiles.[^822]
- However, this put a tremendous strain on the Soviet economy.[^822]
- The US started seriously helping South Vietnam in the Vietnam War in 1965.[^823]
- Chinese wanted Northern Vietnam to invade as Vietnam is on their doorstep. Soviets did not as they didn't want to get involved, but were okay with it once it happened. They didn't sent troops, but they sent advisors and material aid.[^823]
- The US was ground down and lost the will to keep fighting. It was an embarrassment for the US military.[^823]
- Beginning in 1968, Czechoslovakia made some reforms to their economy. It was still socialist, but it was somewhat unique of Soviet communism. This was called the Prague Spring.[^823]
- Brezhnev didn't like this. He thought that it might spread to other countries in eastern Europe.[^823]
- He first tried to come to a deal with Czechoslovakian political leaders. When this didn't work, he sent in the military from many of the countries under Soviet influence.[^823]
- This became known as the Brezhnev Doctrine, which stated that a challenge to communist rule was a threat to all of the Eastern Bloc countries and so would be dealt with by all of the countries' militaries.[^824]
- In the 1970s under Nixon, the US has more open relationships with the Soviet Union and China. They make trade deals and deals to limit the size of the military. This leads to decreased Cold War tensions.[^823]
- During the beginning of the 1970s, the Soviet economy improved greatly, after that though, the growth slowed down.[^823]
- Freedom of the press was restricted.[^823]
- The US economy was pulling away from the Soviet economy, but this was not known at the time.[^823]
- In 1979, the Soviet Union sent forces to Afghanistan.[^825]
- There was a civil war in Afghanistan between communist leaders in support of the Soviet Union and people resistant to them. The resistance was  largely because the pro-communist government was against Islam and violently shut down dissidents.[^826] This resistance movement was called the Mujahideen.[^827]
- The war was tremendously unpopular in the Soviet Union. People compare it to the Vietnam War for the US. They were sucked into it, had no exit strategy, and the Soviets lost a tremendous amount of troops over ten years.[^825]
- This was part of the beginning of the end of the Soviet Union.[^825]
- The US supplied the resistance with advisors and weapons.[^825]
- In November 1982, Brezhnev died.[^828]
- The Soviet economy needed help. Brezhnev was succeeded by Yuri Andropov, followed by Konstantin Chernenko. Both were old and sick, didn't last long, and were unable to reinvigorate the economy.[^829]
- In March 1985, Mikhail Gorbachev became leader.[^830]
- Gorbachev allowed for much more freedom of the press to help expose and remove corruption. The Soviet civilians supported this.[^829]
- In April 1986, the Chernobyl nuclear reactor melted down, releasing nuclear radiation. The government hid this from the public, which caused greater loss of life and was made people (Soviet civilians and the west) mistrust the government. This was particularly bad as the government was talking about being more open.[^829]
- Gorbachev wanted to add some market elements to the economy. He tried to make some major reforms in politics and the economy. It was difficult for the country to adjust.[^831]
- He wanted to end the Cold War, particularly, the arms race. Reagan was antagonistic toward the Soviet Union but Gorbachev stuck with it and eventually, Reagan came around. Similarly, Margaret Thatcher of Great Britain opened relations with Gorbachev, as well.[^831]
- In June, 1987, Reagan made a famous speech at the Brandenburg Gate, where he called for the removal of the Berlin Wall, a symbol of the Cold War.[^832][^831]In November, 1989, the wall was torn down.[^833]

- The reunion of Germany made Gorbachev popular in Germany (and probably much of the western world, but the video didn't say). People say that he should have pushed for Germany to remain neutral and not be allowed to join NATO.[^835]
- However, he was losing popularity at home. (I'm not sure exactly why. This may have been because this move was viewed as giving in to the west, such as with Germany joining NATO.)[^835]
- In 1988, Gorbachev starts allowing the public to vote on government officials. This (in addition to some of his other reforms) made him unpopular amongst his fellow politicians.[^835]
- Many of the countries in the Eastern Bloc started pushing for freedom and being allowed to elect their own leaders. Slowly this started happening.[^835]
- In 1989, the Soviet forces pulled out of Afghanistan. (The video implies that this was unpopular. Maybe the war was unpopular but finally admitting that the Soviet Union lost the war was also unpopular?)[^835]
- In addition, the economy struggled.[^835]
- In August 1991, there was an attempted coup to overthrow Gorbachev. Gorbachev was rescued by Boris Yeltsin, the president of Russia. Yeltsin basically took over the Soviet Union at that point, destroying Gorbachev's power.[^835]
- The Supreme Soviet (similar to the US congress) voted to dissolve the USSR in December, and a few days later, this was official.[^835]
- The 15 countries that made up the Eastern Bloc plus Russia itself each became independent countries.[^835]
- One of the Elephantine Papyri (A4.7) is a letter written from Jedaniah (the leader of the Jewish community in Elephantine). He references the Kohen Gadol, Yehochanan (seemingly Yochanan, the same way Daryahavesh is really Daryaveish). The letter is dated to year 17 of Darius II.[^836]
- According to the Rambam and others, Yochanan was the Kohen Gadol after Shimon Hatzadik.[^837]
- Shimon Hatzadik was the Kohen Gadol for 40 years and Yochanan was the Kohen Gadol for 80 years.[^838]
- We know that Shimon Hatzadik met Alexander the Great.[^836]
- The 17th year of Darius II's reign was 82 years after the end of Darius I's reign (with the proposed 3 year gap). (3 year gap + 21 years of Xerxes + 41 years of Artaxerxes + 17 years of Darius II).[^836]
- According to our proposed chronology, this makes sense. Alexander defeated Darius I. Shimon Hatzadik was the Kohen Gadol for some amount of time afterwards. After Shimon Hatzadik, Yochanan became Kohen Gadol for 80 years, during which time, Jedaniah wrote his letter which later became the Elephantine Papyri.[^836]
- According to conventional history, Alexander defeated Darius III, which was over 150 years after the end of Darius I. Yochanan would not have been the Kohen Gadol then. (I don't know why this can't be referring to some other, unknown Yochanan who was Kohen Gadol).[^836]
- Before beginning Operation Barbarossa, the Germans wanted to secure their southern border. Hungary, Romania, and Bulgaria were on the Axis side, so those countries remained untouched.[^839]
- Yugoslavia was originally pro-axis but the government changed and the new government was pro-Britain. Greece was also pro-Britain. As such, the Germans attacked these countries. They were both defeated in about a month combined.[^839]
- There are 20 documents part of the Elephantine Papyri that have an Egyptian date, a Babylonian date, and a date with the month and year of the Persian king. These were all written during the reigns of Xerxes through Artaxerxes II.[^840]
- The Egyptian calendar has 365 days a year, every year (12 30 day months, and 1 5 day month). The Babylonian calendar is more complex. It is a combined solar and lunar calendar. Months were 29 or 30 days with an extra month every few years (similar to the Jewish calendar). The extra months and 29 versus 30 days in a month was not fixed. (The book implies that the years do not line up perfectly, possibly because of the Babylonian calendar incorporates the fact that the year is 365 and 1/4 days long while the Egyptian calendar does not, causing the Egyptian calendar to go ahead by one day every 4 years.)[^840]
- A historian made the calculation and it came out that 15 of the Elephantine Papyri dates (meaning, the Babylonian and Egyptian dates) were within 2 days of each other, if you assume like the secular historians that the the kings being discussed lived during the times that secular history assumes. The last 5 were off by about a month, but they may have been damaged. Fixing them up also places the dates within 2 days of each other.[^840]
- If we assume like the proposed chronology, the dates would be much farther apart (as the proposed chronology places these kings over 100 years later than the conventional history). This seems to be an indication like the conventional history.[^840]
- However, the modern calculations are based on certain dates that have been double-dated with the Julian Calendar (which was adopted in 45 BCE) and working backwards from there. This takes the assumption that the Egyptian calendar was not tampered with, which may not be the case (such tampering has occurred at other points in history).[^841]
- The Egyptian calendar was fixed so the amount of time between two dates (in the same year, or in different years) is easy calculable. The Babylonian calendar can vary by 1-3 days between years without a fixed pattern (meaning, assuming that both intervals contain the extra month or both do not. In others words, factoring out the extra month and just focusing on the amount of days within the months).[^841]
- 11 of the 18 documents are off by 1-2 days. Historians offer 5 theories for why this was:[^841]
	- Elephantine used a different calendar system than the rest of Babylon.
	- The Babylonian system used sightings of the new moon to determine months. Since Elephantine is at a different longitude than the rest of Babylon, they may have come to different calculations.
	- The Babylonian day begins at sunset. The Egyptian day begins at sunrise.
	- We just can't calculate ancient calendars with modern dates.
	- Elephantine had to rely on their own calculations and they made mistakes due to cloudy weather.
- Response to first point:
- The same Babylonian months were used, and leap years were added at the same time as the rest of Babylon (for most of the papyri).[^841]
- The times that the papyri are a month ahead was right after a leap month was added. This can simply be explained that the fact that the rest of Babylon added a month had not reached Elephantine yet.[^841]
- Response to second point:
- The calculations were made with this discrepancy in the new moon (which varies by 47 minutes based on the difference in longitude). The discrepancies between the dates persist.[^841]
- Response to third point:
- 9 papyri (half of them), are off by a day. It is not likely that so many papyri were written at night (we are talking about before electricity was invented). Also, this does not explain the 2 papyri that were off by 2 days.[^841]
- Response to fourth point:
- For about 95% of dates, modern calculations are accurate. It isn't likely that this group of texts is inaccurate in 11 out of 18.[^841]
- Response to fifth point:
- 4 of the mistakes occurred in the summer (when there is less cloud cover).[^841]
- All of the errors have the Babylonian date one day later. That means that the Babylonian month started a day earlier than expected. If the Elephantine people were making a mistake due to bad weather, they would have began the month later (meaning, if the new moon was obscured by clouds, they would have called the month one day later).[^841]
- It seems unlikely that there would have been 2 day mistakes, so this would not explain those 2 day mistakes.[^841]
- As such, by looking at the Egyptian dates and Persian king years, we can know how many days apart two of the Elephantine Papyri letters were written. We can then look at the Babylonian dates.[^841]
- Torah should only be given over to talmidim who are "ראוי והגון".<span style="visibility:hidden">l</span>[^834] (This seems to go against the way many rishonim learn "העמידו תלמידים הרבה" in addition to the way the מדרש שמואל himself learns it. They all seem to say that Torah should be taught to anyway, except for people who have bad intentions.)
- (Also says that one should be careful when making judgements to take a while and think it over.[^842])
- (Also says that one can't postpone the giving over the din once the judges have come to their decision.[^842])
- A person needs to wait until they have learned enough before paskening shaylos.[^842]
- If one fincialy support Torah, they get the zchus of the learning as if they themselves were learning. (The lashon is that the supporter provides all of the needs to the person learning. I don't know what comes out if the person only supplies some of the needs.)[^843]
- (Also says that talmidim need to be supported for a long time (although he does say that the mishna isn't telling one to support many talmidim. I think the Sforno said to do both).[^843])
- (Also says that the mishna might be saying like Beis Hillel that one should accept all talmidim and not like Beis Shamai that one should only take the talmidim who are yarei shamayim and have good middos.[^843] (He says this as a possible pshat in the mishna ("…ואפשר"). - However, the rebbi should not teach all talmidim the same things. The greater and more proper talmidim should be taught the deeper secrets of the Torah, while those talmidim who are not so good should be taught less and through מתוך שלא לשמה בה לשמה, these talmidim will improve.[^843] (Perhaps this is what the Medrash Shmuel earlier was referring to, namely, that talmidim shouldn't just be taught everything that they want to learn. The rebbi has to teach each talmid according to his level.)
- Potential Shmuz:
- After Klal Yisroel conquered Canaan and divided the land, Reuven, Gad, and half of Menashe left the rest of Klal Yisroel. Upon doing this, they immediately built a mizbeach. During that time it was assur to bring karbanos on bamos as the mishkan is Shilo was set up.[^844] ^cc43d7
- Klal Yisroel sent a delegation to reprimand them for doing this.[^844]
- This was done in part because of fear that Hashem would punish the rest of Klal Yisroel for this,[^845] and because they didn't want the rest of Klal Yisroel to be influenced by this.[^846]
- Perhaps we see the power of influence. We are talking about a great generation who saw many miracles in the conquering of Eretz Yisroel. They obviously knew that it was wrong (the leaders, like Yehoshua and Pinchas presumably told them). So why would they have been effected? Why do you need to send a delegation over to confront them?[^7]
- We see the power of influence. If a delegation was not sent over to confront them, that would show a lack of disapproval and that could negatively affect some people.[^7]
- <span style="color:rgb(255, 192, 0)">Counter:</span> The Ralbag is clear that this was a particularly bad aveirah. The reason why bamos are assur is because we want one place to serve Hashem to emphasize the fact that Hashem is one. Avodah zaros were served all over the place. This was to show a separation between Hashem and Avodah zara. Perhaps this only applies to very serious aveiros. Less serious aveiros may not have an impact.[^7]
- <span style="color:rgb(255, 192, 0)">Counter:</span> The people sinning are frum people (or, at least, they thought they were). Perhaps that has a stronger impact then seeing someone not frum do an aveirah.[^7]
- Potential Shmuz:
- Reuven, Gad, and half of Menashe responded that they had pure intentions. They did not plan on bringing korbanos on the mizbeach they were building. (There is no issur to build a mizbeach if one doesn't bring korbanos on it. I thought there might be a problem imitating the keilim of the mishkan. Perhaps this wasn't an imitation (it may have had different dimensions) or maybe there is no such problem. I am not sure.)[^847] ^ae8c6d
- They built it because of fear that the children of the rest of the rest of Klal Yisroel would make fun of their children and say that Hashem doesn't have the same relationship with them.[^845] This would cause their children to have less yiras shomayim.[^848] This mizbeach would be a sign that this claim was not true and that they had the same relationship with Hashem.[^849]
- Why would the children of Reuven, Gad, and Menashe be effected? These claims are not true. They can can just speak to the leader of their times and find out that these claims aren't true.[^7]
- It seems that this may not have been enough. They still would have been effected by the claims from some of those living in Eretz Yisroel proper. We see how much we are affected by others, even when they are wrong and easily disproven.[^7]
- <span style="color:rgb(255, 192, 0)">Counter:</span> It could be that they really wouldn't have been affected, but it doesn't take much effort to build this mizbeach to take care of the issue, rather then have to go get the leaders and ask them the question.[^7]
- <span style="color:rgb(146, 208, 80)">Counter (to previous point):</span> perhaps the lashon is not so mashma this. The lashon isn't mashma that this was done to prevent the effort to go find the gedolim. It is mashma that it was done to prevent the loss of yiras shomayim.[^7]
- <span style="color:rgb(255, 192, 0)">Counter:</span> Perhaps people may not have expended the effort to go find out that they really did have the same place with Hashem, and these people would have been negatively effected.[^7]
- Hashem doesn't cause Eretz Yisroel to grow more than other places. Produce of every place is decided based on the mitzvos of the people.[^850]
- Hashem doesn't punish people for planning on doing an aveirah that they did yet not commit.[^851]
- WWI massively changed the economic landscape of the world. European countries had borrowed $14 billion from the US. Britain, until the war, was the most powerful country. After the war, the US had 50% of the world's production.[^852]
- India was under British control. They strongly supported Britain in the war, supplying many troops, supplies, and money.[^853] As such, they hoped for independence now that the war was over. However, Britain maintained their control over India.[^852]
- This led to wide protests in India. In April 1919, British soldiers fired for 10 minutes into an unarmed crowd, killing almost 400 and injuring hundreds more. This act was largely supported by people in Britain and the government. This created more negative feelings in India against Britain.[^852]
- Many in the US heard of this and were angered at Britain's bad treatment.[^852]
- The Austria-Hungarian Empire, the Ottoman Empire, the German Empire, and the Russian Empire collapsed between the 1917 and 1922. (Not sure what is meant by German and Russian Empire collapsing. the Russians became part of the Soviet Union and Germany still existed. I guess they were reformed and shrunk?) New countries were formed out of all or some of their lands. All of this was going on as Europe was trying to recover from the war.
- Germany had historically not been a democratic country until near the end of WWI. After the war, the Democratic Socialists were in charge of the government.
- In January 1918, there was an uprising (called the Spartacist Uprising) by some who wanted a communist country similar to the Soviets. However, the uprising failed.[^854]
- The government (called the Weimar Republic) was a parliamentary system. However, there were little restrictions on the minimum size for a party. This made the government extremely weak as any coalition was easily able to be destroyed by small groups. This was part of why the populace (which was originally strongly in favor of the government) strongly voted against it within 20 years.[^855]
- In May 1918, the Great Powers met to discuss the new world order. Germany was not invited which greatly upset them.[^855]
- At the meeting, several new Eastern European countries were created from the collapse of the Austro-Hungarian and Russian Empires (Poland, Czechoslovakia, Yugoslavia, Estonia, Latvia, and Lithuania). The hope was that they would be able to keep communism from the new Soviet Union from "infecting" rest of Europe.[^855]
- Lots of the newly found countries made more sense on a map than in real life. Poland was made up of parts of the Russian Empire and parts of the Austria-Hungarian Empire. As such, different places had used different currencies and there were no roads linking some of the major cities.[^855]
- President Woodrow Wilson called for self-determination for minorities. However, this was difficult to implement as minorities were everywhere. Czechoslovakia, for example, was only around 50% Czech.[^855]
- A system called the Mandate system was created. The idea was that some of the "less developed" countries would be under the control of a "developed country" (basically meaning a Western country) until they were organized enough to rule on their own.[^855]
- Germany had colonies in China. After the war, these were given to Japan. This enraged the Chinese and led to a movement which would eventually turn into the Communist Party.[^855]
- Many people in these "less developed" countries did not like the mandate system.[^855]
- The US Senate voted against the Versailles Treaty. Wilson tried to speak to the American public to get them on his side and put pressure on the Senate. However, he suffered a stroke and was unable to do so. As such, the US did not join the League of Nations. This may have profoundly impacted the coming decades.[^856]
- Britain was left as the sole superpower as the US was isolationist and the Soviet Union was dealing with the Russian Revolution. They didn't spend so much on their military as there wasn't anyone to threaten them.[^856]
- Mussolini and Hitler started their assents in Italy and Germany, respectively.[^856]
- Mussolini was preaching fascism, as a "3rd way", different than communism and capitalism.[^856]
- The Great Depression began in the 1920s.[^857]
- Germany was economically destroyed by the fallout of the war. It lost its colonies, much of its productive land, a large part of its merchant fleet, and had to pay huge reparations.[^857]
- Britain took control of what is today modern Iraq and Jordan and France gained control of modern day Syria and Lebanon. Modern day Israel and Palestine was to be controlled by some sort of international group (I thought they were controlled by Britain). Despite the fact that Britain and France were supposed to just be "caretakers" of these places, they were treated more like colonies.[^857]
- Arab revolts against this arrangement were crushed.[^857]
- In 1919, there was political turmoil in Italy. As such, fascism started becoming popular.[^858]
- Russia tried to regain some of its former lands from Poland. However, Poland was able to hold them off. Eventually, a peace treaty was signed, but it was not expected to last.[^858]
- In the Soviet Union, the Bolsheviks were united and so were able to present a strong vision of the future. As such, they were able to recruit the peasants to their side.[^858]
- The Bolshevik government was inefficient, though, with a ridiculously high amount of employees.[^858]
- In the US, prohibition was underway. Many people (such as Al Capone) smuggled alcohol, anyway. This led to gunfights in city streets, such as Chicago.[^858]
- Britain was losing control of its Middle East colonies and so in March 1921, they set them up as independent countries.[^859]
- Iraq made more sense on a map than in real life. Iraq was made up of three distinct regions, Mosul, Baghdad, and Basra.[^859]
- In 1921, fascists earned a spot in the Italian government.[^859]
- In August 1921, an amount was set for how much Germany owed the allies. The amount was set to around 225 billion marks.[^859]
- Germany decided to print the money to pay the allies. The leaders denied the fact that this would lead the inflation (it ended up leading to the worst hyperinflation in recorded history) (add link). Some wonder if they sincerely believed that, or if they were trying to shake things up, and perhaps cause the allies to forgive the debt.[^859]
- In early 1941, the Germans started redeploying their units to the east to prepare for Operation Barbarossa.[^839]
- The Soviet Union's army was not prepared to fight against the Germans. During the late 1930s, Stalin had killed many of the commanders in the military, who were political rivals to him. This left the military weak.[^839]
- One reason Stalin didn't deploy his troops to defend against the potential invasion was that he didn't want to do anything to provoke Hitler.[^839]
- Operation was to have 3 army groups: Army Group North, South, and Center.[^839]
- North would proceed to Lithuania, Latvia, and Estonia, end then to Leningrad (modern day St. Petersburg), Center would proceed to Smolensk and then Moscow, and South would go through Ukraine to Kiev.[^839]
- ![[Pasted image 20250407030817.png]][^860]
- This plan was risky as it would spread German's forces thin.[^839]
- The Soviet Union only had 2 million or so people ready to fight the Germans but they about 20 million in reserve.[^839]
- The Soviets had more tanks than the Germans. Most of the Soviet tanks were outdated. However, the Soviet Union had just started producing the T-34 tank which was the most advanced tank at that time.[^839]
- Operation Barbarossa began in June. There was very little resistance, especially in for the center group.[^839]
- In early July, Army Group Center surrounded and captured around 300,000 Soviet troops near Minsk (see map).[^839]
- The Ukrainians welcomed the Germans as there was much anti-Soviet feelings there.[^839]
- The Soviet leadership was stunned by the German betrayal and did almost nothing for the first few days. (See previous video that Stalin was worried that he would be killed by his fellow Soviet leaders.)[^839]
- However, Stalin was able to rally the Soviets to fight to defend their country.[^839]
- The British immediately made peace with the Soviets to fight against Germany. They started sending the Soviets, aid.[^839]
- By late July, another 300,000 Soviet soldiers were captured near Smolensk.[^839]
- The Soviets were doing a better job in the south. Hitler sent some of the panzers to the south to help the South Army Group capture Kiev. This gave Moscow more time to gather in troops and build defenses.[^839]
- The panzers from Army Group South and Center met east of Kiev in mid-September. They surrounded Soviet troops, killing or capturing another 500,000.[^839]
- In mid-September, Army Group North surrounded Leningrad. They besieged the city.[^839] Over the course of the siege (which lasted 2 and a half years), around 1 million people died from starvation and up to 2 million total.[^861]
- After the panzers sent to the south had rejoined Army Group Center, the group pushed toward Moscow during September and October. However, bad weather and increased Soviet resistance (partially because of the additional time afforded the Soviets by the panzers being sent south) slowed the German advance.[^839]
- In mid-October, Army Group South reached the Black Sea and surrounded the city of Odessa, capturing another 100,000 soldiers.[^862]
- The weather was having a strong negative affect on the German soldiers. the German military leaders planned for the attack to be completed by the end of the summer so the soldiers didn't have winter clothes.[^862]
- By early December, the Germans hunkered down as it was so cold that tanks wouldn't start up.[^862]
- By mid-December, Stalin repositioned around 500,000 troops from the east who were defending against a potential Japanese attack, after being told by intelligence that Japan had no such plans. These soldiers were well equipped and trained to fight in the winter.[^862]
- With this new force, the Soviets launched a counter-attack. This took the Germans by surprise and they were pushed back.[^862]
- Hitler fired his field commander as well as other military leaders and took command.[^862]
- On December 7, 1941, Japan bombed Pearl Harbor, bringing America into the war. Germany immediately declared war on America.[^862]
- In June 1942, Germany began Case Blue, an operation to seize oil fields in the Caucuses (as Germany was starting to run out of oil).[^862]
- The operation was to have two army groups. Army Group B would proceed to the Don River to protect Army Group A. Army Group A would actually capture the oil fields.[^862]
- ![[Pasted image 20250407032910.png]][^863]
- Hitler ordered Army Group B to capture Stalingrad. Stalingrad was a major industrial city for the Soviet Union, but there was no immediate need to capture it. This decision may have been the turning point in the whole campaign.[^862]
- Hitler order panzers from Army Group B to join Army Group A to help them capture the Caucuses. With their additional help, they were able to get within 70 miles of the Caspian Sea, threatening to cut off Soviet troops. But then Hitler changed his mind again and had the panzers rejoin Army Group B (which was making little progress without the panzers).[^862]
- In August, Army Group A captured the first oil field. However, the Soviets had destroyed it before retreating. Despite their efforts, they were unable to push farther to capture the other oil fields in the area.[^862]
- In August, German troops began their attack on Stalingrad.[^862]
- The German air force bombed the city but the German soldiers still had to fight house to house to clear out the defenders. The Soviets used the sewer system to travel unseen through the city.[^862]
- The Soviets sent a lot of reinforcements to Stalingrad. Many of them were killed crossing the Volga River, but some got through.[^862]
- In November, the Soviets brought major reinforcements to the area and successfully counterattacked the Germans.[^862]
- In late November, two Soviet armies met on the west of Stalingrad, cutting off the German army from resupply.[^862]
- Hitler did not allow his army to retreat. He was told by leaders of the Luftwaffe that they could airlift supplies to the troops in Stalingrad. Practically, however, the air force, partially due to bad weather and increased Soviet air presence, the German air force was not able to provide close to enough supplies.[^862]
- In mid-December, the Germans launched Operation Winter Storm to try and break the German out of Stalingrad. This was unsuccessful.[^862]
- By the end of January, the Germans in Stalingrad surrendered.[^862] Germans and their allies with them suffered over 800,000 casualties (dead, wounded, or captured).[^864]
- Army Group A was forced to pull out before the Soviet force at Stalingrad cut off their retreat.[^865]
- In January 1943, the Soviet Union attacked the Germans on a wide front. By the end of February, the Soviets had pushed the Germans back.[^865]
- The Soviets had more manpower by this point, and better technology with the T-34 and the Katyusha rocket launcher.[^865]
- The Soviets pushed a bulge into the German lines at the city of Kursk. Hitler wanted to take advantage of that by surrounding the army there.[^865]
- ![[Pasted image 20250407091355.png]][^865]
- The Germans at this point began production of the Tiger and Panther tanks which could take on the T-34.[^865]
- The attack was scheduled for July.[^865]
- Soviet intelligence warned them of the incoming attack and so they build defensive positions and massively reinforced the area, heavily outnumbering the Germans.[^865]
- The northern German attack made little progress and suffered heavy losses. The southern side made more progress, but then Soviet reinforcements came and shut it down. (This was the largest tank battle in history, mentioned earlier.)[^865]
- The Red Air Force defeated the Luftwaffe and established air superiority. They were further able to attack the German ground troops.[^865]
- By the end of July, the Germans were retreating.[^865]
- Germans suffered 50,000 casualties and lost 700 tanks.[^865]
- Hashem blessed Klal Yisroel because they did not immediately start fighting against the Reuven, Gad, and half of Menashe. They first spoke to them about what they were doing.[^866] ^697ef2
- It turned out that not only were they not doing something wrong, they were even doing something that would protect the rest of Klal Yisroel from doing the wrong thing and receiving punishment.[^867]
- I don't really understand why they would have attacked them immediately without hearing what they had to say. It seems to me that perhaps they would have done this because they thought it was obvious that they had bad intentions by making this mizbeach (as mefarshim, the Mincha Ketana in particular, explain).[^7]
- This perhaps indicates how difficult it is to hold back on out judgement and to be willing to challenge out initial assumptions. This generation was on a very high madreiga. In addition, Gad, Reuven, and half of Menashe hadn't done anything that made it seem that they would suddenly start rebelling against Hashem. In addition, in truth, they were doing nothing wrong and, in fact, were doing something that would help prevent the rest of Klal Yisroel from being ovver an aveirah. Yet, it seems that it was difficult for the rest of Klal Yisroel to be patient and listen to what Gad, Reuven, and half of Menashe had to say for themselves.[^7]
- A indication about what one's true intentions are when one plans on delivering tochacha can be seen from what one's reaction is if it turns out that the tochacha does not need to be delivered. If the person is upset, then it is probably from a bad middah. If the person is happy that the ratzon of Hashem had not been violated, then this is a sign that they had proper intentions.[^868]
- The Germans were being pushed back throughout 1943.[^869]
- Hitler took increased control of the army. He basically never allowed his army to retreat until it was too late.[^869]
- This led to:
	- The army retreating late in a haphazard way that caused unneeded losses of life and equipment (like in Kharkov, and in the Crimea in the summer of 1943, and in Korsun in February 1944).[^869]
	- The soldiers ignoring his orders (like in Leningrad in January 1944 and Odessa in April 1944)[^869]
- Bands of partisans attacked retreating Germans.[^869]
- The Germans were ferocious against the civilian populations the traveled through.[^869]
- They also used a scorched earth strategy to try and slow the advancing Soviets.[^869]
- the Soviets were advancing on a wide front, stretching from Rostov to Smolensk.[^869]
- The Germans set up defenses along the Dnieper River after blowing up the bridges across it. This held up the Soviet army for October, but by November, the Soviets were able to push the Germans back.[^869]
- In January and February 1944, the Ukrainian army surrounded the German army in the town of Korsun. Hitler refused to allow a retreat. The German army was decimated and only a few troops were able to escape after they decided to retreat anyway.[^869]
- In March, the Soviets surrounded a massive German army in Kamenets-Podolsky. The German field marshal, Erich von Manstein, demanded that Hitler allow the army to retreat. Eventually Hitler allowed it. The troops were able to escape but they lost much of their heavy equipment.[^869]
- Hitler didn't like the fact that von Manstein was asking for so many retreats, so Hitler fired him.[^869]
- In April, Soviet armies threatened to trap German forces in Odessa. Hitler ordered them not to retreat. The soldiers ignored his orders.[^869]
- In January 1944, Soviet forces pushed the Germans back from Stalingrad, which had been under siege for nearly 2 and a half years.[^869]
- Hitler ordered his army to stay put, but after a few days of fighting, the army ignored his orders and retreated.[^869]
- In March, pro-German Finland sent a delegation to the Soviet Union asking for peace. The Soviet Union demanded land and money. Finland refused and prepared for war.[^869]
- During these advanced, the Red Air Force dominated the skies. Hitler was forced to divide his air force to defend Germany against US and British bombers.[^869]
- Over 100,000 German troops were trapped behind Soviet lines in Crimea after Hitler refused to allow them to retreat.[^869]
- In April and May, the Soviet army pushed the German army back to the coast. Around 40,000 German troops were able escape by ship, by around 30,000 were trapped and forced to surrender after the Soviet artillery cut off additional ships from coming.[^869]
- The Germans were defending a massive front (nearly 1400 miles) and their armies were vastly overextended. However, as usual, Hitler refused to retreat and consolidate his forces.[^869]
- In June through September 1944, the Soviets fought against Finland and were able to defeat them. Finland was forced to give up land and money.[^869]
- Also in June, the Soviet army fought the Germans in what is modern-day Belarus.[^869]
- Hitler thought that the Soviets would attack farther south and so he sent his reinforcements there, against the advice of his intelligence officers.[^869]
- Soviets were able to surround German soldiers in Vitebsk and Minsk due to Hitler's refusal to allow them to retreat. By the time the orders came, many soldiers were lost in the retreats.[^869]
- The Soviet army pushed into Lithuania and Latvia.[^869]
- In July 1944, the Soviets liberated the Majdanek concentration camp near Lublin. This was the first time the Soviets saw the German's "final solution". The Soviets told the Western countries about this but they dismissed the reports.[^869]
- Over 300,000 Axis troops had been killed and 150,000 taken prisoner over the summer.[^869]
- Once again, German troops were threated to be captured in Riga, and once again, Hitler refused to allow a retreat. They were eventually pushed to the coast and were able to hold out until the end of the war.[^869]
- Soviet forces starting slowing down at this time to try and spread communism.[^869]
- In 1944, the Polish Army rose up against the German's (the Warsaw Uprising). However, the army was aligned with Polish government which was anti-communist. As such, the Soviets refused to help them, even though they were not far. The rebellion was crushed.[^869]
- Instead of pushing toward Germany, the Soviet army moved south through the Balkans.[^869]
- Many of the countries which would eventually be liberated by the Soviet Union became communist.[^869]
- German troops were surrounded at Kishinev, Romania. A late retreat was called but the Germans lost 180,000 troops.[^869]
- In late August, pro-German Romania surrendered. The Soviets captured the Romanian oil fields. This was a big blow against the Germans as this was their main oil supply.[^869]
- The Soviet army moved through neutral Bulgaria (which would eventually become part of the Eastern Bloc) before pushing through Yugoslavia and then Hungary in October.[^869]
- German reinforcements held pro-German Hungary for 6 weeks, but then were defeated.[^869]
- In Germany, right-winger assassinate numerous members of the center and center left government between 1919 and 1923 (I don't know what is meant by right and left. Economically, I think total communism is the extreme left, and socialism is center-left. I guess capitalism is right? But I don't think that was what the right-wing Germans were fighting for.)[^870]
- In Austria, Germany, and Italy, the economic situation is terrible with high inflation and unemployment. Other countries gave some help to Austria.[^870]
- In Italy, the unrest led to the fascists taking charge. The king didn't want a civil war so he was willing to abdicate to Mussolini rather than cause a war. (This was considered a quazi-coup, I think.)[^870]
- England also suffered high unemployment, although nowhere nearly as bad as these Germany, Italy, and Austria.[^871]
- 1922 was the beginning of material progress. Cars started becoming more widespread, refrigerators are entering peoples' homes, radio and TV are expanding, and people are going to the movies.[^871]
- There is a movement away from the church as people now spend their Sundays driving places.[^871]
- Germany had not paid its necessary reparations to France. As per the agreement Germany signed this allowed the French to occupy and take land producing much of Germany's resources, which they did in the summer of 1923.[^872]
- The government told the people to strike and not help out the French.[^872]
- However, this led to massive inflation due to the lack of production, the worst hyperinflation in recorded history.[^872]
- By the fall, the government compelled people to work, but there was no bringing down the spiraling inflation.[^872]
- In November, Hitler rallied support to try and take over the government. However, guards fired on his march and Hitler was arrested. By the time he was released from prison, the inflation had stabilized (I didn't get how exactly. It seemed that it was because of private banks which regulated currency and foreign countries saving money in German banks because of high interest rates but I don't have clarity.)[^872]
- Japan recovered quickly from the war. However, in September 1923, a massive earthquake rocked Japan. This was then followed by a tsunami. And that was followed up by fires. Tokyo and Yokohama were largely decimated and around 140,000 people were killed.[^873]
- This led to economic disaster in Japan as the country was forced to take massive loans at terrible interest rates. This led to political unrest in the coming years.[^873]
- With all of this political unrest, the General of Spain declared himself dictator of Spain in September, 1923, with the king's support.[^873]
- In the winter of 1923/24, in the Soviet Union, Lenin was dying. After his death, Stalin took over (see Soviet History).[^873]
- The Ku Klux Klan was extremely popular during this time. It was originally anti-Catholic. They feared the pope taking over the White House.[^873]
- In 1924, Calvin Coolidge was elected president. He wanted to shrink government down in size.[^874]
- In 1924, France started suffering from serious inflation.[^874]
- In the election of 1924 in Italy, the fascists emerged with an overwhelming majority of seats. The started laying the groundwork for a totalitarian state. A politician who questioned the legitimacy of the election disappeared and was later found dead. Newspaper editors were replaced by one's who were pro the government.[^874]
- The US and other governments tried to find ways to get Europe, and Germany in particular, back on its feet. They renegotiated the reparations payments.[^874]
- In the Paris Settlement, Japan had proposed a racial equality settlement. Britain rejected this. In 1921, Britain did not renew the Anglo-Japanese alliance. In 1924, the US did not allow Japanese to immigrate to America. This led to a rise in Japanese nationalism.[^874]
- There was overt racism toward the people of Africa.[^874]
- Hitler was released from prison in December 1924.[^874]
- This was an age of eugenics and racial superiority.[^875]
- In August 1925, the Syrians revolted against French rule (called the Syrian Revolution). The French paid Syrian soldiers less than French soldiers. The French required the Syrians to seek permission to do even minor things.[^875]
- The revolts were shut down hard by the French.[^875]
- The French bombed Damascus. The world condemned France for this action.[^875]
- However, a biased inquiry found nothing. Syrians who traveled to give testimony in France were refused.[^875]
- British troops in Shanghai opened fire on an unarmed crowd, killing 12.[^875]
- This nationalist movement was taken over by Chiang Kai-shek.[^875]
- France and German made a treaty signed in October 1925. This allowed Germany to regain economic footing.[^875]
- Within a year, Germany was allowed to join the League of Nations. Relations between Germany and the other European countries improved.[^875]
- However, extremism on the right and left were increasing in German.[^875]
- According to the Alter, the reason why the Klal Yisroel stayed in Mitzraim from Rosh Hashana until Pesach was so Hashem could show his presence in the world to the Bnei Yisroel. Each makka added something to Klal Yisroel's emunah.[^876]
- The briah is constantly being recreated for each person.[^876]
- Rebbi Question: Why does Hashem constantly recreate the world? Hashem is always watching the world and has hasgacha pratis. The Alter looks at the makkos as Hashem constantly recreating the world. What is this added element that the world is being constantly recreated, over classic hashgacha pratis.[^876]
- Answer: Elsewhere, the Alter says similar. The Alter says that Hashem constantly keeps the land above the water. This raises for us a level achrayus and chashivus. Hashem is doing this for every single person, individually. Hashem creates the world for every single person (bishvili nivra haolam). As such, every moment, Hashem is recreating the world every moment for us. That means that our actions are of such importance. The whole briah is being recreated for every individual every moment. This means that, our actions at every moment warrant the creation of the world. That gives our actions tremendous importance, and puts on us a tremendous achrayis.[^876]
- Sometimes, we feel, "who cares about what I am doing?". This perspective is totally incorrect.[^876]
- The Alter says that this is why there is a halacha to put geulah before tefillah. A person should refocus on this perspective when one is about to start davening shemonah esrei.[^876]
- A bochur was invited to the seder of the Chofetz Chaim. The bochur asked his rebbi Reb Yeruchim what he should do. Reb Yeruchim told him that he should go home.[^876]
- The sefer that quoted this story said that the reason for this was because the point of the seder is to connect back to your parents, back to their parents, etc. That is the point of the seder. To instill that connection back and back and back to the first seder. Going to the Chofetz Chaim's seder wouldn't instill this.[^876]
- The "עיקר" is to make fences around what the torah assurs to make sure that one doesn't accidentally come to violate the Torah's commands. When one does this, their intent should be to protect themselves from violating the Torah's commandments, not to add on something extra.[^877]
- Yehoshua was chosen as the leader after Moshe because he was mekabel everything from Moshe.[^878]
- Chazal were able to maintain the continuation of Torah perfectly until the Anshei Kneses Hagidolah, despite the churban which should have caused problems with the transition. This was because of the rachamei shamayim.[^879]
- A chochom needs to:[^880]
	- Think about and plan his actions before executing them.
	- Say things in few words.
	- Say things in a way that the listener can accept.
	- Be fluent in what he is going to say.
- Moshe received the Torah from Hashem in its pure form. He didn't make any interpretations into his own words or anything like that. He just had the Torah as Hashem created it. This was unique. No other transfer of the Torah was this pure.[^881]
- The entire Klal Yisroel was taught the Torah. However, Yehoshua, and the leaders after him, were responsible to continue the transmission of the Torah by making sure that it was kept by Klal Yisroel. And the way they would do this would be through punishing people who did not follow it and rewarding those who did (as most people are motivated by reward and punishment).[^881]
- Only by putting a specific person in charge, could this be successful.[^881]
- In addition, there are emergencies when beis din needs to enforce beyond the letter of the law to keep the Torah alive ('עת לעשות לה). However, this needs someone of great chochma and daas to know when this is. Yehoshua, along with the leaders of the generation (and similarly for every generation) were responsible for deciding if the situation was desperate enough.[^881]
- This is a reason why Torah shebaal peh was not written down and instead given over to the chachamim. This way, they would be able to enact law harsher than the written law during emergencies.[^881]
- History can be used to indicate Hashem's existence.[^882]
- This is far more open to interpretation. This is far less clear than scientific proof.[^882]
- However, there is a certain advantage to this kind of proof. This is more personal. Science is more dry. As such, this kind of proof can affect one in a way that science may not be able to.[^882]
- There are some unique things about Klal Yisroel's history:
	- Nearly all nations begin as sovereign powers in a specific location. Klal Yisroel went from a family to a nation while enduring slavery.[^882]
	- Klal Yisroel has been scattered around the world and has not been a sovereign power in a country from the 2nd Beis Hamikdash until the creation of the state of Israel (and even that is not particularly religious). Most nations in such a situation would become assimilated and lose their identity.[^882]
	- Klal Yisroel has been uniquely attacked by others in terms of the consistency and ferocity. They have been subject to abuse in nearly every country they settled in for a variety of (and sometimes opposite) reasons. This would also normally destroy a nations identity.[^882]
	  It can be hard for modern people to think about what expulsion means. One has to realize that the government did not provide aid for poor people, there were no services that helped people who couldn't speak the language, and all the possessions one was able to bring was what fit into a wagon (assuming one was able to bring their possessions with them at all or get the adequate value for things they would sell, which was not guaranteed).[^882]

- As such, the National Socialist German Workers' Party (the Nazi Party) started to gain popularity with the unrest.[^883]
- The party was racist and antisemitic. They blamed Jews for Germany's defeat in WWI.[^883]
- The party made grand but vague promises about restoring Germany to greatness. They didn't really have specific plans or policies.[^883]
- The party grew in popularity. This was mostly because of the terrible situation and the party promised to improve things.[^883]
- After a series of elections in 1932 in which the Nazi party were extremely popular, Hitler was offered to be the chancellor of Germany. In January 1933, he became chancellor.[^883]
- In late February, a fire was started in the Reichstag (German parliament) building.[^883]
- The Nazis blamed the communists for this and pushed a pair of laws to combat communism, called the Reichstag Fire Decree, which allowed for the imprisonment of anyone against the Nazis, and the Enabling Act, which allowed the Nazis to act without going through the Reichstag.[^883]
- In effect, this gave the Nazis tremendous control over Germany.[^883]
- The communists were not popular in Germany. The rich and poor did not like collectivization as they saw it in the Soviet Union.[^883]

- In national elections in March, 1933, the Nazis took nearly 45% of the vote. Despite not having a majority, they had nearly total power (shortly after this Hitler eliminated all political parties except the Nazis, and issued decrees without going through the Weimar Republic[^907]). This was the last democratic election in Germany until after WWII.[^908]
- One thing we need to keep in mind is that while we have the benefit of hindsight, it was far from obvious to the people at the time what would play out.[^908]
- In the end of June and beginning of July 1934, Hitler executed political rivals within his political party, such as the heads of the Sturmabteilung (the SA. This was a military group independent of the army). He and the SA were gaining popularity and were becoming a threat to Hitler and the army (who Hitler wanted to be the military force and who he wanted to control).[^909][^908]
- Anyone against the Nazis (Communists, Social Democrats, and Jews) were persecuted.[^910]
- The Nazis were worried about their image to the German public, so they made sure to have young leaders in addition to starting the Hitler Youth. The Hitler Youth also was a way of educating/brainwashing the youth. In many situations, the kids would then go and influence the parents.[^910]
- The Hitler Youth movement was largely compulsory. In addition, it was presented as similar to Boy Scouts, so people were okay with sending their kids there.[^910]
- The Nazis felt that education was dangerous so they had little except to educate the kids on Germany's need and right to expand.[^910]
- The Nazis took control of all aspects of society, such as the arts. This made it impossible for Jews to join.[^910]
- Schools began being run by Nazis supporters. In some, students were forced to do the Nazi salute and say "heil Hitler".[^910]
- The press, radio, and movies were taken over by the government to spread propaganda to the masses.[^910]
- The Germans took the 1936 Olympics (held in Berlin) to show the world how peaceful they were. A black American athlete dominated the Olympics and the Berlin fans went wild over him, further showing how tolerant they were.[^910]
- If we assume that the Elephantine Papyri were written between 314 BCE and 233 BC, then there are 15 exact matches, 3 times that the Babylonian date is one day ahead, and none that are two days ahead.[^911]
- These 3, 1-day discrepancies can be explained by the papyri being written at night (as the Babylonian day begins at sunset and the Egyptian doesn't begin until the next sunrise.[^911]
- Conclusions:
- Alexander defeated Darius I (Darius the Great), not Darius III. He did so in 317 BCE.[^912]
- Xerxes became the Emperor of the Persian Empire (which was subservient to the Macedonians) in 314. The Persian Empire lasted for over 150 more years.[^912]
- The Greek version of the chronology is incorrect.[^912]
- The Nazis came to power largely because of dissent over the unemployment. This was solved by:[^913]
	- Programs from before Hitler were starting to make a positive affect on the economy.
	- The government gave money to private businesses to hire people.
	- Manipulation of statistics (such as getting women to stay home rather than work, and then not count them as unemployed as they were not part of the labor force).
	- Rearmament.
	- Forcing the unemployed to do join public works programs (the Volunteer Labor Service).
- Even though Germany was not allowed to rearm under the Treaty of Versailles, they got around this by calling this "job creation" and sometimes lying about what they were producing (they claimed they were producing tractors when they were really producing tanks).[^913]
- Germany stepped up its antisemitism. Historically, Germany was not antisemitic. Many Jews fled from other countries that were antisemitic to Germany to escape the antisemitism (such as Poland, Romania, and the Soviet Union).[^914]
- Only a small minority of Germans believed the anti-Jewish propaganda but people were willing to turn a blind eye and go along with it if it meant that Germany would become great again.[^914]
- In November 1938, a Jewish man shot an important German government official because of the harsh treatment of the Jews.[^914]
- The German government responded by having the SS and SA (with some Hitler Youth) attack thousand of Jewish businesses and shuls and sending around 30,000 Jewish men to concentration camps.[^915] The death toll in total (including suicide and people dying from maltreatment in concentration camps) is estimated between 1000-2000.[^916] Some civilians participated as well.[^915] This became known as Kristallnacht.
- Before then, the Jews were successful, and there were even some wealthy Jews, but in no way did they control the economy.[^914]
- Many Jews fled after Kristallnacht as they realized where things were going.[^914]
- Hitler's popularity grew in the mid 1930s through 1940. During this time, Hitler gained much territory either with no fighting (like taking over Czechoslovakia), or with easy military victories (like Poland).[^914]
- Hitler wanted to conquer territory to the east to make sure his army would have enough food. This was a major reason why Germany lost WWI.[^914]
- Despite Germany rearming, the world largely ignored it (Germany's tricks didn't fool anyone for too long) as there was tremendous anti-war sentiment in these other countries.[^914]
- Vladimir Lenin's brother was killed for attempted assassination of the Russian Czar in 1887. This (along with his father's sudden death a year earlier) had a strong impact on Vladimir (he was a teenager at the time) and drove him to extremist politics.[^917]
- In the beginning of the 1800s, Russia was a backwards place. Most people were serfs, the economy was mostly rural, and the czar and the nobility had absolute power.[^917]
- There was much dissent in Russia over this (western democracies were growing at this time).[^917]
- Alexander II emancipated the serfs in 1861 to make Russia a freer place. After that, he, his son (Alexander III), and his grandson (Nicholas II), stopped the modernization (although they varied in their harshness against it).[^917]
- However, people already started tasting the freedom and so there was extremely strong dissent against this.[^917]
- In addition, a massive famine in 1891 killed several hundred thousand, further leading to discontent.[^917]
- This was the environment that Lenin was growing up in.[^917]
- In his teenage years, Lenin read a lot of the works of political thinkers. He really liked the ideas of The Communist Manifesto, which didn't like how industrialization led to factory owners taking advantage of workers and proposed a system in which the workers would own the means of production. [^917] {I need to come back to this. I need a better definition of communism. Also, there is a difference between Marxism and Communism. I need to get it clear. Also need to get clear the difference between communism and socialism.}
- In the 1890s, Lenin moved to Moscow and joined the Social Democrats (communist supporters), where he published Marxists writings. He was arrested for this and exiled to Siberia. In 1900 he was allowed to leave.[^917]
- He decided to leave Russia as he wanted to continue to write and he didn't want to get arrested again. His works were smuggled into Russia.[^917]
- Many communists believed that society would naturally move toward communism.[^917]
- Lenin did not agree with this view. He felt that armed conflict was needed.[^917]
- He also felt that once one nation became communist, more and more would follow.[^917]
- As such, all acts were justified as it was for the sake of global communism.[^917]
- In 1903, there was a meeting of the Russian Social Democratic Labor Party RSDLP. A combination of many different pro-communist groups). At the meeting, some (led by Lenin) wanted only committed revolutionaries to be allowed to join the party, while others wanted to allow in anyone to appeal to the masses.[^918]
- These became factions within the communist party called the Bolsheviks and Mensheviks, respectively. The Bolsheviks were initially the minority but quickly gained favor.[^918]
- In 1905, unarmed workers who marched on the palace were shot (Bloody Sunday). In addition, the Russians were defeated in the Russo-Japanese War. These events set off massive strikes and mutiny in the army.[^918]
- Lenin wanted to take advantage of this momentum.[^918]
- However, the czar made concessions to the people, giving them rights, and establishing a parliament (called, the Duma). The czar later reneged on his promises. The original parliament was considered "too radical" and disbanded. The next parliaments were ineffective. Further rebellion was crushed.[^918]
- Joseph Stalin, a member of the RSDLP, advocated acts of terror, such as stealing from the government. There was little support for this.[^918]
- Over the coming years, Lenin's influence and health, declined.[^918]
- In 1914, WWI broke out. Russia and Austria-Hungary were at odds with each other as they wanted to dominate the region which had been dominated by the now-failing Ottoman Empire.[^918]
- ![[Pasted image 20250422012052.png]][^919]
- ![[Pasted image 20250422012219.png]][^920]
- The Russian army was poorly trained and equipped and did poorly against the advanced German army.[^918]
- Nicholas II took control of the army and for a while, the army did well, but this was then crushed. This also caused famine within Russia (I assume because the fertile areas of Russia, like Ukraine, were destroyed).[^918]
- In addition, a creepy individual named Rasputin had a lot of influence over the Russian family.[^918]
- All this led to widespread unrest within Russia, including amongst the army. In March 1917, Nicholas was forced to resign and a provisional government led by the Duma took charge.[^918]
- The Germans helped Lenin get back into Russia to try and destabilize it. By mid March, Lenin was back kin Russia.[^918]
- The government quickly became unpopular as Russia continued to get defeated in the war. Lenin gave speeches about workers' rights to further stir up the masses.[^918]
- The new government originally tried to stamp out the communists. However, the military tried to coup the government and they were forced to turn to the communists for help. This strengthened the communist position.[^918]
- The Bolsheviks were the main communist party now as the Mensheviks historically tried to collaborate with the Duma, while the Bolsheviks, historically, wanted no compromises with the government. This made the Mensheviks very unpopular.[^918]
- This was the perfect storm for the Bolsheviks and in October, they couped the government (the October Revolution).[^918]
- The Bolsheviks (who now called themselves the Communist Party) were originally very tolerant. Minorities (which Russia was full of) were considered equal to native Russians and were allowed to declare independence and allowed for alternate political parties. However, by the end of 1918, both of these were rescinded.[^921]
- There were some very liberal reforms, such as a maximum working day (up to 8 hours). This was something that countries in Europe had not yet achieved.[^921]
- Lands were confiscated by the government and redistributed to the poor (they would eventually become collective farms).[^921]
- The Church was largely removed from power, in line with Marxist ideology (I am not sure why). It was not allowed to own land or teach religion.[^922]
- The communists set up their own schools to educate the masses. Until then, only the elite were educated.[^921]
- There were multiple assassination attempts on Lenin. On the 3rd attempt, he was shot twice and nearly died. He didn't die immediately, but his health, which was already declining, now got even worse.[^921]
- Many were not happy with the communists and created a counterrevolutionary army. The Bolsheviks were called the Red Army and were led by Leon Trotsky, and the counterrevolution was called the White Army.[^921]
- The White Army was formed of the Church and former nobles who had lost their power, other political groups that did not like the fact that the communists took absolute power, and some in the military.[^921]
- In addition, the army was in a terrible position as the Germans had pushed the Russian army back and many Russians were deserting.[^921]
- As such, the Bolsheviks needed to make pull out of the war so they could focus on their internal problems.[^921]
- In March 1918, they signed treaty of Brest-Litovsk (Brest is the city of Brisk). Russia had to give up many territories and in exchange for peace with the Germans. They gave up Lithuania, Latvia, Estonia, Poland, Belarus, Ukraine, and some land previously taken from the Ottoman Empire.[^921]
- ![[Pasted image 20250422224639.png]][^923]
- Some of the allies in WWI didn't like the idea of a radical Marxist government taking over Russia and so the US, Britain, France, and Japan all sent forces and supplies to help the White Army.[^921]
- Poland, Ukraine, and Lithuania, all fought against Russia to try and establish their independence.[^921]
- With all of these opposing forces, the Red Army start off poorly.[^921]
- Under such threat, the new government created a secret police force (called the Cheka) to deal with counterrevolutionaries. This force was brutal. It arrested thousands who were imprisoned or sent to Siberia without a trial. This was known as The Red Terror.[^921]
- In some ways, the new Bolshevik government was no better than the czar's government that they replaced. However, one has to keep in mind the pressures they faced when making these judgements.[^921]
- In 1919, Britain and the west were forced to deal with other conflicts, such as the Irish War of Independence against the British. This helped the Red Army recover and win the war. By 1920, the war was largely over, with some mopping up until 1922. The war with Poland also came to an end around then.[^921]
- In the early 1920s, Lenin's health continued to fail. He suffered some strokes and was left partially paralyzed.[^921]
- In a surprising move, Lenin, supported some capitalism and private property in the economy as he wanted to encourage economic growth. This was effective. After Lenin's death, this was slowly revoked.[^921]
- Lenin didn't want Stalin to take charge as he felt that he did not possess the understanding of the ideology well.[^921]
- However, Trotsky (the person Lenin preferred) was recovering from sickness in the Caucasus and Stalin lied to him about Lenin's funeral date. As such, Stalin took control and Trotsky was eventually exiled.[^921]
- While Lenin caused many atrocities, that was nothing compared to what Stalin was going to do.[^921]
- When appointing a judge, one should take time to deliberate who is the best candidate.[^924]
- The person chosen should initially try to turn down the appointment and certainly not be happy about it.[^924]
- People shouldn't rush to adjudicate issues in court. Rather, they, or a friend, should make some sort of compromise. This helps keep peace between the parties. Going to court makes this difficult.[^924]
- A person should speak with respect to the other party in court as not doing this is an insult to the judge.[^924]
- The chachamim make gezeiros for people as well as individual's for themselves in areas that need extra reinforcement.[^925]

As first, Hashem did not tell Kla Yisroel about the fact that they would abandon the Torah and be punished by Hashem "abandoning" them. He waited until Mishna Torah to do this (seemingly, because they would not have been able to handle this at first).[^926]
- It isn't clear to me if this is referring to not teaching halacha, but it may be (it mentions teaching mitzvos in a measured way). This may be a source for not teaching people who are unaffiliated all mitzvos at once.[^7]
- There are some Bible critics that point out that in different places (like Mishna Torah vs the rest of the Torah), different points are mentioned. This may be a response to that. This was done intentionally.[^7]
- There may have been no nevuah between Yehoshua and Shmuel,[^927] there may have been, but it was not important to record for posterity,[^928] or it may be that the people before Shmuel were so great that they didn't need to leave other people to be able to receive nevuah. Beginning from Shmuel, either because of the lower madreiga of the navi or the rest of the people, the navi had to seclude himself from others.[^927]
- People are unable to change the plans of Hashem in any way. Whatever Hashem has planned will happen exactly as it should happen.[^929]
- Sometimes (probably just about always) there are steps needed to make something happen. Hashem wanted these steps to happen as they did.[^929]
- Hashem may sometimes put thoughts into peoples' heads to take certain actions.[^930]
- The Greeks modified the historical record to make it seem that the Greek Empire began with the end of the Persian Empire. They changed the historical records to move the Persian Empire back over 150 years so the end of Darius III lined up with Alexander's victory.[^931]
- Greeks were notorious for rewriting history as they saw fit.[^931] (This is based on The Chronology of Ancient Kingdoms by Isaac Newton. He is referring to the fact that kings' reigns from before Cyrus were clearly lengthened. I think there wasn't much documentation that made its way down to the later, but still ancient, historians (I saw this around page 60 in [this PDF](https://books.googleusercontent.com/books/content?req=AKW5QafgSMKmCx1oHdwSym9sU5uazhKZ5H-GWRgMNUSGLCuQgsCXRd6EmWa-mjm2NK7B7fZqgIXkg2WJtZAuJfpam1HAV6_-7vE9xWRoHFDy-ARy91SME7L7c9XxXdlveW63VJLrErPz6YHtowtwGNwv5iA7VIs5CXiHon7G0G2V4N7pzFT6CA2JUQXSCzKvr2EFM7FF4dYXIOTJZbtJV0nrNKa3B0uXcCzcHwFLA11_NxTln9D512ydMuPiYG5iQfEdtLFI83GnB3bP3LuyQTo1NtzeZEf9TQ) but I can't find it now). I don't think it is clear from there that the Greeks (or anyone else) went on a massive historical revisionist campaign.)
- However, this was not enough. The Babylonian calendar (which the astronomical diaries and the Persian calendar are based) are lunar calendars which have leap years every few years (as discussed previously). Simply altering dates back 150+ years could create problems with lining up the texts that referenced leap years.[^931]
- The Babylonian calendar is based on a fixed 19 year cycle (like the Jewish calendar). As such, the Greeks pushed everything back to a multiple of 19.[^931]
- According to our theory, there were 158 years between the actual fall of the Persian Empire (which would have occurred in 160 BCE) and the year Alexander defeated the Persians (318 BCE).[^931]
- As such, everything (the Persian Empire and Alexander's conquest) had to be pushed back an additional 13 years, making the Persian Empire 171 years earlier than it actually was and moving Alexander's victory from 318 BCE to 331 BCE.[^931]
- This helps explain some difficulties. Herodotus and Thucydides discuss eclipses. However, they misdate them by a matter of years and days respectively. This is difficult to explain as these people were writing about events they lived through. However, if we take our assumption that the dates were later rewritten by the Greeks, it makes sense how mistakes creeped in.[^931]
- Many (secular) philosophers talk about how one should live their life and all that. The Torah is unique as it can be traced back to Hashem.[^932]
- Moving Alexander's reign back to 331 BCE created a new problem. Year 311 was considered "Year One" for a new Greek calendar system. According to the Uruk King List, the length of reigns and their years would be as follows:[^933]
	- Alexander (7 years): 331-324
	- Philip (6 years): 324-318
	- Antigonus (6 years): 318-312
	- Seleucus I (31 years): 312-281
- Nothing significant happened in year 331. As such, as extra year needed to be added to Alexander's reign to line up the beginning of Seleucus I with Year One of this new calendar system.[^933]
- The way the Greeks added 13 years was by doing the following: they made it seem that Antiochus II, who reigned for 15 year alongside Seleucus II according to out theory, reigned before him, adding 16 years in all. They then subtracted 3 years, 2 from Antiochus I and 1 from Seleucus I. This explains the discrepancies between the Uruk King List and the traditional king lists.[^933]
- This also explains why there are no contemporary sources found from Alexander's time and for around 100 years years afterwards, despite the fact that it is known that around at least 45 people documented events in those times.[^934]
- Part of Egypt was under Persian control for much of the Persian Empire. As such, many documents were dated using Persian dates. Documents dated by the Persian kings did not need to be changed. However, documents dated using a double-date of the Egyptian and Persian calendars (which was adopted from the Greeks) would cause problems.[^934]
- The Egyptian calendar is a solar calendar and exactly 365 days long. Since the solar year is 365 1/4 days, the new year falls back one day every four years.[^934]
- The Babylonian (and Persian) calendar was a lunar calendar that instituted leap months every 2 or 3 years so the new year would stay around the Spring Equinox. As such, the Egyptian calendar would fall back compared to the Babylonian calendar as well. (Meaning, the Egyptian new year would come out 1 day earlier compared to the Babylonian calendar every four).[^934]
- By comparing the two dates, one can see around how long before a document was written by looking at the how much the Egyptian calendar had fallen back compared to the Babylonian one if one knew the current Egyptian and Babylonian dates. As such, shifting the Persian Empire back 171 years would make the Babylonian and Egyptian calendars off by 42/43 days (171 ÷ 4 = 42.75).[^934]
- To correct for this, the Greeks had the Egyptians adjust their calendar by skipping 42/43 days so the Egyptian date is 42/43 days later (meaning, if the month of December was skipped, January 1st would be a month early, and in a month, it will be February 1st rather than January 1st, as it would have been) to hide this.[^934]
- A similar thing was done in 1582 when 10 days in October were skipped.[^934]
- Doctoring all double-dated texts would have simply been too difficult.[^934]
- The Astronomical Diaries were either doctored or destroyed. Doctoring them was simple: just rewrite the diaries using the astronomical event that happened 168 (for the Darius and earlier) or 171 (for Xerxes and later) years earlier. The earlier diaries for which they did not know the astronomical event that happened 171 earlier were simply destroyed.[^934]
- Doing this also allowed the Greeks to get rid of the suspicious 3 year gap between Darius and Xerxes.[^934]
- In addition, even the later diaries did not all have to be rewritten. Only some of them did. The rest were able to be destroyed as well.[^934]
- This explains why there are far more diaries beginning with the Seleucid era comparer to earlier.[^934]
- The diaries were kept together in archives so the Greeks easily had access to all of them (they went from the Babylonians to the Persians who conquered them, to the Greeks who conquered them).[^934]
- In November 1932, the Nazi party won the most seats in the Reichstag. However, they refused to join a coalition, leaving the government at an impasse. President Hindenburg made Hitler Chancellor in 1933 to break the impasse.[^935]
- In 1934, Hindenburg died and Hitler declared himself president.[^935]
- By 1935, the Germans had the largest air force in Europe.[^935]
- In 1935, Germany passed the Nuremberg Laws which forbade Jews from marrying Aryans and deprived them of citizenship.[^935]
- In 1935-6, Hitler sent the military to reoccupy the Saarland and the Rhineland, lands that had been Germany's but been taken because of the Versailles treaty. People thought that Hitler just wanted to take back what he felt was rightfully Germany's and would stop there.[^935]

As such, they invaded Manchuria (northern China) in 1931 to gain resources. (Although they also had their eyes on some of the European colonies.)[^936]

- The world condemned Japan at the new League of Nations, but they didn't do anything, so Japan didn't care.[^937]
- Japan wanted to conquer China but feared a Soviet invasion, so in 1936, they signed a treaty with Germany to deal with the Soviets.[^937]
- By this time, China was undergoing a civil war between Chiang Kai-shek and the Kuomintang Republic of China against Mao Zedong and the Chinese Communist Party (CCP). This made them an easy target (especially considering Japan's advanced military).[^937]  {Need to look into this war.}
- In 1937, they invaded China.[^937]
- The Kuomintang and the CCP united to fight against Japan, but they were no match and were pushed back.[^937]
- The Japanese committed many atrocities in this war, particularly at the city of Nanking (called the Rape of Nanking) where they potentially killed 300,000 civilians and other things in 6 weeks.[^937]
