- The US provided Chiang Kai-shek with a large loan but that was it.[^937]
- Italy's economy was in terrible shape in the early 1920s and there was discontent.[^938]
- In 1922, Benito Mussolini and his party of the Fascists {need to look into what fascism is} marched on the capital in Rome. The king of Italy had Mussolini create a government as he didn't want to start a civil war.[^938]
- Mussolini used this to become dictator.[^938]
- He created massive public works programs to deal with the unemployment and improve industry. This made his government popular.[^938]
- He created a powerful army, air force, and navy. (His navy was by far the most powerful in the Mediterranean.)[^938]
- The country handled the Great Depression comparatively well.[^938]
- Mussolini had expansionist ambitions and in 1935, he invaded Abyssinia (modern day Ethiopia) in Africa. Italy already had colonies nearby so this made sense.[^938]
- ![[Pasted image 20250425025417.png]][^938]
- The Emperor of Abyssinia appealed in person to the League of Nations, but they did basically nothing.[^938]
- Abyssinia was easily overrun by the modern Italian army.[^938]
- France and Britain's economies never fully recovered from WWI, let alone, once the Great Depression hit.[^939]
- Both countries were traumatized by WWI and were dealing with unruly colonies (such as in the Middle East).[^939]
- Because of the economic issues, both countries' armies were relatively small and lacked modernization.[^939]
- The French made a defensive line along their border (the Maginot Line) that (theoretically) linked up with Belgian defenses to hold back Germany. They had little thought of going on the offensive.[^939]
- They also knew that the Italians had more ships in the Mediterranean.[^939]
- As such, neither country wanted to get into a war.[^939]
- In Spain, 1935, the left-wing parties won the election.[^940]
- The right-wing parties were concerned that they would make the country communist. Many in the military were right-wingers and so in 1936, there was a mutiny amongst many in the Spanish army.[^940]
- Francisco Franco, a former leader in the army who was thrown out, became their leader.[^940]
- Hitler (who was strongly anti-communist) and Mussolini provided soldiers and weapons to help Franco win the war.[^940]
- The Republicans asked Britain, France, and Soviet Union for help. France and Britain refused. The Soviet Union provided some help. They hoped to keep Germany and Italy occupied with Spain while the Soviet Union built its military. In addition, many volunteers from different countries fought for Spain, as well. However, the help was still no match for what Germany and Italy provided.[^940]
- In addition, there was some infighting amongst different factions in the Republicans.[^940]
- As such, they were slowly pushed back and in March 1939, the war was over.[^940]
- In 1935, Hitler revealed the existence of the Luftwaffe and increased the German army to 300,000. This violated the Versailles Treaty, but Hitler didn't care. France and Britain started to rearm.[^941]
- Austria had many pro-Nazis (there were many ethnic Germans and many who spoke German) who wanted to unite the country with Germany.[^941]
- In March 1938, Austria planned to have a vote if they should become part of Germany.[^941]
- Hitler was nervous how the vote would come out and so he sent in his army. The army arrived to cheering crowds. No fighting took place. Hitler then declared that Austria was part of Germany and Austria agreed. This was known as the Anschluss.[^941]
- There were some ethnic Germans in northwest Czechoslovakia (an area called the Sudetenland). Czechoslovakia was created by the Treaty of Versailles and some ethnic Germans, who were part of the Austrian Empire, were pushed into this new country.[^942]
- Hitler wanted to annex the Sudetenland but the Czechoslovakian government refused. They started to mobilize. They had strong fortifications on their borders and so Germany backed off.[^942]
- In September, Prime Minister Neville Chamberlain was concerned about potential war. He made a deal with Hitler that if the Sudetenland was given over to Germany, they would stop trying to annex more countries. This was known as the Munich Agreement.[^942]
- Chamberlain flew back to Britain with the paper Hitler signed declaring "a peace in our time".[^942]
- Without all its fortifications that were in the Sudetenland, Czechoslovakia was far weaker.[^942]
- In March 1939, Hitler threatened to attack the rest of Czechoslovakia, as Hitler wanted some of its industry, gold, and resources, and wanted to get rid of the threat of its army and airfields.[^943] The Czechoslovakian government capitulated as it was not strong enough to fight[^944] and it basically became part of Germany although it remained an independent country.[^945]
- ![[Pasted image 20250428201249.png]][^946]
- This was the first time Hitler captured non-German territory. This alerted the French and British that Hitler didn't care about the Munich Agreement. They gave him an ultimatum that if Germany attacked Poland, there would be war.[^942]

- Germany had far more soldiers, tanks, and planes than the Polish.[^948]

- Germany, because it had its army partially stationed in East Prussia and in Czechoslovakia, had Poland surrounded on 3 sides. They launched attacks from all sides.[^948]

- It is not clear why the Greeks distorted the Persian history. Some possible theories:[^958]
	- They wanted to make the Greek Empire seem like it had total control of the (rather large) area that was really ruled by the Persians (although they were a vasal under the Greeks).
	- They wanted to take credit for Persian discoveries in science or philosophy and so they made it seem that the Persian Empire ended long before.
	- Sefer Daniel (8 : 3-11, 20-25 and 11 : 2-45) predicts many events in the Greek era (such as a Greek king defeating the fourth Persian king, the empire being divided into four after the death of the Greek king, the kingdom in the south (Egypt, ruled by Ptolemy and his successors) and the north (ruled by Antiochus and his successors). It also predicts their eventual demise.
	  As the predictions proved to be accurate, there may have been a tremendous loss of morale in the empire. As such, they changed the Persian history to discredit the predictions in Daniel.
- The German fleet was vastly outnumbered by the British. Britain declared a total blockade on Germany.[^959]
- Germany used its fleet with about 40 U-boats to attack ships bringing supplies to Britain. During 1939, around 100 were destroyed. The U-boats also destroyed some military vessels, including an aircraft carrier.[^959]
- Some civilian ocean liners were accidentally destroyed as well.[^959]
- The Germans were using a new kind of mine that was set off by the magnetic charge of a passing ship. The British discovered this in the winter of 1939/40 after losing many ships. They used a technique called degaussing to minimize ships' magnetic signature, which largely protected them from the mines.[^960]
- France had a far larger force than Britain along the German-French-Belgian borders, so they were in command. Their plans were defensive. They expected a long drawn-out war similar to WWI.[^961]
- The Soviet Union attacked Finland in November 1939. The Soviet Union was afraid that the Germans would attack the Soviet Union through Finland.[^961]
- ![[Pasted image 20250428221305.png]][^961]
- The Finish were heavily outnumbered but the Soviet Union army was badly disorganized because of Stalin's purges and so they took heavy casualties.[^961]
- However, by March 1940, the Soviet Union had conquered Finland.[^961]
- Germany relied on iron shipped from Norway. Hitler was concerned that the allies would cut this off, so in April he invaded Norway (and Denmark, which happened to be in the way).[^961]
- ![[Pasted image 20250428232611.png]][^430]
- The invasions lasted around a day before both countries fell.[^961]
- The British with some Norwegian troops were able to grab a foothold in late May in Norway. However, the German army was too large and so they left in early July.[^961]
- The original German plan was to go through Belgium to avoid the Maginot Line to attack Paris. This was the same plan used by Germany in WWI.[^962]
- ![[Pasted image 20250429002250.png]][^962]
- The allies were expecting this and planned to move soldiers from the French border through Belgium to the Dyle River to defend.[^962]
- ![[Pasted image 20250429003344.png]][^962]
- Hitler and other German leaders didn't like the plan very much, so when the plans accidentally got revealed to the allies, Erich von Manstein (one of the German planners) proposed that the Germans move their soldiers much closer to the Maginot Line to avoid the ally troops.[^962]
- ![[Pasted image 20250429003649.png]][^962]
- The German troops would have to travel through the Ardennes, which was hilly and woody. The allies assumed it would be impassable for tanks, so they had very few soldiers there.[^962]
- ![[Pasted image 20250429004203.png]][^963]
- The Germans would then be able to slip behind the French and British soldiers and surround them.[^962]
- ![[Pasted image 20250429004714.png]][^962]
- The allies had a slight advantage in terms of numbers of soldiers and tanks. The French and German tanks each had different advantages and disadvantages over each other.[^962]
- The Luftwaffe was far superior to the French air force and the British kept much of their air force at home.[^962]
- The German used their tanks in their own separate units (Panzer divisions) to facilitate their quick-striking blitzkrieg. The French used them to support soldiers and so they dispersed them. This was one of the big differences in the coming battles.[^962]
- Churchill became prime minister in May 1940.[^964]
- On May 10, the Nazis launched attacks on Holland and Belgium and made rapid advances into both countries.[^964]
- The allies executed their plan and moved soldiers to the Dyle Line.[^964]
- The Nazis moved the majority of their panzers south into the Ardennes and avoided the ally forces. By May 12, they were through the Ardennes and quickly started to move behind the ally front lines.[^964]
- Allied bombers and whatever forces were in the area tried to stop the Germans, but they were brushed aside.[^964]
- The panzers went on ahead of the main army. Because of this, the French tried to counterattack the main army. However, due to poor organization, this failed.[^964]
- The French commander realized that his army was about to be encircled and he tried to pull his forces out, but he was too late. By May 20, the Germans had reached the English Channel, cutting France in two.[^964]
- The way Hashem operates can sort-of be broken down into three levels, in a way that is relatable to us:[^965]
	- There is the ratzon of Hashem, which is what Hashem wants.
	- There is chochma, which is Hashem "making plans" for attaining the ratzon.
	- And then there is maaseh, which is carrying out the chochma.
- Human beings have these three things, too.[^965]
- There is nothing more basic than the ratzon (other than perhaps a different ratzon, but eventually,, there will be a ratzon which is the bedrock).  Once we reach that point, the answer to the question of "why is this person's ratzon this way?" is "that's just the way it is".[^965]
- In a similar way, there is a certain bedrock level of ratzon that we cannot go deeper (I don't think it is simply because that is the way it is. It's probably more that we just can't understand the next level).[^965]
- On May 21, British tanks attacked the German lines, but they were also repulsed. Their tanks were made for infantry support and so were slow and didn't have strong weapons. They weren't made to fight panzers.[^966]
- The Germans decided to go east and deal with the allied army rather than south into France. They pushed the allied forces to the ports of Boulogne, Calais, and Dunkirk.[^966]
- ![[Pasted image 20250429143953.png]][^966]
- However, the panzer crews were exhausted, the panzers needed repairs, and the soldiers and supplies were far behind the panzers, so they besieged the allies at Boulogne and Calais, but didn't push on to Dunkirk. On May 26, they started toward Dunkirk, but were further delayed by bad weather.[^966]
- This gave the allies time to try to arrange an escape by boat. Many British ships, including many small private boats, came to ferry the troops off. The British air force held the Luftwaffe off and a French rearguard held the Germans army out. This was called Operation Dynamo.[^966]
- 300,000 soldiers were brought to safety, but Britain lost most of its heavy weaponry.[^966]
- By mid-June, the Germans occupied Paris and raised the swastika flag on the Eifel Tower. Hitler then gave the French the terms for an armistice agreement in the same train car in which the Germans were given an armistice agreement at the end of WWI, to further humiliate the French.[^966]
- Hitler though that now that Britain was alone, they would soon ask to negotiate a peace with the Germans.[^966]
- One thing to keep in mind is that while the mashalim about Hashem are helpful, they are far from perfect.[^965]
- Another point about ratzon is that a ratzon implies a lack of something. A mother has a certain "need" to help her child. This is not true about Hashem's ratzon.[^965]
- (I am not fully sure about the idea that we can't go deeper than someone's bedrock ratzon. A person may have a certain ratzon because it makes them feel good. People have a desire for kavod, or for friendship, or for food, because it gives them a pleasurable feeling. And even this pleasurable feeling we can say comes from certain chemicals released in the brain. Either the mashal doesn't go farther than ratzon when it comes to Hashem, or perhaps for non-physical things, like friendship, we can ask why the brain releases the chemicals when one spends time with friends or whatever it is. I am not sure about this second point.)
- Three reasons why it is important to understand what Hashem's underlying ratzon for mitzvos are:
	- It gives us structure to the mitzvos. This makes it easier for us to accept them.[^965]
	- This gives us the ability to do lifnim meshuras hadin. For example, someone can think that the purpose of shabbos is to spend time with one's family and so one invites his whole family every week. While that may be very nice, that is not the point of shabbos.[^965] (I think R' Lopiansky might be referring to reasons for mitzvos. IDK if  ones needs to know deepest levels of ratzon. Shabbos for example, is about Hashem created the world in 6 days and rested on 7th. Testimony of emunah. There may be deeper levels but IDK if that is necessary.)
	- It allows one to tap into the correct emotions for a mitzva.[^965] (See previous point in parentheses.)
- 3 ratzons of Hashem (I don't think this is an all inclusive list. I think these are examples but I am not sure. These may be 3 very integral understandings):[^965]
	- Hashem's goodness (meaning kindness. "למען טובו יתברך")
	- Hashem's emes. ("חותם של הקב"ה אמת")
	- Hashem's honor/glory. ("למען כבודו יתברך")
- Honor/glory hard to relate to as ratzon. Seems ugly. Even if not ugly (classic response, Hashem really deserves kavod. People don't really deserve kavod), why does Hashem care about what we say about him. (Perhaps somewhat invalid question. Can't really ask about bedrock ratzon.)[^965]
- This helps us get an appreciation for Hashem's greatness. We give a gadol kavod for same reason. Gadol doesn't want kavod. But we do it to strengthen our appreciation for what the gadol represents (being an eved Hashem).[^965]
- Different groups have their own understanding of the baseline ratzon of Hashem (chassidim vs misnagdim, etc.). As long as it fits with all mitzvos and is based on passukim, it is valid. This is "אלו ואלו".<span style="visibility:hidden">l</span>[^965]
- While Britain had a vast empire of colonies, it was not able to bring those soldiers in, at least at the beginning of the war.[^967]
- Hitler offered Britain the right to keep its colonies if it wouldn't interfere with Germany's plans to take over the rest of Europe.[^967]
- Chamberlain refused to surrender. The British people were strongly in favor of this.[^967]
- Hitler wanted to send troops across the English Channel (called Operation Sea Lion), but the British navy which was far larger than the German one, barred the way.[^967]
- The Luftwaffe far outnumbered the Royal Air Force (RAF), although the British planes (the Hawker Hurricane and Supermarine Spitfire) were arguably better than the German planes (the Messerschmitt Bf 109 and 110) (particularly the Spitfire).[^967]
- The British invented radar and set up radar towers along the coast. The Germans didn't target these very much as they thought that radar was not particularly important. This was a mistake.[^967]
- In August, Germany began bombing RAF airfields and the RAF fighters struggled to hold them off. With the help of radar, though, Britain was able to know where and how many German fighters were attacking.[^967]
- British shipping was hammered as the RAF did not have enough planes to defend merchant ships.[^967]
- On August 24, London was bombed (it is debated if London was the target or pilots got lost at night. Most think it was a mistake[^968][^969][^970]). Britain responded by bombing Berlin. This enraged Hitler and he decided to switch targets from the RAF airfields to cities, particularly, London.[^967][^971] These bombing raids occurred from September until May 1941 (known as the Blitz). Over 40,000 British civilians were killed in these raids.[^972][^973]
- According to some, if Hitler would have kept attacking the RAF, they would have been knocked out and Hitler would have been able to establish the air superiority needed to launch Operation Sea Lion.[^967][^974]
- By May, Hitler stopped bombing Britain as he realized that he wasn't really getting anywhere.[^967]
- This became known as the Battle of Britain.[^967]
- The American people were extremely against going to war.[^975]
- Roosevelt wanted to help Britain and he gave speeches which slowly shifted public opinion in favor of helping Britain.[^975]
- He expanded the US army.[^975]
- The US helped Britain by:
	- supplied weapons, ships, and money to Britain (as well as weapons and money to the Chinese, who were battling the Japanese). Because of this, the US arms industry was in full swing by the time they joined the war.[^975]
	- Sent ships to patrol Iceland to relieve British ships there (who didn't want Hitler to take it).[^975]
	- Sent military escorts with merchant ships (particularly those carrying American supplies for Britain).[^975]
	- Had his generals make joint plans with the British.[^975]
- Hitler told his commanders not to attack American ships as not to provoke America, but there were a few accidents. Despite this, the American public was still against going to war.[^975]
- The Germans would encrypt their messages using the Enigma Machine.[^976]
- By July 1939, Poland had made some progress in cracking the code. They passed this information on to Britain and France before getting invaded by Germany and destroyed all evidence of what they did. Despite torture, nobody revealed anything.[^976]
- British mathematician Alan Turing as well as others were able to crack the code by 1941. This became known as Ultra (for ultra secret).[^976]
- Britain wanted to make sure Germany didn't figure out that its encryption was compromised and so they did not act on any Ultra information without developing a cover story for how they got the information. (Germany never realized the Enigma Code was cracked.)[^976]
- Britain organized teams of commando units to launch quick attacks on the coast. Britain launched two attacks on Norway in 1941. Both were successful. They destroyed military installations, war factories, captured German prisoners, brought back Norwegian volunteers. Arguably most importantly, then also retrieved a German Enigma machine, which helped the British crack the code.[^976]
- In one raid, a soldier sent a telegram to "A. Hitler, Berlin" asking him where the German soldiers were after Hitler promised no British soldier would step foot on the European continent.[^976]
- This increased British morale and made Hitler think that the British were going to invade Norway. He left 250,000 troops there (who could have been used elsewhere).[^976]
- Britain help organize resistance movements, particularly in France. The Germans captured some of these people.[^976]
- The BBC sent out encouragement to the occupied countries and encoded messages for the resistance movements.[^976]
- In August 1940, Japan, which wanted access to more resources, took advantage of France's defeat and seized their colony of French Indo-China.[^977]
- The US was alarmed by Japan's aggression and stopped selling oil to Japan.[^977] The US was the largest oil producer by far, producing 60% of all oil at the time.[^978]
- Japan decided to double down rather than back down. They thought that if they could land a decisive blow on the US Pacific Fleet, America would rather make peace and get off Japan's back rather than fight a long war.[^977]
- Japan set out their fleet in November toward Pearl Harbor, Hawaii.[^977]
- As a decoy, Japan sent a delegation to discuss peace with the US.[^977][^979]
- In the early morning of December 7, Japan launched their attack on Pearl Harbor. Dozens of American ships as well as nearly 200 planes were destroyed. However, the US carriers escaped damage as they were not in the port at the time.[^977]
- The American civilians became enraged and wanted to respond. America started mobilizing.[^977]
- Japan started conquering Western colonies all over southeast Asia. Over the next 6 months, they kicked Britain out of Malaya (despite being massively outnumbered and having to capture Singapore, an island fortress), Burma, and Hong Kong (the map below is inaccurate about the year Hong Kong was taken) the US out of the Philippines, and the Dutch out of the Dutch East Indies.[^977]
- ![[Pasted image 20250430014540.png]] (This picture needs to be attributed to By Kokiri at English Wikipedia, modifications by Huhsunqu and Markalexander100. - This file was derived from: Japanese Empire.png, CC BY-SA 3.0, https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=472828)
- During the capture of Malaya, Japanese planes destroyed 2 British capital ships. This showed that even powerful ships require air cover. Battleships and cruisers were becoming far less important that aircraft carriers.[^977][^980]
- In April 1942, the US bombed Japanese cities. B-25 bombers were launched off of carriers, even though they were not designed for carrier takeoffs.[^977] This attack increased American morale.[^981]
- Japan decided to capture the Solomon Islands, Papua New Guinea, and Midway, to be able to have a string of fortified bases around Japan.[^977]
- After Moshe, there was no nevuah until Shmuel (not sure if this is literal because Yehoshua had nevuah). Shmuel then improved the middos of Klal Yisroel and nevuah came back until Chagai.[^982] (It sounds like the Tiferes Yisroel is taking the opinion that there was no nevuah during the days of the shoftim until Shmuel because Klal Yisroel was not on the proper madreiga.)
- There were many on the Anshei Kneses Hagidolah that had nevuah.[^983]
- They saw that nevuah was at an end and so they made many takanos to protect the Torah for the future.[^983]
- A person shouldn't jump to conclusions about other people. Don't make hasty judgements.[^984]
- Similarly one shouldn't be too quick to think that one or one's family is acting properly.[^984]
- One should give direction to his family about how to act properly. This is far more effective than having others give tochacha. (Obviously, one needs to guide oneself as well.)[^984]
- People need to make their own personal gedarim to make sure that they don't put themselves in a place where they may come to do an aveira.[^984]
- According to secular history, the second Beis Hamikdash was destroyed in 70 CE. According to Judaism, it was destroyed in 69 CE. (This is far from universally accepted as Rabbi Hool will point out later. Perhaps that should be mentioned first, rather than at the end the way Rabbi Hool presents it.) This can be calculated in numerous ways:[^985]
	- Method 1:
		- If you add the years from Adam until Shes was born, and then from Shes's birth until his son Enosh's birth (as stated in the Torah), and you keep doing that until Noach, it will come out that Noach was born in year 1056.
		- The mabul happened when Noach was 600 years old (so 1656).
		- Arpachshad was born two years after the flood. If you add the years from Arpachshad's birth until his son Shelach's birth (as stated in the Torah), and you keep doing that until Yitzchak was born, you will arrive at year 2048.
		- Klal Yisroel left Mitzraim 400 years later (year 2448).[^986]
		- The first Beis Hamikdash was built 480 years later (year 2928)[^987] and stood for 410 years (year 3338).[^988]
		- The second Beis Hamikdash was built 70 years later (year 3408)[^989] and stood for 420 years (year 3828).[^988]
		- The current system we use to count the Jewish year is different than the system used in Tanach and Seder Olam. In the current system, the year of creation is counted as year 2 (called Molad Bahard). So if we adjust our calculations to use Molad Baharad, the second Beis Hamikdash was destroyed in year 3829.
		- The current Jewish year is 5785. The second Beis Hamikdash was destroyed 1956 years ago (5785 - 3829 = 1956). The current secular year is 2025. This means that the second Beis Hamikdash was destroyed in year 69 CE (2025 - 1956 = 69).
	- Method 2:
		- The fall of the Persian Empire occurred in 318 BCE.[^989]
		- The destruction of the second Beis Hamikdash occurred 386 years later.[^989] This is year 69 CE.
	- Method 3:
		- The Jews began dating their shtaros according to "Minyan Shtaros Alpha" ("year one" of the Greeks).[^989]
		- Many other nations did this as well. However, some started from year 312 BCE and some from 311 BCE.
		- "Alpha" sounds like it was the earlier date (the first one).[^990] (I don't find this compelling.[^7])
		- 380 years passed from the beginning of minyan shtaros until the fall of the Second Beis Hamikdash.[^991] This is year 69 CE. (Unless my math is off, this is year 68 CE. Beginning in year 312 BCE as year 1 of Minyan Shtaros, year 1 BC is year 312 of Minyan Shtaros. There is no year zero so year 1 CE is year 313 of Minyan Shtaros. 67 years later is year 68 CE and year 380 of Minyan Shtaros. Perhaps you have to assume that Minyan Shtaros began in year 311 BCE and year 380 is year 69 CE. There is no need to make assumptions that "Alpha" meant the earlier year. An alternative might be that the gemara does not mean that the destruction occurred ain the 380th year Perhaps it means that it occurred after 380 years. This may be necessary to say for some rishonim discussed later.[^7])
	- Method 4:
		- The most recent shemitah year was year 2022. Current shmita years are based on Rambam,[^992] based off Seder Olam.
		- Seder Olam says that the Beis Hamikdash was destroyed in the year after shmita, but the Rambam has a way of interpreting it to mean that the destruction occurred in a shmita year.[^993]
		- As such, the destruction could not have occurred in year 70 CE as that was not a shmita year. Year 69 was 279 shmita cycles before 2022 (2022 - \[277 x 7] = 69).
- All these methods work with the assumption that when Seder Olam discussed the destruction of the Second Beis Hamikdash, he meant it occurred in its 420th year. The Rambam[^993] and Rosh[^991] take this assumption.
- However, other rishonim (Rashi,[^994] the Rashbam[^995] (not sure if this is the actual Rashbam or the Rashbam who was one of the baalei Tosafos), and Tosafos[^991]) say that Seder Olam meant that the Second Beis Hamikdash stood for 420 full years and was destroyed in its 421st year, year 70 CE, in line with secular history.
- This would address the 1st, 2nd, and 4th methods. As for the 3rd method, we can just take the assumption that the Jews began counting Minyan Shtaros from year 311 BCE.[^7]
- By spring 1942, the US codebreaking team had a decent grasp on the Japanese military and diplomatic codes. The Japanese did not find out that the US had done this until after the war.[^996]
- Papua New Guinea was extremely important for the allies to hold because if Japan was able to take it, Australia and New Zealand would be cut off from the rest of the allies.[^996]
- In early May, the Japanese took the Solomon Islands and then set out through the Coral Sea toward Papua New Guinea.[^996]
- From the codebreakers, the US knew what was coming and sent 2 carriers and some other ships to take on the larger Japanese fleet, containing two carriers, one light carrier and other ships.[^996][^997]
- ![[Pasted image 20250430220432.png]][^998]
- By NormanEinstein - Own work, CC BY-SA 3.0, https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=5827423
- The battle ended with both sides losing some ships and planes. The US lost a carrier and had another damaged. The Japanese lost the light carrier and had a regular carrier damaged. (The surviving US carrier was the Yorktown, which comes up again.)[^999]
- The Japanese won the battle in the sense that they sank more important ships than they lost. However, in a strategic sense, the US won as:
	- Japan called off there invasion of Papua New Guinea and so Australia was not cut off.[^999]
	- This was the first Japanese advance that was pushed back and increased US morale.[^999]
	- The Japanese carriers that were used in this attack were not able to be used at Midway. (Even the undamaged carrier lost too many planes).[^1000]
- The Japanese wanted a decisive battle as they knew that the longer the war was drawn out, the more the American industrial power would make a difference.[^996]
- Japan decided to attack Midway as this would threaten Pearl Harbor. This would get America to commit it forces.[^996]
- They first planned on sending a small force to the Aleutian Islands as a decoy to get the US to send forces there. However, because of the codebreakers, the US knew this was a feint and didn't bite.[^996]
- ![[Pasted image 20250501003310.png]][^996]
- The Japanese approached Midway with 4 aircraft carriers. The US had 3, plus the airfield on Midway itself.[^1001]
- Because the US knew what the Japanese were doing, they were able to surprise the Japanese fleet.[^996]
- At first, the Japanese thought the American fleet was not at Midway. The Japanese commander had reserve bombers outfitted with weapons to attack the island, rather than torpedoes to attack ships.[^996]
- While the weapons change was occurring, a US fleet was spotted, although it took some time to find out its composition. Meanwhile, Japanese bombers were returning from Midway and needed to either land, or ditched into the sea. The Japanese decided to switch the weapons on the reserve bombers back while the bombers from Midway landed.[^1002]
- During that time, the US carriers launched their own dive bombers and torpedo bombers. They were able to sink 3 of the Japanese carriers.[^996]
- The fourth Japanese carrier was able to send out two waves of planes and damaged the US carrier Yorktown.[^996][^1003]
- After that, though, the Japanese carrier was sunk.[^996][^1004]
- The Yorktown was able to pull itself out of the battle, but was struck by a torpedo from a Japanese U-boat a few days later and was sunk.[^996][^1005]
- The Japanese lost its dominance in the Pacific in one battle. This changed the course of the war.[^996]
- In July, the Japanese landed soldiers on Papua New Guinea. They pushed across toward the capital, Port Moresby.[^1006]
- ANZAC (Australian and New Zealand Army Corps) troops fought back. The Japanese and the ANZAC troops both fought well in the jungle, but slowly the Japanese gained ground.[^1006]
- Both sides suffered from the jungle conditions, such as constant rain and malaria.[^1006]
- In September, US troops and air support arrived. This turned the tide and they were able to push the Japanese back.[^1006]
- The Japanese rarely surrendered and so most of the Japanese soldiers died fighting.[^1006]
- ![[Pasted image 20250501025549.png]][^1007]
- Also in July, the Japanese started building an airfield on the island of Guadalcanal (one of the Solomon Islands).[^1006]
- In August, the US sent in troops to try and capture the airfield before it was completed, and then complete it themselves and use it for US planes.[^1006]
- After the troops landed, a Japanese fleet came by and sunk or chased off the US support vessels for the operation. Still, the US was able to capture and finish the airfield.[^1006]
- The Japanese sent many troops to try and recapture the airfield. The US troops, with help of air power from the new runway, held them off. The fighting ended in February 1943.[^1006]
- All the details of all halachos,[^1008] perush of the Torah, midrashos[^1009] (not sure if this means Midrash), Mishna, Gemara, Agadah, and whatever psakim were going to later be established by a talmid[^1010] were given to Moshe at Har Sinai.
- Mussolini had big dreams of expanding Italian territory.[^1011]
- He planned on taking British-controlled Egypt and Sudan to make a contiguous piece of Italian land as the Italians had Libya and Abyssinia (present-day Ethiopia). The Italians heavily outnumbered the British.[^1011]
- ![[Pasted image 20250501194926.png]][^1011]
- In September 1940, the Italians invaded Egypt and made some progress. In December Britain counterattacked and pushed Italy out of Egypt and halfway through Libya (called Operation Compass).[^1011]
- The Italians had over 130,000 soldiers captured, over 5000 killed, and lost hundreds of tanks and planes. Britain lost under 600 soldiers and a several dozen planes.[^1012] (I don't know how many tanks but I assume not many.)
- The British also invaded Abyssinia in June 1940 and were victorious by November 1941.[^1013]
- According to Seder Olam {I don't know where}, Achashveirosh of the megillah ruled between Cyrus and Darius the Great.[^1014]
- This doesn't fit the standard chronology. According to the standard chronology, there was only one king who ruled for more than a few months during that time and that was Cambyses II (the son of Cyrus). He ruled for 8 years (9 if you count his accension year). According to the megillah, Achashveirosh ruled for at least 12 years.[^1014]
- The accepted belief is that Achashveirosh is Xerxes. This is because Xerxes' name in Old Persian is Khshayarsha, which is somehat similar to Achashveirosh. However, there are a few problems with this:
	- The previously mentioned Seder Olam.[^1014]
	- Esther was a tzadeikes {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">need primary source</span>}.[^1014] Xerxes' wife, named Amestris, was known for her cruelty (at least according to the Greek historians, which may be inaccurate).[^1014][^1015]
	- The simple reading of the megillah sounds like the Jews were in exile at the time of the purim story (like 2:5-6 which talks about how Mordechai was exiled from Yerushalayim).[^1014]
	- The Midrash {<span style="color:rgb(255, 0, 0)">need source</span>} says that the feast at the beginning of the megillah was thrown in celebration that the "70 years" predicted for the galus had passed without Klal Yisroel being redeemed.[^7]
- In April 1939, Italy occupied Albania. In October 1940, they invaded Greece. The Italians heavily outnumbered the Greeks but by March 1941, they had been pushed out of Greece and were being driven out of Albania. Britain then sent troops from Libya to help.[^1016]
- In April, Hitler decides to send 500,000 German troops through Yugoslavia into Albania and Greece to help his Italian allies.[^1016]
- He quickly makes progress and pushes all the way to the southern tip of Greece.[^1016]
- The British forces retreat to the island of Crete. The German army followed.[^1016]
- They sent in paratroopers to capture airfields so they could fly in the rest of their troops, and by May, defeated the British in Crete, as well.[^1016]
- ![[Pasted image 20250502024222.png]][^1017]
- This victory helped secure the Mediterranean for the Axis which was important for shipping supplies to Africa.[^1016]
- German forces led by Erwin Rommel (soon to be known as the "Desert Fox") were sent to Libya to help the Italians. The axis forces were larger than the Allied forces.[^1018]
- He quickly pushed the Allies all the way out of Libya. One of Rommel's main tactics was to outflank his opponents, which was possible in the wide open and flat desert.[^1018]
- In the retreat, Australian troops got trapped in the port city of Tobruk, Libya, behind German lines. Allied attempts to break through and rescue them failed.[^1018]
- In November, the Allies were finally successful and pushed the Axis forces back deep into Libya. However, the equipment needed to be repaired and the troops needed to recover.[^1018]
- In January 1942, Rommel advanced again. The Allied forces were recovering and didn't expect Rommel's forces to be ready again so soon, so they were caught off-guard. The Axis made quick progress.[^1018]
- The aroma of a food (reicha) has kashrus significance.[^1019]
- As such, lechatchilah one should avoid cooking dairy (or meat) foods with meat (or dairy) in the same enclosed space. In addition, one should avoid cooking parve foods with meat (or dairy) if one wants to eat it with dairy (or meat).[^1019]
- Bidieved, both foods may be eaten unless:
	- The enclosed space had not vents. This is not relevant to most modern ovens, but is relevant to microwaves.[^1019]
	- If one of the foods was sharp.[^1019]
	- Parve foods cooked with meat (or dairy) can't be eaten with dairy (or meat) as this is not considered bidieved as one can simply choose not to eat the food with the dairy (or meat).[^1019]
- If either of the foods is completely covered with a tight covering then reicha is not an issue (although there are others).[^1019]
- If one is cooking food in a hotel they should double wrap it because of non-kosher residue on the shelves.[^1019]
- New vaccines are tested against established vaccines that are effective against a particular disease if there is an established vaccine. This is because it is considered unethical not give the control group anything real.[^1020]
- Germany pushed the Allies back to El Alamein by July 1942. The Allies set up defenses here which stretched from the Mediterranean in the North to a depression in the south that was impassable to tanks.[^1021]
- The Allies held but could not push the Germans back. They were locked in a stalemate.[^1021]
- The Mediterranean was important for transporting supplies (particularly oil from the Middle East for the Allies).[^1021]
- In Summer 1940 Italy attacked the island of Malta. Malta was a refueling base for Allied ships and Allies launched attacks against Axis shipping.[^1021]
- In the Winer and Spring of 1940-1, British planes torpedo bombed the Italian navy, greatly weakening it.[^1021]
- Germany realized it couldn't rely on Italy for the Mediterranean and began bombing of Malta in Winter 1940-1. This lasted until well into 1942.[^1021]
- In Summer 1942, Britain attempted to resupply Malta. An initial attempt was destroyed, but a second effort got some supplies through.[^1021]
- In October 1942, Allies counterattacked Rommel with American Sherman and Lee tanks and air support.[^1021]
- Rommel had a strong defensive position and the Allies suffered heavy casualties, but the Allies were victorious and Rommel retreated. It was Allies first victory in the region.[^1021]
- At this time, Anglo-American force landed in present-day Morocco and Algeria to the West (called Operation Torch). They threatened to surround Rommel.[^1021]
- Germany reinforced the African army. In February 1943, Rommel counterattacked British forces to east. British forces were forewarned by codebreakers and so Britain won.[^1021]
- Rommel left Africa and told Hitler to abandon Africa but Hitler refused.[^1021]
- The Allies kept advancing and pushed the Germans against the coast until 250,000 Italian and German soldiers had to surrender.[^1021]
- Allies decided to get to Germany through now weakened Italy. They Decided to go through Sardinia.[^1021]
- The Allies laid a false trail to the Axis that Sardinia was just a feint and Sicily was main target. The Germans fell for it. The attack began in February.[^1021]
- The Italian homeland getting attacked brought mass outrage in Italy. A popular uprising overthrew Mussolini and a new government started secret peace talks with Allies.[^1021]
- British forces advanced up the East of Italy and US forces went up the West.[^1021]
- The Allies made progress until weather turned bad in November. They had made it to the defensive line called the Gustav Line.[^1021]
- The US tried to go around on ships and landed in Anzio. However, the German forces held them there.[^1021]
- Numerous attempts to break through failed. The Allies stuck at Gustav Line and Anzio until late Spring. Then they broke through the line, and the forces at Anzio broke out.[^1021]
- The Allies made it to Rome (the Italian capital) in June.[^1021]
- German forces retreated to Gothic Line. In September, the Allies punched through but were stopped by bad weather.[^1022] Allies pushed through in Spring 1945.[^1021]
- Mussolini was shot by Italian partisans and his corps was hung.[^1021]
- In summer 1941, Germany invaded the Soviet Union. As such, the Blitz in Britain stopped as the Luftwaffe was largely diverted.[^1023]
- In spring 1942 Britain started producing new 4 engine bombers. These planes could carry far larger payloads.[^1023]
- When Britain started bombing, they targeted strategic targets.[^1023]
- Problems: 
	- The bombs were totally inaccurate (one report said that 10% landed within 5 miles of the target).[^1023]
	- Germans powerful air defenses. 10% of bombers destroyed per raid on avg.[^1023]
- As such, Britain changed tactics to carpet bombing and night raids.[^1023]
- In the summer 1942, the US forces arrived with the new B-17 Flying Fortress. The plane had a "bomb sight" (so bombs would officially be more accurate) and had many guns so it (theoretically) didn't need fighter escort.[^1023]
- As such, the US went back to the old system of focusing on specific targets. Britain stuck with carpet bombing.[^1023]
- There was a massive bombing campaign in Winter 1942-3 which focused on industrial Ruhr region.[^1023] This caused German supply shortages.[^1024]
- British attacks were extremely destructive and they didn't suffer many losses at first.[^1023]
- Over time, Germany got better at defense and by Spring, losses were back up to 10%[^1023] partially due to improved radar.[^1025]
- The Allies invented "Window". This was strips of aluminum and glass dropped from planes in a wide area, which jammed radar.[^1026] Britain began to use this in Summer 1943 and losses dropped.[^1023]
- In July-August 1943, the Allies firebombed Hamburg (called Operation Gomorrah). 40,000 people were killed.[^1023]
- By Spring 1944, Germany increased their anti-air guns, put many on towers to increase visibility, increased lights, and improved warning systems.[^1027] Allied losses climbed back up to 10%.[^1023]
- In addition, German morale wasn't breaking either.[^1023]
- US targeted bombing wasn't very effective. Americans attack on targets beyond fighter escort range were a disaster.[^1023]
- Bombing was not winning the war as hoped.[^1023]
- In summer 1944 (d-day {discussed later}), Allies started advancing on ground in Europe.[^1023]
- In 1944, the P-51 Mustang engine was changed. This gave it longer range, allowing it to escort bombers.[^1023]
- This allowed the US to reintroduced targeted attacks during the day on bridges and German resupply efforts which proved to be effective.[^1023]
- Britain bomber command preferred night raids and carpet bombing, so they went back to that.[^1023]
- In February 1945, the Allies bombed Drezdin, killing 50,000. The city had no military significance. British people were upset by this.[^1023]
- In all, the bombing campaign killed many civilians, killed many British crews and wasn't as effective as hoped.[^1023]
- Small German battleships and U-boats destroyed British merchant ships. German battleships were very fast and able to escape large ships. 130 merchant ships destroyed in year and a half (doesn't seem to fit with earlier statistic of 100 in 1939.)[^1028]
- During the war, Germany built two battleships, called the Bismarck and the Tirpitz.[^1028] Heaviest battleships ever built in Europe.[^1029] The Bismarck was sunk in 1941 without making much impact on the war by British torpedo bombers and navy.[^1028]
- Britain helped resupply the Soviet Union once Germany attacked. Germany sent forces into the North Sea to stop them, including the Tirpitz.[^1028]
- Britain launched numerous attacks (submarine, carrier) against the Tirpitz but they were largely ineffective.[^1028]
- In 1944, the Tirpitz was sunk by massive bombs from British bombers. In the end, the Tirpitz did not significant impact on the war, either.[^1028]
- German U-boats menaced British shipping. Britain didn't have a lot of anti-submarine weaponry. Carriers with anti-submarine weapons were used but weren't very effective.[^1028]
- Got far worse after fall of France and gave Germany access to Atlantic ports.[^1028]
- 100 ships destroyed by the end of 1939 (don't know what the stat is.)[^1028]
- By October 1940, 40% of British merchant shipping destroyed.[^1028]
- British anti-sub tech improved by Spring 1941.[^1028]
- "Corvette" ship began being produced. Planes had depth charges. New radar able to be equipped to ships was added to detect uboats on surface. Radio detection equipment to get location of German ships sending radio transmissions called Huff Duff[^1028] (High Frequency Detection Finding, to HF/DF, to Huff Duff[^1030]). British losses shrank by over half.[^1028]
- Germany started losing top U-boat commanders.[^1028]
- In May 1941, Britain captured a U-boat with Enigma machine and code book. This helped codebreakers overall and specifically helped steer ships away from German U-boat locations.[^1028]
- In December 1941 Germany declared was on US and started targeting US. The US did not listen to British advice of grouping ships together and lost 400 in 3 months.[^1028]
- US began sending ships in convoys and losses started falling.[^1028]
- In July 1942, Germany focused attacks on the Mid-Atlantic Gap (also called the Black Pit). Area of the mid-Atlantic too far for anti-submarine aircraft. Attack was very effective throughout winter and spring 1942-1943, although Germany also suffered losses from new Allied tech and tactics[^1031] (see below).[^1028]
- In late 1942 Britain developed:
	- New tactics. Small fleets set up to patrol.[^1028] Convoy protection ships couldn't leave convoys. This new group could pursue subs.[^1032]
	- New weapon called "Hedgehog". Cluster of mortar shells fired from ships that would explode after a few seconds underwater.[^1028]
	- Planes equipped with powerful searchlight. Turned on suddenly to surprise subs on the surface and destroy them.[^1028]
- German Uboat losses increased. In May 1943, 1/4 uboats were sunk.[^1028]
- New US anti-submarine planes with additional fuel were invented (closed Mid-Atlantic Gap).[^1028]
- Germany tried countering by adding by adding radar, anti-air guns and new "snorkel" (so they could stay underwater and inlet air) to uboats, However, too little, too late.[^1028]
- Britan ran commando missions along German-occupied coast to destroy German stuff and to test/learn about German defense. Taught Allies lessons.[^1033]
- Attempt in Dieppe France was Allied disaster. Taught Allies:[^1034]
	- Surprise is required.
	- Heavy artillery barrage required.
	- Don't take on German ports.
	- Proper reconnaissance.
- D-day/Overlord.
- Germans preparations. Massive walls (although wasn't completed) and gun emplacements, mined beaches. Began in 1942. Many troops, but area too big.[^1033]
- ![[Pasted image 20250508000012.png]][^1035]
  By Uberstroker, CC BY-SA 3.0, https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=34817779
- Idea 1: Large panzer division in reserve.[^1033]
- Probs: Have to deal with Allied air power.[^1033] Also, limited fuel. {need source}
- Idea 2: As such, have troops on beaches in advance to quickly crush attack.[^1033]
- Germans did both. Some reserve and some scattered. This ended up being the worst idea.[^1033]
- 2 options for Allied landings: Pas de Calais and Normandy. PdC shorter trip across water and shorter to Germany. But more obvious. Germany had strong defense.[^1033]
- Reconnaissance began in Fall 1943 in North France. Potentially heaviest reconnaissance mission ever.[^1033]
- Needed a port but knew they couldn't attack one after Dieppe disaster.[^1033]
- Idea: Take beach (not by port) and create an artificial harbor (called mulberries).[^1033]
- Another prob: How to get the first wave of troops onto the beaches.[^1033]
- Technical advances: Different ways to get tanks to overcome physical obstacles Like mobile bridges. Called "funnies" (they looked ridiculous).[^1033]
- Laid false trails using German double agents. Created "fake army" in Kent near PdC.[^1033]
- Inflatable tanks, wood planes, radio transmissions, soldiers walking around.[^1033]
- Eisenhower in charge. British Montgomery second in command.[^1033]
- 2 months in advance air attacks. Many targeted Pdc to further show PdC was target.[^1033]
- June 6 airborne units captured bridges and nearby villages. Attack started early morning.[^1033]
- Countermeasures (things that appear on radar) by PdC made PdC seem like target.[^1033]
- Bad weather convinced Germany that Allies wouldn't attack. Rommel and other leaders left to Germany and Paris.[^1033]
- Germany didn't have warning at Normandy.[^1033]
- Allies under heavy German fire and German obstructions.[^1033]
- Multiple beach landing. Omaha beach had trouble. High cliffs. Slowly made progress.[^1033]
- "Funnies" held troops advance.[^1033]
- By afternoon, beachheads established.[^1033]
- Hitler originally thought Normandy was a feint. Allowed mobile reserves to attack too late.[^1033]
- Over 100,000 troops landed by night.[^1033]
- Began moving inland. Wooded area good for German defenses.[^1033]
- Air force and French underground resistance took out panzer reserves.[^1033]
- Germany killed French civilians in response.[^1033]
- Largely British with Canada {won't refer to Canada but they were there} to the east and US to the west.[^1033]
- 6 days after landing Britain advanced on Caen. Tiger tanks destroyed Sherman tanks. Advance failed.[^1033]
- US Advance on Cherbourg. Slow going. Bed weather destroyed/damaged mulberries. Slowed resupply. Eventually taken. But port destroyed. Took 1 month to be fixed.[^1033]
- Second assault on Caen. Bad weather (mud and no air support visibility). Pushed back.[^1033]
- Weather cleared. Bombers trashed defenses. Destroyed city good for defense. Allies stopped again.[^1033]

- v-2 rocket in September. Mobile launcher. Longer range. Traveled 3.5k mph. Couldn't be intercepted. Attacks continued for 6 months until German lines pushed back.[^1037]

- Current strategy: advance along wide front.[^1037]
- Needed new strategy to deal with short supplies.[^1037]
- Operation Market Garden: Mid September. Focused attack in one location and drive through defences until Germany. Main attack from Belgium, Airborne troops ahead would take bridges.[^1037]
- Mixed results with airborne troops. Some bridges taken. Others met strong resistance or bridge blown up by Germans.[^1037]
- Main force was ambushed by Germans with anti-tank weapons.[^1037]
- Progress was made, but one of the bridges was not able to be taken. Market Garden was abandoned.[^1037]

- Japan largest empire 1943. Expanded empire=more resources.[^1045]
- Army's plan:[^1045]
- ![[Pasted image 20250506220329.png]][^1045]
- Cut off Japan from SW occupied territories. Army would lead (largely land based operations). Douglas MacArthur (US General) wanted to recapture Philippines. He made promise to return after getting kicked out by Japanese.[^1045]
- Navy's plan: Island hopping. More direct route to Japan and also cut off supplies.[^1045]
- ![[Pasted image 20250506220444.png]][^1045]
- ![[Pasted image 20250507001251.png]][^430]
- Both plans put into action.[^1045]
- Army targeted Japanese base in Rabaul. 2 pincers. One up Papua New Guinea. One up Solomon Islands.[^1045] Operation from June until March.[^1046] Rabaul was cut off.[^1045]
- In Papua New Guinea. Advance slowed by thick jungle and Disease.[^1045]
- ![[Pasted image 20250506234830.png]][^1045]
- Navy: New Essex class carriers. New planes (Hellcat, Corsair, and Helldiver). Better and more planes than Japan.[^1045]
- US landed in Tarawa in November 1943. US disaster. Extremely strong defenses. Many heavy and light guns, pillboxes, and trench system.[^1047] Low tide and high reefs stopped landing craft.[^1048] Marines had to wade.[^1045]
- Massive US casualties. Slow advance. Nearly 1700 US troops killed and 2000 wounded. Over 4500 Japanese forces killed.[^1049]
- US learned lessons such as to have better reconnaissance, more pre-landing bombardment, waterproof gear (like radios).[^1050]
- Japanese fought to death. Only 17 captured alive.[^1045]
- Arrived at Marshall Islands in January 1944. Bombed and shelled for a month before assault in February.[^1045]
- Some islands fell quick but some were wooded and good for defense. The US used flamethrowers and grenades. The Japanese used Bonsai charges (reckless charges at US troops). Over 11k Japanese to over 600 US deaths.[^1051]
- Atoll after Atoll taken (some bypassed and cutoff).[^1045]
- Arrived arrived at Mariana Islands in June 1944. Islands were significant to cut off supply routes and within bombing distance of Japan.[^1045]
- Marines attacked island of Saipan. Mountains and caves made attack difficult.
- Japan sent carrier fleet. US sent carrier force to intercept. Met in mid-June. US radar gave warning. Intercepted Japanese planes. Largest carrier air battle.[^1045]
- US 15 carriers: 900 aircraft. Japan 9 carriers 500 planes. However, Japan pilots were rookies. Experienced pilots had been killed. US pilots were better.[^1045]
- US subs torpedoed 2 carriers quickly. Near nightfall, another Japanese carrier spotted near outer range of fuel limits. US sent planes and destroyed carrier. Returning planes had hard time finding carrier and landing in the dark. Many were ditched or crash landed.[^1045]
- US victory. Japanese navy driven off and weakened. 300 Japanese aircraft lost.[^1045]
- US captured Saipan in July. Japanese civs committed suicide rather than be captured.[^1045]
- Japanese soldiers used bonsai charges and refused to surrender. Japan lost 32k. US lost 3k.[^1045]
- Army and Navy both completed 1st phase by summer 1944.[^1045]
- ![[Pasted image 20250506222603.png]][^1045]
- Navy plan was quicker and avoided Philippines (see above).[^1045]
- However, Roosevelt chose army plan and wanted Navy to back them.[^1045]
- Operation began in October 1944.[^1052] US landing in Philippines went smoothly. Japan expected them to land on main island of Luzon. US landed on smaller island of Leyte.[^1045]
- ![[Pasted image 20250507003137.png]][^430]
- Japan navy plan to push US back. Pincer strategy and decoy from north with 4 carriers.[^1045]
- ![[Pasted image 20250506222853.png]][^1045]
- US Planes attacked north pincer. North pincer appeared to fall back. US fleet went after decoy.[^1045]
- Then large part of remaining US ships ambushed southern pincer.[^1045]
- North pincer turned back toward US landing while dark arrived, surprising Americans. Fleet included super battleship Yamato.
- Few American forces remained to cover landing and US planes were equipped with bombs for land targets, not ships.
- At last second, Japan pulled back. The commander thought it was a US trap.[^1045]
- In late October, 4 Japanese carriers from the feint were sunk.[^1045]
- Fall 1944, little Japan ship forces left.[^1045]
- Japan created Kamikaze (suicide pilots) units.[^1045]
- Started deploying kamikazes in late October against US ships, causing serious damage.[^1045]
- Japanese soldiers carried mines dove under US tanks.[^1045]
- Took 2 months. 70k Japanese troops lost. 16k US.[^1045]
- US proceeded to island of Luzon.[^1045]
- US forces approached capital of Manila in January 1945. Main Japanese commander withdrew troops to preserve ancient architecture. Junior commanders didn't leave. US and Japan fought in the capital and destroyed much of it.[^1045]
- Last stronghold in city was at Drum Fort. US packed vents with explosives and blew it up.[^1045]
- 100k Philippine civilians killed. Many massacred by Japanese.[^1045]
- Philippines mission took many US lives, as feared by critics of plan.[^1045]
- US navy blockaded Japan. Running out of supplies by late 1944.[^1045]

- Danial 6 1 says that Darius the Mede became ruler of Chaldea (part of the Babylonian Empire) after the fall of the Babylonians.[^1059]
- According to Josephus and ancient Persian texts (presented by Ibn Ezra Daniel 6 1) Darius the Mede was Cyrus's father-in-law. Gave empire to Cyrus after dying.[^1059]
- Herodotus (lived shortly after), Xenophon, a Babylonian Chronicle (BM 35382 also called the Nabonidus Chronicle), and Cyrus Cylinder don't mention Darius the Mede and portray Cyrus as main figure in conquest.[^1059]
- Cyrus was the ruler of the Persians for many years before conquest of Babylonia.[^1059]
- Uruk King List and standard King List show Cyrus ruling right after Nabonidus.[^1059] (Nabonidus was last Babylonian King. Belshazzar was acting king under Nabonidus. {Need source})
- Ancients texts from Cyrus first year refer to previous year as year 17 of previous king. Nabonidus ruled for 17 years according to king lists and 1000s cuneiform tablets through year 17, but none beyond.[^1059]
- Perhaps Darius the Mede is Gobryas. Was first governor of Babylonia under Cyrus.[^1059]
- Probs: 
	- No documents dated to Gobryus until Cyrus fourth year. By Cyrus second year, he was called "Cyrus, King of Babylon" Also, Cyrus issuing massive decrees in Babylon (allowing people to return to home countries). As such, seems that Gobryus didn't weild king-like power in Babylonia.[^1059]
	- Daniel 6 1-2 talks about Darius setting things up across whole empire. Gobryus was just in Babylonia.[^1059]
- It seems that Darius the Mede is another name for Cyrus.
- According to Herodotus and Xenophon, Cambyses's was partially Median (his father's mother's father was Astyages, the Median king Cyrus defeated).[^1060]
- Darius was sometimes used as a throne name (like pharaoh of Egypt).[^1060]
- Many people in Tanach went by multiple names. (Sarah and Yiskah, Shifrah and Yocheved, Puah and Miriam, Yisro had multiple names, Daniel and Hasach, and others.)[^1060]
- Yeshaya 45 1 says that Cyrus was the main person in the conquest of Babylonia. This contradicts a theoretical new person Darius the Mede who conquered Babylonia.[^1060]
- Daniel 5 31 says that Darius the Mede was 62 years old at the time of the conquest of Babylonia. According to Cicero, Cyrus died at 70. According to the standard king list, Cyrus ruled for 9 years, making him around 62.[^1060] (He would have been 61, I think, if we don't count the accension year. Perhaps Cicero was off by a year.[^7] How this fits wit Seder Olam will be explained later.)
- Probs:
	- Daniel 9 1 says that Darius the Mede was appointed king over Babylonia. Cyrus conquered Babylonia and became king. He wasn't appointed.[^1060]
	- Daniel 6 28 and Seder Olam's chronology[^1061] imply that Cyrus and Darius the Mede are two separate people.[^1060]
- Perhaps at times, Darius the Mede refers to Cambyses (he was obviously also part Median, like his father).[^1060]
- According to the Nabonidus Chronicle, Cambyses performed the religious rituals the king of Babylonia does.[^1060]
- Numerous cuneiform tablets refer to Cambyses as the King of Babylon, many of them dated to Cyrus's first year of rule over Babylon.[^1060]
- None of the cuneiform tablets are dated to a second year of Cambyses in Babylon. This fits with Seder Olam who says that Cambyses ruled for only a year.[^1060]
- Darius the Mede is called the son of Achashveirosh. Achashveirosh was another throne name for Cyrus.[^1060]
